# The Atlas


# The Transport Atlas

> **One-line picture.** Almost every equation in this atlas is the same sentence:
> *things move, and nothing is lost.* The rest is bookkeeping: what moves (the flux),
> how it moves (the constitutive law), what it is made of (the state), and what pushes it.

Transport phenomena is the study of things that are **not in equilibrium** and therefore
move. Heat flows down a temperature gradient, salt spreads through water, momentum diffuses
across a shear layer, neutrons leak out of a reactor, probability drifts over a barrier,
traffic jams propagate upstream. The mathematics is astonishingly reusable, which is why
this atlas has a category of *bridges* to finance, biology, images, plasmas and machine learning.

::: epigraph
**πάντα ῥεῖ** · *all things flow*

All things flow — so the only honest way to describe anything is to say what moves, what carries it, and
what is conserved while it does. This atlas writes that sentence fifteen hundred ways; the
[Panta Rhei part](../13-panta-rhei/index.md) is the shortest of them.
:::

## The four levels

Every note is written as a ladder. Read only as far down as you need:

| Level | The question | Audience |
|---|---|---|
| **L1** | *What is it, in everyday words?* No mathematics. | curious beginner |
| **L2** | *Why does it happen, and what is the object?* Mechanism, the term in the equation, the dimensionless number. | undergraduate |
| **L3** | *What does the mathematics guarantee?* Well-posedness, spectra, limits, invariants, asymptotics. | graduate |
| **L4** | *What breaks, what is unsolved, how do you compute it?* Schemes, stability, open problems, structure. | practitioner |

The site is built four times: `dist/` (everything), and `dist/L1`, `dist/L2`, `dist/L3`
(the same notes with the deeper rungs trimmed away). Same URLs, shallower content.

## Universality: the strongest version of the claim

The atlas ends with a part devoted to the claim that this mathematics is not one subject among many but a
*common calculus of redistribution*: [Universality](../11-universality/index.md). Start with
[why everything flows](../11-universality/01-why-everything-flows.md) for the five structures that make a
model a transport model (balance, generator, functional, optimality, symmetry), then use the
[lookup table](../11-universality/02-decision-tree-and-lookup.md) to translate an object from *your* field.
The domain notes cover [mind and cognition](../11-universality/03-cognitive-science-and-mind.md),
[society, economics and language](../11-universality/04-social-behaviour-economics-language.md),
[algorithms](../11-universality/05-computation-and-algorithms.md),
[physics and field theory](../11-universality/06-physics-chemistry-and-field-theory.md),
[inference and information](../11-universality/07-information-statistics-and-inference.md) and
[mathematics itself](../11-universality/08-mathematics-itself.md) — and it ends, deliberately, with
[the limits of the claim](../11-universality/09-limits-of-the-claim.md), because a universality claim that
cannot state its boundaries is not a claim.

## The mechanisms that keep coming back

The atlas now has a part devoted not to *fields* but to **behaviours**: [recurring mechanisms](../12-recurring-mechanisms/index.md).
Nine of them — equilibria, linearisation and stability, bifurcation, nonlinearity and mode coupling, chaos,
criticality, scaling and self-similarity, duality and transforms, noise — each written as a ladder from a
child-sized picture at L1 to what a practitioner computes at L4, with its own suspicion list. The part ends
with the [intersections map](../12-recurring-mechanisms/10-intersections-map.md), which is the *generative*
piece: put two mechanisms in one equation and a named phenomenon appears (solitons, Turing patterns,
stochastic resonance, delay-induced chaos, avalanches), and a
[bibliography](../12-recurring-mechanisms/11-bibliography.md) marked by source type — primary, review,
sceptical — so that every claim can be checked rather than believed.

## Panta Rhei: the theme, taken seriously

Finally, a part named after the oldest version of the claim: [Panta Rhei](../13-panta-rhei/index.md) —
*all things flow*. It reads [Heraclitus' fragments](../13-panta-rhei/01-heraclitus-and-the-river.md) as a
question about identity under replacement, and answers it with the mathematics of this atlas (steady state,
Lagrangian structure, invariant measure). It takes [beauty](../13-panta-rhei/02-beauty-and-the-flow.md)
seriously enough to explain why flow makes patterns the eye trusts, treats
[synchrony](../13-panta-rhei/03-collective-behaviour-and-synchrony.md) as a transport problem for phase
density, gives the [arrow of time](../13-panta-rhei/04-irreversibility-and-the-arrow-of-time.md) its
transport statement, and finishes at the
[crossings](../13-panta-rhei/05-crossings-and-cross-fertilisation.md) — where one field's mechanism solves
another field's problem, including the cases where that went wrong — before closing on
[the river as a model](../13-panta-rhei/06-the-river-as-a-model.md).

Every figure here is generated from a script and audited for legibility (text is never below
8 pt, nothing is clipped, no two labels overlap), and seven of them are **animations** —
the phenomena that *are* motion, numbered A1–A7, which play in the web and single-file editions
and become four-panel filmstrips in print.

The loops are real: homogenisation feeds subsidence, kinetic theory feeds turbulence closures, and optimal transport connects diffusion to geometry.](concept-map.pdf)

## Two habits that repay themselves

> **Trap number one: mistaking a *name* for an *equation*.** "Dispersion", "convection",
> "diffusion" and "absorption" each mean three or four different things in different
> communities. The [words that collide](../09-polysemy/index.md) notes exist to dismantle them.

> **Trap number two: forgetting the dimensions.** Before solving anything, form the
> dimensionless groups. They tell you which terms you may drop, how fine the grid must be,
> and whether your answer will be diffusion-like, wave-like or shock-like.
> See [dimensionless number cards](../10-appendix/02-dimensionless-number-cards.md).

time, so it is never out of date.

# Foundations


# Foundations: the grammar of transport

Everything downstream in this atlas is written in a small vocabulary. Nine notes build it,
in dependency order. If you have never seen a PDE, start at 1 and walk down; if you have,
use the table as a checklist of the language.

| # | Note | The one thing it gives you |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | [Balance laws & flux](01-balance-laws-and-flux.md) | why *every* transport equation is a divergence plus a source — and why the conservative form is non-negotiable |
| 2 | [The material derivative](02-material-derivative.md) | the dictionary between following a parcel and watching a field; where advection comes from |
| 3 | [Constitutive relations](03-constitutive-relations.md) | the closure step: Fick, Fourier, Darcy, Ohm, Newton — and the entropy constraint behind them |
| 4 | [PDE taxonomy & boundary conditions](04-pde-taxonomy-and-boundary-conditions.md) | elliptic / parabolic / hyperbolic, characteristics, well-posedness, and the BC catalogue |
| 5 | [Dimensionless numbers](05-dimensionless-numbers.md) | the ratios that decide which term wins — Pe, Re, Ra, Da, Kn, Nu … |
| 6 | [The spectral view](06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md) | one Fourier transform turns transport into arithmetic; Green functions as propagators |
| 7 | [Entropy & variational structure](07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md) | why transport equations are stable: Lyapunov functionals, maximum principles, gradient flows |
| 8 | [Random walks, Langevin & response](08-random-walks-langevin-response.md) | the microscopic origin of diffusion, the FDT, and why fluctuation and dissipation are one thing |
| 9 | [Linear response & memory](09-linear-response-and-memory.md) | what happens when the past matters: Green–Kubo, Mori–Zwanzig, and the origin of all "effective" models |

## Three ideas to carry everywhere

1. **Conservation is geometry.** Fluxes are vectors; the divergence theorem converts
   "what crosses the boundary" into "what accumulates inside". Nothing else in the subject
   is as reliable as this.
2. **Every closure is a bet.** The balance law is exact; the constitutive law is a model.
   The interesting questions are always about the validity range of the bet — and the
   dimensionless numbers in note 5 are how you check it.
3. **Transport is smoothing, translating, or sharpening.** Parabolic operators smooth
   (diffusion), hyperbolic operators translate or steepen into shocks (advection), and
   source terms amplify or kill (reactions). Recognise which one you are looking at and
   half the analysis is done.

![**Figure 2.** The bookkeeping: a control volume, its flux budget, and the single equation that the rest of the atlas elaborates.](balance-control-volume.pdf)

> **Where to jump first.** If you only read two notes in this atlas, read
> [balance laws & flux](01-balance-laws-and-flux.md) and
> [dimensionless numbers](05-dimensionless-numbers.md). They are the two that transfer
> directly to almost any new problem in any field.

## Going further than second order

The nine notes above cover the classical core. Three more take the same language to **any order, any
dimension and any geometry** — which is what makes the classification claim general rather than a
statement about second-order equations in two variables:

| # | Note | The one thing it gives you |
|---|---|---|
| 10 | [General PDE classification](10-general-pde-classification.md) | the principal symbol; elliptic/hyperbolic/parabolic/dispersive *for any order, any $d$, and for systems*; symmetric hyperbolic, Petrovsky, weakly hyperbolic, constrained systems |
| 11 | [Higher order & dispersive](11-higher-order-and-dispersive.md) | parity and sign rules for third-, fourth- and higher-order operators; instability bands; Cahn–Hilliard, Kuramoto–Sivashinsky, thin films, plate equations |
| 12 | [Dimensions, geometry & manifolds](12-dimensions-geometry-and-manifolds.md) | how $d$ changes Green functions, recurrence and critical dimensions; anisotropic scaling; Laplace–Beltrami on curved spaces; fractals and spectral dimension |

> **Read them in that order.** 10 gives the general classification, 11 shows what changes with order, 12
> shows what changes with dimension and geometry. Together they close the gap that the classical
> two-variable table leaves open — and they are the prerequisites for the
> [universality part](../11-universality/index.md), where the same structures are recognised in fields that
> are not physics at all.

# Balance laws & flux


# Balance laws & flux

> **One-line picture.** Draw any closed surface in the world. Whatever changes inside it must
> be accounted for by what crosses the surface and by what is created or destroyed within it.
>
> **Prerequisites.** None. This is the first note. · **Leads to.**
> [material derivative](02-material-derivative.md), [constitutive relations](03-constitutive-relations.md).

![**Figure 3.** The bookkeeping. A flux $\mathbf{J}$ crossing the boundary $\partial V$ of a control volume $V$, together with volumetric sources and sinks $f$. The integral statement and its local form say exactly the same thing whenever the fields are smooth.](balance-control-volume.pdf)

## L1 · What it is, in words

Imagine a bathtub with a tap and a drain. The amount of water in the tub changes because of
two things only: what comes in and out through the pipes, and what appears or disappears
inside (a leak, say). Nothing else is possible. If you could suddenly have more water than
the pipes deliver, you would have found a new law of physics.

That is the whole idea. Pick *any* quantity — heat, salt, momentum, electric charge,
probability, cars on a road, money in accounts — and the same sentence applies: **the rate of
change inside a region equals the net flow across its boundary plus the net production
inside**. That sentence, written with calculus, is called a *balance law* or *conservation
equation*, and every equation in this atlas is a special case of it.

## L2 · Why the term looks the way it does

Let $c(\mathbf{x},t)$ be the density of "stuff" per unit volume, and $\mathbf{J}$ the flux
vector: the amount crossing unit area per unit time, in a specified direction. For an
arbitrary region $V$ with outward normal $\mathbf{n}$,

$$\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_V c\,\mathrm{d}V
= -\oint_{\partial V}\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n}\,\mathrm{d}A+\int_V f\,\mathrm{d}V .$$

* The minus sign is a convention with content: $\mathbf{n}$ points *out*, so
  $\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n}>0$ means leaking, not gaining.
* $f$ collects everything that is not transport: chemical production, radioactive decay,
  gravity, pressure work, buoyancy — anything that changes the amount without moving it
  across the boundary.

Now apply the divergence theorem $\oint_{\partial V}\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n}\,\mathrm{d}A
=\int_V \nabla\cdot\mathbf{J}\,\mathrm{d}V$. Since $V$ was arbitrary, the integrands must match
pointwise:

$$\boxed{\;\partial_t c+\nabla\cdot\mathbf{J}=f\;}$$

This is the *differential* (local) form. Everything else is a choice of $c$, a choice of
$\mathbf{J}$, and a choice of $f$:

| Quantity $c$ | Flux $\mathbf{J}$ | Source $f$ | Constitutive law |
|---|---|---|---|
| mass concentration | $\mathbf{u}c-D\nabla c$ | reactions | Fick |
| energy (heat) | $\mathbf{u}(\rho c_p T)-k\nabla T$ | $\dot q$, viscous $\Phi$, radiation | Fourier |
| momentum | $\rho\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}-\boldsymbol{\tau}$ | $-\nabla p+\rho\mathbf{g}$ | Newtonian (Newton) |
| electric charge | $\mathbf{J}_{\rm cond}+\rho_e\mathbf{u}$ | — | Ohm |
| probability density | drift $\times\rho$ | — | Fokker–Planck |
| cars per metre | $f(c)=c(1-c)$ | on/off ramps | LWR traffic model |

Notice that the *structure* is identical while the physics differs entirely. That is the
reason this atlas can put batteries, black holes and traffic jams in the same document.

> **Deeper (L3).** Note that the derivation assumed $c$ and $\mathbf{J}$ are differentiable.
> When they are not — at a shock, a phase boundary, a fracture — the *integral* form survives
> and gives a **jump condition** instead (see below). Physicists and engineers therefore
> prefer to start from the integral statement, because it is the one that keeps holding when
> the mathematics of the local form has already broken down.

# The material derivative


# The material derivative

> **One-line picture.** You can describe a river by drifting down it in a boat, or by standing
> on the bank watching the water pass. Both are complete; the material derivative is the
> translation service between them.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [balance laws & flux](01-balance-laws-and-flux.md) · **Leads to.**
> [advection](../02-bulk/01-advection.md)

![**Figure 4.** Two books of one story. Left: parcels followed individually (Lagrangian). Right: fields sampled at fixed points (Eulerian). The material derivative is the exchange rate.](lagrangian-eulerian.pdf)

## L1 · What it is, in words

Put a leaf in a stream. The leaf travels. Now stand on a bridge above the same stream with a
thermometer: the reading drops because *colder water arrived*, not because this water cooled.
Two different rates of change exist — the rate **of a thing** (what the leaf experiences) and
the rate **at a place** (what the instrument reads). The material derivative is the first,
expressed in terms of the second plus the motion.

## L2 · The mathematical object

$$\frac{\mathrm{D}c}{\mathrm{D}t}=\underbrace{\partial_t c}_{\text{local}}+\underbrace{\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla c}_{\text{carried}}.$$

Derivation in one line: follow the parcel, so $c(\mathbf{x}(t),t)$ with $\dot{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{u}$
and the chain rule gives $\partial_t c+\dot{\mathbf{x}}\cdot\nabla c$. **The advection term is
not a new physical effect; it is the chain rule applied to motion.**

| Curve | Fixed | What it shows |
|---|---|---|
| pathline | which parcel | trajectory of one fluid element |
| streamline | which instant | tangent to $\mathbf{u}$ in a frozen snapshot |
| streakline | which source | where dye from one point has gone (what you photograph) |

They coincide only in steady flow; most confusion about "following the flow" is confusion
between these three.

Mass obeys the balance law with $c=\rho$, $\mathbf{J}=\rho\mathbf{u}$, $f=0$:

$$\partial_t\rho+\nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf{u})=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\frac{\mathrm{D}\rho}{\mathrm{D}t}+\rho\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}=0 .$$

A parcel's density falls exactly as fast as the parcel expands, so $\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}=0$
means *the material does not compress* — it is a statement about the substance, not about
speed. (Nozzle flow can be nearly incompressible at 500 m/s; sound in water is compressible
at millimetres per second.)

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

**Reynolds transport theorem.** For a volume moving with the material,

$$\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{V(t)}c\,\mathrm{d}V=\int_{V(t)}\big[\partial_t c+\nabla\cdot(c\mathbf{u})\big]\mathrm{d}V,$$

which is why conservative and advective forms agree for smooth fields and **disagree at
shocks**, where the advective form is simply wrong — see
[conservation laws & shocks](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md).

**Objectivity.** Constitutive laws must not depend on the observer's rotation. Written
naively, $\mathrm{D}\boldsymbol\tau/\mathrm{D}t$ is not objective; the corrections (Jaumann,
Oldroyd) are exactly the corotational terms in viscoelastic models —
[memory & viscoelastic transport](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md).

**Circulation.** For inviscid barotropic flow with conservative body forces, Kelvin's theorem
makes circulation around a material loop constant: vorticity is carried, not created.

## L4 · Numerics, limits, frontiers

* **Eulerian** codes stand on the bank: face fluxes require
  [limiters](../07-numerics/04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md) to keep fronts monotone.
* **Lagrangian** codes (SPH, vortex, PIC) ride the boat: advection is free of numerical
  diffusion, at the cost of particle disorder, remeshing and modelled collisions.
* **Semi-Lagrangian** schemes follow parcels only for the departure step —
  stable at CFL $>1$, but they leak mass unless made conservative, which is fatal for
  climate and reservoir models.
* **ALE** moves the mesh with the interface and keeps the bulk Eulerian: the standard tool
  for free surfaces and [moving boundaries](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md).
* **Open.** Lagrangian data assimilation (updating particle clouds from Eulerian
  observations); objectivity-preserving learned closures.

## Words that collide

> **Advective vs conservative form:** $\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla c$ and $\nabla\cdot(\mathbf{u}c)$
> differ by $c\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}$ — identical for smooth or incompressible fields,
> catastrophic across shocks. **"Convective derivative"** in fluid mechanics means the whole
> $\mathrm{D}/\mathrm{D}t$; in heat transfer "convection" means diffusion *plus* advection.

## Checks

1. Steady flow ($\partial_t\mathbf{u}=0$): can a parcel still accelerate? *(Yes —
   $\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u}\neq 0$ in a nozzle.)*
2. Why is the conservative form preferred in codes that must resolve shocks? *(Jump
   conditions follow from it automatically.)*
3. Where do the corotational terms in Oldroyd-B come from? *(Enforcing frame invariance on a
   tensor.)*

# Constitutive relations


# Constitutive relations (closure)

> **One-line picture.** A balance law says what is conserved but nothing about what the
> substance does. A constitutive law is the empirical bet that closes the gap — and every such
> bet is a bet about a range of validity.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [balance laws & flux](01-balance-laws-and-flux.md) · **Leads to.**
> [diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md), [conduction](../03-microscopic/03-conduction.md),
> [Darcy flow](../02-bulk/08-porous-media-and-darcy.md)

## L1 · What it is, in words

"Nothing is lost" is true but empty: it does not say whether heat crawls or races through
copper. To use the balance law you must add a statement of what materials do when pushed out
of equilibrium. People mill flour down a chute, crowd through a doorway, queue at a checkout:
the *rule of flow* is a property of the material and the situation, not of arithmetic.

Two features are striking: the rules are almost always *flow proportional to a gradient*, and
the constant of proportionality is the only thing distinguishing copper from air.

## L2 · The catalog

$$\text{flux}=-\,(\text{transport property})\times\nabla(\text{potential})$$

| Domain | Law | Coefficient | Typical value |
|---|---|---|---|
| mass: $\mathbf{J}=-D\nabla c$ | Fick (1855) | $D$ | $10^{-9}$ m²/s in water |
| heat: $\mathbf{q}=-k\nabla T$ | Fourier (1822) | $k$; $\alpha=k/\rho c_p$ | 0.6 W/m·K water, 400 copper |
| momentum: $\boldsymbol\tau=\mu(\nabla\mathbf{u}+\nabla\mathbf{u}^{\!\top})$ | Newton/Stokes | $\mu$, $\nu$ | $10^{-3}$ Pa·s water |
| porous: $\mathbf{q}=-(K/\mu)\nabla p$ | Darcy (1856) | permeability $K$ | $10^{-12}$ m² sand |
| charge: $\mathbf{i}=-\sigma\nabla\phi$ | Ohm (1827) | $\sigma$ | $6\times10^7$ S/m copper |
| ions: Nernst–Planck + Soret | — | $D$, $s$ | — |
| radiation (thick limit) | Rosseland | $\kappa$ | — |
| elasticity: $\boldsymbol\sigma=\mathbb{C}:\boldsymbol\varepsilon$ | Hooke | $\mathbb{C}$ | — |

Anisotropy turns scalars into tensors: laminated rock transports far faster along bedding, so
$K$ becomes a symmetric positive-definite matrix, and groundwater dispersion becomes a tensor
aligned with the flow — [shear dispersion](../02-bulk/06-shear-dispersion-taylor-aris.md).

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees (and forbids)

**Entropy production fixes the signs.** Write fluxes and forces as vectors, $\mathbf{J}=L\mathbf{X}$.
The second law demands $\dot S=\mathbf{X}^{\!\top}L\mathbf{X}\ge0$ for every admissible
$\mathbf{X}$, so $L$ must be symmetric positive semidefinite. This one inequality is why
diffusivity, conductivity and viscosity are positive and why flux–force matrices are symmetric.

**Onsager reciprocity (1931).** $L_{ij}=L_{ji}$: cross-effects are forced symmetries, not
optional extras. Soret (a temperature gradient drives mass) pairs with Dufour (a concentration
gradient drives heat); Seebeck pairs with Peltier —
[cross-effects](../03-microscopic/06-cross-effects-and-multicomponent.md).

**Curie's principle.** In an isotropic medium, a linear law cannot couple a scalar force to a
vector flux. Interfaces and anisotropy break this — which is why surface-driven phenomena
(Marangoni, slip, thermophoresis) look exotic.

**Where linearity dies.** Newtonian viscosity above $\mathrm{Wi}\sim1$; Fick when
$\mathrm{Kn}$ is not small; Fourier in ballistic phonon transport; Darcy at high $\mathrm{Re}$
(Forchheimer) or in tight pores (Klinkenberg). Then go kinetic
([Boltzmann](../05-kinetic/01-boltzmann-equation.md),
[Knudsen](../03-microscopic/05-knudsen-and-rarefied-transport.md)) or nonlocal
([memory & viscoelasticity](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md)).

## L4 · Numerics, limits, frontiers

* **Eddy diffusivity is not a law.** Turbulent transport is *closed*, not derived; the
  gradient-diffusion hypothesis fails in buoyant plumes (counter-gradient fluxes). All of
  $k$–$\varepsilon$, LES subgrid models and learned closures live here —
  [turbulence](../02-bulk/05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md).
* **Coefficients are the hard part.** Much practical work is the inverse problem: infer
  $D(\mathbf{x})$ from measured concentrations —
  [inverse problems](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md).
* **Homogenisation** replaces a heterogeneous medium by an effective tensor and states exactly
  when that is legal — [upscaling](../06-beyond/08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md).
* **Frontiers.** Closures that provably respect the second law; nonlocal constitutive models
  for dense suspensions and rare gases; constitutive learning from force fields.

## Words that collide

> **Diffusivity vs conductivity vs permeability:** a diffusivity is property/storage
> ($D=k/\rho c_p$), permeability only becomes a fluid property after dividing by $\mu$.
> **Constitutive law vs equation of state:** flux versus force, or state versus state.

## Checks

1. Why must $D>0$? *(Otherwise entropy decreases and the maximum principle fails.)*
2. When must Fick's law fail? *($\mathrm{Kn}\gtrsim0.1$; ballistic phonons; polymers with memory.)*
3. Which coupling lets a temperature gradient drive a mass flux, and what is its Onsager partner?
   *(Soret; Dufour.)*

## See also

[Diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md) ·
[Conduction](../03-microscopic/03-conduction.md) ·
[Entropy & variational structure](07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md) ·
[Dimensionless numbers](05-dimensionless-numbers.md) ·
[Equation catalogue](../10-appendix/03-operator-and-equation-catalog.md)

# PDE taxonomy & boundary conditions


# PDE taxonomy & boundary conditions

> **One-line picture.** Ask which type your operator is (it decides the physics *and* the
> numerics), then ask what happens on the boundary (it decides whether a unique answer exists).
>
> **Prerequisites.** [balance laws & flux](01-balance-laws-and-flux.md) · **Leads to.**
> [discretisation](../07-numerics/01-discretization-consistency-stability-convergence.md),
> [conservation laws & shocks](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md)

![**Figure 5.** Hyperbolic: characteristics carry data at finite speed and can collide into shocks. Parabolic: information spreads infinitely fast and gradients smooth. Elliptic: no time at all — a pointwise balance of fluxes.](pde-taxonomy.pdf)

## L1 · Three temperaments

* **Elliptic** — *equilibrium*: flux in equals flux out everywhere at once; the domain feels the
  boundary instantly. Steady conduction, $-k\nabla^2T=f$.
* **Parabolic** — *spreading*: smooths and forgets initial detail. Blurring a photo *is*
  integrating a parabolic equation. Diffusion.
* **Hyperbolic** — *signal carrying*: finite-speed characteristics, shocks, no self-smoothing.
  Advection, waves, traffic.

Real models mix all three, and the winner is decided by dimensionless numbers —
[note 5](05-dimensionless-numbers.md).

## L2 · Order, type, characteristics

For $Au_{xx}+2Bu_{xy}+Cu_{yy}+\cdots=0$, the type is read from $\Delta=B^2-AC$:

| $\Delta$ | Type | Characteristics | Prototype | Behaviour |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| $<0$ | elliptic | none real | $\nabla^2u=f$ | smoothing, global influence |
| $=0$ | parabolic | one family | $\partial_tu=Du_{xx}$ | smooths while propagating |
| $>0$ | hyperbolic | two families | $\partial_tu+cu_x=0$ | finite speed, shocks |

Characteristics are curves $x'(t)=\lambda$ on which the PDE becomes an ODE, and they answer
**where data must be given**: hyperbolic — on a non-characteristic curve; parabolic — initial
data plus boundary data for all $t>0$; elliptic — boundary data only.

**Order** is a separate axis: it fixes how many boundary conditions are needed (fourth-order
plate equations want two per edge) and how wide a stencil must be (3 points for second order,
5 for fourth).

## L3 · Well-posedness and the BC catalogue

Hadamard: a solution **exists**, is **unique**, and depends **continuously** on the data.
Break the third and the problem is ill-posed — the backward heat equation is the canonical
example, and it is exactly why deblurring and inversion are hard
([inverse problems](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)).

| Name | Form | Physics |
|---|---|---|
| Dirichlet | $c=c_b$ | wall held at fixed state |
| Neumann | $-\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n}=q_b$ | insulated wall, fixed heater |
| Robin | $-\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n}=h(c-c_\infty)$ | convective transfer, surface reaction |
| Cauchy | state **and** flux | wave initial data (over-determined if elliptic) |
| outflow / radiation | $\partial_tc+U\partial_nc=0$ | open domain (Danckwerts) |
| periodic | $c(0)=c(L)$ | circular topology, ring reactors |
| interface / jump | $\llbracket\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n}\rrbracket=0$ | material contact; state may jump (Henry) |
| free boundary | $s(t)$ unknown | melting, cracks, shorelines |

Three rules: **flux conditions are more physical than value conditions**; **never let an outflow
boundary be re-entered**; **at an interface, flux is always continuous and the state only if
you decide so.**

## L4 · Numerics and frontiers

* Type dictates the scheme: elliptic → sparse solve with
  [multigrid](../07-numerics/06-linear-solvers-and-preconditioning.md); parabolic →
  [implicit stepping](../07-numerics/02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md); hyperbolic →
  [limiters](../07-numerics/04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md).
* **BCs are a leading source of bugs.** Diagnose by conservation: integrate the boundary flux
  and compare with the change of mass inside. A mismatch *is* a bug.
* **Artificial boundaries** must be indistinguishable from infinity: sponge layers,
  characteristic (non-reflecting) conditions, perfectly matched layers.
* **Frontiers.** Boundary theory for [fractional](../06-beyond/03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md)
  and [nonlocal operators](../06-beyond/11-microcontinuum-and-generalized-continua.md), where
  the boundary is no longer a simple closed curve; learned boundary closures; well-posedness of
  tightly coupled multiphysics.

## Words that collide

> **Order** (derivatives) vs **order** (convergence). **Robin** combines state and flux;
> **Cauchy** prescribes both independently. **Type** is local and can *change* where a
> coefficient changes sign (transonic flow) — codes must cope.

## Checks

1. Type of $\partial_tu=D\partial_x^2u$ with $D<0$? *(Ill-posed; this is backward-in-time heat flow.)*
2. You must inject a fixed mass rate through a wall — which family, and why not the other?
   *(Neumann, since a Dirichlet value would make the injected rate grid-dependent.)*

## See also

[Discretisation](../07-numerics/01-discretization-consistency-stability-convergence.md) ·
[Conservation laws & shocks](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md) ·
[Words that collide: flux, source, sink](../09-polysemy/04-absorption-and-sink-lingo.md)

\newpage

# Dimensionless numbers


# Dimensionless numbers

> **One-line picture.** Every dimensionless group is a race between two processes. Who wins
> decides the physics, the grid, and which term you are allowed to throw away.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [balance laws & flux](01-balance-laws-and-flux.md) · **Leads to.** every
> regime discussion in the atlas, [boundary layers](../02-bulk/04-boundary-layers.md)

![**Figure 6.** The timescales of transport over one metre. Molecular transport is hopeless at engineering scale and effortless at micron scale — that gap *is* the subject.](battle-of-timescales.pdf)

## L1 · What it is, in words

Ask "how long does heat take to cross this?" and answer three ways: carried ($L/U$), diffused
($L^2/D$), reacted ($1/k$). Any ratio of two answers is a dimensionless number. Péclet $10^6$
means advection beats diffusion a million to one, so you may neglect diffusion — until the
sharp front it creates matters.

**They are the only things that transfer between scales.** A 1:10 river model gets sediment
transport wrong unless the Péclet number matches; a 1 mm cooling simulation predicts a 1 mm
chip and nothing else.

## L2 · How to form them

Nothing to guess: make the equation dimensionless. With $c=c^*C$, $\mathbf{x}=L\mathbf{x}^*$,
$t=(L/U)t^*$,

$$\partial_{t^*}C+\mathbf{u}^*\!\cdot\!\nabla^*C=\frac{1}{\mathrm{Pe}}\nabla^{*2}C+\mathrm{Da}\,R^*(C),
\qquad \mathrm{Pe}=\frac{UL}{D},\quad \mathrm{Da}=\frac{kL^2}{D}.$$

| Number | Definition | Race | Small | Large |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Péclet $\mathrm{Pe}$ | $UL/D$ | advection / diffusion | smooth, diffusion-limited | boundary layers, sharp fronts |
| Reynolds $\mathrm{Re}$ | $UL/\nu$ | inertia / viscosity | laminar, reversible | turbulence |
| Rayleigh $\mathrm{Ra}$ | $g\beta\Delta TL^3/\nu\alpha$ | buoyancy / dissipation | conduction only | vigorous convection |
| Damköhler $\mathrm{Da}$ | $kL^2/D$ or $kL/U$ | transport / reaction | transport-limited | thin reaction sheets |
| Nusselt $\mathrm{Nu}$ | $hL/k$ | actual / conductive heat | pure conduction | strong convection |
| Sherwood $\mathrm{Sh}$ | $h_mL/D$ | mass analogue of $\mathrm{Nu}$ | pure diffusion | strong advection |
| Prandtl $\mathrm{Pr}$ | $\nu/\alpha$ | momentum / heat diffusion | liquid metals | oils |
| Schmidt $\mathrm{Sc}$ | $\nu/D$ | momentum / mass diffusion | gases | liquids |
| Knudsen $\mathrm{Kn}$ | $\lambda/L$ | mean free path / size | continuum | rarefied, ballistic |
| Mach $\mathrm{Ma}$ | $U/c_s$ | flow / sound | incompressible | shocks |
| Marangoni $\mathrm{Ma}$ | $(\partial_T\sigma)\Delta TL/\mu\alpha$ | surface tension / viscosity | bulk-driven | surface-driven |
| Weissenberg $\mathrm{Wi}$ | $\lambda\dot\gamma$ | polymer memory / flow | Newtonian | elastic |
| Stefan $\mathrm{St}$ | $c_p\Delta T/\Delta h$ | sensible / latent heat | — | phase change dominates |
| Biot $\mathrm{Bi}$ | $hL/k$ | external / internal resistance | isothermal body | strong internal gradients |

## L3 · Why they are more than bookkeeping

* **They license asymptotics.** $\mathrm{Pe}\to\infty$ produces boundary layers and inner/outer
  expansions; $\mathrm{Kn}\to0$ produces the Chapman–Enskog expansion of kinetic theory
  ([Boltzmann](../05-kinetic/01-boltzmann-equation.md)).
* **They change the mathematics, not just the numbers.** Past a critical $\mathrm{Pe}$ at grid
  scale, the steady advection–diffusion problem loses admissible smooth solutions;
  [nonlinear diffusion](../06-beyond/06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md) can lose
  uniqueness. Regime decides theory.
* **They reveal similarity.** When no intrinsic length or time exists, solutions collapse onto
  one profile: $\sqrt{Dt}$, $\sqrt{\alpha t}$ for a Stefan interface, third-power scaling laws
  for convection. Dimensionless analysis is how you *find* the collapse.

## L4 · Regimes decide algorithms

![**Figure 7.** Advection–diffusion–reaction regime map with typical systems marked. Crossing a line changes the required algorithm: explicit schemes become unstable, centred schemes ring, steady solvers stop converging.](regime-map-pe-da.pdf)

| Regime | Numerical consequence |
|---|---|
| diffusion-dominated ($\mathrm{Pe}\lesssim1$) | implicit stepping, centred fluxes |
| advection-dominated ($\mathrm{Pe}\gg1$) | upwind/TVD or spectral; watch numerical diffusion |
| reaction-dominated ($\mathrm{Da}\gg1$) | implicit reactions, operator splitting, front tracking |
| rarefied ($\mathrm{Kn}\gtrsim0.1$) | kinetic solvers: DSMC, lattice Boltzmann, moment closures |
| chaotic ($\mathrm{Re}\gg10^3$) | LES/DNS; resolution follows the Kolmogorov scale |

> **Trap.** A large number can still be irrelevant: a boundary layer may carry almost none of
> the total flux yet dominate the near-wall solution, and a flame sheet liberates nearly all
> the heat inside a millimetre. Regime maps locate the action — *resolution must follow the
> action, not the domain.*

## Checks

1. 1 cm pipe, 1 cm/s water, dye with $D=10^{-9}$ m²/s: $\mathrm{Pe}$? *($10^4$: advection wins.
   The same dye across a 1 µm membrane: $\mathrm{Pe}\sim10^{-5}$, pure diffusion.)*
2. Why does a flame get thinner as pressure rises? *(Reaction and diffusion scale differently
   with density, so the effective $\mathrm{Da}$ grows and the sheet thins.)*

## See also

[Dimensionless number cards](../10-appendix/02-dimensionless-number-cards.md) ·
[Boundary layers](../02-bulk/04-boundary-layers.md) ·
[Key derivations](../10-appendix/04-key-derivations.md)

# The spectral view


# The spectral view: Fourier, Green functions, propagators

> **One-line picture.** Decompose the state into sine waves; linear transport acts on each wave
> independently — shifting its phase, shrinking its amplitude, or both. Everything else is
> superposition.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [balance laws & flux](01-balance-laws-and-flux.md) · **Leads to.**
> [numerical diffusion & dispersion](../07-numerics/03-numerical-diffusion-and-dispersion-error.md)

![**Figure 8.** One operator, three views: oscillation frequency, damping rate, and the amplitude factor of the discrete schemes you will actually use. A numerical scheme *is* a modified dispersion relation.](spectral-view.pdf)

## L1 · Waves are the natural alphabet

Linear equations never mix wavelengths: each evolves alone. So you only need to know what an
operator does to a single $e^{i(kx-\omega t)}$ — *does the phase move?* (does it travel?) and
*does the amplitude decay?* (does it blur?). Diffusion is pure amplitude loss, fastest at large
$k$, which is why blurring wipes out detail and keeps outlines. Advection is pure phase motion,
identical at every wavelength, which is why a carried cloud keeps its shape.

## L2 · The dictionary

Substituting $e^{i(kx-\omega t)}$ replaces $\partial_t\to-i\omega$, $\nabla\to ik$:

| Equation | Dispersion relation | Reading |
|---|---|---|
| $\partial_tc+U\partial_xc=0$ | $\omega=Uk$ | travel at $U$; shape frozen |
| $\partial_tc=D\nabla^2c$ | $\omega=-iDk^2$ | decay $e^{-Dk^2t}$; short waves die first |
| $\partial_t^2u=c^2\nabla^2u$ | $\omega=\pm ck$ | two directions, no damping |
| Cattaneo / telegraph | $\omega^2+i\omega/\tau=c^2k^2$ | waves that damp into diffusion |
| Cahn–Hilliard | $\omega=-iMk^2(\kappa k^2-f'')$ | *growth* at intermediate $k$: patterns |
| advection–diffusion | $\omega=Uk-iDk^2$ | both effects at once, still independent per $k$ |

The **symbol** of an operator is what you get by replacing derivatives: $\mathcal{L}(ik)$. A
point source's response is the **Green function**, and the solution operator is convolution
with it — transport *is* the promotion of an initial bump into a spread profile.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Semigroup:** $G(t_1+t_2)=G(t_1)*G(t_2)$. Memorylessness makes the Gaussian the unique
  convolution kernel for diffusion — the central limit theorem in operator clothing.
* **Reciprocity:** with symmetric coefficients, $G(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=G(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})$
  — Onsager symmetry, optical reciprocity and elastic Green reciprocity are one idea.
* **Positivity:** diffusion kernels are positive and mass-preserving, so solutions inherit
  positivity — the basis of the maximum principle. Advection kernels are shifts and preserve
  sharpness, which is precisely why *no* linear regularisation can fix a shock.
* **Spectral gap:** the slowest mode sets the relaxation time. On a domain of size $L$,
  diffusion relaxes on $L^2/(\pi^2D)$.
* **Irreversibility:** $\omega=-iDk^2$ cannot be run backwards without infinite amplification —
  the Fourier face of ill-posedness.

## L4 · Why this view runs numerics

* **Methods.** Spectral/pseudo-spectral schemes give exponential convergence for smooth data;
  convolution quadrature and FFT solvers exist because these operators *are* convolutions —
  that is how [fractional and nonlocal](../06-beyond/03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md)
  equations are solved quickly.
* **Errors.** A scheme has its own $\omega_{\rm num}(k)$; damping error $=$ numerical diffusion,
  phase error $=$ numerical dispersion —
  [numerics note 3](../07-numerics/03-numerical-diffusion-and-dispersion-error.md).
* **Aliasing.** Nonlinear terms fold unresolved wavenumbers back into resolved ones; dealiasing
  and skew-symmetric forms exist to prevent it.
* **Tools.** Green functions appear as preconditioners, boundary integral kernels and adjoint
  operators for [inverse problems](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md).
* **Frontiers.** Learning solution operators directly
  ([operator learning](../07-numerics/11-machine-learning-and-operator-learning.md)); spectral
  theory of non-normal operators (transition to turbulence).

## Words that collide

> **Dispersion relation** (frequency vs wavenumber) is unrelated to porous-media *dispersion*
> ([words that collide #1](../09-polysemy/01-dispersion.md)). **Kernel** here is an integral
> kernel, not a machine-learning kernel. **Spectral** may mean spectral *method*, spectral
> *density*, or operator spectrum.

## Checks

1. Why does blurring destroy fine detail first? *($e^{-Dk^2t}$ decays fastest at large $k$.)*
2. A scheme with $|G(k)|<1$ only at large $k$: how does it behave? *(Like diffusion — it is
   committing numerical diffusion.)*
3. What makes the diffusion kernel Gaussian? *(Semigroup plus finite variance — the CLT.)*

## See also

[Random walks & Langevin](08-random-walks-langevin-response.md) ·
[Entropy & variational structure](07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md) ·
[Numerical diffusion & dispersion](../07-numerics/03-numerical-diffusion-and-dispersion-error.md)

\newpage

# Entropy & variational structure


# Entropy & variational structure

> **One-line picture.** Every well-posed transport equation carries a quantity that can only
> go downhill. Find it and you know the long-time behaviour, the stability — and often the
> right numerical scheme.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [constitutive relations](03-constitutive-relations.md) · **Leads to.**
> [optimal transport & gradient flows](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md)

## L1 · A ball in a bowl

A ball never climbs up the bowl by itself. Transport equations behave the same way: they
carry a hilltop function — an energy, an entropy, a free energy — that decreases as time runs
forward, and the state ends up parked at its minimum. This explains why a rod relaxes to a
uniform temperature, why mixtures mix or unmix, and why schemes that violate the trend blow up.
It also explains the arrow of time: the film of a diffusion experiment cannot be run backwards
because $\int c\ln c$ refuses to increase.

## L2 · The standard functionals

| Equation | Monotone functional | Identity |
|---|---|---|
| $\partial_tc=D\nabla^2c$ | energy $\tfrac12\int c^2$ | $\tfrac12\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int c^2=-D\int\|\nabla c\|^2$ |
| $\partial_tc=D\nabla^2c$ | entropy $\int c\ln c$ | $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int c\ln c=-\int D\|\nabla c\|^2/c$ |
| reaction–diffusion | $\int V(c)$, logistic potential | $V'=-R(c)$ |
| Cahn–Hilliard | free energy $\int(\tfrac{\kappa}{2}\|\nabla c\|^2+f(c))$ | decreasing in $H^{-1}$ |
| Fokker–Planck | relative entropy $\int p\ln(p/p_{\rm eq})$ | H-theorem |
| porous medium | $\frac{1}{m-1}\int c^m$ | decays in $W_2$ |

**Maximum principle.** Without sources, $\min c\le c(\mathbf{x},t)\le\max c$ for all time, and a
steady elliptic solution attains its extremes on the boundary. Practical use: if your
simulation ever produces concentrations outside the range of the data and boundary values, it
is wrong — no convergence study needed.

## L3 · Gradient flows: the deep structure, and the metric *is* the physics

$$\partial_tc=-\mathcal{M}\frac{\delta F}{\delta c},\qquad\text{but which metric?}$$

| Equation | $F$ | Metric | Consequence |
|---|---|---|---|
| heat / Fokker–Planck | entropy | Wasserstein $W_2$ | diffusion is downhill transport of entropy |
| Allen–Cahn | free energy | $L^2$ | curvature-driven interface motion |
| Cahn–Hilliard | free energy + gradient penalty | $H^{-1}$ | conserved, coarsening patterns |
| porous medium | internal energy | $W_2$ | finite-speed fronts, Barenblatt profiles |

For $W_2$ the theory (Jordan–Kinderlehrer–Otto, 1998) proves **displacement convexity** of the
entropy along geodesics, which yields contraction estimates, uniqueness and convergence of the
JKO minimisation scheme. It is the strongest available tool for nonlinear diffusion and the
bridge to geometry, optimal transport and — unexpectedly — generative machine learning.

> **Trap.** Not everything is dissipative. Oscillatory (Brusselator), excitable and Hamiltonian
> systems have *no* Lyapunov functional; their attractors are limit cycles, and long-time error
> appears as phase drift. Never impose a gradient-flow scheme on a system that lacks one.

## L4 · Numerics and frontiers

* **Entropy stability.** Designing a scheme so a *discrete* entropy inequality holds buys
  nonlinear stability for free — the principle behind entropy-conservative fluxes and
  summation-by-parts methods ([structure-preserving](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)).
* **Discrete maximum principles and positivity** follow from monotone stencils and consistent
  conservative fluxes; violating them gives negative concentrations and reactive blow-up.
* **Implicit steps are minimisations.** Backward Euler on a gradient flow *is* a JKO step;
  convex splitting exploits this for unconditional stability and monotone energy decay.
* **Where it is hard.** Non-convex free energies give exponentially long metastable plateaux in
  coarsening and phase separation: you need rare-event and coarse-graining methods, not finer
  grids. Long-time *statistics* are the frontier, not short-time accuracy.
* **Frontiers.** Variational structure for coupled multiphysics; learned energy functionals
  that preserve known dissipation; entropy-stable kinetic discretisations.

## Words that collide

> **Entropy** means thermodynamic entropy to a physicist, information entropy to a
> probabilist, and any monotone Lyapunov functional to an analyst — the last is the most
> general. **Free energy** may be Helmholtz or Gibbs depending on what is held fixed.

## Checks

1. Why does the maximum principle fail when you add a source? *(Production can push values
   beyond the boundary data; use a comparison principle against the source instead.)*
2. Which metric makes heat flow the steepest descent of entropy? *(Wasserstein $W_2$.)*
3. Name a transport system with no Lyapunov functional. *(Belousov–Zhabotinsky reaction;
   Hamiltonian dynamics.)*

## See also

[Optimal transport & gradient flows](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md) ·
[Structure-preserving discretisations](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md) ·
[Reaction–diffusion & Turing](../04-interaction/04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md) ·
[Nonlinear diffusion](../06-beyond/06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md)

\newpage

# Random walks, Langevin & response


# Random walks, Langevin & the fluctuation–dissipation theorem

> **One-line picture.** Diffusion is not a fundamental law — it is what randomness looks like
> when you refuse to watch the randomness. And friction is inseparable from the noise that
> accompanies it.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md) · **Leads to.**
> [Fokker–Planck](../05-kinetic/03-fokker-planck-and-kramers.md),
> [anomalous diffusion](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md)

![**Figure 9.** Three views of one random walk: the Green function spreading, individual walkers with typical $\pm\sqrt{t}$ spread, and the MSD for normal, sub-, super- and ballistic transport.](diffusion-suite.pdf)

## L1 · Randomness in, smoothness out

A drunkard steps at random, yet the *distribution* of drunkards spreads smoothly and
predictably — exactly as the diffusion equation says. A drop of ink is the same story with
$10^{20}$ walkers. The deterministic PDE emerges from a probabilistic rule, and its coefficient
measures the steps, not the fluid.

## L2 · From a coin flip to Fick's law

A walker hopping $\pm a$ every $\tau$ obeys
$p_{n+1}(x)=\tfrac12p_n(x-a)+\tfrac12p_n(x+a)$, and the limit $a^2/2\tau\to D$ gives

$$\partial_tp=D\,\partial_x^2p,\qquad\langle x^2\rangle=2Dt\ \text{(1-D)},\quad
D=\frac{a^2}{2\tau}.$$

That limit *is* Fick's second law, and the Green function is Gaussian because of the central
limit theorem. Langevin keeps the dynamics instead of the distribution:

$$m\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{\mathrm{d}t}=-\gamma v+\xi(t),\qquad
\langle\xi(t)\xi(t')\rangle=2\gamma k_BT\,\delta(t-t').$$

Drag and random kicks are **not independent**: whatever removes energy must also supply it. The
overdamped limit gives the **Einstein relation** $D=k_BT/\gamma$, and with Stokes drag the
**Stokes–Einstein** formula $D=k_BT/(6\pi\mu a)$ for $\mathrm{Re}\ll1$.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Fokker–Planck.** The Langevin equation implies
  $\partial_tp=-\nabla\!\cdot\!(p\mathbf{F}/\gamma)+\nabla\!\cdot\!(D\nabla p)$, whose steady
  state is Boltzmann's $p\propto e^{-V/k_BT}$: detailed balance in one line.
* **Fluctuation–dissipation theorem.** The noise spectrum is fixed by the dissipation and the
  temperature (Johnson–Nyquist noise from resistance, Brownian motion from viscosity), which
  is also the microscopic origin of Onsager reciprocity —
  [linear response](09-linear-response-and-memory.md).
* **Second law from reversibility.** Trajectories are reversible; entropy growth is a statement
  about distributions and coarse-graining. Loschmidt's paradox dissolves here.
* **Anomalies are generic.** Heavy-tailed waiting times give subdiffusion, heavy-tailed jumps
  give superdiffusion, correlated velocities give ballistic motion —
  [CTRW](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md).

## L4 · Numerics and frontiers

* **Integrators.** Euler–Maruyama (strong order 1/2), Milstein (order 1). Decide whether you
  need strong convergence (trajectories) or weak (statistics) — it changes the scheme.
* **Itô vs Stratonovich.** With multiplicative noise the two conventions differ by a drift term.
  Choose by how the noise arises in the physical limit, then stay consistent.
* **Monte Carlo cost** is $N^{-1/2}$ in *any* dimension: particle methods win in high dimensions
  and lose in low ones ([particle methods](../07-numerics/09-particle-and-monte-carlo-methods.md)).
* **Rare events** (escape, nucleation, failure) live in exponential tails; direct simulation
  never sees them — importance sampling, forward flux sampling and
  [Kramers theory](../05-kinetic/03-fokker-planck-and-kramers.md) are the tools.
* **Machine learning.** Score-based generative models are reverse-time diffusion SDEs: this
  atlas's Fokker–Planck, entropy and optimal-transport theory is their foundation.

## Checks

1. Why can $D$ and the friction not be chosen independently? *(FDT: both come from the same
   molecular collisions, $D=k_BT/\gamma$.)*
2. A 1 µm bead in water at 300 K: how long to diffuse one micron? *($D\approx4\times10^{-13}$
   m²/s, so tens of seconds — the reason micron-scale biology needs active transport.)*

## See also

[Diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md) ·
[Brownian motion & Einstein relations](../03-microscopic/02-brownian-motion-and-einstein-relations.md) ·
[Fokker–Planck & Kramers](../05-kinetic/03-fokker-planck-and-kramers.md) ·
[Linear response & memory](09-linear-response-and-memory.md)

# Linear response & memory


# Linear response & memory

> **One-line picture.** Kick a material gently and it responds linearly — but not instantly.
> The response is remembered, and the memory is a correlation function of the microscopic
> fluctuations you chose to ignore.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [random walks & Langevin](08-random-walks-langevin-response.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [viscoelastic transport](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md),
> [homogenisation](../06-beyond/08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md)

## L1 · Materials remember

Push honey and let go: it keeps moving. Cool a polymer melt locally: it over-relaxes before
settling. In both cases the flux is not set by the gradient *now*, but by an integral over the
gradients that came before:

$$\mathbf{J}(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{t}M(t-t')\,\mathbf{X}(t')\,\mathrm{d}t' .$$

$M$ is a **memory kernel**. Fick's law, Fourier's law and Newton's law of viscosity are the
special case $M\propto\delta(t-t')$ — an *instantaneous* response. That is a modelling choice,
not a law of nature.

## L2 · Green–Kubo: transport coefficients are correlation functions

The coefficients you measure macroscopically can be computed from equilibrium fluctuations:

| Coefficient | Green–Kubo formula |
|---|---|
| self-diffusion | $D=\frac13\int_0^\infty\langle\mathbf{v}(0)\!\cdot\!\mathbf{v}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t$ |
| shear viscosity | $\eta=\frac{V}{k_BT}\int_0^\infty\langle\sigma_{xy}(0)\sigma_{xy}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t$ |
| thermal conductivity | $k=\frac{V}{3k_BT^2}\int_0^\infty\langle\mathbf{q}(0)\!\cdot\!\mathbf{q}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t$ |
| electrical conductivity | $\sigma=\frac{V}{3k_BT}\int_0^\infty\langle\mathbf{j}(0)\!\cdot\!\mathbf{j}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t$ |

Three consequences: **transport *is* fluctuation** (no fluctuations, no transport); **equilibrium
simulations predict nonequilibrium behaviour** (no gradients required); **correlation functions
are the universal currency**, which is why molecular dynamics measures $D$, $\eta$, $k$ by
integrating autocorrelations. Fourier transforming $M$ gives frequency-dependent coefficients
$D(\omega)$, $\eta(\omega)$ — real part is dissipation, imaginary part reactive response, and
causality ties them together through Kramers–Kronig.

## L3 · Mori–Zwanzig: where effective equations come from

Project the full $10^{23}$-dimensional dynamics onto a few slow variables and you get, *exactly*,

$$\partial_t(\text{slow})=\underbrace{\text{Markovian drift}}_{\text{expected}}
+\underbrace{\int_0^tK(t-s)\,(\text{slow})(s)\,\mathrm{d}s}_{\text{memory}}
+\underbrace{\mathcal{F}(t)}_{\text{noise}},\qquad \text{memory}\leftrightarrow\text{noise by FDT}.$$

So: **you never obtain a closed Markovian equation for the slow variables without either
losing information, or accepting a memory kernel plus noise.** Every subgrid model, eddy
viscosity, effective diffusivity and coarse-grained parameter is an approximation to $K$ and
$\mathcal{F}$. The FDT says which approximations are legal — and why
[homogenised and stochastic models](../06-beyond/08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md) contain
noise even when the original problem had none.

## L4 · Numerics, limits, frontiers

* **Estimating kernels from data.** Integrate equilibrium time-correlation functions over long
  molecular-dynamics runs; the *tail* dominates the error, so use windowing or
  Einstein–Helfand estimators rather than naive trapezoid integration.
* **Power-law memory $=$ fractional derivatives.** $M(t)\sim t^{-\alpha}$ turns the convolution
  into a [fractional operator](../06-beyond/03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md): the origin
  of fractional viscoelasticity, fractional diffusion and
  [history-dependent rheology](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md).
* **Cost of memory.** A convolution in time is $O(N^2)$; sum-of-exponentials, fast-convolution
  and FFT schemes reduce it to $O(N\log N)$ — standard in fractional solvers.
* **Markovianisation.** A memory kernel that is a sum of exponentials can be replaced exactly by
  a few auxiliary variables (generalised Maxwell, Prony series, hidden Markov), making the
  problem local in time again. This is the workhorse of practical viscoelastic simulation.
* **Frontiers.** Learning memory kernels and transfer operators from data (Koopman theory);
  non-Markovian turbulence and climate closures; FDT-respecting learned closure operators
  ([ML for transport](../07-numerics/11-machine-learning-and-operator-learning.md)).

## Checks

1. Why must the Green–Kubo formula use *equilibrium* fluctuations? *(Linear response relates
   nonequilibrium transport to equilibrium correlations; that is the content of the FDT.)*
2. When is a plain diffusion equation legitimate for a viscoelastic solvent? *(When the kernel
   decays much faster than any observation time, so $M\approx$ const.)*
3. What extra objects appear when you eliminate a fast variable? *(A memory integral and a noise
   term, tied together by an FDT.)*

## See also

[Random walks & Langevin](08-random-walks-langevin-response.md) ·
[Memory & viscoelastic transport](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md) ·
[Homogenisation & upscaling](../06-beyond/08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md) ·
[Nonlocal & fractional operators](../06-beyond/03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md)

# General PDE classification


# General classification: any order, any dimension

> **One-line picture.** Forget the discriminant formula for two variables: the general classification is
> *spectral*. Take the highest-derivative part of the operator, replace derivatives by a wavevector, and
> ask what that polynomial does.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [PDE taxonomy & boundary conditions](04-pde-taxonomy-and-boundary-conditions.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Higher order & dispersive](11-higher-order-and-dispersive.md),
> [Dimensions & geometry](12-dimensions-geometry-and-manifolds.md),
> [Integrable systems](../06-beyond/14-integrable-systems-and-solitons.md)

## L1 · Why the two-variable rule is not enough

The familiar rule — elliptic if $B^2-AC<0$, parabolic if $=0$, hyperbolic if $>0$ — applies to
**second-order, linear, two-variable, constant-coefficient** equations. Real problems are routinely none
of those: elasticity and Maxwell are second-order *systems* in three dimensions; Navier–Stokes is
nonlinear; the plate equation is fourth order; Korteweg–de Vries is third order; Cahn–Hilliard is fourth
order; Einstein's field equations are a *constrained* system; the Schrödinger equation is first order in
time with complex coefficients. Classification must survive all of that.

The recipe has three steps and uses only the **highest-order (principal) part** — lower-order terms never
change the type, only the behaviour.

## L2 · The general recipe

Write a linear operator of order $m$ as $L=\sum_{|\alpha|\le m}a_\alpha(\mathbf{x})\partial^\alpha$ and
define the **principal symbol**

$$\sigma_L(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{k})=\sum_{|\alpha|=m}a_\alpha(\mathbf{x})(i\mathbf{k})^\alpha ,$$

a homogeneous polynomial of degree $m$ in the wavevector. Everything follows from this one object:

| Property of $\sigma_L$ | Type | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| $\sigma_L\neq0$ for all real $\mathbf{k}\neq0$ | **elliptic** | interior smoothness; boundary-value problems; infinite signal speed |
| real distinct roots in $\tau$ for all $\mathbf{k}$ | **strictly hyperbolic** | real characteristics; well-posed Cauchy problem; finite speed; shocks |
| real roots, possibly repeated | **weakly hyperbolic** | well-posedness needs the lower-order terms to cooperate |
| complex roots all with $\mathrm{Im}\le0$ | **parabolic** (Petrovsky sense) | instantaneous smoothing in some directions |
| roots crossing into the lower half-plane | **ill-posed** (Hadamard) | tiny data blow up — a sign error, usually |
| $\sigma_L$ real in some cone, complex outside | **mixed / variable type** | transonic flow, Tricomi equation, degenerate edges |

**Characteristics** are the surfaces $\varphi=0$ with $\sigma_L(\mathbf{x},\nabla\varphi)=0$: data may be
prescribed on non-characteristic surfaces, and singularities propagate along characteristics. In $d$
dimensions a characteristic set is a *hypersurface*, and its geometry — not merely its existence —
controls the solution.

**Order matters in ways the classical labels hide:**

| Order | Prototype | What is genuinely new |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | advection, Liouville, Schrödinger (complex) | characteristics are trajectories; systems already interesting |
| 2 | diffusion, wave, elasticity | the classical table |
| 3 | KdV, Airy, Boussinesq | **dispersive**: wave speed depends on $k$ |
| 4 | biharmonic, Cahn–Hilliard, Kuramoto–Sivashinsky | two boundary conditions per edge; stiff; unstable bands |
| 5–6 | KdV hierarchy, thin-film and phase-field-crystal models | delicate well-posedness; isolated unstable wavenumber bands |
| non-integer | fractional diffusion | nonlocal: no local characteristics at all |
| integro-differential | Boltzmann, radiative transfer | classified by symbol *and* kernel |

**Systems.** For $u_t+\sum_jA_j(\mathbf{x})u_{x_j}=B$, look at
$\det(\tau I+\sum_jA_jk_j)=0$:

* **Symmetric hyperbolic** (Friedrichs): all $A_j$ symmetric ⇒ energy estimates, well-posed Cauchy
  problem, finite propagation speed. Most of physics fits: Maxwell, elasticity, Euler, MHD, and
  Einstein's equations in a suitable gauge.
* **Strictly hyperbolic** (Leray–Ohya): real distinct eigenvalues ⇒ well-posed with variable
  coefficients, no derivative loss.
* **Petrovsky parabolic**: diffusion–reaction systems, Navier–Stokes in the right variables.
* **Constrained symmetric hyperbolic**: $\nabla\!\cdot\!\mathbf B=0$, the Einstein constraints,
  incompressibility. Constraints propagate if they hold initially — hence projection, constrained
  transport and constraint-damping in codes.
* **Nonlinear** equations are classified by *linearising about a solution*; the type may change where
  coefficients degenerate, and that is exactly where the interesting mathematics lives (shock
  formation, sonic lines, turning points).

## L3 · What each type buys you (the well-posedness hierarchy)

* **Elliptic** — interior regularity as good as the data; maximum principles; Fredholm alternative;
  unique solvability with boundary data; no real characteristics carrying information.
* **Hyperbolic** — finite propagation speed; energy conservation; local well-posedness in Sobolev
  spaces; smooth solutions may break in finite time (shocks), after which only weak, entropy-selected
  solutions exist ([shocks](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md)).
* **Parabolic** — instantaneous smoothing, interior analyticity for $t>0$, maximum principles,
  semigroup well-posedness; the *backward* problem is ill-posed.
* **Dispersive** (odd-order skew-adjoint part: KdV, Schrödinger) — no smoothing and no shocks:
  oscillations spread to infinity. Strichartz estimates, infinitely many conservation laws, often
  complete integrability ([solitons](../06-beyond/14-integrable-systems-and-solitons.md)).
* **Weakly hyperbolic and degenerate** — the hardest and most physical case: transonic flow, shallow
  water at the shoreline, relativistic fluids at critical speed. Well-posedness becomes a research
  question, and numerics need extra dissipation and entropy fixes.
* **Cauchy–Kowalevski** — analytic data on a non-characteristic surface give a unique analytic
  solution *whatever the type*. Classification is thus about **information flow and stability**, not
  about mere existence.

## L4 · Numerics: the type dictates the algorithm

| Type | Discretisation |
|---|---|
| elliptic | sparse solve; multigrid; exploit symmetry |
| parabolic | implicit or fractional-step time integration; positivity by construction |
| strictly hyperbolic | conservative flux form, characteristic upwinding, limiters |
| weakly hyperbolic / mixed | added numerical dissipation, entropy fixes, careful boundaries |
| constrained systems | projection, constrained transport, staggered/compatible grids |
| higher order | compact/WENO stencils, $C^1$ or mixed elements, split off the stiff part |
| dispersive | high-order or spectral; low-order dissipation destroys the physics |
| nonlocal / fractional | FFT/Toeplitz hierarchical methods; Monte Carlo |

Two habits that save projects: **(1) classify before you discretise** — a mixed-type problem solved
with a parabolic scheme yields nothing but artefacts; **(2) test the symbol** — insert a plane wave into
the *discrete* operator and compare with $\sigma_L$; the difference is your error, and its sign tells you
whether you accidentally added diffusion or dispersion
([spectral view](06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md)).

## Checks

1. What type is KdV, $u_t+u_{xxx}=0$, by the elliptic/parabolic/hyperbolic trichotomy — and what is the
   honest answer? *(Its symbol is purely imaginary: it is none of them; it is dispersive, and the
   classical trichotomy simply does not cover it.)*
2. Why does the discriminant test fail for Navier–Stokes? *(Nonlinear, a system, in three dimensions —
   and classification is a local, linear notion anyway.)*
3. Give an equation whose type changes inside the domain. *(The Tricomi equation $y\,u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0$;
   transonic flow past a wing.)*
4. What does "symmetric hyperbolic" buy you that "strictly hyperbolic" does not? *(Energy estimates for
   *nonlinear* problems and variable coefficients — the reason Friedrichs systems are the workhorse of
   mathematical relativity, MHD and elasticity.)*

## See also

[PDE taxonomy & boundary conditions](04-pde-taxonomy-and-boundary-conditions.md) ·
[Higher order & dispersive](11-higher-order-and-dispersive.md) ·
[Dimensions & geometry](12-dimensions-geometry-and-manifolds.md) ·
[Variational principles & Noether](../06-beyond/15-variational-principles-and-noether.md) ·
[Operator & equation catalogue](../10-appendix/03-operator-and-equation-catalog.md)

# Higher order & dispersive transport


# Higher order & dispersive transport

> **One-line picture.** Add more derivatives and you get richer behaviour: *odd* orders make waves
> without damping; *even* orders smooth or sharpen depending on the sign. Above second order there is no
> classical trichotomy — there are signs, parities and instability bands.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [General PDE classification](10-general-pde-classification.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Integrable systems & solitons](../06-beyond/14-integrable-systems-and-solitons.md),
> [Free boundaries & phase field](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md)

## L1 · Where high-order operators come from

Second-order equations describe flux proportional to a gradient. Higher orders arise whenever the
*flux itself* wants to be smooth, or when the physics is curvature-driven or dispersive:

| Source of higher order | Mechanism | Prototype |
|---|---|---|
| surface energy of an interface | energy penalises curvature ⟹ $\int\kappa^2$ | Cahn–Hilliard (4th), Willmore flow |
| plates and shells | bending stiffness scales as $\partial^4$ | biharmonic plate, thin-film equations |
| surface diffusion | matter moves along a surface to reduce area | Mullins–Sekerka, evaporation fronts (4th) |
| dispersive waves | linear wave speed depends on $k$ | KdV (3rd), Boussinesq, Airy |
| two competing scales | inertial range + dissipation | Kuramoto–Sivashinsky (4th) |
| crystalline order | density waves with one preferred wavelength | phase-field crystal (6th) |
| numerical hyper-diffusion | stabilising a scheme | $-\varepsilon\Delta^2$ — a *model*, not physics |

The last row is a warning: higher-order terms are the easiest way to stabilise a code and the easiest way
to change the physics.

## L2 · Classification by parity and sign

For a constant-coefficient model $u_t=a_m\partial_x^m u$ the dispersion relation is
$\omega=a_m i^{m+1}k^m$, so the parity of $m$ decides the *character* of the operator:

| $m$ | $\omega(k)$ | Character | Well-posed when |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | $a_1k$ | advection / waves, nondispersive | any real $a_1$ |
| 2 | $-ia_2k^2$ | **dissipative** (diffusion) | $a_2>0$ |
| 3 | $a_3k^3$ | **dispersive** (no damping, no growth) | any real $a_3$ |
| 4 | $-ia_4k^4$ | **hyper-diffusive** | $a_4>0$ |
| 5 | $a_5k^5$ | dispersive, higher-order effects | any real $a_5$ |
| 6 | $-ia_6k^6$ | hyper-diffusive | $a_6>0$ |

General rule: **odd orders are dispersive (real $\omega$), even orders are dissipative (imaginary
$\omega$), and the sign of an even-order coefficient decides physically sensible vs explosive at short
wavelengths.** This is a special case of the **Lax–Mizohata condition**: a higher-order evolution
equation is well-posed iff its symbol's eigenvalues satisfy $\mathrm{Re}\,\lambda(\mathbf{k})\le C$ for
all real $\mathbf{k}$. Combine orders and you get the physics:

$$\underbrace{u_t+uu_x}_{\text{steepening}}\underbrace{+\,u_{xxx}}_{\text{dispersion}}=0
\ \text{(KdV)},\qquad
\underbrace{u_t+uu_x+u_{xx}}_{\text{unstable band}}+u_{xxxx}=0\ \text{(Kuramoto–Sivashinsky)} .$$

* **Balancing two powers of $k$ picks a length scale.** In Cahn–Hilliard,
  $\omega(k)=-Mk^2(\kappa k^2-f'')$ is positive only for $0<k<\sqrt{f''/\kappa}$: the **spinodal band**,
  whose width sets the pattern wavelength. In Kuramoto–Sivashinsky, growth $k^2$ competes with damping
  $k^4$: a band of unstable modes, hence spatiotemporal chaos. In thin-film flow the balance involves
  $h^3$ and produces a *finite-wavelength* dewetting instability.
* **Conserved higher-order flows** (Cahn–Hilliard, surface diffusion) are gradient flows in $H^{-1}$;
  their fourth-order structure preserves mass exactly and makes them stiff.
* **Dispersive hierarchies** (KdV, mKdV, fifth-order KdV) form infinite families with soliton solutions
  ([next note](../06-beyond/14-integrable-systems-and-solitons.md)).

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees (and where it is delicate)

* **Higher-order diffusion still smooths, but the scaling changes.** Long waves relax with
  $\tau\sim k^{-4}$ (or $k^{-6}$) instead of $k^{-2}$: better damping of small scales, slower washing-out
  of large ones. Conserved fourth-order flows are *stiff* and their smoothing is slow at large scales —
  which is why coarsening takes so long.
* **The maximum principle is lost above second order.** Positivity of biharmonic problems holds only
  under special boundary conditions (the classical Boggio result for the clamped plate) and fails for
  others: no positivity, no comparison, no automatic bounds. Discrete schemes must enforce positivity
  and mass conservation *explicitly* ([entropy & variational structure](07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md)).
* **Instability is band-limited.** Linear analysis gives a most-unstable wavenumber $k_{\max}$; the
  nonlinear theory then selects a pattern and a coarsening law (Cahn–Hilliard: $L\sim t^{1/3}$;
  Kuramoto–Sivashinsky: spatiotemporal chaos with an attractor dimension growing with the domain size).
* **Lax–Mizohata is the general well-posedness test**: the symbol's eigenvalues must not grow. The
  failures are instructive — negative diffusion ($u_t=-Du_{xx}$), backward biharmonic
  ($u_t=-\Delta^2u$), and fourth-order dispersion with the wrong sign are all ill-posed at short
  wavelengths, and a numerical scheme that "fixes" them has silently solved a different problem.
* **Dispersive equations conserve more than energy.** KdV and its relatives have infinitely many
  conservation laws and (often) complete integrability; the linearised dynamics spreads any localised
  disturbance as $t^{-1/3}$ in $L^\infty$ (Airy-type decay), which is why dispersive numerical schemes
  must have very low dissipation to see the physics.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Stiffness scales as $\Delta x^m$.** An explicit scheme for a fourth-order operator needs
  $\Delta t\lesssim\Delta x^4/(\text{const})$: spectral, implicit or exponential integrators are the
  practical options; splitting the conservative part from the stiff part keeps mass exact.
* **Boundary conditions double.** Two conditions per edge, or a **mixed formulation**:
  Cahn–Hilliard as $u_t=\nabla\!\cdot\!(M\nabla\mu)$, $\mu=f'(u)-\kappa^2\Delta u$ with two second-order
  equations. But be careful — *splitting changes the admissible boundary data*, so it changes the
  physics unless the boundaries are periodic or natural.
* **Patterning needs resolution of the most-unstable scale**, and coarsening needs long-time accuracy:
  adaptive meshes plus energy-stable (convex-splitting) schemes are the standard pair
  ([structure-preserving schemes](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)).
* **Hyperviscosity is a choice you must declare.** Replacing $-\nu\Delta$ by $-\nu_4\Delta^2$ stabilises
  high wavenumbers but flattens spectra and moves dissipation to the wrong scales; if you use it, report
  it and check the inert range.
* **Benchmarks**: Zabusky–Kruskal soliton collision and the KdV invariants; Cahn–Hilliard coarsening
  exponent; the Kuramoto–Sivashinsky attractor statistics; thin-film dewetting front speed
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).

## Checks

1. Which parity of leading order gives dispersion, and which gives dissipation? *(Odd ⟹ dispersive
   (real $\omega$, no growth); even ⟹ dissipative with the sign deciding smoothing versus blow-up.)*
2. Why does Cahn–Hilliard have a pattern wavelength? *(Balance of $k^2$ (destabilising, from $f''$) and
   $\kappa k^4$ (stabilising) defines a band $0<k<\sqrt{f''/\kappa}$.)*
3. Why can you not replace a biharmonic boundary condition by two Neumann ones? *(It is a different
   problem: the solutions, spectra and energy functionals all change.)*

## See also

[General PDE classification](10-general-pde-classification.md) ·
[Integrable systems & solitons](../06-beyond/14-integrable-systems-and-solitons.md) ·
[Free boundaries, level sets & phase field](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md) ·
[Nonlinear diffusion & porous medium](../06-beyond/06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md)
