# Dimensions, geometry & manifolds


# Dimensions, geometry & manifolds

> **One-line picture.** The *dimension* is not a detail: it changes recurrence, Green functions,
> critical exponents and which nonlinearities matter. And when space is curved, the Laplacian becomes the
> Laplace–Beltrami operator and transport follows the geometry.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [The spectral view](06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md),
> [General classification](10-general-pde-classification.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Geoscience & climate](../08-bridges/04-geoscience-and-climate.md),
> [MHD & relativistic transport](../06-beyond/13-mhd-and-relativistic-transport.md)

## L1 · Dimension is physics, not bookkeeping

Three examples of dimension deciding the answer:

* **Random walks return home in 1-D and 2-D but not in 3-D** (Pólya). The drunkard finds his way back;
  the diffusing bird does not. Transport in low dimensions is *recurrent*: boundaries, boundaries layers
  and pollution matter qualitatively differently in a thin film, a 2-D membrane or a 3-D bulk.
* **Green functions change their shape**: the steady Laplacian's response to a point source is
  $G\sim r^{2-d}$, i.e. linear in 2-D (logarithmic, actually $\sim\ln r$) and $1/r$ in 3-D — which is why
  potential theory, electrostatics and groundwater drawdown look so different in different dimensions.
* **Critical dimensions exist.** Nonlinear PDEs have dimensions where the nonlinearity stops being
  relevant: the semilinear heat equation with $u^p$ has $p_c=1+2/d$; the focusing nonlinear Schrödinger
  equation has a mass-critical dimension; Keller–Segel blow-up depends on $d$; KPZ has an upper critical
  dimension 4. Above the critical dimension, mean-field/linear behaviour wins.

## L2 · The formulas that carry $d$

| Object | General dimension $d$ |
|---|---|
| heat kernel | $G=(4\pi Dt)^{-d/2}\exp(-|\mathbf{x}|^2/4Dt)$ |
| mean squared displacement | $\langle r^2\rangle=2dDt$ |
| steady Laplacian Green function | $G\propto r^{2-d}$ ($d\neq2$), $\ \propto\ln r$ ($d=2$) |
| longest relaxation time (domain $L$) | $\tau\sim L^2/(\pi^2 D)$ (independent of $d$) |
| turbulent cascade scale separation | $L/\eta\sim\mathrm{Re}^{3/4}$ (independent of $d$, but intermittency is not) |
| Péclet/Rayleigh/Damköhler | definitions unchanged, but the *operator's* behaviour changes with $d$ |
| finite-difference stability | advective $\Delta t\le\Delta x/(|u|\sqrt d)$, diffusive $\Delta t\le\Delta x^2/(2dD)$ |
| Green–Kubo $D$ (3-D) | $\frac13\int\langle\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{v}\rangle\mathrm{d}t$ (the $\frac13$ is $1/d$) |

**Anisotropic scaling.** When time and space scale differently, write $t\sim x^z$ and call $z$ the
*dynamic exponent*: diffusion $z=2$, waves $z=1$, ballistic motion $z=1$, anomalous
$\langle x^2\rangle\sim t^\alpha$ gives $z=2/\alpha$, and higher-order operators give $z=m$
([higher order](11-higher-order-and-dispersive.md)). Systems with two competing scales can show
Lifshitz-type behaviour with *two* dynamic exponents; this is the language in which growth models,
turbulence and glasses are compared.

**Geometry.** On a curved surface or manifold, $\nabla\to\nabla_g$, $\nabla^2\to\Delta_g$ (the
Laplace–Beltrami operator), and the heat kernel knows the curvature:

$$G(t,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sim(4\pi t)^{-d/2}e^{-d_g^2/4t}\big(1+\tfrac16\mathrm{Ric}\,t+\cdots\big),$$

where $d_g$ is the geodesic distance and the correction involves the Ricci curvature. Consequences:
diffusion on a sphere is *not* diffusion in a box (no boundary, but two poles, and spherical harmonics
replace Fourier modes); coasts, ridges and curvature concentrate or dilute transport; on a sphere the
Laplacian's spectrum is $\ell(\ell+1)/R^2$, which sets climate modes and Rossby-wave scales.

| Setting | Geometry | What replaces Fourier |
|---|---|---|
| flat $d$-D box | Euclidean | sines/cosines |
| sphere / ocean / atmosphere | $\mathbb{S}^2$ | spherical harmonics |
| membrane, interface | surface with curvature | Laplace–Beltrami eigenfunctions |
| curved spacetime (relativity) | Lorentzian manifold | mode decomposition in a foliation |
| porous fractal medium | fractal, no smooth metric | spectral dimension |
| network | graph | graph Laplacian eigenmodes |

**Fractals and spectral dimension.** On a fractal, the *walk dimension* replaces the Euclidean one:
$P(t)\sim t^{-d_s/2}$ defines the **spectral dimension** $d_s$ (Sierpiński gasket $d_s\approx1.365$),
and transport is generically anomalous ($\alpha=d_s/d_w<1$). This is how a long-tailed
[CTRW](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md) becomes geometry rather than randomness. Graphs work
the same way: the return probability of a random walk measures an effective dimension, and small-world
networks have $d_s\sim\infty$ in the short-time sense (fast mixing, slow saturation).

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Recurrence and transience.** In $d\le2$ a random walker returns to its neighbourhood infinitely
  often; in $d\ge3$ it may never come back. Practical consequence: in 2-D, local release is felt
  *everywhere eventually* (logarithmic potential), while in 3-D it can be diluted away forever. The
  critical mass in [Keller–Segel](../04-interaction/06-chemotaxis-and-keller-segel.md), $8\pi/\chi$, is
  literally the coefficient of the 2-D log potential — a blow-up threshold written by the dimension.
* **Critical dimensions.** Semilinear diffusion $u_t=\Delta u+u^p$ has critical exponent $p_c=1+2/d$;
  NLS has mass- and energy-critical dimensions; KPZ has upper critical dimension 4; the Ising/$\phi^4$
  story has $d_c=4$. Above $d_c$ the nonlinearity is *irrelevant* in the renormalisation-group sense and
  mean-field behaviour returns — the cleanest way to see that "dimension" is a mathematical variable,
  not a physical accident.
* **Dimension enters the exponents explicitly.** The porous-medium Barenblatt profile has
  $\alpha=d/(d(m-1)+2)$; the diffusion-limited aggregation and coarsening exponents differ above and
  below their critical dimensions; biological scaling laws (metabolic rate $\propto M^{3/4}$, Kleiber)
  come from network *dimensions*, not from physics constants.
* **The heat kernel *is* the geometry.** The short-time expansion of $\int G$ recovers the area, the
  total curvature and (in 2-D) the Euler characteristic of a surface, and the long-time decay rate is the
  spectral gap, which is bounded by the Cheeger isoperimetric constant: **transport sees shape** — mixing
  time is geometry, and bottlenecks are curvature and topology.
* **Curvature focuses and defocuses.** Positive curvature concentrates heat (shorter return times);
  negative curvature spreads it (exponential volume growth → very slow return). This is the transport
  picture behind chaotic billiards, Anosov flows and the ergodicity of geodesic motion.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Spheres and manifolds need their own grids.** Cubed-sphere or icosahedral grids avoid the polar
  singularity; spherical harmonics are spectrally exact but awkward with sharp features; geodesic
  finite elements (cotangent Laplacian on triangulations) discretise $\Delta_g$ directly on real
  geometry.
* **Never forget the metric factors.** In curvilinear coordinates the divergence, gradient and volume
  element carry the Jacobian ($r$, $r^2\sin\theta$); discretising the operators without the metric
  produces a *spurious source* that violates conservation — the most common bug in spherical transport
  codes.
* **Dimension changes the stability constants** ($\Delta t\le\Delta x^2/(2dD)$, $\Delta t\le\Delta
  x/(|u|\sqrt d)$) and therefore the practicality of explicit schemes: what works in 1-D is hopeless in
  3-D.
* **Geodesic distance is a Hamilton–Jacobi problem**: use fast marching on meshes, not graph distances,
  when curvature matters ([Hamilton–Jacobi](../06-beyond/02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md)).
* **Benchmarks**: heat-kernel decay versus the exact spectrum on spheres and tori; mixing time on graphs
  versus the spectral gap; the $d$-dimensional Barenblatt profile; return probabilities on fractals
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).

## Checks

1. Why does a drunkard get home in 2-D but a diffusing particle can be lost in 3-D? *(Recurrence holds
   only for $d\le2$: the Green function's decay still gives infinite expected visits.)*
2. Where does the famous $8\pi$ in the Keller–Segel blow-up threshold come from? *(The two-dimensional
   logarithmic Green function.)*
3. What does the spectral gap tell you about a shape? *(The slowest mixing mode — bounded above by the
   Cheeger constant, so bottlenecks and thin necks set the mixing time.)*

# Bulk fluid transport


# Bulk fluid transport

This category is about the **flow itself**: how a velocity field carries things, how it is
generated, where it concentrates, and how it fails to be smooth.

| # | Note | The one thing it gives you |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | [Advection](01-advection.md) | the first-order term, characteristics, and the discretisation problem that never goes away |
| 2 | [Convection & buoyancy](02-convection-and-buoyancy.md) | advection that drives itself; Rayleigh–Bénard; $\mathrm{Nu}\sim\mathrm{Ra}^{1/3}$ |
| 3 | [Incompressible flow, vorticity & pressure](03-incompressible-flow-vorticity-and-pressure.md) | pressure as a constraint; vorticity transport and stretching |
| 4 | [Boundary layers](04-boundary-layers.md) | where the transport action is; similarity and separation |
| 5 | [Turbulence & eddy diffusivity](05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md) | enhanced transport, the closure problem, and resolution limits |
| 6 | [Shear dispersion (Taylor–Aris)](06-shear-dispersion-taylor-aris.md) | how a velocity gradient becomes an enormous effective diffusion |
| 7 | [Mixing & chaotic advection](07-mixing-and-chaotic-advection.md) | stretch, fold, and diff: mixing without randomness |
| 8 | [Porous media & Darcy](08-porous-media-and-darcy.md) | averaging the pores away; permeability, dispersion, upscaling |
| 9 | [Non-Newtonian rheology](09-non-newtonian-rheology.md) | when the constitutive law carries memory or a threshold |

![**Figure A.** Advection, diffusion and reaction together: the shapes you will meet in every application, from a river tracer to a microfluidic channel.](advection-vs-diffusion.pdf)

## How these notes fit together

Read **[advection](01-advection.md)** first: it is the term that appears in every other note here,
and the term that exposes numerical schemes. Then choose a direction:

* **Rising heat and self-driven flow** → [convection](02-convection-and-buoyancy.md) → [turbulence](05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md).
* **Walls and surfaces** → [boundary layers](04-boundary-layers.md) → [heat–mass–momentum analogy](../08-bridges/01-analogy-and-circuit-models.md).
* **Spreading and mixing** → [shear dispersion](06-shear-dispersion-taylor-aris.md) → [mixing](07-mixing-and-chaotic-advection.md).
* **Subsurface flow** → [Darcy](08-porous-media-and-darcy.md) → [wells & breakthrough](../04-interaction/12-wells-injection-efflux-and-breakthrough.md) → [subsidence](../04-interaction/13-subsidence-and-poroelasticity.md).
* **Complex materials** → [non-Newtonian rheology](09-non-newtonian-rheology.md) → [memory & viscoelastic transport](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md).

> **Recurring lesson of this category.** The velocity field decides *where* transport happens, and
> it is almost always *not* uniform. Multi-scale structure — boundary layers, pores, eddies,
> streamlines — is the rule, which is why everything here ends in an averaging or upscaling step.

\newpage

# Advection


# Advection

> **One-line picture.** A leaf on a river is not swimming. Whatever is dissolved or suspended in
> the flow goes wherever the flow goes — and the flow does not care what it is carrying.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [material derivative](../01-foundations/02-material-derivative.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [convection & buoyancy](02-convection-and-buoyancy.md),
> [boundary layers](04-boundary-layers.md), [shocks](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md).
> **In the equation:** the first-order term $\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla c$ or $\nabla\cdot(\mathbf{u}c)$.

![**Figure 10.** The three personalities of scalar transport. Advection translates a profile without changing it; diffusion spreads it without moving it; together they shift *and* smear. The fourth panel is what you measure downstream: a breakthrough curve whose shape reveals the Péclet number.](advection-vs-diffusion.pdf)

## L1 · Being carried versus spreading

Put a drop of dye in a still glass: it spreads by diffusion, in every direction, slowly. Put the
same drop in a river: it travels downstream while spreading. The travelling part is advection —
passive entrainment of stuff by the bulk motion of a carrier fluid.

| Word | Who moves the stuff? | Self-driven? |
|---|---|---|
| **advection** | the bulk velocity field | no — the field exists for other reasons |
| **diffusion** | molecular randomness | no — happens even at rest |
| **convection** | the transported quantity itself | yes — heat creates the flow that carries the heat |

Advection is the great equaliser: it does not care whether the cargo is salt, heat, momentum,
radioactivity, a pathogen or a probability density. It only cares that a velocity field exists.

## L2 · The term, and how to solve it

$$\underbrace{\partial_tc+\nabla\cdot(\mathbf{u}c)=0}_{\text{conservative}}
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\underbrace{\partial_tc+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla c=-c\,\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}}_{\text{advective}} .$$

For incompressible flow the two are identical — which is why the distinction is so often
forgotten, and why codes that advect concentration in compressible flow get the wrong answer
across shocks.

The exact solution is the **method of characteristics**: along $\dot{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x},t)$
the concentration is constant, $c(\mathbf{x},t)=c_0\big(\mathbf{X}(0;\mathbf{x},t)\big)$, where
$\mathbf{X}$ is the backward trajectory. Advection is *pure relabelling of parcels* — no
exchange, no smoothing, no loss.

| Variant | Equation | Behaviour |
|---|---|---|
| constant velocity | $\partial_tc+U\partial_xc=0$ | rigid translation |
| divergence-free field | $\partial_tc+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla c=0$ | parcels preserve volume; all $L^p$ conserved |
| compressible field | $\partial_t\rho+\nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf{u})=0$ | density changes as parcels compress |
| nonlinear (Burgers) | $\partial_tu+u\partial_xu=0$ | characteristics collide: shock in finite time |
| self-advection | $\partial_t\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u}=\cdots$ | the field carries itself: turbulence |

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Invariants.** $\int c\,\mathrm{d}V$ is constant; in divergence-free flow every $L^p$ norm, and
  both the maximum and the minimum, are preserved. Advection is reversible and has *no smoothing
  whatsoever*.
* **No self-repair.** Nothing is damped, so sharp fronts never soften on their own. Conversely,
  smooth data stay smooth only if $\mathbf{u}$ is smooth. At a discontinuity the limit is
  non-unique: you need an **entropy condition**
  ([shocks](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md)).
* **Gradient catastrophe.** For inviscid Burgers, smooth data break at
  $t^*=-1/\min_x\partial_xu_0(x)$ — the first time two characteristics meet. Afterwards only a
  weak solution exists, and *diffusion is what selects the physical one*. This is why numerical
  diffusion sometimes quietly saves a simulation and sometimes quietly ruins it.
* **Passive is an assumption, not a definition.** Real scalars feed back: heat changes density and
  drives motion ([convection](02-convection-and-buoyancy.md)); combustion releases heat; particles
  alter rheology. "Passive scalar" is a limit, valid when feedback is dynamically negligible.
* **Vorticity is advected, not created.** In inviscid barotropic flow the vorticity equation is
  advection plus stretching, and circulation is conserved (Kelvin). Vorticity is manufactured at
  boundaries and by baroclinicity — nowhere else.

## L4 · Numerics: why advection is where schemes are judged

Advection has no self-correcting mechanism, so it exposes every flaw in a discretisation.

* **CFL.** Explicit schemes need $U\Delta t/\Delta x\le1$ (upwind) or $\le1/\sqrt d$: a statement
  about domains of influence, not about accuracy ([CFL & stiffness](../07-numerics/02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md)).
* **Centred schemes ring; upwind schemes smear.** By Godunov's theorem, a *linear monotone* scheme
  is at best first-order accurate, so escaping the trade-off requires nonlinearity —
  [limiters, TVD and WENO](../07-numerics/04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md).
* **Read the error.** Damping error $=$ numerical diffusion (fronts thicken); phase error $=$
  numerical dispersion (wiggles trail the front). Both are catalogued in the
  [error portrait](../07-numerics/03-numerical-diffusion-and-dispersion-error.md).
* **Semi-Lagrangian** schemes beat the CFL limit by tracing characteristics backwards, but they
  leak mass unless made conservative — fatal in climate and reservoir modelling.
* **Boundaries.** Outflow must be characteristic-aware (Danckwerts/zero-gradient), or a plume
  reflects off the edge of the world
  ([boundary conditions](../01-foundations/04-pde-taxonomy-and-boundary-conditions.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Conservative advection on the sphere and on unstructured meshes; learned
  advection operators (translation is exactly what generic neural surrogates get worst —
  [operator learning](../07-numerics/11-machine-learning-and-operator-learning.md)); advection
  with [memory](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md).

## Words that collide

> **Advection vs convection:** [words that collide #2](../09-polysemy/02-convection.md).
> **Dispersion** in numerics is the phase error above; in hydrogeology it is mechanical spreading
> ([#1](../09-polysemy/01-dispersion.md)). **"Transport"** alone may mean advection, or the whole
> equation.

## Checks

1. In a steady, divergence-free 2-D flow, can a tracer maximum grow? *(No: pure advection
   preserves the range and every $L^p$ norm.)*
2. Why can no linear monotone scheme be second-order accurate? *(Godunov: second-order accuracy
   requires new extrema, which monotonicity forbids unless the limiter is nonlinear.)*
3. Where does the entropy condition come from physically? *(Microscopic viscosity or diffusion:
   the physically realised weak solution must dissipate energy.)*

# Convection & buoyancy


# Convection & buoyancy

> **One-line picture.** Convection is advection that drives itself: the transported quantity
> changes the density or the surface tension, the change moves the fluid, and the motion
> transports the quantity.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [advection](01-advection.md),
> [dimensionless numbers](../01-foundations/05-dimensionless-numbers.md) · **Leads to.**
> [turbulence](05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md),
> [geoscience & climate](../08-bridges/04-geoscience-and-climate.md)

![**Figure 11.** Rayleigh–Bénard convection: hot fluid rises, cold fluid sinks, and above a critical Rayleigh number the conduction-only state gives way to rolls. Heat transport then follows a power law — an instability becomes a transport law.](rayleigh-benard.pdf)

## L1 · Four ways to make a fluid move by transporting something

* **Natural (free)** — density differences created by the transported quantity. Warm air rises;
  water near $4^\circ$C has $\beta\approx0$, which is why lakes freeze from the top.
* **Forced** — an external agent (pump, fan, wind) drives flow past a surface. The velocity
  field is known, so the transport problem is linear advection–diffusion.
* **Mixed** — both matter; the ratio is $\mathrm{Ri}=\mathrm{Gr}/\mathrm{Re}^2$.
* **Marangoni** — a surface-tension gradient pulls fluid along an interface, from low $\sigma$ to
  high $\sigma$. It dominates wherever interfaces are present and gravity is weak: drying
  coffee drops, welding pools, thin films, microgravity processing.

## L2 · The equations.

Boussinesq approximation (density variations matter only in the buoyancy term):

$$\rho_0(\partial_t\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u})
=-\nabla p+\mu\nabla^2\mathbf{u}+\rho_0\beta(T-T_0)\mathbf{g},\qquad
\partial_tT+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla T=\alpha\nabla^2T,$$

with $\beta=-(1/\rho)\partial\rho/\partial T$. Marangoni convection replaces buoyancy by an
interfacial stress balance $\mu\,\partial_nu_{\rm t}=\sigma_T\,\partial_{\rm t}T+\sigma_c\,\partial_{\rm t}c$.

**The instability.** Perturb a quiescent conducting layer. Buoyancy amplifies on
$\sqrt{L/(g\beta\Delta T)}$ while viscosity and conduction damp on $L^2/\nu$ and $L^2/\alpha$.
Their ratio is the Rayleigh number, and the layer destabilises at $\mathrm{Ra}_c\approx1708$
(no-slip, rigid–rigid) — a *threshold*, not a gradual change.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Onset is a linear eigenvalue problem.** Below $\mathrm{Ra}_c$ the conduction state is the
  unique attractor; above it a bifurcation selects a wavelength $k_c\sim1/L$. The pattern scale
  comes from the *linear* problem, not the nonlinear dynamics — the same lesson as in
  [Turing patterns](../04-interaction/04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md).
* **Above onset, transport becomes a power law.** $\mathrm{Nu}\sim\mathrm{Ra}^{1/3}$ classically,
  $\sim\mathrm{Ra}^{0.30}$ in hard turbulence (with debated logarithmic corrections). These
  exponents are derived from boundary-layer/shear-layer balances, not fitted blindly.
* **Linear $\neq$ nonlinear stability.** The linear threshold does not guarantee that the
  bifurcated state is stable; energy methods give a lower *guaranteed* threshold. The gap
  between them is a standard caution.
* **Rotation and stratification change the physics**: Coriolis and buoyancy frequencies add
  $\mathrm{Ro}$, $\mathrm{Ta}$ and stratification parameters, producing Taylor–Proudman columns,
  internal waves and double-diffusive staircases. **Double diffusion** can be unstable even when
  the net density profile is stable, because heat and salt diffuse at different rates.

## L4 · Numerics, limits, frontiers

* **The coupling is stiff.** Momentum and energy must be advanced together; segregated schemes
  can diverge at strong coupling, and monolithic solves need coupled preconditioners
  ([coupling](../07-numerics/05-operator-splitting-and-multiphysics-coupling.md)).
* **Incompressibility** makes pressure a Lagrange multiplier solved by a Poisson problem each
  step ([vorticity & pressure](03-incompressible-flow-vorticity-and-pressure.md)).
* **Rayleigh–Bénard is a standard benchmark**: onset threshold, $\mathrm{Nu}(\mathrm{Ra})$ curves
  and pattern selection are used to verify codes
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Interfacial convection** needs resolved, temperature-dependent surface tension and a
  deformable interface: [level sets or phase fields](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md).
* **Frontiers.** Reactive porous-media convection, magnetoconvection and dynamos, and the extreme
  regime $\mathrm{Ra}\sim10^{15}$ (Sun, Earth's core) where neither experiment nor DNS is
  possible and scaling theory is the only predictor.

## Checks

1. Water at $4^\circ$C has $\beta=0$: is it convecting? *(No — buoyancy vanishes, which is why
   deep lake water sits at $4^\circ$C.)*
2. Why does $\mathrm{Ma}\sim L^2$ while $\mathrm{Ra}\sim L^3$? *(Surface effects are interfacial,
   buoyancy is volumetric — hence Marangoni wins in small systems, buoyancy in large ones.)*
3. Heated vertical plate in air: which numbers set the heat transfer? *($\mathrm{Ra}$, $\mathrm{Pr}$;
   laminar correlation $\mathrm{Nu}\sim\mathrm{Ra}^{1/4}$.)*

## See also

[Advection](01-advection.md) ·
[Turbulence & eddy diffusivity](05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md) ·
[Boundary layers](04-boundary-layers.md) ·
[Geoscience & climate](../08-bridges/04-geoscience-and-climate.md)

# Incompressible flow, vorticity & pressure


# Incompressible flow, vorticity & pressure

> **One-line picture.** Incompressibility is not an equation for pressure — it is a *constraint*.
> Pressure is the enforcer: it takes whatever value is needed to keep the velocity field
> divergence-free, instantly and globally.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [material derivative](../01-foundations/02-material-derivative.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [boundary layers](04-boundary-layers.md),
> [turbulence](05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md), [MHD](../06-beyond/13-mhd-and-relativistic-transport.md)

## L1 · Two ideas in words

**Pressure is a Lagrange multiplier.** In a gas, pressure is thermodynamic: compute it from
density and temperature. In water you can raise the pressure without changing the density at all,
so the momentum equation cannot *find* it. Instead impose "every parcel keeps its volume", and
pressure becomes the field that makes that true — non-local, instantaneous, and fixed by the
boundary conditions. Physically, sound carries the message at $c_s\sim1500$ m/s; since that is
much faster than anything else in the flow, the approximation works.

**Vorticity is the fluid's memory of rotation.** $\boldsymbol\omega=\nabla\times\mathbf{u}$
measures local spin, and in an inviscid fluid it is *carried and stretched*, not created.
Turbulence, mixing, lift and most structure lives in $\boldsymbol\omega$, not in $\mathbf{u}$.

## L2 · The equations

$$\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}=0,\qquad
\rho(\partial_t\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u})=-\nabla p+\mu\nabla^2\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{f}.$$

Taking the divergence (and using the constraint) gives the **pressure Poisson equation**

$$\nabla^2p=-\rho\,\nabla\cdot(\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u})+\nabla\cdot\mathbf{f},$$

*elliptic*, with no time derivative: pressure adjusts globally, instantly. Taking the curl gives
the **vorticity equation**

$$\partial_t\boldsymbol\omega+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol\omega
=\boldsymbol\omega\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u}+\nu\nabla^2\boldsymbol\omega
+\frac{1}{\rho^2}\nabla\rho\times\nabla p,$$

read as: carried (advection) $+$ **stretched** (amplified: the engine of turbulence) $+$ smoothed
by viscosity $+$ created baroclinically where $\nabla\rho$ and $\nabla p$ misalign (sea breezes,
and any non-barotropic fluid).

**Helmholtz decomposition** $\mathbf{u}=\nabla\times\boldsymbol\psi+\nabla\phi$ separates the
solenoidal and gradient parts; in 2-D it collapses to a streamfunction
$\mathbf{u}=\nabla^\perp\psi$ whose level curves are streamlines. This is the algebra behind
projection methods.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Non-locality is real.** The pressure problem is elliptic: a change anywhere is felt
  everywhere. Well-posedness forbids prescribing both $p$ and $\partial_np$ on the same piece of
  boundary.
* **Compatibility condition.** With pure Neumann pressure data, a solution exists only if
  $\oint\partial_np=0$. In codes this appears as a constant null space of the pressure operator;
  forgetting it gives a solver that never converges.
* **Vorticity theorems.** Kelvin (circulation conserved on material loops), Helmholtz (vortex lines
  move with the fluid), Bjerknes (baroclinic generation). Together: no vorticity can be made in
  the interior of a barotropic inviscid flow — all of it comes from boundaries, initial data, or
  non-barotropic effects.
* **2-D and 3-D are qualitatively different.** In 2-D, stretching vanishes identically, enstrophy
  is bounded, and the energy cascade runs *up* scale. In 3-D, stretching amplifies vorticity and
  the finite-time blow-up question is a Millennium Prize problem.
* **Energy balance.** $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{\rho}{2}\int|\mathbf{u}|^2
  =-\mu\int|\boldsymbol\omega|^2+\text{boundary work}$: vorticity *is* the dissipation.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Projection / fractional step.** Advance momentum without pressure, then project onto
  divergence-free fields by solving for a pseudo-pressure. Splitting error is $O(\Delta t)$ near
  boundaries unless higher-order rotational forms are used.
* **Staggered grids** (MAC) put velocity components on faces, pressure at cell centres: the
  discrete gradient is then the exact adjoint of the discrete divergence, so the pressure matrix
  is symmetric and has the correct null space.
* **Pressure is the expensive part.** Every step needs an elliptic solve — FFT if periodic,
  [multigrid or preconditioned Krylov otherwise](../07-numerics/06-linear-solvers-and-preconditioning.md).
* **Vorticity formulations** (vortex methods, vortex-in-cell) avoid pressure entirely: superb for
  wakes and external flows, awkward with boundaries and viscous diffusion.
* **Frontiers.** Wall models that respect vorticity generation; pressure solvers at $10^{12}$
  unknowns; regularity of 3-D Navier–Stokes.

## Checks

1. Why is there no equation of state for $p$ here? *(Density is constant by assumption, so
   thermodynamics cannot determine pressure; the constraint does.)*
2. Where does a smoke ring's vorticity come from? *(Generated at the nozzle boundary; then purely
   advected and stretched.)*
3. Why does 2-D turbulence look so different? *($\boldsymbol\omega\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u}\equiv0$: no
   stretching, no forward enstrophy cascade.)*

## See also

[Boundary layers](04-boundary-layers.md) ·
[Turbulence & eddy diffusivity](05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md) ·
[Advection](01-advection.md) ·
[MHD & relativistic transport](../06-beyond/13-mhd-and-relativistic-transport.md)

\newpage

# Boundary layers


# Boundary layers

> **One-line picture.** Wherever a fast flow meets a surface, all the action is squeezed into a
> thin layer: outside it viscosity is irrelevant, inside it viscosity is the only thing that
> matters.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [advection](01-advection.md),
> [dimensionless numbers](../01-foundations/05-dimensionless-numbers.md) · **Leads to.**
> [turbulence](05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md),
> [heat–mass–momentum analogy](../08-bridges/01-analogy-and-circuit-models.md)

## L1 · Prandtl's 1904 idea

At high Reynolds number, viscosity acts only in a thin layer near the wall, of thickness
$\delta\sim L/\sqrt{\mathrm{Re}}$, which gets *thinner* as the flow gets faster. Outside the layer
the flow is effectively inviscid and can be computed with potential theory; inside, viscous stress
balances inertia; the two are glued by matched asymptotics. The payoff: drag, heat transfer, mass
transfer and separation become properties of a few millimetres of fluid — the difference between a
wing that flies and a wing that stalls.

## L2 · The equations, and their scaling

With $\partial_y\sim1/\delta\gg\partial_x\sim1/L$, Navier–Stokes collapses to

$$u\partial_xu+v\partial_yu=-\frac{1}{\rho}\partial_xp+\nu\partial_y^2u,\qquad \partial_xu+\partial_yv=0,$$

with pressure *imposed by the outer flow* (because $\partial_yp\sim\delta/L$). For a flat plate this
has the Blasius similarity solution in $\eta=y\sqrt{U/(\nu x)}$:
$\delta\approx5x/\sqrt{\mathrm{Re}_x}$, $C_f\approx0.664/\sqrt{\mathrm{Re}_x}$.

| Layer | Diffusivity | Thickness | Wall quantity |
|---|---|---|---|
| velocity | $\nu$ | $\delta_v\sim L/\sqrt{\mathrm{Re}}$ | drag, $\tau_w$ |
| thermal | $\alpha$ | $\delta_T\sim\delta_v\mathrm{Pr}^{-1/3}$ | $\mathrm{Nu}$ |
| concentration | $D$ | $\delta_c\sim\delta_v\mathrm{Sc}^{-1/3}$ | $\mathrm{Sh}$ |

This is the origin of the **heat–mass–momentum analogy**: with $\mathrm{Pr}=\mathrm{Sc}=1$ the
three layers are identical, so one correlation serves all three
([circuit analogy](../08-bridges/01-analogy-and-circuit-models.md)).

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Similarity is the default.** With no intrinsic length scale, the solution depends on $x$ and
  $y$ only through $\eta\propto y/\sqrt{\nu x/U}$; that is why such solutions exist and why
  numerical schemes converge quickly on them.
* **Separation is triggered, then inviscid.** An adverse pressure gradient drives the wall shear
  $\partial_yu|_{\rm wall}\to0$; beyond that point the layer detaches and the whole "outer flow
  plus thin layer" structure collapses. This is why stall cannot be predicted from potential flow.
* **Stability and transition.** Laminar layers become unstable (Tollmien–Schlichting waves, then
  turbulent spots) and the turbulent layer thickens as $x\mathrm{Re}^{-1/5}$ with much larger wall
  shear. Transition location is the hardest quantity in aerodynamics, set by tiny receptivity
  details.
* **Matched asymptotics is portable.** The same inner/outer split describes high-$\mathrm{Pe}$
  transport, thin reaction sheets, lubrication films and
  [sharp interfaces](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md).

## L4 · Numerics

* **Never use uniform grids.** Wall-normal clustering or wall functions are mandatory; a uniform
  grid at $\mathrm{Re}=10^6$ is hopeless.
* **Near-wall resolution dominates the cost** of CFD: $y^+\lesssim1$ for low-Reynolds treatments,
  with wall-resolved LES scaling near $\mathrm{Re}^{1.8}$.
* **Wall functions** (log-law bridges) are cheap and treacherous: they fail in adverse pressure
  gradients, separation, and at high $\mathrm{Pr}$ or $\mathrm{Sc}$.
* **Verification cases**: Blasius skin friction, rotating-disk Nusselt, the Pohlhausen family
  ([benchmarks](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).

## Checks

1. At $\mathrm{Re}=10^6$ on a 1 m plate, how thick is the layer? *($\approx5$ mm — and that film
   sets the drag.)*
2. Why is pressure constant across the layer? *(Because $\partial_yp\sim\delta/L\ll1$; the outer
   flow dictates it.)*
3. Why does the analogy need $\mathrm{Pr}=\mathrm{Sc}=1$ to be exact? *(Only then do the three
   profiles coincide.)*

## See also

[Turbulence & eddy diffusivity](05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md) ·
[Heat–mass–momentum analogy](../08-bridges/01-analogy-and-circuit-models.md) ·
[Engineering devices](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md) ·
[Knudsen & rarefied transport](../03-microscopic/05-knudsen-and-rarefied-transport.md)

# Turbulence & eddy diffusivity


# Turbulence & eddy diffusivity

> **One-line picture.** Turbulence is advection run wild: swirls of every size carry heat, mass
> and momentum far faster than molecules can — and no closed equation exists for how fast.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [convection & buoyancy](02-convection-and-buoyancy.md),
> [incompressible flow](03-incompressible-flow-vorticity-and-pressure.md) · **Leads to.**
> [mixing & chaotic advection](07-mixing-and-chaotic-advection.md),
> [ML & operator learning](../07-numerics/11-machine-learning-and-operator-learning.md)

## L1 · What is happening

Stir coffee: the swirls break into smaller swirls, and those into smaller ones, until viscosity
stops the smallest. Along the way they stretch and fold the fluid, transporting everything they
carry enormously faster than molecular diffusion — milk spreads through a cup in seconds where
diffusion alone would take hours. Two consequences dominate practice: transport is enhanced by
$10^3$–$10^6$ (eddy diffusivity $\gg$ molecular), and **that enhancement is not a material
property** — it depends on the flow, geometry and history, so it must be modelled.

## L2 · Cascade and the closure problem

**Kolmogorov (1941).** Energy enters at scale $L$, cascades through an inertial range, and
dissipates at the Kolmogorov scale

$$\eta=\left(\frac{\nu^3}{\varepsilon}\right)^{1/4}\!\sim L\,\mathrm{Re}^{-3/4},\qquad
E(k)\sim\varepsilon^{2/3}k^{-5/3}.$$

Hence a DNS needs $\sim\mathrm{Re}^{9/4}$ grid points and $\sim\mathrm{Re}^{3/4}$ time steps: about
$\mathrm{Re}^3$ work.

**Closure.** Averaging Navier–Stokes introduces the Reynolds stress

$$\partial_t\langle u_i\rangle+\langle u_j\rangle\partial_j\langle u_i\rangle
=-\partial_i\langle p\rangle/\rho+\nu\nabla^2\langle u_i\rangle-\partial_j\langle u_i'u_j'\rangle,$$

and the equations for $\langle u_i'u_j'\rangle$ involve triple correlations, forever. This is not
a technical gap but a structural fact — the same conclusion as
[Mori–Zwanzig](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md).

| Rung | Idea | Cost | What is hidden |
|---|---|---|---|
| RANS | model *all* fluctuations with $\nu_t$ | tiny | physics, inside constants |
| LES | resolve large eddies, model small | moderate | subgrid model error |
| DNS | resolve down to $\eta$ | $\mathrm{Re}^3$ | resolution error only |

**Eddy diffusivity** $D_t\sim u'\ell$ is the transport analogue of eddy viscosity, and rests on the
*gradient-diffusion hypothesis* — turbulent transport always down the mean gradient. It fails in
buoyant plumes and near walls, where counter-gradient fluxes appear.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Deterministic chaos, not noise.** Turbulence is a property of Navier–Stokes at high
  $\mathrm{Re}$; stochastic terms enter only when you average.
* **Scaling laws follow from hypotheses.** $-5/3$ rests on locality and self-similarity of the
  cascade; intermittency corrections (multifractals) show those hypotheses are approximate, with
  deviations growing with $\mathrm{Re}$.
* **Statistics are reproducible, trajectories are not.** Sensitivity to initial data forecloses
  pointwise prediction but not mean-field prediction — hence the engineering focus on means,
  variances and fluxes.
* **Anisotropy breaks universality.** Walls, stratification and rotation inject large-scale
  anisotropy; universal constants shift and logarithmic corrections appear.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Resolution follows the smallest active scale**: $\Delta x\approx\eta$ for DNS; inertial-range
  grids for LES; no scale resolution for RANS.
* **Numerical diffusion can masquerade as turbulence.** Coarse advection schemes act like extra
  eddy viscosity; evaluate a scheme by its *effective* viscosity in the inertial range.
* **Conservation matters.** Kinetic-energy-conserving (skew-symmetric) convection discretisations
  keep spectra honest; energy-manufacturing schemes give meaningless cascades.
* **Benchmarks**: decaying isotropic turbulence, channel flow at matched $\mathrm{Re}_\tau$,
  Rayleigh–Bénard at matched $\mathrm{Ra}$
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Learned closures with enforced symmetries and realizability; stochastic subgrid
  models derived from memory kernels; wall models beyond $\mathrm{Re}\sim10^{10}$ for geophysical
  and astrophysical flows.

## Checks

1. Why does DNS cost scale as $\mathrm{Re}^3$? *(Points $\sim\mathrm{Re}^{9/4}$ and steps
   $\sim\mathrm{Re}^{3/4}$.)*
2. When is a constant eddy diffusivity acceptable? *(Homogeneous, isotropic turbulence and mean
   fields only — never where counter-gradient transport occurs.)*
3. What is the single sentence version of the closure problem? *(Averaging creates a new unknown,
   whose equation creates another, without end.)*

## See also

[Mixing & chaotic advection](07-mixing-and-chaotic-advection.md) ·
[Shear dispersion](06-shear-dispersion-taylor-aris.md) ·
[Linear response & memory](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md) ·
[ML & operator learning](../07-numerics/11-machine-learning-and-operator-learning.md)

# Shear dispersion (Taylor–Aris)


# Shear dispersion (Taylor–Aris)

> **One-line picture.** When the fluid moves faster in some places than others, a blob of tracer
> gets *sheared* into a long stripe, and only the cross-stream diffusion that folds it back makes
> the spread look like (much larger) diffusion.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [advection](01-advection.md), [diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [porous media & Darcy](08-porous-media-and-darcy.md),
> [words that collide #1](../09-polysemy/01-dispersion.md)

![**Figure 13.** The mechanism (Taylor, 1953): a parabolic Poiseuille profile stretches a blob along the channel while cross-stream diffusion continually re-injects material into different streamlines. After a while the blob is Gaussian again — with a diffusion coefficient far larger than the molecular one.](taylor-aris.pdf)

## L1 · The observation that started it

Inject dye into a pipe. You expect it to travel at the mean velocity and spread by molecular
diffusion, $D\sim10^{-9}$ m²/s — i.e. barely at all over a metre. What you actually observe is a
blob that spreads by centimetres over that distance: an *effective* diffusivity $10^{5}$ times
larger. Taylor's 1953 explanation: the parabolic velocity profile smears the blob into a thin
parabola (fast centre, slow walls), while molecular diffusion shuffles material across
streamlines. The two together are equivalent to a single Fickian diffusion with

$$D_{\rm eff}=D+\frac{a^2U^2}{48D}\qquad\text{(tube radius }a,\ \text{mean }U),$$

valid once the tracer has had time to sample all streamlines, $t\gg a^2/D$.

## L2 · Why the $U^2/D$ is not a mistake

The counter-intuitive form — diffusion in the *denominator* — is the heart of the mechanism:

| Regime | Time scale | Behaviour |
|---|---|---|
| $t\ll a^2/D$ | shear dominates | the blob is a parabola, spread grows as $Ut$ |
| $t\gg a^2/D$ | cross-stream mixing complete | Gaussian, spread grows as $\sqrt{D_{\rm eff}t}$ |
| $D\to0$ | no cross-stream mixing | no Fickian limit at all: a frozen striped pattern |

Small molecular diffusivity makes the *early* spread violent (the stripe is longer) but delays the
onset of the Fickian regime. In porous media, the same mechanism with many flow paths is called
**mechanical (hydrodynamic) dispersion**, described by a tensor with longitudinal and transverse
components aligned with the flow:

$$D_L=\alpha_L|\mathbf{v}|+D_m,\qquad D_T=\alpha_T|\mathbf{v}|+D_m,\qquad \alpha_L\approx(10{-}100)\alpha_T .$$

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Moment analysis.** Track $\int c\,\mathrm{d}x$, $\int xc\,\mathrm{d}x$, $\int x^2c\,\mathrm{d}x$:
  the centre of mass moves at the *flux-averaged* velocity, and the variance grows linearly for
  large $t$ with slope $2D_{\rm eff}$. Dispersion coefficients are defined by long-time *moments*,
  not by local fluxes.
* **It is homogenisation in miniature.** The solvability condition of a cell problem produces
  exactly $a^2U^2/48D$; see [upscaling](../06-beyond/08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md).
* **Skewness and preasymptotics.** At intermediate times the distribution is skewed (a long tail
  of slow material near the walls). Breakthrough curves that "have a tail" are usually in this
  preasymptotic regime — very common in groundwater, where $t\gg a^2/D$ may take years.
* **Validity conditions matter.** Taylor–Aris assumes steady laminar flow, uniform tube, and
  $\mathrm{Pe}\,a/L$ small enough that the tracer samples the profile. Reactive transport,
  turbulence and dead-end pores all modify the result, sometimes decisively
  ([sorption](../04-interaction/07-sorption-and-retardation.md)).

## L4 · Numerics

* **Resolution requirement:** the cross-stream profile must be resolved or the effective
  dispersion is *numerical*, not physical. Codes that use coarse transverse grids invent their own
  $\alpha_T$.
* **Grid orientation artifacts:** in isotropic media $\alpha_L/\alpha_T$ ratios of 100 mean a
  rotated flow direction has enormous effective dispersion; tensor rotation is a standard source
  of bugs.
* **Benchmarks:** the classic two-region (mobile–immobile) tests and column experiments; use
  breakthrough curves, not snapshots, to fit dispersion
  ([measurement](../10-appendix/05-measurement-and-experiments.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Non-Fickian dispersion in heterogeneous media with broad residence-time
  distributions — the crossover to [anomalous transport](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md).

## Checks

1. Why does a *smaller* molecular diffusivity give a larger effective dispersion? *(The sheared
   stripe is longer; cross-stream mixing is slower, so the Fickian limit is delayed — but the
   asymptotic coefficient grows as $1/D$.)*
2. At what time is the Taylor–Aris formula valid? *($t\gg a^2/D$.)*
3. Why do transverse and longitudinal dispersivities differ by orders of magnitude? *(Flow paths
   differ mostly along the flow direction; crossing streamlines requires molecular or turbulent
   motion.)*

## See also

[Porous media & Darcy](08-porous-media-and-darcy.md) ·
[Turbulence & eddy diffusivity](05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md) ·
[Words that collide: dispersion](../09-polysemy/01-dispersion.md) ·
[Homogenisation & upscaling](../06-beyond/08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md)

# Mixing & chaotic advection


# Mixing & chaotic advection

> **One-line picture.** Perfectly smooth, deterministic flow can mix like a random one. The trick
> is not randomness but *stretching and folding* — the baker's transformation performed by the
> fluid itself.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [advection](01-advection.md), [turbulence](05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [chaotic mixing in applications](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md),
> [anomalous diffusion](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md)

## L1 · Mixing without randomness

Stir a cup slowly at low Reynolds number — honey, not water — and the fluid comes back to where it
started when you reverse the stroke. It looks reversible. Do it many times with a slight twist
each stroke and suddenly the dye is smeared everywhere, irreversibly, even though the flow is
laminar, steady in time and utterly deterministic. That is **Lagrangian chaos**: trajectories
separate exponentially even when the velocity field is smooth and simple.

Mixing has two stages, and confusing them causes most mistakes:

1. **Stirring** — kinematics. Stretch the interface between fluids; this *increases the area*
   available for molecular diffusion. No mass is mixed yet.
2. **Diffusion** — physics. Molecules must actually cross the interface; only then is mixing
   complete. Stirring shortens the distance they must cross, from millimetres to nanometres.

So a "perfectly mixed" tank is one where the striation thickness is small enough that diffusion
finishes on the time scale you care about — a *quantitative* criterion, not a hand-wave.

## L2 · How to measure mixing and transport by entrainment

| Quantity | Meaning |
|---|---|
| strain rate / stretching rate | local rate at which material lines grow |
| Lyapunov exponent $\lambda$ | $\delta(t)\sim\delta_0e^{\lambda t}$ for neighbouring parcels |
| Poincaré map | stroboscopic view; its fixed points organise the chaos |
| striation thickness $s(t)$ | distance across which diffusion must act; $s\sim s_0e^{-\lambda t}$ |
| Lyapunov (finite-time) exponents | local diagnostics for real, non-uniform flows |
| coherent structures (LCS) | ridges of the finite-time Lyapunov field: barriers to transport |

A practical rule of thumb: for a scalar to be uniform at scale $\ell$ you need
$t_{\rm mix}\approx\lambda^{-1}\ln(L/\ell)$ — *logarithmic* in the scale ratio, which is why
stirring is so effective.

**Jets, plumes and wakes** are the large-scale cousins of mixing: momentum-driven jets entrain
ambient fluid at a rate set by the shear-layer velocity ratio, buoyancy-driven plumes entrain in
proportion to a buoyancy flux, and wakes entrain in proportion to drag. They all share the same
signature: velocity and concentration profiles become **self-similar** downstream, with widths
growing linearly and centre-line values decaying as a power law,

$$u_c\sim x^{-1/3}\ (\text{jet}),\qquad u_c\sim x^{-1/2},\ w_c\sim x^{-1/3}\ (\text{plume}),$$

which is nothing but a statement that entrainment is the dominant transport mechanism there.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Exponential separation under bounded velocity fields is possible,** and generically occurs in
  2-D time-periodic and 3-D steady incompressible flows. Chaos needs no turbulence.
* **Advection alone cannot mix.** It rearranges; diffusion completes the job. This is why
  Reynolds-number-free microfluidic devices can be designed to mix using chaotic advection
  [with no turbulence at all].
* **Barriers exist.** In incompressible 2-D flow, streamlines are invariant curves; with time
  dependence, KAM tori and LCS remain *transport barriers*, and transport between regions is
  organised by the hyperbolic invariant manifolds that are responsible for lobe dynamics.
* **Batchelor scaling.** In a smooth chaotic flow, the scalar spectrum develops a $k^{-1}$ range
  above the stirring scale, sharpening gradients until diffusion takes over at the Batchelor
  scale — the reason we call turbulence a mixer but not a pure mixer.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Numerical diffusion is the enemy** in mixing studies: a first-order scheme's artificial
  diffusion can destroy the striations that carry the physics. Use high-order or spectral
  advection, or particle tracking, if you care about interfaces.
* **Particle tracking and lobe analysis** are the standard tools: integrate trajectory equations
  for many seeds, then compute finite-time Lyapunov exponents on the cloud
  ([particle methods](../07-numerics/09-particle-and-monte-carlo-methods.md)).
* **Mixing measures for validation**: decay of variance ("intensity of segregation"), striation
  thickness distributions, and Poincaré sections.
* **Frontiers.** Optimal mixing protocols (controlling the flow to maximise mixing for a given
  energy input) and mixing in [graph/network analogues](../08-bridges/09-networks-society-and-traffic.md);
  chaotic advection interacting with [memory or yield stress](../02-bulk/09-non-newtonian-rheology.md).

## Checks

1. Why does stirring not mix by itself? *(It rearranges the interface; molecules still have to
   cross it.)*
2. Why is $t_{\rm mix}$ logarithmic in the scale ratio? *(Stretching is exponential, so the
   distance to be diffused shrinks exponentially.)*
3. What is an LCS in one sentence? *(A ridge of the finite-time Lyapunov field — a moving barrier
   that material does not cross.)*

## See also

[Turbulence & eddy diffusivity](05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md) ·
[Shear dispersion](06-shear-dispersion-taylor-aris.md) ·
[Networks, society & traffic](../08-bridges/09-networks-society-and-traffic.md)

# Porous media & Darcy's law


# Porous media & Darcy's law

> **One-line picture.** You cannot solve Navier–Stokes in every pore, so you average: the net
> effect of ten million tortuous passages is a linear law with one material constant.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [constitutive relations](../01-foundations/03-constitutive-relations.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [subsidence](../04-interaction/13-subsidence-and-poroelasticity.md),
> [wells & breakthrough](../04-interaction/12-wells-injection-efflux-and-breakthrough.md),
> [homogenisation](../06-beyond/08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md)

## L1 · Why a single constant works

Push water through sand slowly and the flow rate is proportional to the pressure gradient. That is
all Darcy's law says. It is astonishing that it works, because the pore-scale flow is a mess of
separating and reattaching streams. The reason is averaging: at low Reynolds number the viscous
resistance of a statistically homogeneous medium is linear in the driving force, so an effective
coefficient exists.

The law has a validity range, and knowing its edges is the whole art:

| Regime | Law | Condition |
|---|---|---|
| creeping, low $\mathrm{Re}$ | $\mathbf{q}=-(K/\mu)(\nabla p-\rho\mathbf{g})$ | $\mathrm{Re}_{\rm pore}\lesssim1$ |
| inertial corrections | Forchheimer: add $-\beta\rho|\mathbf{q}|\mathbf{q}$ | $\mathrm{Re}_{\rm pore}\gtrsim1{-}10$ |
| near a free surface or fracture | Brinkman: add $\mu_{\rm eff}\nabla^2\mathbf{q}$ | boundary-layer-like transitions |
| very tight pores | Klinkenberg slippage | gas mean free path $\sim$ pore size |
| consolidation/compaction | poroelastic (Biot) | when the matrix deforms |

## L2 · From pores to a tensor

With porosity $\theta$ (void fraction) and Darcy (seepage) velocity $\mathbf{q}$, the actual pore
velocity is $\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{q}/\theta$, and mass balance gives the **advection–dispersion
equation**

$$\partial_t(\theta c)+\nabla\cdot(\mathbf{q}c-\theta\mathbf{D}\cdot\nabla c)=R(c),\qquad
K=\frac{\theta d^2}{180(1-\theta)^2}\ (\text{Kozeny–Carman}, d=\text{grain size}).$$

* **Permeability is a tensor** in layered or fractured rock; flow anisotropy of $10^3$ is common,
  which is why horizontal wells exist.
* **Dispersion is a tensor too**, aligned with the flow, with
  $\alpha_L\gg\alpha_T$ ([shear dispersion](06-shear-dispersion-taylor-aris.md)).
* **Capillarity matters** at infiltration fronts and in the vadose zone: $K$ and $p$ become
  functions of saturation (Richards' equation), turning the problem *nonlinear* and often
  hysteretic ([variational inequalities](../06-beyond/12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md)).

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Upscaling is exact in the limit.** Volume averaging or two-scale asymptotics produce Darcy's
  law with $K$ computed from a *cell problem* on the pore geometry — a genuine theorem, not a
  fudge ([homogenisation](../06-beyond/08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md)).
* **Elliptic steady problems, parabolic transients.** The steady flow (and pressure) solves an
  elliptic problem; pressure transients obey a diffusion equation with hydraulic diffusivity
  $T/S$ — identical mathematics to heat conduction, which is why well-test analysis was built from
  heat-conduction solutions.
* **Reciprocity and symmetry.** $K$ is symmetric positive definite; a consequence is that
  streamlines and potential gradients are orthogonal only in isotropic media, and that well
  patterns have reciprocal responses.
* **Dispersion coefficients are asymptotic.** Valid only for lengths much larger than a
  heterogeneity correlation length; practical aquifers often violate this, producing non-Fickian
  plumes ([anomalous transport](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md)).

## L4 · Numerics

* **Grid orientation artifacts** appear with anisotropic dispersion tensors; keep the tensor
  symmetric and check rotation invariance with a diagonal-flow test.
* **Reservoir simulators live here**: IMPES (implicit pressure, explicit saturation) versus fully
  implicit, upwinding for saturations, and [limiters](../07-numerics/04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md)
  to control front smearing. Mass conservation is non-negotiable and is why finite-volume
  discretisations dominate.
* **Heterogeneity modelling** (geostatistical realisations, upscaling of correlated random fields)
  is the main computational cost — a study may require hundreds of realisations for uncertainty
  quantification ([UQ and inversion](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)).
* **Benchmarks**: the Henry problem (density-driven flow), the McWhorter–Sunada problem, and
  analytical well-test solutions (Theis, Cooper–Jacob)
  ([measurement](../10-appendix/05-measurement-and-experiments.md)).

## Checks

1. Why is Darcy's law linear while the pore-scale flow is not? *(Averaging at low
   $\mathrm{Re}_{\rm pore}$: inertia is negligible, so resistance is linear.)*
2. What happens when $\mathrm{Re}_{\rm pore}$ grows? *(Forchheimer's quadratic correction — the
   flow is no longer proportional to the gradient.)*
3. Why is permeability not a property of the solid alone? *(It depends on pore geometry; and in
   the slip regime on the gas mean free path as well.)*

## See also

[Shear dispersion](06-shear-dispersion-taylor-aris.md) ·
[Homogenisation & upscaling](../06-beyond/08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md) ·
[Subsidence & poroelasticity](../04-interaction/13-subsidence-and-poroelasticity.md) ·
[Geoscience & climate](../08-bridges/04-geoscience-and-climate.md)

\newpage

# Non-Newtonian rheology


# Non-Newtonian rheology

> **One-line picture.** Water's viscosity is a number; paint's viscosity is a *story* about how it
> has been stirred. The constitutive law becomes a model of history and the mathematics of
> transport changes with it.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [constitutive relations](../01-foundations/03-constitutive-relations.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [memory & viscoelastic transport](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md),
> [variational inequalities](../06-beyond/12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md)

## L1 · What changes, and why it matters

* **Shear thinning** (ketchup, blood, polymer solutions): apparent viscosity falls with shear rate.
  Its opposite, **shear thickening** (cornflour suspensions), stiffens under impact.
* **Yield stress** (toothpaste, concrete, mud): below a threshold the material does not flow at
  all. Plug flow appears in pipes, and unsheared regions can be stranded mid-channel.
* **Viscoelasticity** (polymer melts, mucus, dough): stress depends on deformation *history* with a
  memory time $\lambda$. Materials recover shape, climb rotating rods, swell at dies and recoil.

Non-Newtonian transport is therefore not a footnote: it is where
[memory](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md) enters fluid mechanics.

## L2 · Models

| Model | Form | Captures |
|---|---|---|
| power law | $\mu_{\rm app}=m\dot\gamma^{\,n-1}$ | shear thinning ($n<1$) / thickening ($n>1$) |
| Carreau–Yasuda | smooth transition with plateaux | realistic viscosity curves |
| Bingham / Herschel–Bulkley | flow only if $\tau>\tau_y$ | yield stress, plug flow |
| Maxwell (upper convected) | $\lambda\overset{\triangledown}{\boldsymbol\tau}+\boldsymbol\tau=2\mu\mathbf{D}$ | stress relaxation, normal stresses |
| Oldroyd-B | Maxwell + solvent viscosity | Boger fluids, elastic instabilities |
| Giesekus / PTT | nonlinear stress terms | shear thinning, finite extensibility |
| fractional Maxwell | $\lambda^{\alpha}D_t^{\alpha}\boldsymbol\tau+\boldsymbol\tau=\cdots$ | broad relaxation spectra |

Two numbers do the work: $\mathrm{Wi}=\lambda\dot\gamma$ (memory time vs deformation time) and
$\mathrm{De}=\lambda/t_{\rm obs}$. Above $\mathrm{Wi}\sim1$ elasticity dominates, with asymmetric
velocity profiles, secondary flows in straight channels, and purely elastic turbulence at
$\mathrm{Re}\ll1$.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **The type changes.** Viscoelastic models are *hyperbolic* in the stress (stress propagates at
  finite speed) and parabolic in velocity; the mixed type, plus loss of positive definiteness in
  some regimes, is why high-Weissenberg simulations fail. The high-$\mathrm{Wi}$ problem remains
  open.
* **Objective derivatives are mandatory.** Stress must be advected frame-invariantly (Jaumann,
  upper/lower convected): the corotational terms come from
  [objectivity](../01-foundations/02-material-derivative.md) and are physics, not algebra.
* **Yield stress is a variational inequality.** The yielded/unyielded interface is unknown and must
  be found as part of the solution; hysteresis means answers depend on history
  ([variational inequalities & hysteresis](../06-beyond/12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md)).
* **Non-monotone laws destroy well-posedness.** A $\tau(\dot\gamma)$ curve with a decreasing branch
  makes the steady problem ill-posed (shear banding) unless regularised by gradient terms or
  stress diffusion.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Mixed formulations** need LBB-stable elements or stabilisation; naive pairs lock or oscillate.
* **Separate physical from numerical instability** in the high-$\mathrm{Wi}$ regime: mesh
  refinement studies plus DEVSS/SUPG-type schemes are the usual defences.
* **Yield-stress solvers** use augmented Lagrangian or regularised bi-viscosity models; the
  regularisation parameter controls the resolvable sharpness of the plug boundary.
* **Benchmarks**: 4:1 contraction, viscoelastic flow past a sphere (drag reduction, negative wake),
  lid-driven cavity at matched $\mathrm{Wi}$ ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Thixotropic and ageing materials; elastic turbulence as a mixing mechanism;
  nonlocal rheology in dense suspensions.

## Checks

1. What distinguishes shear thinning from viscoelasticity? *(The first depends only on the current
   shear rate; the second on history.)*
2. Why is rod-climbing impossible in a Newtonian fluid? *(It requires normal-stress differences,
   hence elasticity.)*
3. Why do yield-stress problems need free-boundary methods? *(The plug boundary is an output.)*

## See also

[Memory & viscoelastic transport](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md) ·
[Variational inequalities & hysteresis](../06-beyond/12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md) ·
[Constitutive relations](../01-foundations/03-constitutive-relations.md)

# Microscopic transport


# Microscopic transport

This category explains **where the coefficients come from**. Everything here answers one of two
questions: *what is the microscopic mechanism?* and *when does the macroscopic law stop being valid?*

| # | Note | The one thing it gives you |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | [Diffusion (Fick)](01-diffusion.md) | the archetype: Green function, $\sqrt{Dt}$, maximum principle, in one place |
| 2 | [Brownian motion & Einstein relations](02-brownian-motion-and-einstein-relations.md) | the 1905 argument and the relations every simulation uses |
| 3 | [Conduction (Fourier)](03-conduction.md) | heat by contact, phonons and electrons, contact resistance |
| 4 | [Diffusion coefficients](04-diffusion-coefficients.md) | three routes to $D$, and where each route fails |
| 5 | [Knudsen & rarefied transport](05-knudsen-and-rarefied-transport.md) | the continuum's expiry date: slip, jump, ballistic transport |
| 6 | [Cross-effects & multicomponent diffusion](06-cross-effects-and-multicomponent.md) | Soret, Dufour, Maxwell–Stefan: coupling as a rule, not an exception |
| 7 | [Charged & electrochemical transport](07-charged-and-electrochemical-transport.md) | Nernst–Planck, PNP, Debye layers, drift–diffusion |
| 8 | [Anomalous diffusion](08-anomalous-diffusion.md) | when $\langle x^2\rangle$ is not linear: CTRW, Lévy, non-ergodicity |

![**Figure B.** One random walk seen three ways — Green function, trajectories and mean squared displacement. Almost every note in this category is a variation on these three pictures.](diffusion-suite.pdf)

## The through-line

Molecular transport is **slow and patient**: $t\sim L^2/D$. That single scaling explains the sizes that
biology adopts, why groundwater plumes take decades to move, and why engineering almost always replaces
molecular transport with advection or convection. Read [diffusion](01-diffusion.md) first, then follow the
question you actually have: *where does $D$ come from?* ([coefficients](04-diffusion-coefficients.md)) ·
*when does the law fail?* ([Knudsen](05-knudsen-and-rarefied-transport.md)) ·
*what if there is another gradient?* ([cross-effects](06-cross-effects-and-multicomponent.md)) ·
*what if the walk is not simple?* ([anomalous](08-anomalous-diffusion.md)).

\newpage

# Diffusion (Fick)


# Diffusion (Fick)

> **One-line picture.** Random molecular motion evens out concentrations, producing a flux
> proportional to the gradient — and a very specific, very slow kind of spreading.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [constitutive relations](../01-foundations/03-constitutive-relations.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Brownian motion](02-brownian-motion-and-einstein-relations.md),
> [conduction](03-conduction.md), [anomalous diffusion](08-anomalous-diffusion.md)

![**Figure 14.** The three faces of diffusion: the Green function spreading as $\sqrt{Dt}$, individual random walkers, and the mean squared displacement defining the normal case $\langle x^2\rangle=2Dt$.](diffusion-suite.pdf)

## L1 · What it is

Put sugar in a still cup of tea and wait. Nothing stirs, yet the sugar spreads: molecules are
jostled billions of times a second, and although no molecule is *directed* anywhere, more leave the
dense region than enter it. The net effect is a patient flow from dense to dilute.

The patience is the point. Molecular diffusion moves matter over microns in milliseconds and over
metres in years — which is why biology and engineering deploy advection, convection or active
transport whenever something must move quickly
([battle of timescales](../01-foundations/05-dimensionless-numbers.md)).

## L2 · Fick's two laws

$$\mathbf{J}=-D\nabla c \ \ \text{(first)},\qquad \partial_tc=\nabla\cdot(D\nabla c)\ \ \text{(second)} .$$

For a point release in 1-D the solution is the Gaussian kernel
$c=\frac{M}{\sqrt{4\pi Dt}}e^{-x^2/(4Dt)}$, which encodes the whole personality of diffusion:

| Feature | Scaling | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| width | $\sqrt{4Dt}$ | spreading slows: diffusive, not ballistic |
| peak height | $\propto t^{-1/2}$ | a pulse fades while conserving mass |
| penetration depth | $L\sim\sqrt{Dt}$ | the ubiquitous square-root law |
| time to cross $L$ | $t\sim L^2/D$ | 4$\times$ the distance costs 4$\times$ the time |
| MSD | $\langle x^2\rangle=2Dt$ | the definition of normal diffusion |

Anisotropy replaces $D$ by a symmetric positive-definite tensor, and tortuosity reduces it in
porous media, $D_{\rm eff}=D\theta/\tau$.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Maximum principle.** Solutions lie between the extremes of the data — no overshoots, ever. This
  is the basis of positivity in every transport solver.
* **Infinite propagation speed.** The Gaussian is nonzero everywhere for any $t>0$; information
  spreads infinitely fast, if faintly. This is the flaw that motivates
  [Cattaneo's hyperbolic heat equation](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md).
* **Irreversibility.** High wavenumbers decay as $e^{-Dk^2t}$
  ([spectral view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md)), the backward problem
  is ill-posed, and entropy grows ([variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md)).
* **Degeneracy changes the class.** If $D(c)\to0$ as $c\to0$, diffusion still holds but propagation
  speed becomes *finite* and free boundaries appear —
  [porous-medium equation](../06-beyond/06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md).
* **It is a metric phenomenon.** Heat flow is steepest descent of entropy in the Wasserstein metric
  ([optimal transport](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md)).

## L4 · Numerics

* **Stiffness.** Explicit schemes need $\Delta t\le\Delta x^2/(2D)$: halving the grid quadruples the
  step count. Implicit schemes remove the limit at the cost of an elliptic solve per step
  ([time integration](../07-numerics/02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md)).
* **Positivity.** Centred advection plus diffusion produces undershoots (negative concentrations),
  which wreck reaction terms; use monotone or
  [flux-limited schemes](../07-numerics/04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md).
* **Varying coefficients** must be discretised conservatively, with harmonic-type face averaging, or
  a spurious source appears at material interfaces
  ([structure-preserving schemes](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)).
* **Benchmarks**: the Gaussian itself (via manufactured solutions), interface problems with jumps in
  $D$, and the Stefan problem for the degenerate case
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).

## Checks

1. Glucose ($D\approx7\times10^{-10}$ m²/s) across 1 mm, then 1 m? *(≈24 minutes; ≈45 years.)*
2. Why does the peak decay while the area is constant? *(Conservation fixes area; spreading forces
   height down.)*
3. When is Fick's law invalid even though diffusion is the mechanism? *(When the mean free path
   approaches the device size — [Knudsen transport](05-knudsen-and-rarefied-transport.md).)*

## See also

[Brownian motion & Einstein relations](02-brownian-motion-and-einstein-relations.md) ·
[Conduction](03-conduction.md) ·
[Anomalous diffusion](08-anomalous-diffusion.md) ·
[Words that collide: diffusion & conduction](../09-polysemy/03-diffusion-and-conduction.md)

# Brownian motion & Einstein relations


# Brownian motion & Einstein relations

> **One-line picture.** Watch a pollen grain under a microscope and it jitters. The jitter is
> molecules; the smoothed-out law of the jitter is Fick's law; and the relation between them fixes
> the size of atoms.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [diffusion](01-diffusion.md),
> [random walks](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md) · **Leads to.**
> [Fokker–Planck & Kramers](../05-kinetic/03-fokker-planck-and-kramers.md),
> [anomalous diffusion](08-anomalous-diffusion.md)

## L1 · The experiment and the argument

In 1827 Brown saw pollen grains jittering in water and could not explain it. In 1905 Einstein
proposed that the grains are being kicked by molecules too small to see, and turned that hypothesis
into a testable number:

$$\langle x^2\rangle=2Dt,\qquad D=\frac{k_BT}{6\pi\mu a}.$$

Measure the jitter of a sphere of known radius in water of known viscosity at known temperature and
you have measured $k_B$ — hence Avogadro's number, hence the size of molecules. This is one of the
arguments that made atoms unavoidable, and Perrin's experiments (1908) settled it.

## L2 · Three relations everyone uses

| Relation | Statement | Use |
|---|---|---|
| MSD law | $\langle r^2\rangle=6Dt$ (3-D) | extract $D$ from trajectories |
| Einstein (diffusion–mobility) | $D=\mu_{\rm mob}k_BT$ | link transport to response |
| Stokes–Einstein | $D=k_BT/(6\pi\mu a)$ | size from diffusion, diffusion from size |
| Stokes–Einstein–Debye (rotational) | $D_{\rm rot}=k_BT/(8\pi\mu a^3)$ | fluorescence anisotropy, NMR relaxation |
| Smoluchowski (suspensions) | collect $D$ and drift for settling | sedimentation equilibrium |

The Einstein relation is the simplest instance of the
[fluctuation–dissipation theorem](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md): the same
molecular collisions that cause diffusion also cause drag. Consequently **you never fit both** — one
determines the other, and a simulation that violates this violates the second law.

Einstein's other gift to transport theory is the osmotic-pressure interpretation: a suspension
sedimenting under gravity settles until the sedimentation flux balances the diffusive flux, giving
the barometric profile $c(z)\propto e^{-mgz/k_BT}$. This is the equilibrium face of transport:
**flux balance *is* equilibrium.**

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **The microscopic scale sets the coefficient.** $D$ is a function of temperature, molecular size
  and solvent viscosity — not of the apparatus. That is why $D$ is a *material property* in the
  macroscopic equation.
* **Reynolds-number dependence.** Stokes–Einstein assumes $\mathrm{Re}\ll1$; larger particles or
  lower viscosity require corrections (Oseen, Faxén, or the full mobility tensor for anisotropic
  bodies), and near walls the mobility is reduced by hydrodynamic interactions.
* **Concentration dependence.** In concentrated suspensions, many-body hydrodynamic interactions
  reduce $D$ (and cause short-time/long-time discrepancies). Hard-sphere suspensions show
  $D\to0$ as jamming is approached — a transport coefficient controlled by geometry.
* **Finite particle size.** $D=k_BT/(6\pi\mu a)$ breaks down for solutes comparable to the solvent
  molecule (sub-nanometre), which is why measured diffusion of small ions is far below the
  hydrodynamic prediction (see [diffusion coefficients](04-diffusion-coefficients.md)).

## L4 · Measurement and simulation

* **Measurement.** Dynamic light scattering (DLS) measures $D$ from intensity autocorrelations;
  PFG-NMR measures displacement; single-particle tracking gives full distributions and detects
  anomalous behaviour ([measurement](../10-appendix/05-measurement-and-experiments.md)).
* **Simulation hierarchy.** Molecular dynamics (all-atom) → Brownian dynamics (Langevin with
  hydrodynamic interactions) → DPD/dissipative particle dynamics → continuum diffusion. Choosing
  the rung is an explicit trade between fidelity and time scale.
* **Trajectory analysis.** Always fit $\langle r^2\rangle$ with sub-sampling to avoid correlation
  bias; distinguish short-time and long-time diffusion coefficients in crowded systems.
* **Frontiers.** Anomalous diffusion in living cells (cytoskeletal obstacles, active motors);
  hydrodynamically interacting colloids near jamming; quantum limits of Brownian motion.

## Checks

1. Why does raising the temperature increase $D$ *and* decrease the viscosity, both raising $D$?
   *($D=k_BT/6\pi\mu a$: $T$ up, $\mu$ down, both increase $D$ — a double effect.)*
2. A protein of 5 nm radius in water at 300 K: what $D$? *($\approx4\times10^{-11}$ m²/s: a
   micron in ~10 s — the reason cells use motors.)*
3. What does sedimentation equilibrium tell you about the diffusion coefficient? *(Balancing
   sedimentation and diffusion fluxes gives a barometric profile whose decay length is set by
   $k_BT$.)*

## See also

[Diffusion](01-diffusion.md) ·
[Random walks, Langevin & response](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md) ·
[Diffusion coefficients](04-diffusion-coefficients.md) ·
[Biology & medicine](../08-bridges/03-biology-and-medicine.md)
