# Conduction (Fourier)


# Conduction (Fourier)

> **One-line picture.** Heat is the jitter of atoms. Conduction is that jitter being passed along
> from neighbour to neighbour without the material moving — one bucket brigade, no crowd.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [diffusion](01-diffusion.md),
> [constitutive relations](../01-foundations/03-constitutive-relations.md) · **Leads to.**
> [phase change & Stefan](../04-interaction/09-phase-change-and-stefan.md),
> [engineering devices](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md)

## L1 · What it is

Touch a metal spoon to a flame. The handle warms long before the flame reaches it. No material has
travelled: the energy passes down the rod as atomic vibrations (phonons) and, in metals, as mobile
electrons. It is a *contact* mechanism — it needs a medium, and it stops dead at a vacuum (which is
why vacuum flasks exist).

The same mathematics describes charge conduction in wires, thermal conduction in windows and
diffusion in a beaker; only the coefficient and the carrier changes. This is the first place where
the [heat–mass–momentum analogy](../08-bridges/01-analogy-and-circuit-models.md) pays off.

## L2 · Fourier's law and the heat equation

$$\mathbf{q}=-k\nabla T,\qquad \rho c_p\,\partial_tT=\nabla\cdot(k\nabla T)+\dot q_{\rm gen},$$

with thermal diffusivity $\alpha=k/(\rho c_p)$. Typical values tell you most of what you need:

| Material | $k$ (W/m·K) | $\alpha$ (m²/s) | Remark |
|---|---|---|---|
| copper | 400 | $1.1\times10^{-4}$ | electrons dominate |
| water | 0.6 | $1.4\times10^{-7}$ | convection usually beats conduction |
| air | 0.026 | $2\times10^{-5}$ | insulators trap air |
| silica aerogel | 0.015 | — | nanostructure below gas mean free path |
| diamond | 2000 | — | phonon transport at its best |
| human tissue | 0.2–0.6 | — | drives thermal therapies |

For steady 1-D conduction, this collapses to the **thermal resistance** network
$R=L/(kA)$, in series and parallel exactly like resistors — the reason thermal engineers draw
circuits, not PDEs.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Maximum principle and smoothness.** Temperature never overshoots the data, and interior
  gradients relax; the steady problem has no interior extrema.
* **Interfaces and jump conditions.** The flux is continuous while $k$ jumps, so the *gradient* has
  a kink: $\llbracket k\partial_nT\rrbracket=0$. Getting this wrong is the classic source of a
  spurious interface source in codes. In practice a thin layer of imperfect contact adds a
  **contact resistance** $R_{\rm c}=1/h_{\rm c}$ — often dominant in electronics.
* **Ballistic limits.** Fourier's law assumes carriers scatter many times across the device. As
  the mean free path approaches the feature size, conduction becomes *ballistic* and the law fails:
  nanowires, thin films and graphene show size-dependent $k$, and heat can travel as waves
  (second sound) — see [Cattaneo & hyperbolic heat](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md).
* **Carrier physics gives the coefficient.** Kinetic theory gives $k\approx\frac13 C v\ell$ with
  $C$ the heat capacity, $v$ the carrier speed, $\ell$ the mean free path. In metals, the
  Wiedemann–Franz law ties $k$ to electrical conductivity, $k/(\sigma T)\approx L_0$ — a reminder
  that transport coefficients are linked by their microscopic carriers.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Multi-material solvers** must conserve flux at interfaces; use harmonic averaging of $k$ on
  faces ([structure-preserving](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)).
* **Nonlinear materials** ($k(T)$, phase change with latent heat) make the problem nonlinear:
  effective-heat-capacity or enthalpy methods are standard, with the sharp latent-heat release
  handled implicitly or by [front tracking](../04-interaction/09-phase-change-and-stefan.md).
* **Radiation couples in at high temperature** (furnaces, re-entry, combustion) and is non-local —
  you need [radiative transfer](../05-kinetic/04-radiative-transfer.md), not a bigger $k$, though
  the Rosseland diffusion approximation is often a good first step.
* **Benchmarks**: 1-D composite wall, the Stefan problem, and pulsed laser heating (thermoreflectance)
  for extracting $k$ and interface conductance
  ([measurement](../10-appendix/05-measurement-and-experiments.md)).

## Checks

1. Why does a metal feel colder than wood at the same temperature? *(Much larger $k$ and $\alpha$:
   it draws heat from your finger faster.)*
2. Why must the gradient kink at a material interface? *(Flux continuity with different $k$.)*
3. When does Fourier's law fail? *(When the carrier mean free path is comparable to the device —
   nanoscale and ultrafast regimes.)*

## See also

[Diffusion](01-diffusion.md) ·
[Phase change & Stefan](../04-interaction/09-phase-change-and-stefan.md) ·
[Radiation & neutron transport](../05-kinetic/04-radiative-transfer.md) ·
[Heat–mass–momentum analogy](../08-bridges/01-analogy-and-circuit-models.md)

\newpage

# Diffusion coefficients


# Diffusion coefficients

> **One-line picture.** Fick's law is easy; the number in it is not. $D$ is a hydrodynamic
> statement about molecules that you can estimate in three ways, none of which is exact.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [diffusion](01-diffusion.md) · **Leads to.**
> [Knudsen & rarefied transport](05-knudsen-and-rarefied-transport.md),
> [Boltzmann equation](../05-kinetic/01-boltzmann-equation.md)

## L1 · Three routes to one number

1. **Empirical correlations** — Stokes–Einstein for large solutes, Wilke–Chang for liquids,
   Fuller for gases. Fast, broadly accurate, silent about limits.
2. **Kinetic theory** — mean free path over thermal speed, refined by Chapman–Enskog; excellent for
   dilute gases and for gases with known intermolecular potentials.
3. **Simulation** — molecular dynamics with Green–Kubo integrals
   ([linear response](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md)); the only route when the
   medium is complex (electrolytes, polymers, confining pores).

## L2 · The estimates

| Case | Formula | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| dilute gas (hard spheres) | $D\approx\frac13\bar v\lambda$, $\lambda\propto1/(n\sigma)$ | $D\propto T^{3/2}/p$: **independent of pressure at fixed composition** in mole fraction terms, decreasing with $p$ |
| dilute gas (real potential) | Chapman–Enskog: $D\propto T^{1.5{-}1.8}/(p\sigma^2\Omega)$ | collision integral $\Omega$ from the potential |
| large sphere in liquid | $D=k_BT/(6\pi\mu a)$ | Stokes–Einstein (valid for $a\gg$ solvent size) |
| liquid self-diffusion | $D\sim k_BT/(\zeta)$ with $\zeta$ from viscosity | free-volume and Arrhenius forms; strong $T$ dependence |
| electrolyte at infinite dilution | Nernst–Einstein: $D_i=RT\lambda_i/(z_i^2F^2)$ | links $D$ to molar conductivity |
| porous medium | $D_{\rm eff}=D\theta/\tau$ | tortuosity $\tau>1$; measured, never guessed |
| in a membrane/tissue | $D_{\rm eff}$ plus obstructions | crowding makes it anomalous at short times |

**Two scaling laws worth memorising:** in gases, $D$ rises steeply with temperature
(roughly $T^{1.8}$) and falls as $1/p$; in liquids, $D$ follows an Arrhenius or free-volume law and
is fairly insensitive to pressure but very sensitive to viscosity and structure.

## L3 · Where the clean formulas fail

* **Small solutes.** Stokes–Einstein overestimates $D$ for molecules comparable to the solvent;
  the effective hydrodynamic radius is not the molecular radius.
* **Concentration.** In non-ideal mixtures, the driving force is the *gradient of chemical
  potential*, not of concentration, so Fick's $D$ becomes activity-corrected,
  $D=D_{\rm MS}(1+\partial\ln\gamma/\partial\ln c)$ ([Maxwell–Stefan](06-cross-effects-and-multicomponent.md)).
* **Non-ideal and multicomponent systems** require a diffusivity *matrix*, and ternary systems can
  show uphill diffusion (a component moving up its own gradient) — impossible for a scalar $D$.
* **Crowding and confinement.** Diffusion slows and often becomes anomalous in the presence of
  obstacles, binding sites and attractive walls; the long-time $D$ can be orders of magnitude below
  the short-time value ([anomalous diffusion](08-anomalous-diffusion.md)).
* **Cross-effects.** A temperature gradient adds a Soret flux, an electric field an ionic one —
  measured $D$ then depends on the experiment
  ([cross-effects](06-cross-effects-and-multicomponent.md)).

## L4 · Measurement and numerics

* **Standard methods**: diaphragm cell (classic, slow), Taylor dispersion in a capillary (precise),
  PFG-NMR, DLS, and electrochemical techniques for ions
  ([measurement](../10-appendix/05-measurement-and-experiments.md)).
* **Molecular dynamics pitfalls**: finite-size corrections ($D$ depends on box size), thermostat
  artifacts, and correlation-function tails; report the estimator and the fit window.
* **Inverse use**: fit $D(\mathbf{x},c)$ from column or field data — usually
  [ill-posed and regularised](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md).
* **Frontiers.** Machine-learned diffusion coefficients that respect thermodynamic consistency;
  concentration-dependent diffusivity in battery electrolytes; nanoconfined transport.

## Checks

1. Why does gas-phase $D$ fall as pressure rises but rise faster than linearly with temperature?
   *($D\propto T^{1.5}/p$: the mean free path sets both.)*
2. Why can Stokes–Einstein fail for a 0.2 nm ion? *(The hydrodynamic radius concept breaks down at
   molecular scales; hydration shells dominate.)*
3. What does tortuosity do to $D_{\rm eff}$? *(Reduces it, roughly as $\theta/\tau$ with
   $\tau$ typically 1.5–5.)*

## See also

[Diffusion](01-diffusion.md) ·
[Knudsen & rarefied transport](05-knudsen-and-rarefied-transport.md) ·
[Cross-effects & multicomponent](06-cross-effects-and-multicomponent.md) ·
[Boltzmann equation](../05-kinetic/01-boltzmann-equation.md)

\newpage

# Knudsen & rarefied transport


# Knudsen & rarefied transport

> **One-line picture.** Continuum laws assume molecules collide with each other far more often
> than with the walls. Shrink the device (or thin the gas) and that assumption dies — transport
> becomes ballistic, and slip replaces no-slip.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [diffusion coefficients](04-diffusion-coefficients.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Boltzmann equation](../05-kinetic/01-boltzmann-equation.md),
> [lattice-Boltzmann & particles](../05-kinetic/06-lattice-boltzmann-and-particle-kinetics.md)

![**Figure 15.** The Knudsen ladder. As the mean free path grows relative to the device, molecules stop behaving collectively, the hydrodynamic closure is lost, and only kinetic descriptions remain.](knudsen-regimes.pdf)

## L1 · The number that decides everything

$$\mathrm{Kn}=\frac{\lambda}{L}=\frac{\text{mean free path}}{\text{device size}} .$$

| Range | Regime | What you may use |
|---|---|---|
| $\mathrm{Kn}<0.01$ | continuum | Navier–Stokes + Fick/Fourier/Fourier, no-slip |
| $0.01-0.1$ | slip flow | NS with velocity-slip and temperature-jump conditions |
| $0.1-10$ | transition | no hydrodynamic closure: DSMC, moment methods, LBM |
| $>10$ | free molecular | ballistic molecules; direct wall-to-wall transport |

Everyday examples that sit in the awkward middle: micro-electromechanical sensors, vacuum gauges,
aerosols, hypersonic re-entry, shale-gas pores (nanometres at reservoir pressures), and the gas in
insulating aerogels.

## L2 · What replaces the constitutive laws

* **Velocity slip:** $u_{\rm wall}=\sigma_\lambda\lambda\,\partial_nu|_{\rm wall}$, with
  $\sigma_\lambda\approx1$. The no-slip condition is a large-$\mathrm{Kn}$ casualty, and slip adds
  an apparent reduction in drag and an increase in flow rate (apparent permeability in tight
  porous media — the Klinkenberg effect).
* **Temperature jump:** a discontinuity proportional to $\mathrm{Kn}$ appears between wall and gas,
  modifying heat transfer at micro scales.
* **Knudsen diffusion** in a pore: molecules hit walls more often than each other, so the flux is
  set by wall collisions rather than by molecule–molecule scattering:
  $D_K=\frac{2r_p}{3}\sqrt{8RT/\pi M}$, which is *independent of pressure* and important in
  catalysis and shale gas.
* **Combined regimes** use the Bosanquet formula $1/D=1/D_{\rm mol}+1/D_K$; the total conductance
  behaves like two resistors in series.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **The continuum limit is a theorem, not an assumption.** Chapman–Enskog expansion of the
  [Boltzmann equation](../05-kinetic/01-boltzmann-equation.md) in $\mathrm{Kn}$ recovers
  Navier–Stokes at $O(\mathrm{Kn})$ and the Fourier/Fick laws with explicitly computable
  coefficients. Corrections at $O(\mathrm{Kn}^2)$ are the Burnett and super-Burnett terms, and
  they are famously unstable and non-hyperbolic — a warning that higher-order continuum closures
  are not automatically improvements.
* **Memory appears.** Relaxation-time (BGK) treatments give first-order-in-time corrections that
  augment the flux, i.e. a
  [Cattaneo-type hyperbolic heat equation](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md) with
  finite propagation speed; the kinetic origin of finite-speed diffusion is here.
* **Reciprocity and symmetry survive.** Linearised kinetic operators are self-adjoint with respect
  to a suitable inner product, which is why Onsager symmetry holds even far from the continuum.
* **Boltzmann's H-theorem** guarantees an entropy that decreases under collisions; the macroscopic
  consequences are the positivity of transport coefficients everywhere in the kinetic regime.

## L4 · Numerics

* **DSMC** (direct simulation Monte Carlo) is the workhorse for $\mathrm{Kn}\gtrsim0.1$: sample
  particles, move them ballistically, collide them stochastically using the collision integral.
  Cost scales with the number of collisions, not the number of grid cells.
* **Moment methods** (Grad-13, R13, regularised) run at continuum-like cost with kinetic accuracy in
  modest $\mathrm{Kn}$, but need care to stay realisable (positive distribution).
* **Hybrid continuum–kinetic** coupling is the standard practical compromise: kinetic near walls and
  shocks, continuum in the bulk.
* **Lattice-Boltzmann** extends to $\mathrm{Kn}\sim0.1$ with careful wall treatment; slip is
  produced naturally by kinetic wall rules.
* **Frontiers.** High-speed rarefied flows (hypersonics), droplet/aerosol transport, micro-scale
  vacuum-pumped devices, and hybrid solvers with adaptive domain decomposition.

## Checks

1. Mean free path in air at 1 atm is $\approx68$ nm. Where does a MEMS channel with 1 µm gap sit?
   *($\mathrm{Kn}\approx0.07$: slip regime — measurable extra flow.)*
2. Why is Knudsen diffusion pressure-independent? *(Wall collisions, not molecule–molecule
   collisions, set the transport.)*
3. Why are Burnett equations rarely used? *(They can violate stability and the second law at
   $O(\mathrm{Kn}^2)$.)*

## See also

[Boltzmann equation](../05-kinetic/01-boltzmann-equation.md) ·
[Diffusion coefficients](04-diffusion-coefficients.md) ·
[Lattice-Boltzmann & particle kinetics](../05-kinetic/06-lattice-boltzmann-and-particle-kinetics.md) ·
[Cattaneo & hyperbolic heat](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md)

# Cross-effects & multicomponent diffusion


# Cross-effects & multicomponent diffusion

> **One-line picture.** Forces do not act in isolation: a temperature gradient can drag mass, a
> concentration gradient can drag heat, and in a mixture of three species one component can move
> *up* its own gradient.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [diffusion](01-diffusion.md),
> [constitutive relations](../01-foundations/03-constitutive-relations.md) · **Leads to.**
> [charged transport](07-charged-and-electrochemical-transport.md),
> [linear response](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md)

## L1 · Forces and fluxes come in families

In a system with several gradients, the flux of each conserved quantity depends on *all* the
forces. The thermodynamic skeleton is $\mathbf{J}_i=\sum_j L_{ij}\mathbf{X}_j$ with $L$ symmetric
(Onsager) and positive semidefinite (second law). Two consequences follow immediately:

* **Cross-effects appear** wherever a force of one type drives a flux of another.
* **They come in reciprocal pairs** — you cannot have one without the other.

The practical reason to care is that *measured* transport coefficients are not the intrinsic ones:
a "diffusion coefficient" measured in a temperature gradient is a mixture of $D$ and the Soret
coefficient, and a "thermal conductivity" measured in a concentration gradient includes Dufour.

## L2 · The named effects

| Effect | Force → flux | Where it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Soret (thermodiffusion) | $\nabla T\to$ mass flux | isotope separation, mantle differentiation, polymer characterisation |
| Dufour | $\nabla c\to$ heat flux | combustion, mixtures with large $\mathrm{Le}$ |
| Thermophoresis | $\nabla T\to$ particle drift | soot deposition, aerosol sampling |
| Diffusiophoresis | $\nabla c\to$ particle drift | drying films, chromatography |
| Seebeck / Peltier | $\nabla T\leftrightarrow$ current | thermoelectrics |
| Maxwell–Stefan | species friction | distillation, membranes, electrolyte transport |
| Osmosis | $\nabla c\to$ solvent flow | membranes, cells, desalination |

**Maxwell–Stefan** is the honest multicomponent formulation: rather than assigning a diffusion
coefficient to each species independently, it balances the driving force on each species against
*friction with every other species*,

$$\nabla\mu_i=-\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{x_j}{\mathcal{D}_{ij}}(\mathbf{v}_i-\mathbf{v}_j),$$

The concentration-based "Fickian" diffusivity matrix you fit afterwards is a derived object with
built-in non-ideality (activity coefficients) and can have negative eigenvalues — producing
**uphill diffusion**, where a component moves from low to high concentration. This is not a
paradox: Fick's law is a special case, not a law of nature.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Onsager symmetry pins the cross-coefficients together.** Measure Soret accurately and you know
  the Dufour coefficient; wrong-sign pairs violate the second law.
* **Curie's principle limits what can appear.** In isotropic media, linear coupling of different
  tensorial order is forbidden — which is why Soret produces *no* cross-effect with viscous stress,
  but interfaces and anisotropy break the restriction and produce new effects.
* **Stability can be broken by cross-effects.** Double-diffusive instability (warm salty water) and
  Soret-driven convection arise even when each individual gradient is diffusively stable: the
  *difference* in diffusivities is the instability mechanism.
* **Negative eigenvalues are physical.** Uphill diffusion appears in non-ideal mixtures and
  electrolytes; codes that assume a positive-definite scalar diffusivity silently misbehave here.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Use Maxwell–Stefan when species counts change** (reactive, multicomponent or non-ideal
  systems); use Fick when the mixture is dilute and near-ideal.
* **Consistency conditions.** In a closed system, the diffusive fluxes must sum to zero
  ($\sum\mathbf{J}_i=0$) and mass fractions must stay bounded — small inconsistencies produce slow
  drift and negative concentrations.
* **Watch stiffness.** Cross-effects couple the energy and species equations, forming stiff systems
  that need implicit treatment ([time integration](../07-numerics/02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md)).
* **Benchmarks**: the Soret-driven Benard problem, ternary diffusion in ideal and non-ideal
  mixtures, and standard electrolyte test problems
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Cross-effects in ion channels and nanofluidics; thermodiffusion in crowded
  biological media; coupled transport in battery and fuel-cell electrodes.

## Checks

1. Can a temperature gradient drive mass without any concentration gradient? *(Yes — Soret; and its
   reciprocal, Dufour, drives heat with a concentration gradient.)*
2. Is uphill diffusion a violation of the second law? *(No: the relevant force is the chemical
   potential gradient, which still points downhill.)*
3. Why do you need a matrix, not a number, for a ternary mixture? *(Each species' flux depends on
   every other species' force.)*

## See also

[Charged & electrochemical transport](07-charged-and-electrochemical-transport.md) ·
[Constitutive relations](../01-foundations/03-constitutive-relations.md) ·
[Linear response & memory](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md) ·
[Double diffusion in geoscience](../08-bridges/04-geoscience-and-climate.md)

# Charged & electrochemical transport


# Charged & electrochemical transport

> **One-line picture.** Ions respond to two drives at once — concentration gradients (diffusion)
> and electric fields (migration) — and they *create* the fields they respond to.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [diffusion](01-diffusion.md),
> [cross-effects](06-cross-effects-and-multicomponent.md) · **Leads to.**
> [biology & medicine](../08-bridges/03-biology-and-medicine.md),
> [engineering devices](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md)

## L1 · Two forces, one species

Salt in water and electrons in silicon obey the same transport equation. Each charged species
carries a flux with a diffusive part and a migration part:

$$\mathbf{J}_i=-D_i\nabla c_i-\frac{z_iF}{RT}D_i c_i\nabla\phi+\mathbf{u}c_i,\qquad
-\varepsilon\nabla^2\phi=F\sum_i z_ic_i .$$

The first line is the **Nernst–Planck** equation; the second is **Poisson's** equation. Together
they are the **Poisson–Nernst–Planck (PNP)** system. Note the built-in Einstein relation between
mobility and diffusivity ($D_i=u_iRT/F$) — the electrostatic face of the
[fluctuation–dissipation theorem](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md).

## L2 · The approximations that make it tractable

| Approximation | Statement | When valid |
|---|---|---|
| electroneutrality | $\sum z_ic_i\approx0$ | away from interfaces, $L\gg\lambda_D$ |
| Debye layer | $\lambda_D=\sqrt{\varepsilon RT/(F^2\sum z_i^2c_i)}$ | sets the interfacial length scale (0.3–100 nm in water) |
| linearised PB | $zF\phi/RT\ll1$ | small potentials: Gouy–Chapman vs Debye–Hückel |
| single-ionic drift–diffusion | neglect $\nabla\phi$ from carriers | low-injection semiconductors |
| ambipolar diffusion | electrons and holes move together | photoconductors, plasmas |
| limiting current | one species exhausted at the electrode | electrodialysis, batteries at high rate |

In semiconductors the same equation is written
$\mathbf{J}_n=qD_n\nabla n+q\mu_nn\nabla\psi$ plus a recombination term; in plasmas, ambipolar
diffusion keeps quasineutrality by tying electron and ion fluxes together. **One equation, three
communities, three vocabularies** — see
[words that collide #3](../09-polysemy/03-diffusion-and-conduction.md).

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Electroneutrality is an asymptotic limit.** As $\lambda_D/L\to0$, PNP converges (after matching
  boundary layers) to the electroneutral drift–diffusion system — the mathematical reason bulk
  electrolytes have no field, while interfaces always do.
* **Debye layers are boundary layers.** The potential decays exponentially over $\lambda_D$, giving
  a surface capacitance $\varepsilon/\lambda_D$ and a streaming potential when fluid flows past a
  charged wall. Overlapping Debye layers change the transport law entirely (nanochannels).
* **Nonlinearity and non-ideality.** Activity coefficients, ion pairing and steric (finite-size)
  effects matter at high concentration; then Nernst–Planck must be generalised (Maxwell–Stefan with
  electroneutrality constraints, or PNP with modified electrochemical potentials).
* **Charges create structure.** Space-charge regions, concentration polarisation and (in extreme
  cases) electroconvective instability are *consequences* of the coupling, not add-ons; they are the
  reason electrodialysis and battery charging hit limits.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Stiffness is severe** because $\varepsilon$ is tiny: Poisson couples over nm while devices are
  mm. Implicit or multiscale treatments, and asymptotic (electroneutral) formulations, are standard.
* **Current conservation** must be enforced exactly — non-conservative discretisations produce
  spurious charge accumulation and non-convergence. Use finite-volume or mixed FEM with consistent
  fluxes ([structure-preserving schemes](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)).
* **Positivity** is mandatory: negative concentrations or densities break the Poisson coupling.
  Reconstruct in log-variables (Slotboom transform) when needed.
* **Benchmarks**: the Gouy–Chapman profile, limiting-current polarisation curves, the PNP system for
  a perm-selective membrane, and the standard SOC/diode curves for semiconductor solvers
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Ion transport in nanochannels and biological pores; electroconvection; coupled
  electro-chemo-mechanics in solid electrolytes (dendrites, fracture).

## Checks

1. Why does the bulk of an electrolyte show no electric field? *(Electroneutrality at leading
   order; fields live in Debye layers of thickness $\lambda_D$.)*
2. What is the transport analogue of the Einstein relation here? *(Mobility and diffusivity are
   proportional: $D=uRT/F$.)*
3. Why is PNP numerically stiff? *(The Poisson length scale $\lambda_D$ can be 5 orders of magnitude
   below the device scale.)*

## See also

[Cross-effects & multicomponent](06-cross-effects-and-multicomponent.md) ·
[Diffusion](01-diffusion.md) ·
[Biology & medicine](../08-bridges/03-biology-and-medicine.md) ·
[Engineering devices](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md)

\newpage

# Anomalous diffusion


# Anomalous diffusion

> **One-line picture.** Fick's law assumes a walker with finite step variance and finite mean
> waiting time. Break either assumption and diffusion stops being Fickian — but it remains
> *transport*, with laws of its own.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [diffusion](01-diffusion.md),
> [random walks](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md) · **Leads to.**
> [nonlocal & fractional operators](../06-beyond/03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md),
> [stochastic PDEs & random media](../06-beyond/07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md)

![**Figure 16.** Mean squared displacement for the four transport classes. All are called diffusion; only $\alpha=1$ is Fickian, and the exponent identifies the mechanism.](diffusion-suite.pdf)

## L1 · The generalisation, and where it comes from

Define $\langle x^2\rangle\sim t^{\alpha}$. Normal diffusion is $\alpha=1$; **subdiffusion** $\alpha<1$
comes from trapping, crowding and viscoelastic drag; **superdiffusion** $\alpha>1$ comes from long
jumps, active motion and turbulence; $\alpha=2$ is ballistic.

| Mechanism | Exponent | Setting |
|---|---|---|
| waiting times $\psi(\tau)\sim\tau^{-1-\gamma}$ | $\alpha=\gamma$ | traps, sticking, glassy dynamics |
| Lévy flights, jumps $\sim|x|^{-1-\beta}$ | $t^{1/\beta}$ scaling, infinite variance | plasma transport, animal search |
| viscoelastic drag / memory kernel | $\alpha<1$, fractional | polymer solutions, cytoskeleton |
| correlated velocities (persistent walk) | $\alpha\approx2$, crossing to 1 | active matter, swimmers |
| broad velocity distribution in a heterogeneous medium | $\alpha\approx1$ with heavy tails | fractured rock, groundwater |
| continuous-time random walk (CTRW) | framework for all of the above | the standard model |

Keeping waiting times random makes the walk subdiffusive; keeping jump lengths heavy-tailed makes
it superdiffusive. In the continuum limit both become **fractional** equations
([fractional operators](../06-beyond/03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md)):

$$\partial_t^{\alpha}c=D_{\alpha}\,\partial_{|x|}^{\beta}c .$$

## L2 · How to tell them apart in data

* **Log–log MSD slope** is the first diagnostic — and the one most often misreported because the fit
  window decides the answer.
* **Distribution shape:** subdiffusion keeps a Gaussian core with long tails; Lévy flights have
  heavy tails in the displacement distribution itself.
* **Ergodicity breaking:** for subdiffusive CTRW, time averages and ensemble averages disagree, so
  single-particle tracking and bulk measurements return different "diffusion coefficients". A real
  effect, not an error bar.
* **Ageing:** transport depends on how long the system has been running, violating the assumptions
  of standard inverse methods.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **The Laplacian is special.** The fractional Laplacian $(-\Delta)^{\beta/2}$ generates a Lévy
  process: nonlocal, scale-invariant, and requiring *global* boundary conditions — locality of
  physics is lost.
* **Relaxation stops being exponential.** Time-fractional equations decay as Mittag-Leffler /
  power laws and never fully forget initial data, which is why memory effects persist in glasses and
  cells for experimental ages.
* **Variance may not exist.** For superdiffusive Lévy transport $\langle x^2\rangle=\infty$: second
  moments are the wrong descriptor, so use scaling functions or fractional moments instead.
* **Anomaly can be transient or asymptotic.** Finite traps eventually exhaust (crossover to
  normal); fractal trap distributions sustain anomaly forever. The crossover time is often the
  number that matters in practice.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Fractional solvers**: convolution quadrature, FFT for periodic problems, Toeplitz and
  sum-of-exponentials schemes for long history
  ([fractional operators](../06-beyond/03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md)).
* **Monte Carlo is easiest**: simulate CTRW or Lévy walkers directly instead of assembling dense
  nonlocal matrices ([particle methods](../07-numerics/09-particle-and-monte-carlo-methods.md)).
* **Do not fit Fickian models to anomalous data**: the fit returns a length- and time-dependent $D$,
  which is a symptom of model error, not a coefficient.
* **Benchmarks**: CTRW exponent recovery, subdiffusive Barenblatt-type profiles, Lévy tail
  distributions ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Anomalous transport in cells and tissues; non-ergodic groundwater transport;
  anomalous heat conduction at the nanoscale.

## Checks

1. Which two random-walk assumptions does Fick's law require? *(Finite jump variance and finite mean
   waiting time.)*
2. Why do time-averaged and ensemble measurements disagree in subdiffusive CTRW? *(Ergodicity is
   broken.)*
3. Which operator replaces $\nabla^2$ for Lévy flights? *(The fractional Laplacian
   $(-\Delta)^{\beta/2}$, $\beta<2$.)*

## See also

[Diffusion](01-diffusion.md) ·
[Nonlocal & fractional operators](../06-beyond/03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md) ·
[Stochastic PDEs & random media](../06-beyond/07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md) ·
[Shear dispersion](../02-bulk/06-shear-dispersion-taylor-aris.md)

# Interaction, source & sink


# Interaction, source & sink

Everything so far in the atlas *moves* things. This category changes them: reactions transform, surfaces
capture, phases change, wells inject, and dissipation converts motion into heat. Formally, these are the
right-hand-side terms — but physically they are where the interesting behaviour (nonlinearity, patterns,
shocks, thresholds) comes from.

| # | Note | The one thing it gives you |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | [Reaction kinetics & chemical sinks](01-reaction-kinetics-and-sinks.md) | orders, Arrhenius, saturation, scavenging — the source term that makes equations nonlinear |
| 3 | [Autocatalysis & oscillations](03-autocatalysis-and-oscillations.md) | clocks, excitability and what happens when a Lyapunov function disappears |
| 4 | [Reaction–diffusion & Turing](04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md) | diffusion creating pattern; wavelength selection |
| 5 | [Fronts & travelling waves](05-fronts-and-travelling-waves.md) | invasion, flames and nerve impulses as one object; speed selection |
| 6 | [Chemotaxis & Keller–Segel](06-chemotaxis-and-keller-segel.md) | directed cell motion, aggregation and blow-up |
| 7 | [Sorption & retardation](07-sorption-and-retardation.md) | why pollutants arrive late: isotherms, retardation, chromatography |
| 8 | [Absorption, dissolution & interfacial transfer](08-absorption-dissolution-and-interfacial-transfer.md) | the mass-transfer coefficient, two-film theory, reaction enhancement |
| 9 | [Phase change & the Stefan problem](09-phase-change-and-stefan.md) | moving boundaries paid for in latent heat |
| 10 | [Sedimentation, deposition & filtration](10-sedimentation-deposition-and-filtration.md) | particles that fall and stick; cake resistance |
| 11 | [Accretion, ablation & surface growth](11-accretion-ablation-and-surface-growth.md) | domains that grow and shrink; residual stress |
| 12 | [Wells, injection, efflux & breakthrough](12-wells-injection-efflux-and-breakthrough.md) | point sources and the measurement that characterises a site |
| 13 | [Subsidence & poroelasticity](13-subsidence-and-poroelasticity.md) | transport that changes the solid — permanently |
| 14 | [Viscous dissipation & heating](14-viscous-dissipation-and-heating.md) | the one-way conversion of motion into heat |

![**Figure C.** The shapes source and sink terms create: an advected and diffusing pulse, with a
breakthrough curve that carries the fingerprint of every interaction the solute experienced.](advection-vs-diffusion.pdf)

## Three ideas that organise this category

1. **Zero-order terms change amplitude; nonlinear terms change structure.** Exponential decay, logistic
   growth, shock-forming isotherms and Turing patterns all come from the same algebraic term $R(c)$.
2. **Interfaces are where the rate law lives.** Sorption, absorption, phase change, filtration and growth
   are all *boundary* phenomena: get the interface condition wrong and no amount of interior accuracy
   helps.
3. **Feedback makes transport stiff and history-dependent.** Temperature-dependent viscosity, Arrhenius
   kinetics, plasticity and hysteresis all mean the transport coefficients depend on the answer —
   which is where coupled, implicit solvers become unavoidable.

\newpage

# Reaction kinetics & chemical sinks


# Reaction kinetics & chemical sinks

> **One-line picture.** Put a local algebraic function on the right-hand side of the transport
> equation and everything can change: sharp fronts, multiple steady states, oscillations, and a
> stiffness problem for your solver.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [balance laws](../01-foundations/01-balance-laws-and-flux.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [autocatalysis](03-autocatalysis-and-oscillations.md),
> [reaction–diffusion & Turing](04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md),
> [fronts](05-fronts-and-travelling-waves.md)

## L1 · The rate laws, and what each one does

Reaction terms are **local** in space (no spatial derivatives) but usually **nonlinear** in
concentration. The order of the reaction is the exponent with which concentration enters:

| Rate law | Form | Characteristic behaviour |
|---|---|---|
| zero order | $R=k_0$ | constant production until a reactant runs out: linear growth |
| first order | $R=-k_1c$ | exponential decay, half-life $\ln2/k_1$ |
| second order | $R=-k_2c^2$ | decays as $1/t$; sharp fronts in space |
| bimolecular | $R=-kc_Ac_B$ | couples two species; quadratic in each |
| Michaelis–Menten / Hill | $R=-V_{\max}c/(K_m+c)$, $c^n/(K^n+c^n)$ | saturating (zero-order at high $c$, first-order at low $c$); Hill gives switch-like response |
| autocatalytic | $R=+kc(1-c)$ | growth that accelerates: fronts and travelling waves |
| Langmuir–Hinshelwood | $R\propto c/(1+Kc)^2$ | surface catalysis; can be non-monotone |
| scavenging (pseudo-first order) | $R=-k'c$ with $k'=k c_{\rm scav}$ | removal by an abundant partner: radical chemistry, quenching |

Temperature enters through **Arrhenius**: $k=Ae^{-E_a/(RT)}$. Doubling the rate roughly needs
$10$–$20$ K for typical $E_a$ — which is why combustion is nonlinear in the worst way, and why
biological rates are so temperature-sensitive.

## L2 · Why scavenging deserves its own name

A scavenger is a third species that removes your target by reaction — the OH radical removing a
pollutant, an antioxidant removing a radical, a chelator sequestering a metal. Two regimes matter:

* **Abundant scavenger** — the reaction is pseudo-first-order and you can forget the scavenger
  entirely; decay is exponential.
* **Depletable scavenger** — the removal rate slows as the scavenger is used up, producing
  non-exponential decay, and you must solve a coupled pair of PDEs
  ([cross-effects](../03-microscopic/06-cross-effects-and-multicomponent.md)).

Numerically, this is the classic first-order/second-order coupling problem: the same code structure,
two very different behaviours.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Comparison and positivity.** Monotone (decreasing) sinks preserve positivity and, with a
  maximum principle, let you bound solutions without solving them.
* **Multiple steady states.** Non-monotone kinetics or autocatalysis can produce several steady
  solutions — the classic $\mathrm{Da}$–$c$ hysteresis curve — with ignition and extinction points
  ([variational inequalities & hysteresis](../06-beyond/12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md)).
* **Competition between transport and reaction.** With $\mathrm{Da}\gg1$ the reaction is confined to
  thin sheets (flames, biofilms), producing boundary-layer structure; with $\mathrm{Da}\ll1$ the
  reaction is space-filling and transport-limited.
* **Stiffness.** Rate constants can exceed transport rates by many orders of magnitude (radical
  chemistry, combustion): eigenvalues of the reaction Jacobian span $10^{10}$, which forces
  implicit integration ([stiffness](../07-numerics/02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md)).

## L4 · Numerics

* **Treat sinks implicitly.** An explicit negative source can drive concentrations negative and blow
  up; implicit treatment is unconditionally positive for linear decay.
* **Operator splitting** separates transport and reaction, each with an appropriate method (Strang
  splitting for second order), at the cost of a commutator error that matters when both are fast
  ([splitting & coupling](../07-numerics/05-operator-splitting-and-multiphysics-coupling.md)).
* **Preserve positivity and invariant regions.** Adaptive time stepping driven by relative change
  (not absolute) is essential when concentrations span many decades; log-space formulations help.
* **Benchmarks**: the ROBER problem (classic stiff kinetics), the Oregonator, and Arrhenius
  ignition-delay correlations ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Learned kinetic mechanisms consistent with thermodynamics; multiscale coupling of
  quantum chemistry to reactor-scale transport; stochastic reaction networks at low copy numbers
  ([stochastic PDEs](../06-beyond/07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md)).

## Checks

1. Which rate law gives exponential decay, and which gives a $1/t$ tail? *(First order; second
   order.)*
2. Why is Arhenius temperature dependence dangerous numerically? *(Small temperature errors produce
   exponentially large rate errors, coupling the energy equation to the chemistry very strongly.)*
3. When can a scavenging reaction be treated as first order? *(When the scavenger concentration is
   approximately constant — abundant or continuously replenished.)*

## See also

[Autocatalysis & oscillations](03-autocatalysis-and-oscillations.md) ·
[Reaction–diffusion & Turing](04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md) ·
[Fronts & travelling waves](05-fronts-and-travelling-waves.md) ·
[Time integration & stiffness](../07-numerics/02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md)

\newpage

# Autocatalysis & oscillations


# Autocatalysis & oscillations

> **One-line picture.** A reaction that produces its own catalyst accelerates itself; couple two of
> those with opposite signs and you get a clock. Nothing oscillates but the chemistry.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [reaction kinetics](01-reaction-kinetics-and-sinks.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [reaction–diffusion & Turing](04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md),
> [fronts & travelling waves](05-fronts-and-travelling-waves.md)

## L1 · Why oscillations are surprising

Most of this atlas is about things running downhill: entropy up, free energy down, gradients
smoothed. Oscillating chemistry violates that picture — the concentration of a species rises and
falls forever, without any external clock. The resolution is that a Lyapunov function is *not
guaranteed*: nonlinear systems can possess stable limit cycles instead of fixed points, and then the
"arrow" is replaced by a closed orbit.

The ingredients are always the same: **positive feedback** (autocatalysis, or inhibition of an
inhibitor) plus **delayed negative feedback** (depletion of a reactant), and enough nonlinearity to
keep the oscillation bounded.

## L2 · The archetypes

| Model | Skeleton | Behaviour |
|---|---|---|
| logistic growth | $\dot c=kc(1-c/K)$ | autocatalytic saturation; sigmoid approach to $K$ |
| Lotka–Volterra | $\dot x=x(a-by),\ \dot y=y(-c+dx)$ | conservative cycles (not a limit cycle) |
| Brusselator | feedback between two species | canonical limit-cycle oscillator |
| Oregonator | Belousov–Zhabotinsky reduction | clock reaction, excitable waves |
| glycolysis (Sel'kov) | product activates its own production | biochemical oscillations |
| excitable media (FHN) | fast activator, slow inhibitor | threshold behaviour, pulses, spiral waves |
| calcium dynamics | channels with feedback | intracellular waves |

The practical difference between an *oscillator* and an *excitable* medium is whether small
perturbations die out (excitable: they must exceed a threshold) or grow into a full cycle
(oscillatory).

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Limit cycles are attractors.** For a stable limit cycle, trajectories converge to the orbit
  regardless of initial conditions: amplitude and frequency are properties of the dynamics, not of
  the data. (Contrast Lotka–Volterra, where the amplitude depends on the initial condition —
  structurally unstable.)
* **Hopfn bifurcations create them.** A fixed point loses stability as a parameter crosses a
  critical value and a small-amplitude cycle is born; the Poincaré–Andronov–Hopfn normal form gives
  the frequency at onset. This is the standard route in chemical and biological oscillators.
* **No Lyapunov function exists.** Consequently: no maximum principle, no monotone functional, no
  automatic contraction. Numerical schemes have no free nonlinear stability; check invariant regions
  explicitly.
* **Excitable media support waves and spirals.** A threshold-plus-refractory structure (as in
  cardiac tissue) gives travelling pulses, spiral waves and, if the medium is large enough,
  turbulence-like breakup — the mechanism of cardiac fibrillation and of chemical wave chaos.
* **Sensitivity to parameters is the norm.** Period and amplitude depend on rate constants
  exponentially (Arrhenius), so small temperature changes can switch a system from steady to
  oscillatory — which is exactly why biological clocks are temperature-compensated by design.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Long integration times are unavoidable.** Statistics of the cycle require many periods; error
  accumulates as *phase* drift rather than amplitude growth, so diagnostics must be
  phase-aware (Poincaré sections, period detection).
* **Stiffness and multiple time scales** (fast activator, slow inhibitor) demand implicit or
  IMEX schemes ([time integration](../07-numerics/02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md));
  explicit schemes are limited by the fastest scale and can spend all their effort there.
* **Positivity matters.** Oscillatory kinetics with negative excursions in a numerical scheme
  destroys the dynamics: use positivity-preserving or log-space integration.
* **Benchmarks**: Oregonator period/amplitude curves, FHN pulse speed, and spiral-wave tip
  trajectories ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Stochastic oscillations at low copy numbers; oscillatory dynamics coupled to flow
  (oscillating chemical reactors); control of fibrillation; single-cell clocks in tissues.

## Checks

1. Why is Lotka–Volterra *not* a genuine oscillator in the engineering sense? *(Its cycles depend on
   initial conditions: no attracting limit cycle, structurally unstable.)*
2. What is the difference between oscillatory and excitable? *(Excitable needs a suprathreshold
   perturbation to fire; oscillatory does not.)*
3. Which structural feature makes the analysis so much harder here than for diffusion?
   *(Absence of a Lyapunov function: no monotonicity, no maximum principle.)*

## See also

[Reaction kinetics](01-reaction-kinetics-and-sinks.md) ·
[Reaction–diffusion & Turing](04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md) ·
[Fronts & travelling waves](05-fronts-and-travelling-waves.md) ·
[Entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md)

# Reaction–diffusion & Turing patterns


# Reaction–diffusion & Turing patterns

> **One-line picture.** Diffusion normally erases structure. Add a fast inhibitor to a slow
> activator and diffusion *creates* it — with a wavelength set by the chemistry, not the domain.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [reaction kinetics](01-reaction-kinetics-and-sinks.md),
> [spectral view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md) · **Leads to.**
> [chemotaxis](06-chemotaxis-and-keller-segel.md),
> [free boundaries & phase field](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md)

![**Figure 17.** Left: the dispersion relation of a Turing instability. Only a band of wavenumbers has positive growth rate, so the pattern has an intrinsic wavelength. Right: a Gray–Scott simulation, in which spots organise themselves with no template and no boundary data.](turing-patterns.pdf)

## L1 · The counter-intuitive idea

In 1952 Turing asked how a uniform ball of cells develops spots, stripes and branches without a
blueprint. His answer: a chemical system that is *stable without diffusion* can be destabilised by
diffusion if one species diffuses much faster than the other.

The intuition is **local activation with long-range inhibition**: a spot of activator grows, produces
an inhibitor, the inhibitor spreads far and fast, and suppresses neighbouring spots, leaving a
periodic pattern whose spacing is set by the ratio of diffusion coefficients.

## L2 · The mathematics of the instability

Linearise about the uniform state, $\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}_0+\delta\mathbf{u}e^{\sigma t+i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}$;
the growth rate solves

$$\det\!\big[\sigma\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{J}+\mathbf{D}k^2\big]=0,$$

with $\mathbf{J}$ the reaction Jacobian and $\mathbf{D}=\mathrm{diag}(D_u,D_v)$. Turing's conditions:
$\mathrm{tr}\,\mathbf{J}<0$ and $\det\mathbf{J}>0$ (locally stable) yet $\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma>0$ for some
$k>0$ — impossible unless the diffusivities differ substantially. The result is a *band* of unstable
wavenumbers, hence a preferred wavelength.

| Pattern class | Mechanism | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Turing (stationary) | activator–inhibitor, unequal $D$ | spots/stripes on fish, digits |
| Turing–Hopf | oscillatory instability at $k\neq0$ | chemotactic banding, travelling patterns |
| spinodal / Cahn–Hilliard | free-energy descent, no reaction | phase separation, polymer blends |
| Gray–Scott families | activator with depletion | self-replicating spots, coral growth |
| Swift–Hohenberg | model near convection onset | Rayleigh–Bénard rolls |

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Wavelength selection, not uniqueness.** The most unstable $k$ sets the scale, but the domain size
  then determines how many spots fit, producing multistability (hexagons, stripes, mixed states).
* **Weakly nonlinear reduction.** Near onset, amplitude equations (Swift–Hohenberg type) describe
  pattern selection and stability using a handful of ODEs — this is how hexagon-versus-stripe
  competition is decided analytically.
* **Patterns may never settle.** Coarsening, spot splitting and defect motion can continue
  indefinitely; the asymptotic state is sometimes a sponge, not a crystal.
* **Mobility contrast is required.** Biologically this is achieved by making the inhibitor a small,
  fast-diffusing molecule and the activator membrane-bound — or by replacing diffusion with active
  transport ([chemotaxis](06-chemotaxis-and-keller-segel.md)).

## L4 · Numerics

* **Resolution.** The grid must resolve the intrinsic wavelength by roughly ten points, and the
  unstable band must lie inside the resolved wavenumber range; a coarse grid can *hide* a genuine
  instability.
* **Boundary conditions.** Neumann boundaries pin nodes at the edges; use periodic boundaries or
  large domains to see intrinsic patterns.
* **Stiffness.** Reaction rates often exceed diffusion rates by orders of magnitude: implicit or IMEX
  splitting is standard ([splitting & coupling](../07-numerics/05-operator-splitting-and-multiphysics-coupling.md)).
* **Benchmarks**: Gray–Scott pattern families, Brusselator wavelength predictions, Turing–Hopf
  travelling bands ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Turing patterns in tissue (pigment, digits, hair); stochastic patterns at low copy
  numbers; patterns on growing domains; learned networks reproducing observed morphology.

## Checks

1. Why does diffusion destabilise here when it stabilises single-species systems? *(The inhibitor
   diffuses far faster, so inhibition is non-local while activation stays local.)*
2. Which species is fast? *(The inhibitor.)*
3. Why is the wavelength independent of the domain? *(It is set by the instability band in $k$.)*

## See also

[Reaction kinetics](01-reaction-kinetics-and-sinks.md) ·
[Autocatalysis & oscillations](03-autocatalysis-and-oscillations.md) ·
[Chemotaxis & Keller–Segel](06-chemotaxis-and-keller-segel.md) ·
[Entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md)

# Fronts & travelling waves


# Fronts & travelling waves

> **One-line picture.** A reaction that turns "off" into "on" produces a self-sustaining front whose
> speed is dictated by the *leading edge*, where the chemical is barely present.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [reaction kinetics](01-reaction-kinetics-and-sinks.md),
> [autocatalysis](03-autocatalysis-and-oscillations.md) · **Leads to.**
  [phase change & Stefan](09-phase-change-and-stefan.md),
> [biology & medicine](../08-bridges/03-biology-and-medicine.md)

![**Figure 18.** A Fisher–KPP front at four times: the profile translates without changing shape, and the speed is set by the edge of the wave where the nonlinearity is still linear. Right: the Stefan free boundary, where latent heat slows the interface to $\sqrt{t}$.](fronts-and-stefan.pdf)

## L1 · Three applications, one object

A flame front, an epidemic wave, a spreading population, a nerve impulse, an invading tumour, a
curing resin — all are fronts where a state invades a rest state. They share three questions:
*Does a front exist? How fast does it move? Is it the only one?*

## L2 · The mathematics

For the Fisher–KPP equation, $\partial_tc=D\partial_x^2c+kc(1-c)$, look for travelling waves
$c(x,t)=f(x-ct)$. Substituting reduces the PDE to an ODE whose phase-plane analysis gives:

* fronts exist **only** for $c\ge c_{\min}=2\sqrt{Dk}$;
* the realised speed is exactly the minimum, $c=2\sqrt{Dk}$, selected by the leading edge where
  $c\ll1$ and the equation is linear;
* the profile decays exponentially ahead of the front, $c\sim e^{-cx/D}$.

| Front type | Selection rule | Speed | Examples |
|---|---|---|---|
| **pulled** (KPP) | the leading edge sets it | $c_{\min}=2\sqrt{Dk}$ | epidemics, invasive species |
| **pushed** | the bulk sets it | larger than $c_{\min}$ | some bistable systems |
| bistable | Maxwell/equal-area rule | unique, parameter-independent | phase boundaries, nerve pulses |
| excitable pulses | dispersion relation | a function of refractory time | cardiac, neural, chemical waves |

The distinction between pulled and pushed fronts is not academic: for pulled fronts the front speed
is sensitive to the *tail* of the initial condition and to any change in the reaction at low
concentrations, while for pushed fronts it is robust.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Speed selection is a theorem**, not a fit. The KPP result (Kolmogorov–Petrovskii–Piskunov 1937;
  Fisher 1937) is one of the cleanest in nonlinear PDEs.
* **Approach to the asymptotic speed is slow.** Bramson's correction: the front position converges
  to $2\sqrt{Dk}\,t$ only up to a logarithmic term, $\sim-\frac{3}{2c}\ln t$ — a famous trap when
  fitting front positions from experiments or simulations.
* **Sharp fronts require nonlinearity of a different kind.** Degenerate diffusion (e.g., the
  porous-medium equation) produces *compact-support* fronts with well-defined interfaces and finite
  propagation speed ([porous medium](../06-beyond/06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md)),
  which demands free-boundary or [level-set](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md)
  methods rather than fine grids over a tail.
* **Stability.** KPP fronts are stable to perturbations; non-minimal speeds are unstable. Bistable
  fronts can be unstable to curvature (leading to interface instability: Mullins–Sekerka,
  Saffman–Taylor).
* **Curvature and quenching.** In 2-D and 3-D, curved fronts move faster (or slower for concave
  fronts); the eikonal relation $v_n=c-\kappa D_{\rm eff}$ links front dynamics to
  [Hamilton–Jacobi equations](../06-beyond/02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md), and
  heat losses (quenching) can extinguish thin flames.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Resolving a tail that spans many orders of magnitude** is expensive. Adaptive meshes or
  transformation of variables (log-form) are often required to capture both the tail and the bulk.
* **Front position must be defined carefully** (a threshold, or a first moment), and reported with
  the Bramson correction in mind for pulled fronts.
* **Level-set and front-tracking methods** are superior when the front is genuinely a moving
  interface with its own internal structure; phase-field methods are robust for topology changes
  ([free boundaries](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md)).
* **Benchmarks**: KPP speed convergence (including the log correction), bistable front speed, and
  spiral-wave frequency in excitable media
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Fronts in heterogeneous and random media (speed depends on the environment);
  reactive fronts in porous media and fractures; conservation laws with source terms and the
  transition to detonations.

## Checks

1. Why is the KPP speed the *minimum* possible speed? *(Faster speeds are also solutions, but only
   the minimum is stable/selected.)*
2. What is the Bramson correction and why does it matter? *(A $-\frac{3}{2c}\ln t$ term in front
   position: naive fits to $ct$ give biased speeds.)*
3. When is front tracking better than a fine grid? *(When the interface has internal structure
   spanning scales far below the domain, or when topology changes must be handled.)*

## See also

[Autocatalysis & oscillations](03-autocatalysis-and-oscillations.md) ·
[Phase change & Stefan](09-phase-change-and-stefan.md) ·
[Hamilton–Jacobi & viscosity solutions](../06-beyond/02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md) ·
[Biology & medicine](../08-bridges/03-biology-and-medicine.md)

# Chemotaxis & Keller–Segel


# Chemotaxis & Keller–Segel

> **One-line picture.** Bacteria do not diffuse; they *run and tumble* up a gradient. The resulting
> flux is directed transport, and it can destabilise an initially uniform suspension into clumps.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md),
> [reaction–diffusion](04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md) · **Leads to.**
> [biology & medicine](../08-bridges/03-biology-and-medicine.md),
> [nonlinear diffusion](../06-beyond/06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md)

## L1 · Active transport instead of random walks

A swimming cell samples its environment and biases its motion toward higher concentrations of an
attractant. Macroscopically that is a flux $-\chi c\nabla S$ with a *chemotactic sensitivity* $\chi$
(m²/s per unit gradient), added to diffusion. The classical model (Keller–Segel, 1970; Patlak 1953)
reads

$$\partial_tc=\nabla\!\cdot\!\big(D\nabla c-\chi c\nabla S\big),\qquad
\underbrace{\partial_tS=D_S\nabla^2S+kc-\lambda S}_{\text{signal produced by the cells}} .$$

Cells both *sense* and *produce* the signal, which closes a positive feedback: more cells → more
attractant → more cells arrive. The competition between diffusion (spreading) and chemotaxis
(aggregation) is controlled by $\chi c_0/D$, the chemotactic Péclet number.

## L2 · Aggregation, blow-up, and what limits it

| Behaviour | Condition | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| uniform decay | weak chemotaxis, strong diffusion | no pattern |
| aggregation into a peak | above threshold | clumping (biofilms, slime moulds) |
| finite-time blow-up (2-D) | mass above $8\pi D/\chi$ | mathematical singularity: infinite density |
| pattern/band formation | signal dynamics + growth | travelling chemotactic bands |
| travelling pulses | excitable signal dynamics | bacterial waves (E. coli rings) |

In 3-D and in 2-D with sufficient diffusion, global solutions exist; in 2-D below the critical
diffusion, solutions can concentrate in finite time — a rare, concrete instance of a PDE blow-up that
is biologically meaningful, because real systems are saved by saturation, volume exclusion or
signal degradation (patched models).

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Free energy and critical mass.** Keller–Segel systems possess a Lyapunov functional (a
  chemotactic free energy); its structure gives the critical-mass threshold for blow-up and the
  "chemotactic collapse" analysis. Saturation terms or nonlinear diffusion remove the singularity.
* **Cross-diffusion structure.** The system is a cross-diffusion problem: the flux of one species
  depends on the gradient of another, and the matrix of diffusivities is *not* positive definite —
  hence standard maximum principles fail, and entropy methods become essential.
* **Patterns and bands.** Adding growth or signal kinetics produces travelling bands and
  periodic patterns; these are the mechanism behind bacterial ring patterns and macrophage
  recruitment in wounds.
* **Biological realism limits the model.** Cells have finite speed, finite sensing length and finite
  size; run-and-tumble models, kinetic equations and stochastic simulations replace the macroscopic
  flux when gradients are steep relative to the cell's motion
  ([kinetic theory](../05-kinetic/02-bgk-vlasov-and-plasma-kinetics.md)).

## L4 · Numerics

* **Positivity and mass conservation** are essential and hard: use finite-volume discretisations
  with upwind (Patlak) fluxes, which are positivity-preserving.
* **Blow-up needs handling**: adaptive refinement, regularisation (saturation or volume filling), or
  rescaling near the singularity; plain uniform grids cannot resolve collapse.
* **Stiffness from the signal equation** (fast production/degradation) calls for implicit treatment
  ([time integration](../07-numerics/02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md)).
* **Benchmarks**: the critical-mass blow-up computation, travelling chemotactic bands, and
  single-peak aggregation profiles
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Chemotaxis in tumours and immune response; coupling with fluid flow (swimming in
  mucus); active matter and phase separation driven by activity
  ([quantum & condensed matter](../08-bridges/10-quantum-and-condensed-matter.md)).

## Checks

1. Why can aggregation occur when the chemical signal is produced by the same cells that respond to
   it? *(Positive feedback: arrival begets attractant.)*
2. What stops blow-up in reality? *(Saturation of the chemotactic response, volume exclusion, signal
   degradation — mathematically, extra terms that restore coercivity.)*
3. Why do standard maximum principles fail? *(The diffusivity matrix is not positive definite:
   cross-diffusion is anti-diffusive.)*

## See also

[Diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md) ·
[Reaction–diffusion & Turing](04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md) ·
[Biology & medicine](../08-bridges/03-biology-and-medicine.md) ·
[Nonlinear diffusion](../06-beyond/06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md)

# Sorption, partitioning & retardation


# Sorption, partitioning & retardation

> **One-line picture.** Some of the solute spends its time stuck to the solid. It still travels, but
> only for the fraction of the time it is dissolved — so it arrives later, and its tail is longer.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [advection](../02-bulk/01-advection.md), [diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [porous media](../02-bulk/08-porous-media-and-darcy.md),
> [wells & breakthrough](12-wells-injection-efflux-and-breakthrough.md)

## L1 · Adsorption versus absorption

* **Adsorption** — attachment to a *surface* (activated carbon, clay, biofilm, catalyst).
  The metaphor is a sponge: molecules are sucked onto the interface.
* **Absorption** — uptake into the *bulk* of another phase (CO₂ into water, water into a polymer).
  The metaphor is dissolution.

Both reduce the dissolved concentration and hence **retard** transport. They differ in mechanism, in
saturation behaviour and usually in time scale — adsorption is often fast and reversible, absorption
is often slow and limited by interfacial resistance
([absorption & interfacial transfer](08-absorption-dissolution-and-interfacial-transfer.md)).

## L2 · Isotherms and the retardation factor

Write the mass balance for a two-phase system (dissolved concentration $c$, sorbed mass per solid
mass $F$):

$$\partial_t(\theta c+\rho_bF)+\nabla\cdot(\mathbf{u}c-\mathbf{D}\cdot\nabla c)=R(c).$$

For a **linear isotherm** $F=K_dc$ this collapses to the same advection–dispersion equation with a
retarded velocity:

$$R=1+\frac{\rho_bK_d}{\theta},\qquad v_{\rm eff}=\frac{v}{R},\qquad D_{\rm eff}=\frac{D}{R}.$$

| Isotherm | Form | Signature on the breakthrough curve |
|---|---|---|
| linear | $F=K_dc$ | shifted curve, no shape change |
| Langmuir | $F=F_{\max}K_Lc/(1+K_Lc)$ | sharpening front, tail at low $c$ |
| Freundlich | $F=K_Fc^{n}$, $n<1$ | self-sharpening at low $c$, spreading at high $c$ |
| BET | multilayer | high-concentration curvature |
| kinetic (two-site) | $\partial_tF=k_ac-k_dF$ | early breakthrough plus long tail (non-equilibrium) |

**Chromatography** is exactly this problem with a packed column and an injected pulse: retention time
*is* retardation, and peak asymmetry *is* kinetic sorption — which is why transport theory and
separation science share their equations.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Nonlinear isotherms change wave structure.** Convex isotherms (Langmuir) produce *shock-like*
  sharp fronts; concave ones (Freundlich) produce rarefaction-like spreading. The names come from
  [conservation-law theory](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md), and the front speed is
  given by a Rankine–Hugoniot relation with the sorbed mass included — the reason column
  experiments can be used to *measure* isotherms.
* **Retardation is not a delay.** It changes both velocity and dispersion ($v/R$, $D/R$), so the
  *shape* of a breakthrough curve, not just its position, distinguishes sorption from a
  velocity change.
* **Hysteresis is common.** Desorption often follows a different isotherm from adsorption (slow
  release, irreversible binding), so the answer depends on history —
  [variational inequalities & hysteresis](../06-beyond/12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md).
* **Kinetic sorption breaks the Fickian assumption.** Two-site models and mass-transfer-limited
  sorption generate long tails and early breakthrough simultaneously, and are the standard
  explanation of non-Fickian field plumes
  ([words that collide #1](../09-polysemy/01-dispersion.md)).

## L4 · Numerics

* **Couple the sorbed phase as an ODE per control volume** (implicit, for stiffness) rather than
  adding an artificial diffusion. Two-site models are stiff: the fast site requires implicit
  treatment.
* **Nonlinear isotherms** need Newton iteration inside each time step; ensure the isotherm's
  derivative stays positive to preserve monotonicity.
* **Hysteresis** requires state variables (loading history) — a naive steady isotherm cannot
  reproduce it.
* **Benchmarks**: linear-retardation step response, Langmuir shock speeds, and the two-site
  breakthrough with a known analytic solution
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Competitive multicomponent sorption for remediation; sorption in nanoporous media
  with confinement effects; reactive surfaces with coupled redox chemistry.

## Checks

1. Why does a linear isotherm shift but not distort a breakthrough curve? *(It is a constant scaling
   of velocity and dispersion: $R$ is concentration-independent.)*
2. Why does Langmuir sorption sharpen fronts? *(Convex isotherm: high concentrations travel faster
   through the isotherm than low ones, so the front steepens.)*
3. How does kinetic sorption produce both early breakthrough and a long tail? *(Fast sites saturate
   immediately, slow sites release slowly.)*

## See also

[Absorption & interfacial transfer](08-absorption-dissolution-and-interfacial-transfer.md) ·
[Conservation laws & shocks](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md) ·
[Wells & breakthrough](12-wells-injection-efflux-and-breakthrough.md) ·
[Chromatography in engineering devices](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md)

\newpage

# Absorption, dissolution & interfacial transfer


# Absorption, dissolution & interfacial transfer

> **One-line picture.** Two phases in contact exchange mass until the *chemical potentials* match.
> The rate is limited by how fast molecules can cross two thin films.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [phase change & Stefan](09-phase-change-and-stefan.md),
> [engineering devices](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md)

## L1 · What "mass transfer coefficient" hides

CO₂ dissolves in water; oxygen dissolves in blood; a tablet dissolves in the stomach. In each case
mass crosses a phase boundary, and the rate is written as

$$\dot m = k_L\,a\,(c^*-c),\qquad k_L=\frac{D}{\delta},$$

where $c^*$ is the concentration *that would be in equilibrium* with the gas (Henry's law,
$c^*=p/H$), $a$ is interfacial area per unit volume, and $\delta$ is the effective film thickness.
The coefficient $k_L$ is not fundamental: it is a *compressed* description of the boundary layer on
the liquid side (and often a second film on the gas side).

## L2 · Where $k_L$ comes from

| Model | Picture | Result | Use |
|---|---|---|---|
| two-film (Whitman) | stagnant films both sides | $1/K_{\rm ov}=1/k_G+1/(Hk_L)$ | design of absorbers |
| penetration (Higbie) | fresh elements exposed for time $t_e$ | $k_L=2\sqrt{D/(\pi t_e)}$ | short contact, bubbles |
| surface-renewal (Danckwerts) | elements replaced randomly | $k_L=\sqrt{Ds}$ | falling films, stirred tanks |
| boundary-layer | laminar film on a wall | $k_L\propto D^{2/3}$ | pipe flow, flat plates |
| Kolmogorov/turbulent | eddies sweep the interface | $k_L\propto(D/\nu)^{1/2}$ or $D^{2/3}$ | oceans, reactors |

Two robust scaling facts: $k_L$ grows with diffusivity ($k_L\propto D^{1/2}$ to $D^{2/3}$), and it
grows with stirring — which is why the same physical absorption can be limited by diffusion in a
still glass and by interfacial area in a sparged column.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Interface conditions are the crux.** The equilibrium relation at the interface (Henry,
  Raoult, partition coefficient) sets $c^*$; the fluxes must be equal and opposite. Using the wrong
  "continuity" condition (concentration instead of chemical potential) is the classic modelling error.
* **Reaction enhances absorption.** If the dissolved species reacts in the liquid film, the
  concentration gradient steepens: the enhancement factor $E$ ranges up to
  $E\approx\sqrt{Dk_2c_B}/k_L$ for fast second-order reactions (used in CO₂ scrubbing). The
  mathematics is *reactive* boundary layer theory.
* **Dissolution coupled to transport** produces shrinking particles (Noyes–Whitney kinetics plus
  diffusion), moving interfaces, and in extreme cases
  [free-boundary problems](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md).
* **Nonlinearity and hysteresis** appear when the equilibrium relation is nonlinear (solubility
  changes with composition, salting-out effects) or when history matters (glasses, hydrates).

## L4 · Numerics and measurement

* **Do not resolve the film.** Films are often micrometres; use a two-film boundary condition with a
  measured $k_L$ rather than a mesh down to nano-scale.
* **Mass balances must include the interfacial area** ($a$, m²/m³): forgetting it is the standard
  error in multiphase reactor models.
* **Multiphase solvers** (VOF, level set, phase field) resolve the interface movement and need
  consistent mass transfer and surface tension
  ([free boundaries](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md)).
* **Measurement**: wetted-wall columns, stirred cells, and the classic Danckwerts plot for
  $k_L$ extraction ([measurement](../10-appendix/05-measurement-and-experiments.md)).
* **Frontiers.** CO₂ capture with reactive solvents; oxygen transfer in bioreactors at high cell
  density; interfacial transport in microfluidic and nanoporous systems.

## Checks

1. Why is $k_L$ larger in a stirred vessel? *(Thinner films and surface renewal: $\delta$ smaller,
   renewal rate $s$ larger.)*
2. Why can reaction *increase* the absorbed flux beyond the physical limit? *(It consumes the
   dissolved species inside the film, steepening the gradient: the enhancement factor.)*
3. What is the correct interfacial condition? *(Equality of chemical potentials, i.e. the partition
   relation — not equality of concentrations.)*

## See also

[Sorption & retardation](07-sorption-and-retardation.md) ·
[Phase change & Stefan](09-phase-change-and-stefan.md) ·
[Heat–mass–momentum analogy](../08-bridges/01-analogy-and-circuit-models.md) ·
[Engineering devices](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md)
