# Phase change & the Stefan problem


# Phase change & the Stefan problem

> **One-line picture.** A phase boundary is a surface whose *position* is an unknown of the problem,
> and whose motion is paid for in latent heat.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [conduction](../03-microscopic/03-conduction.md) · **Leads to.**
> [free boundaries & phase field](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md)

![**Figure 19.** Temperature profiles on the solid side at four times, and the interface growing as $\sqrt{t}$ because latent heat must be conducted away before the boundary can advance.](fronts-and-stefan.pdf)

## L1 · The energy budget of a moving interface

Heat ice and it does not warm monotonically: it sits at $0^\circ$C while melting, because the energy
is spent breaking bonds. In continuum terms the interface advances only as fast as latent heat can be
conducted away:

$$\rho L\dot s=\llbracket k\partial_nT\rrbracket \quad\text{(Stefan condition)},$$

with $L$ the latent heat. This is a **free-boundary problem**: $s(t)$ is part of the solution.

## L2 · The classical solution and its scaling

For one-phase melting from a wall held at a fixed temperature there is a similarity solution,

$$s(t)=2\lambda\sqrt{\alpha t},\qquad\text{with }\lambda\text{ fixed by }
\mathrm{St}\,\lambda e^{\lambda^2}\mathrm{erf}(\lambda)=\text{const},\ \ \mathrm{St}=c_p\Delta T/L .$$

The $\sqrt{t}$ law is the same diffusion scaling as everywhere else, with a prefactor that latent heat
*reduces* — which is why melting ice is so stubbornly slow.

| Situation | Distinctive physics |
|---|---|
| one-phase melting | similarity solution, $s\propto\sqrt{t}$ |
| two-phase Stefan | heat flows in both phases; two jump conditions |
| alloy solidification | mushy zone: solid fraction varies across a temperature interval |
| evaporation / condensation | interfacial resistance plus vapour diffusion; boiling adds nucleation |
| curved interfaces | Gibbs–Thomson: $T_{\rm int}=T_m-\Gamma\kappa$ gives cells and dendrites |
| ablation | the receding surface is the design goal (heat shields, laser machining) |

**Gibbs–Thomson** is why snowflakes have structure: curvature depresses the local melting
temperature, so tips grow faster and side-branches melt back. That is the Mullins–Sekerka
instability — a free-boundary instability driven purely by diffusion.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Well-posedness via the enthalpy formulation.** Writing $\partial_tH=\nabla\cdot(k\nabla T)$ with
  $H(T)$ monotone gives weak solutions, existence and uniqueness; the interface is recovered
  afterwards.
* **Planar fronts are unstable at long wavelengths,** with a selected pattern wavelength set by the
  balance between diffusion and surface tension — the same logic as
  [Turing patterns](04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md).
* **History matters.** Fast cooling gives metastable phases, segregation and residual stress: the
  microstructure depends on the whole thermal path, not the final state
  ([accretion & surface growth](11-accretion-ablation-and-surface-growth.md)).
* **Interface kinetics can dominate** at nanoscales: attach rates, not heat flow, set the speed.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Three families of methods**: front tracking (accurate, fails on topology change), enthalpy/fixed
  domain (handles topology, smears the interface), level set / phase field (both, at the cost of a
  thin-interface model) — [free boundaries](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md).
* **Resolution.** One cell of interfacial smear yields $O(\Delta x)$ interface-temperature error;
  phase-field widths must be tied to the grid.
* **Iteration per step** for temperature-dependent properties and latent-heat release; Newton with a
  consistent Jacobian converges in a few iterations.
* **Benchmarks**: the Stefan similarity solution, the gallium melting benchmark, dendritic tip
  velocities ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Phase change in porous media with flow; additive-manufacturing melt pools; permafrost
  thaw and sea ice ([geoscience & climate](../08-bridges/04-geoscience-and-climate.md)).

## Checks

1. Why does the interface move as $\sqrt{t}$? *(Diffusion-controlled: heat must pass through a
   growing layer.)*
2. What does Gibbs–Thomson add to the Stefan condition? *(A curvature-dependent melting temperature,
   hence interface instability and microstructure.)*
3. What is the price of the enthalpy method? *(No tracking, but a smeared interface and reduced
   interface accuracy.)*

## See also

[Conduction](../03-microscopic/03-conduction.md) ·
[Free boundaries, level sets & phase field](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md) ·
[Fronts & travelling waves](05-fronts-and-travelling-waves.md) ·
[Engineering devices](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md)

\newpage

# Sedimentation, deposition & filtration


# Sedimentation, deposition & filtration

> **One-line picture.** Suspended particles have their own drift velocity relative to the fluid, and
> they accumulate wherever the flow deposits them. Two sentences, and a whole industry.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md), [advection](../02-bulk/01-advection.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [wells & breakthrough](12-wells-injection-efflux-and-breakthrough.md),
> [engineering devices](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md)

## L1 · Settling: a drift term, not a diffusion

For a small sphere in a viscous fluid, gravity balances drag:

$$v_s=\frac{(\rho_p-\rho)g\,d^2}{18\mu}\quad\text{(Stokes)},\qquad
\mathbf{v}_s=v_s\,\hat{\mathbf{g}} .$$

Sedimentation is therefore *advection with an extra velocity*: the transport equation gains a
gravitational flux $\mathbf{J}_{\rm sed}=c\mathbf{v}_s$, linear in $c$ and always in the same
direction. Nothing exotic — until concentration rises, when particles hinder each other
($v_s\to v_s(1-\phi)^n$, Richardson–Zaki), and when turbulence competes with settling to set a
vertical profile (the Rouse profile: $c(z)\propto(z/h)^{-{\rm Ro}}$).

## L2 · Deposition, resuspension and filtration

Deposition is a *boundary* phenomenon: the rate at which particles stick to a wall is written as a
deposition velocity $k_d$ (m/s) and enters as a Robin condition

$$-D\partial_nc=k_dc \quad\text{at the wall}.$$

| Mechanism | Dominant particles | Deposition velocity |
|---|---|---|
| Brownian diffusion | $<0.1\ \mu$m | increases as size decreases |
| interception / impaction | $1-10\ \mu$m | peaks at a few microns |
| sedimentation | $>10\ \mu$m | increases with $d^2$ |
| electrostatic attraction/repulsion | all charged | depends on surface charge, ionic strength |
| resuspension | any deposited | threshold shear stress |

The famous **minimum in deposition efficiency at ~0.3 µm** is *why* this size is the hardest for
respiratory protection, and why fine-particle air pollution penetrates deep into the lungs.

**Filtration** adds a growing, moving boundary: the filter cake accumulates and its hydraulic
resistance increases (Darcy flow through a compressible porous medium), which is why constant-pressure
filtration shows declining flux ($t/V$ linear vs $V$), and why cross-flow filtration exists at all.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Settling is a first-order term, so it needs upwinding.** Numerically, sedimentation behaves like
  advection and inherits all its pathologies (oscillations, numerical diffusion) and its fixes
  ([limiters](../07-numerics/04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md)).
* **Polydispersity creates continuous spectra of velocities.** The transport equation becomes a
  population balance (integro-differential in size) with size-dependent transport — the natural home
  of [nonlocal operators](../06-beyond/03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md) and of
  moment methods.
* **Hindered settling and flux-limiting.** In a batch column, the well-known Kynch theory derives
  from a *conservation law* with a non-convex flux $c\,v_s(c)$: the result is shocks and rarefactions
  that predict the appearance of a clear interface and of a rising sludge blanket
  ([conservation laws & shocks](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md)).
* **Deposition competes with diffusion and reaction** at surfaces, producing the standard
  resistances-in-series structure: mass transfer to the wall, surface reaction, and (for filtration)
  the cake resistance.

## L4 · Numerics and measurement

* **Do not model deposition as a negative source in the bulk.** Deposition is a boundary flux; smearing
  it into the volume destroys the near-wall profile, which is usually the whole point of the model.
* **Moving boundaries for cakes and sludge**. A growing deposit changes the domain; use moving meshes
  or [level sets](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md).
* **Benchmarks**: Stokes settling of a monodisperse suspension (analytic Kynch solution),
  constant-pressure filtration ($t/V$ plots), and the Rouse profile in a channel
  ([measurement](../10-appendix/05-measurement-and-experiments.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Microplastic transport in oceans; deposition in the lung (dosimetry); electrokinetic
  deposition; cake compressibility in membrane bioreactors.

## Checks

1. Why is deposition a boundary condition rather than a source term? *(Particles must reach the wall
   before they can stick; the physics lives in the near-wall flux.)*
2. Why does deposition efficiency have a minimum near 0.3 µm? *(Diffusion dominates below, impaction
   above; neither is efficient in between.)*
3. Why does a sludge-blanket interface appear spontaneously? *(Non-convex flux in a conservation law:
   shocks form without any external trigger.)*

## See also

[Advection](../02-bulk/01-advection.md) ·
[Conservation laws & shocks](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md) ·
[Wells & breakthrough](12-wells-injection-efflux-and-breakthrough.md) ·
[Measurement & experiments](../10-appendix/05-measurement-and-experiments.md)

\newpage

# Accretion, ablation & surface growth


# Accretion, ablation & surface growth

> **One-line picture.** Sometimes the interesting boundary is not where the material is, but where it
> *stops being*. Accretion adds mass to a surface; ablation removes it; both make the domain an
> unknown.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [phase change & Stefan](09-phase-change-and-stefan.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [free boundaries & phase field](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md),
> [engineering devices](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md)

## L1 · Growth versus removal

Two identical mathematical situations with opposite signs:

* **Accretion** — mass arrives at a surface and becomes part of the solid: crystal growth,
  electrodeposition, additive manufacturing, biofilm growth, snow accumulation, arterial plaque.
* **Ablation** — mass leaves: sublimation of a heat shield, laser machining, corrosion, dissolution,
  wear, glacier melt.

Both are described by a surface velocity along the normal,

$$\dot{\mathbf{x}}=v_n\mathbf{n},\qquad v_n\ \text{from a flux balance or a rate law},$$

so the *geometry* is an unknown and the domain changes shape as the solution proceeds. The essential
difference from melting/solidification is that new material arrives with its own **stress-free
reference configuration**, which is why growth generically leaves residual stress behind.

## L2 · The mechanisms and their rate laws

| Process | Rate law | Transport-limited by |
|---|---|---|
| electrodeposition | Butler–Volmer + mass transfer | ion transport in the electrolyte ([Nernst–Planck](06-chemotaxis-and-keller-segel.md)) |
| chemical vapour deposition | surface reaction + gas diffusion | boundary-layer mass transfer |
| corrosion | Tafel kinetics + ohmic drop | oxygen supply, passivation films |
| additive manufacturing | melt-pool solidification | heat conduction, Marangoni flow |
| biofilm growth | nutrient uptake × yield | substrate diffusion into the film |
| wear / erosion | threshold impact energy | local impact rate |
| scale/fouling | supersaturation kinetics | heat/mass transfer to the wall |

Common structure: **a surface reaction coupled to transport through a boundary layer**. This is why
these problems look like [conduction](../03-microscopic/03-conduction.md) with a Robin boundary whose coefficient
evolves, and why the same dimensionless groups ($\mathrm{Sh}$, $\mathrm{Da}$) reappear.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **The moving-domain formulation is exact but awkward.** In the reference (material) frame the
  balance laws pick up the surface velocity; in the spatial frame the interface is a
  [free boundary](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md). Choose by asking
  whether the *solid* or the *space* is the natural coordinate.
* **Morphological instability is generic.** Diffusion-limited growth is unstable to protrusions
  (Mullins–Sekerka, dendritic growth, electrodeposition dendrites, corrosion pits): flat surfaces
  grow bumps, bumps grow faster, and shapes like frost, dendrites and pits result. Stability requires
  surface energy, kinetics or transport obstacles.
* **Residual stress is a memory effect.** Material deposited at different times has different thermal
  or chemical histories, so the final body contains an internal state that depends on the *whole*
  deposition path — a reminder that transport problems can be path-dependent without being
  dissipative ([memory & viscoelasticity](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md)).
* **Interface evolution is Hamilton–Jacobi-like.** Purely geometric front motion (uniform normal
  velocity plus curvature) reduces to an
  [eikonal/level-set equation](../06-beyond/02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md) whose
  solutions may develop corners — needing viscosity solutions or level-set discretisations.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Arbitrary Lagrangian–Eulerian (ALE)** meshes move with the surface; remeshing is needed when
  distortion becomes extreme. Quality metrics and conservative remapping interpolate the solution
  between meshes.
* **Level-set / phase-field methods** avoid remeshing and handle topology changes (droplet mergers,
  pore closure) but require careful reinitialisation and ghost-fluid treatments.
* **Immersed-boundary / cut-cell** methods keep a fixed grid and modify stencils near the interface —
  cheap, but notorious for small-cell stability problems.
* **Benchmarks**: one-dimensional Stefan-type growth, dendritic tip velocity, galvanostatic
  deposition shape evolution, and additive-manufacturing melt-pool dimensions
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Coupled electro-chemo-mechanics in solid-state batteries (dendrites, fracture);
  growth of living tissue with residual stress; machine-learned interface kinetics.

## Checks

1. Why does growth generically leave residual stress, unlike melting? *(New material arrives in its own
   stress-free state; the mismatch must be stored elastically.)*
2. Why are protrusions unstable in diffusion-limited growth? *(They reach fresh reactants first — the
   Mullins–Sekerka mechanism.)*
3. Why does ALE need remeshing? *(The mesh distorts with the boundary; element quality degrades until
   the solve fails.)*

## See also

[Phase change & Stefan](09-phase-change-and-stefan.md) ·
[Moving meshes & parallel decomposition](../07-numerics/08-mesh-adaptivity-moving-domains-and-parallel.md) ·
[Hamilton–Jacobi & viscosity solutions](../06-beyond/02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md) ·
[Engineering devices](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md)

# Wells, injection, efflux & breakthrough


# Wells, injection, efflux & breakthrough

> **One-line picture.** A well is a point source or sink. Its mathematics is a Green function, and
> the curve it produces downstream is the most informative measurement in subsurface transport.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [porous media & Darcy](../02-bulk/08-porous-media-and-darcy.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [subsidence & poroelasticity](13-subsidence-and-poroelasticity.md),
> [inverse problems](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)

## L1 · Point sources and point sinks

Injecting or extracting at rate $Q$ gives a source term $Q\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}_0)$. Because the
pressure equation is linear and elliptic, solutions superpose — the whole theory of wells is built
from one solution: the **Theis** solution for a pumping well in a confined aquifer,

$$s(r,t)=\frac{Q}{4\pi T}\,W\!\left(\frac{r^2S}{4Tt}\right),\qquad
W(u)=\int_u^\infty\frac{e^{-z}}{z}\,\mathrm{d}z .$$

The logarithm-like well function is the exponential-integral sibling of a diffusion Green function:
pressure transients obey a diffusion equation with hydraulic diffusivity $T/S$. Practical
consequences include the straight-line $s$ vs $\log t$ analysis of pumping tests and the
"radius of influence" (a fiction, but a useful one: it is where drawdown becomes immeasurable).

## L2 · The vocabulary, and what each term means physically

| Term | Meaning | Mathematics |
|---|---|---|
| **injection / influx** | forcing mass *in* | source $S(\mathbf{x},t)$ or inflow boundary |
| **extraction / effluent** | removing mass | negative source (sink) |
| **efflux / outflow** | mass leaving a boundary | boundary flux, or zero-gradient (Danckwerts) condition |
| **jetting** | directed high-velocity efflux | prescribed velocity profile at an orifice |
| **capture zone** | region that eventually reaches the well | particle-tracking streamlines; dividing surface |
| **breakthrough curve** | concentration history at a downstream probe | the fingerprint of transport processes |
| **tracer test** | inject a pulse, measure the response | inverse problem for $v$, $D$, $R$, $k$ |
| **recycled coupling** | injection and extraction interact | superposition, plus flow-field change if rates are large |

The **breakthrough curve** is the workhorse: its first moment gives the mean velocity, its second
moment the dispersion, its shift the retardation factor, its tail the kinetic sorption, its shape at
early time the short-circuiting through fractures. Fitting all of these simultaneously is
[an inverse problem](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md) — and it is usually
ill-posed, so regularisation and multiple observation points are essential.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Linearity and superposition** for pressure (Darcy flow) and for dilute solute transport. Drawdown
  from many wells is a sum of Theis solutions; this is why well-field design is combinatorial rather
  than computational.
* **Capture zones are exact objects.** In a steady divergence-free flow, the capture zone boundary is
  a streamline; for a uniform flow plus a well it is the well-known paraboloid (Strack's solution),
  and its width far upstream is exactly $Q/(Ub)$ — the pumping rate divided by the aquifer
  transmissivity per unit thickness.
* **Green-function reciprocity.** The response at A due to a source at B equals the response at B due
  to a source at A: a theorem with concrete consequences for monitoring network design
  ([spectral view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md)).
* **Nonlinear complications.** Density-driven flow (saltwater intrusion), unsaturated flow,
  non-Darcy near-well effects, and multiphase flow break superposition; then you need the full
  coupled system ([variational inequalities](../06-beyond/12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md)).

## L4 · Numerics and measurement

* **Point sources need special treatment**: a delta function cannot live on a grid. Distribute the
  source over a few cells (or use a well index / Peaceman correction) so that the pressure response
  matches the analytic solution for the same grid.
* **Injection/production wells are the stiffest part** of reservoir simulators: cross-flow between
  layers, wellbore storage, and changing rates all need implicit treatment.
* **Outflow boundaries** must be non-reflecting (Danckwerts) or the plume will bounce; and an inflow
  boundary needs a specified *state*, not just a flux.
* **Benchmarks**: Theis drawdown, Strack capture-zone geometry, and 1-D column breakthrough with
  retardation ([measurement](../10-appendix/05-measurement-and-experiments.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Coupling well operations with geomechanics (subsidence, induced seismicity);
  geothermal doublets; CO₂ injection with dissolution and trapping;
  [data assimilation](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md) of field
  breakthrough curves.

## Checks

1. Why can you superpose well solutions? *(Pressure obeys a linear elliptic equation; the constraint
   and boundary conditions are linear.)*
2. What sets the far-field width of a well's capture zone? *($Q/(Ub)$: the pumping rate divided by the
   ambient flow per unit thickness.)*
3. Why is a tracer breakthrough curve so informative? *(Its moments encode velocity, dispersion,
   retardation, kinetic sorption and fracture short-circuiting simultaneously.)*

## See also

[Porous media & Darcy](../02-bulk/08-porous-media-and-darcy.md) ·
[Subsidence & poroelasticity](13-subsidence-and-poroelasticity.md) ·
[Sorption & retardation](07-sorption-and-retardation.md) ·
[Inverse problems & data assimilation](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)

\newpage

# Subsidence & poroelasticity


# Subsidence & poroelasticity

> **One-line picture.** Fluids in pores carry part of the load. Take them out and the solid must carry
> more — so it compacts, and the surface above sinks, usually permanently.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [porous media & Darcy](../02-bulk/08-porous-media-and-darcy.md),
> [wells](12-wells-injection-efflux-and-breakthrough.md) · **Leads to.**
> [geoscience & climate](../08-bridges/04-geoscience-and-climate.md),
> [homogenisation](../06-beyond/08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md)

![**Figure 20.** Left: drawdown spreading outward from a pumped well (Theis). Right: the compaction bowl at the surface, driven by the increase in effective stress as pore pressure falls.](subsidence-poroelasticity.pdf)

## L1 · Effective stress in one line

Terzaghi's principle: the deformation of a saturated porous solid responds to the **effective stress**

$$\sigma'=\sigma-p .$$

Pump the pore pressure down and the effective stress rises, even though the total load did not
change. The matrix compresses, porosity and permeability drop, and the ground surface subsides —
by centimetres to metres in Mexico City, Venice, and the Central Valley of California. The coupling is
two-way: compaction changes $K$ and $\theta$, which changes the flow
([Darcy](../02-bulk/08-porous-media-and-darcy.md)), which changes the pressure.

## L2 · The equations (Biot) and the simpler limit

Biot's theory couples momentum balance of the solid skeleton with Darcy flow of the fluid:

$$\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol\sigma'-\nabla p=\mathbf{0},\qquad
\partial_t\!\left(\frac{p}{M}+\alpha\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}\right)=\nabla\cdot\frac{K}{\mu}\nabla p,$$

with $\alpha$ the Biot coefficient and $M$ the Biot modulus. The standard simplification
(one-dimensional consolidation, Terzaghi) yields ever-familiar mathematics:

$$\frac{\partial p}{\partial t}=c_v\frac{\partial^2p}{\partial z^2},\qquad
c_v\sim O\!\left(\frac{K({\rm bulk\ modulus})}{\mu}\right),$$

a diffusion equation again — this time for pressure, with a *rate* of subsidence set by how fast
pressure diffuses through a low-permeability layer (clay: years to decades).

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Two time scales.** An instantaneous, elastic (reversible) response plus a slow, draining
  (partly irreversible) response: this is the classic dual behaviour seen in field data, and the
  reason subsidence continues long after pumping stops.
* **Irreversibility is a constitutive property.** Elastic poroelasticity is reversible; real
  reservoirs compact plastically (grain rearrangement, pore collapse). Repressurising recovers only
  part of the deformation — the aquifer "remembers" that it was squeezed.
* **The Mandel–Cryer effect** demonstrates the coupling: pore pressure *rises* temporarily in the
  middle of a loaded sample even while it drains at the boundaries. Any purely decoupled model gets
  this wrong, which is why it is a standard verification test.
* **Stability of coupling.** Monolithic coupling with a proper preconditioner is needed;
  staggered schemes can become unstable when the medium is nearly incompressible
  ([coupling](../07-numerics/05-operator-splitting-and-multiphysics-coupling.md)).
* **Surface footprint.** The subsidence bowl is smoother and wider than the drawdown cone because
  elastic deformation integrates over the whole deforming volume — a Green-function statement
  ([spectral view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md)).

## L4 · Numerics and measurement

* **Coupling strategy**: fully implicit (robust, expensive) versus fixed-stress or undrained splits
  (accurate and stable for moderate coupling); the fixed-stress split is the industry standard.
* **Mesh and geometry**: compaction of thin, soft layers controls surface movement; resolving those
  layers (or using an equivalent lumped model) is essential — see
  [adaptive meshes](../07-numerics/08-mesh-adaptivity-moving-domains-and-parallel.md).
* **Measurement**: levelling surveys, GNSS, InSAR (satellite radar interferometry) for the surface;
  extensometers in wells for in-situ compaction; pressure transducers for drawdown.
* **Benchmarks**: the Mandel problem, Terzaghi consolidation curves, and the Barry–Mercer
  injection test ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Induced seismicity from pore-pressure changes; CO₂ storage with geomechanical
  feedback; geothermal production; coupling with a changing water table (sea-level rise and
  aquitard drainage).

## Checks

1. Why does the effective stress rise when you pump? *($\sigma'=\sigma-p$ with the total load
   unchanged.)*
2. Why does subsidence lag pumping by years? *(Pressure must diffuse through low-permeability
   layers: $c_v=L^2/t$.)*
3. What does the Mandel–Cryer effect demonstrate? *(That the coupling is genuinely two-way: pressure
   can rise in the interior while the sample drains.)*

## See also

[Wells, injection & breakthrough](12-wells-injection-efflux-and-breakthrough.md) ·
[Porous media & Darcy](../02-bulk/08-porous-media-and-darcy.md) ·
[Multiphysics coupling](../07-numerics/05-operator-splitting-and-multiphysics-coupling.md) ·
[Geoscience & climate](../08-bridges/04-geoscience-and-climate.md)

\newpage

# Viscous dissipation & heating


# Viscous dissipation & heating

> **One-line picture.** Stir honey and your arm gets tired and the honey warms up. Ordered kinetic
> energy has been degraded into random thermal motion, and there is no way back.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [incompressible flow & vorticity](../02-bulk/03-incompressible-flow-vorticity-and-pressure.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [turbulence](../02-bulk/05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md),
> [entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md)

## L1 · The one-way street

Dissipation is the *irreversible* conversion of mechanical energy to internal energy. It appears in
the momentum equation as friction and in the energy equation as a source:

$$\rho c_p\left(\partial_tT+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla T\right)=\nabla\cdot(k\nabla T)+\Phi,\qquad
\Phi=2\mu\,\mathbf{D}:\mathbf{D},\qquad \mathbf{D}=\tfrac12(\nabla\mathbf{u}+\nabla\mathbf{u}^{\top}).$$

The same quantity, integrated over the flow, is the *dissipation rate*
$\varepsilon=2\nu\langle\mathbf{D}:\mathbf{D}\rangle$, which sets the smallest scale of turbulence,
$\eta=(\nu^3/\varepsilon)^{1/4}$ ([turbulence](../02-bulk/05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md)) and
fixes the high-wavenumber end of the energy spectrum.

Related heating terms that are usually lumped with it: **Joule heating** $\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{E}$ in
current-carrying fluids and solids, **dielectric heating**, and **ohmic losses** in
[Nernst–Planck](../03-microscopic/07-charged-and-electrochemical-transport.md) systems.

## L2 · When does it matter?

| Situation | Criterion | Why dissipation matters |
|---|---|---|
| ordinary water flow | $\mathrm{Br}\ll1$ | neglect it; heating is immeasurable |
| high-speed gas | $\mathrm{Ma}\gg1$ | adiabatic wall temperature exceeds freestream: thermal protection design |
| polymer processing | $\mathrm{Br}\sim O(1)$ | dissipation can dominate the melt temperature and change the viscosity (a *coupled* nonlinearity) |
| lubrication films | $\mathrm{Br}\gtrsim1$ | friction-generated heat thins the lubricant and can cause failure |
| Earth's mantle | — | viscous heating competes with radiogenic heating in the energy budget |
| rheometry | $\mathrm{Na}\sim1$ | shear heating biases measured viscosity: the classic instrument artifact |

The **Brinkman number** $\mathrm{Br}=\mu U^2/(k\Delta T)$ compares viscous heating to conducted heat,
and the **Nahme number** $\mathrm{Na}$ compares it to conduction in a channel.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Positivity and irreversibility.** $\Phi\ge0$ always: dissipation can only heat, never cool. This
  is why the entropy production inequality in
  [constitutive theory](../01-foundations/03-constitutive-relations.md) is automatic for Newtonian
  fluids.
* **Energy budget closure.** In the mechanical energy balance,
  $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int\frac{\rho}{2}|\mathbf{u}|^2=-\int\Phi+\text{work}$: dissipation
  is the sink that makes the equation consistent, and the exact quantity exchanged for heat.
* **Thermodynamic consistency requires different temperatures for different ingredients** in
  non-Newtonian and reacting flows; using a single $T$ blindly produces violations of the second law
  in strong shear.
* **Coupled feedback.** When viscosity is temperature-dependent (Arrhenius, polymer melts),
  dissipation and temperature form a positive feedback loop that can thermal-runaway — a genuine
  instability of [rheology](../02-bulk/09-non-newtonian-rheology.md), not a numerical artifact.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Include $\Phi$ implicitly or it will overshoot.** In high-speed and polymer flows $\Phi$ is a
  strong nonlinear source; explicit treatment makes the temperature spike.
* **Compressible codes must include the pressure-work and dissipation terms** to get wall
  temperatures and shock heating right; isothermal and incompressible formulations simply cannot
  capture them.
* **Verification**: manufactured solutions with prescribed heat sources, Couette-flow analytic
  temperature profiles, and shock-tube benchmarks (which include viscous and conduction terms)
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Dissipation in micro- and nano-flows (rarefied effects reduce it); coupling with
  chemical reactions in combustion (ignition by viscous heating); entropy-stable discretisations that
  bound the *numerical* dissipation
  ([structure-preserving schemes](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)).

## Checks

1. Why can dissipation never cool a fluid? *($\Phi=2\mu\mathbf{D}:\mathbf{D}\ge0$: it is a sum of
   squares.)*
2. What sets the Kolmogorov scale? *(Balance of dissipation rate and viscosity:
   $\eta=(\nu^3/\varepsilon)^{1/4}$.)*
3. Why does shear heating bias rheometry? *(The sample heats itself; viscosity depends
   exponentially on temperature, so the measured value is not the isothermal one.)*

## See also

[Turbulence & eddy diffusivity](../02-bulk/05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md) ·
[Entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md) ·
[Non-Newtonian rheology](../02-bulk/09-non-newtonian-rheology.md) ·
[Structure-preserving discretisations](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)

# Kinetic & radiative transport


# Kinetic & radiative transport

The previous categories all assumed a continuum: densities and fields varying smoothly in space. This
category removes that assumption. When the mean free path is not small (rarefied gases, plasmas,
nanoscale heat), or when the transported quantity *is* a probability density, the state is a distribution
in phase space and the equation is kinetic.

| # | Note | The one thing it gives you |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | [The Boltzmann equation](01-boltzmann-equation.md) | distribution functions, collision invariants, the H-theorem, and Chapman–Enskog |
| 2 | [BGK, Vlasov & plasma kinetics](02-bgk-vlasov-and-plasma-kinetics.md) | relaxation models, Landau damping, why dissipation needs no collisions |
| 3 | [Fokker–Planck & Kramers escape](03-fokker-planck-and-kramers.md) | drift–diffusion in probability space; barrier crossing |
| 4 | [Radiative transfer & neutron transport](04-radiative-transfer.md) | directional transport with in-scattering: an integro-differential equation |
| 6 | [Lattice-Boltzmann & particle kinetics](06-lattice-boltzmann-and-particle-kinetics.md) | solving kinetics directly: LBM, DSMC and friends |

![**Figure D.** The Knudsen ladder: as the mean free path grows relative to the device, the hydrodynamic
closure is lost and only kinetic descriptions survive.](knudsen-regimes.pdf)

## The through-line

Every note here is obtained from a *distribution* by taking moments, and every one faces the same
**closure problem**: the equation for a moment depends on the next moment up
([Boltzmann](01-boltzmann-equation.md) → [BGK/Vlasov](02-bgk-vlasov-and-plasma-kinetics.md)). The
practical hierarchy is:

* **Resolve the distribution**: DSMC, lattice-Boltzmann, PIC, discrete velocity — accurate, expensive.
* **Truncate with a model**: BGK relaxation, Grad-13 moments, Braginskii anisotropic transport.
* **Take the hydrodynamic limit**: Navier–Stokes, Fourier and Fick with coefficients computed by
  Chapman–Enskog — valid only when $\mathrm{Kn}$ is small.

The same structure describes light and neutrons ([radiative transfer](04-radiative-transfer.md)) and
probability densities ([Fokker–Planck](03-fokker-planck-and-kramers.md)) — three applications that share
not a single physical concept beyond the mathematics itself.

> **Reading tip.** If you understand one note in this category thoroughly, make it
> [Fokker–Planck & Kramers](03-fokker-planck-and-kramers.md): it connects to diffusion, to stochastic
> processes, to reaction rates and to modern machine learning
> ([diffusion models](../08-bridges/07-machine-learning-and-data.md)).

\newpage

# The Boltzmann equation


# The Boltzmann equation

> **One-line picture.** Give up fields and track how many molecules are at each position with each
> velocity. Add collisions. Viscosity, conduction and diffusion fall out of the averages.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Knudsen & rarefied transport](../03-microscopic/05-knudsen-and-rarefied-transport.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [BGK & Vlasov](02-bgk-vlasov-and-plasma-kinetics.md),
> [lattice-Boltzmann](06-lattice-boltzmann-and-particle-kinetics.md)

## L1 · The object and the equation

The fundamental object is the distribution $f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v},t)$: density of molecules in
*phase space*. Its evolution balances free flight against collisions:

$$\partial_tf+\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf x}f+\frac{\mathbf F}{m}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf v}f=\mathcal C[f],$$

and the continuum fields are its moments:
$\rho=\int mf\,\mathrm d\mathbf v$, $\rho\mathbf u=\int m\mathbf v f\,\mathrm d\mathbf v$,
$\rho e=\int\frac12m|\mathbf v-\mathbf u|^2f\,\mathrm d\mathbf v$. You bother because the continuum
equations fail when $\mathrm{Kn}$ is not small, when geometry is smaller than the mean free path, or
when the *tail* of the velocity distribution drives the physics (evaporation, threshold chemistry,
plasma instabilities).

## L2 · Collisions, invariants, closure

Collisions conserve mass, momentum and energy — the **collision invariants** — which is exactly why
moment equations reproduce the continuum conservation laws. But moments generate a **hierarchy**
(density → velocity → pressure tensor → heat flux → …): the kinetic version of the closure problem
([Mori–Zwanzig](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md)). Two standard closures:

* **Chapman–Enskog** expands in $\mathrm{Kn}$; first order returns Navier–Stokes with *computable*
  coefficients ($\mu\propto\sqrt T$, $k$, $D$ in terms of collision integrals).
* **Grad / moment methods** assume a distribution shape (Hermite expansion about a Maxwellian) and
  truncate; higher moments add non-physical modes unless regularised.

**H-theorem (1872):** collisions force $H=\int f\ln f\,\mathrm d\mathbf v$ to decrease, equilibrium is
Maxwell–Boltzmann, and entropy production is $\ge0$ — the microscopic origin of the second law and of
the positivity of every transport coefficient.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Conservation is exact for any admissible collision operator**, which is why lattice-Boltzmann and
  moment methods are conservative by construction.
* **Equilibrium and stability.** The Maxwellian is the unique equilibrium; linearised collisions have
  negative-definite structure, so the kinetic equation is stable — and with it the dissipative
  continuum equations it generates.
* **Second order is dangerous.** Burnett/super-Burnett equations lose hyperbolicity and can violate
  the second law: kinetic theory is authoritative, its continuum truncations are not.
* **Phenomena with no continuum analogue**: velocity slip, temperature jump, the Knudsen flow-rate
  minimum, thermal transpiration, and rarefied-gas non-Newtonian behaviour in shocks.

## L4 · Numerics

* **DSMC** solves it directly by Monte Carlo flight and stochastic collisions — the reference method
  for $\mathrm{Kn}\gtrsim0.1$, with cost set by collisions not cells.
* **Discrete-velocity methods** (lattice-Boltzmann style) are efficient at low $\mathrm{Ma}$ with
  well-understood accuracy ([lattice-Boltzmann](06-lattice-boltzmann-and-particle-kinetics.md)).
* **Conservation and positivity must be enforced**: naive collision discretisations give negative
  populations and violate H.
* **Benchmarks**: shock structure, Couette flow across a wide $\mathrm{Kn}$ range, the Knudsen minimum
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Hypersonic rarefied aerodynamics, fusion edge plasmas, granular gases, quantum kinetic
  equations ([quantum & condensed matter](../08-bridges/10-quantum-and-condensed-matter.md)).

## Checks

1. Why do moment equations conserve mass and momentum regardless of closure? *(Collision invariants.)*
2. What does the H-theorem buy macroscopically? *(Entropy production $\ge0$ and positivity of transport
   coefficients.)*
3. Why are Burnett equations rarely used? *(Loss of hyperbolicity; possible second-law violation.)*

## See also

[BGK & Vlasov](02-bgk-vlasov-and-plasma-kinetics.md) ·
[Knudsen & rarefied transport](../03-microscopic/05-knudsen-and-rarefied-transport.md) ·
[Fokker–Planck & Kramers](03-fokker-planck-and-kramers.md) ·
[Lattice-Boltzmann](06-lattice-boltzmann-and-particle-kinetics.md)

# BGK, Vlasov & plasma kinetics


# BGK, Vlasov & plasma kinetics

> **One-line picture.** Replace the collision integral by "relax toward equilibrium" and the kinetic
> equation becomes usable; drop collisions entirely and long-range forces take over — that is plasma
> physics.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Boltzmann equation](01-boltzmann-equation.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [MHD & relativistic transport](../06-beyond/13-mhd-and-relativistic-transport.md),
> [plasma & astrophysics](../08-bridges/05-plasma-and-astrophysics.md)

## L1 · Two idealisations

* **BGK (Bhatnagar–Gross–Krook):** $\mathcal C[f]\approx-(f-f_{\rm eq})/\tau$. One parameter, exact
  conservation, correct H-theorem, and a *relaxation-time* interpretation of viscosity and diffusion
  ($\mu\approx p\tau$). Its simplicity is why it underlies lattice-Boltzmann and much of DSMC practice.
* **Vlasov:** collisions are negligible relative to the *mean field* (Coulomb forces in plasmas,
  gravity in galaxies), so $\mathcal C=0$ and the force term contains the self-consistent field
  $\mathbf F=-q\nabla\phi$, $\nabla^2\phi=-\rho_q/\varepsilon$ (Poisson). The result is a
  **collisionless** kinetic equation whose dynamics are surprisingly rich.

## L2 · What the two models predict

| Model | Key phenomenon | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| BGK | relaxation to local Maxwellian; slip/jump boundary layers | cheap kinetic solver for $\mathrm{Kn}\sim0.1{-}10$ |
| BGK | finite propagation of viscous/conduction response | origin of [Cattaneo-type hyperbolic heat](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md) |
| Vlasov–Poisson | **Landau damping**: waves decay with no dissipation | energy goes to the *distribution*, not to heat — experimentally confirmed |
| Vlasov–Poisson | kinetic instabilities (two-stream, beam-plasma) | drives turbulence and heating in fusion devices |
| Vlasov–Poisson | plasma sheaths, quasineutrality breakdown | the boundary layers of plasma transport |
| Vlasov + gravity | Jeans instability, collisionless relaxation (violent relaxation) | structure formation in astrophysics |

The essential message of plasma kinetics is that **dissipation does not require collisions**.
Landau damping is phase mixing: particles with velocities near the wave phase speed exchange energy
with the wave, and the information is transferred to ever-finer structure in velocity space —
reversible in principle, irreversible in practice. This is a transport phenomenon in the *kinetic*
sense: it moves energy in phase space rather than in physical space.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **BGK preserves exact conservation and an H-theorem**, so it inherits the positivity of transport
  coefficients — a rare case where a simplification is provably safe.
* **Landau damping is a theorem for analytic data (Villani–Mouhot 2010)** but is *not* damping for
  rough data: the electric field decays while the distribution develops fine structure. Decay is a
  statement about smoothness, not about energy loss.
* **Quasineutrality is a limit.** Debye-scale boundary layers (sheaths) persist even when
  $\lambda_D/L\to0$; neglecting them gives wrong wall fluxes — the plasma analogue of
  [electroneutrality and Debye layers](../03-microscopic/07-charged-and-electrochemical-transport.md).
* **The fluid limit needs care.** Chapman–Enskog for magnetised plasmas produces anisotropic
  transport (Braginskii): $\kappa_\parallel\gg\kappa_\perp$, with heat flowing along field lines — the
  reason a tokamak's transport is dominated by field-line geometry.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Particle-in-cell (PIC)** is the workhorse: sample the distribution with macroparticles, deposit
  charge to a grid, solve the field, push the particles. Noise scales as $1/\sqrt{N}$; energy
  conservation must be monitored.
* **Velocity-space discretisation** (finite volume/semi-Lagrangian on a $(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v})$ grid)
  avoids noise but suffers from filamentation, which must be controlled by conservative,
  positivity-preserving schemes.
* **Asymptotic-preserving schemes** are essential: they must behave sensibly as
  $\lambda_D/L\to0$ and as collision time $\to0$, without resolving either scale.
* **Benchmarks**: Landau damping rates, two-stream instability growth rates, the Braginskii transport
  coefficients, and sheath profiles
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Gyrokinetic turbulence for fusion; cosmic-ray transport in magnetised turbulence;
  reconnection and collisionless shocks.

## Checks

1. What does BGK replace, and what does it preserve? *(The collision integral; conservation laws, H
   and positivity.)*
2. How can a wave damp with no collisions? *(Phase mixing: energy moves to fine velocity-space
   structure.)*
3. Why is plasma thermal conduction anisotropic? *(Charged particles move freely along field lines but
   are confined across them.)*

## See also

[The Boltzmann equation](01-boltzmann-equation.md) ·
[Charged & electrochemical transport](../03-microscopic/07-charged-and-electrochemical-transport.md) ·
[MHD & relativistic transport](../06-beyond/13-mhd-and-relativistic-transport.md) ·
[Plasma & astrophysics](../08-bridges/05-plasma-and-astrophysics.md)

# Fokker–Planck & Kramers escape


# Fokker–Planck & Kramers escape

> **One-line picture.** If a particle diffuses and is also pushed, its *probability density* obeys a
> transport equation with drift and diffusion. Equilibrium is Boltzmann; escape over a barrier is
> exponential in the barrier height.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [random walks & Langevin](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [stochastic PDEs](../06-beyond/07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md),
> [optimal transport & gradient flows](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md)

![**Figure 21.** Left: probability density in a double well, leaking from one metastable state to the other. Right: Kramers/Arrhenius scaling — the escape rate falls exponentially with barrier height over temperature.](kramers-escape.pdf)

## L1 · From trajectories to densities

A Langevin particle in a potential $V$ obeys $\gamma\dot x=-V'(x)+\xi(t)$. Its probability density
obeys the **Fokker–Planck equation** (Kolmogorov forward equation)

$$\partial_tp=\partial_x\!\left(\frac{V'(x)}{\gamma}p\right)+D\,\partial_x^2p
=\partial_x\!\left[\frac{1}{\gamma}\left(V'p+k_BT\,\partial_xp\right)\right],$$

and the bracket makes the physics obvious: the drift and the diffusion are locked together at
equilibrium, and the steady state is $p_{\rm eq}\propto e^{-V/k_BT}$ — **detailed balance**, the
probabilistic statement of the [fluctuation–dissipation theorem](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md).

Note the shape: this is a conservation law with a flux (current) — the same structure as every other
transport equation in this atlas, with "density of substance" replaced by "density of probability".

## L2 · Kramers' escape problem

Put the particle in a metastable well of depth $\Delta V$ separated by a barrier. Naively it needs
energy $\Delta V$ to escape, and the Arrhenius/Kramers law says the rate is

$$k_{\rm esc}\sim A\,e^{-\Delta V/k_BT},$$

with a prefactor $A$ that itself depends on friction (the **Kramers turnover**: too little friction and
the particle cannot gain energy, too much and it cannot cross — escape is *slowest* at an
intermediate friction, a genuinely counter-intuitive result).

| Limit | Behaviour | Setting |
|---|---|---|
| zero temperature | no escape | classical freezing |
| moderate friction | Kramers turnover | reactions in solution |
| low friction | energy diffusion limited | gas-phase reactions |
| high friction | spatial diffusion limited | reactions in viscous media |
| quantum | tunnelling below the barrier | isotope effects, catalysis |

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **The generator/adjoint pairing.** Fokker–Planck is the forward equation while its adjoint
  (backward Kolmogorov) governs expectation values and first-passage times. Solving the backward
  equation gives mean-first-passage times directly — the cleanest route to Kramers' formula.
* **Entropy increases.** The relative entropy $\int p\ln(p/p_{\rm eq})$ decreases monotonically
  (H-theorem again), which is why Fokker–Planck is a
  [gradient flow in the Wasserstein metric](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md)
  — the JKO structure — with both exponential and polynomial relaxation depending on the potential.
* **Eigenvalue problem and spectral gap.** For convex $V$ the spectrum is discrete and the relaxation
  is exponential with rate set by the smallest nonzero eigenvalue; for double wells the gap is
  exponentially small, which is exactly why barrier crossing is rare and hard to sample.
* **Metastability and mean first passage.** The exponentially small gap implies an exponentially long
  time to explore all states: the practical definition of a metastable system, and the reason
  coarse-graining in time is unavoidable ([memory & linear response](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md)).

## L4 · Numerics

* **Direct solvers** for Fokker–Planck in low dimensions: spectral methods in space, implicit time
  stepping, conservative formulations. They beat particle methods when $d\le3$.
* **Monte Carlo** (Langevin/Brownian dynamics) for high dimensions: error $N^{-1/2}$ but no meshing,
  and it can get stuck — barrier crossing is rarely observed directly.
* **Rare-event methods**: importance sampling, umbrella sampling, forward flux sampling, transition
  path sampling; all exploit the fact that the *rate*, not the trajectory, is what you need.
* **Consistency checks**: Boltzmann equilibrium recovery, detailed balance in the discrete scheme, and
  zero-current conditions as $t\to\infty$
  ([verification](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Kramers theory with non-Markovian memory, non-equilibrium driving, and applications to
  protein folding, nucleation, and battery ageing
  ([biology & medicine](../08-bridges/03-biology-and-medicine.md)).

## Checks

1. Why is the equilibrium distribution Boltzmann? *(Drift and diffusion are locked by the FDT, so the
   zero-current condition forces the Gibbs form.)*
2. Why is escape slowest at intermediate friction? *(Energy and spatial diffusion must both succeed —
   the Kramers turnover.)*
3. Why is direct simulation bad at measuring escape rates? *(The waiting time is exponential in the
   barrier; you may never see an event.)*

## See also

[Random walks, Langevin & response](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md) ·
[Optimal transport & gradient flows](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md) ·
[Stochastic PDEs & random media](../06-beyond/07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md) ·
[The Boltzmann equation](01-boltzmann-equation.md)

# Radiative transfer & neutron transport


# Radiative transfer & neutron transport

> **One-line picture.** Radiation travels in straight lines until it is absorbed or scattered, so its
> transport equation is *directional* — and the scattering term makes it non-local in angle.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [conduction](../03-microscopic/03-conduction.md) · **Leads to.**
> [plasma & astrophysics](../08-bridges/05-plasma-and-astrophysics.md),
> [engineering devices](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md)

![**Figure 22.** Extinction along a ray (Beer–Lambert), the angular structure inside a scattering medium, and the two limiting regimes — ballistic when thin, diffusive when thick. The crossover is where numerical schemes suffer most.](radiative-transfer.pdf)

## L1 · The equation and its meaning

Intensity $I(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol\Omega,\nu,t)$ obeys the **radiative transfer equation** (RTE):

$$\frac1c\partial_tI+\boldsymbol\Omega\cdot\nabla I
=-\kappa_aI-\sigma_sI+j_\nu
+\sigma_s\!\int_{4\pi}I(\boldsymbol\Omega')\Phi\,d\Omega' ,$$

i.e. absorption, out-scattering, emission, in-scattering. The directional derivative is first order,
but the in-scattering integral makes the equation **integro-differential**: light arriving here may
have come from anywhere, including places whose radiation depends on here. Non-locality is the
defining feature, and it is why radiation cannot be handled by a bigger conductivity except in limits.

The **neutron transport equation** has the same structure with capture, scattering and a fission
multiplication term — the basis of reactor criticality, shielding and dosimetry.

## L2 · The two limits and the parameters that interpolate

| Regime | Criterion | Approximation |
|---|---|---|
| optically thin | $\tau=\int\kappa\,ds\ll1$ | Beer–Lambert $I=I_0e^{-\tau}$; rays decouple |
| optically thick | $\tau\gg1$ | **Rosseland diffusion** $\mathbf q=-\frac{4\pi}{3\kappa}\nabla B$: radiation becomes conduction |
| scattering-dominated | albedo $\omega_0\to1$ | diffusive even when thin: long photon paths |
| strongly forward-scattering | asymmetry $g\to1$ | delta-Eddington, similarity transformations |
| neutron, multiplying | $k_{\rm eff}=1$ | criticality: an eigenvalue problem |

| Method | Idea | Cost |
|---|---|---|
| discrete ordinates ($S_N$) | discretise directions, sweep | cheap; ray effects on coarse angular grids |
| Monte Carlo | sample photon/neutron histories | exact geometry, flexible; noisy ($N^{-1/2}$) |
| moment methods (P1, P3) | spherical harmonics expansion | fast, poor at boundaries and when thin |
| diffusion / flux-limited diffusion | asymptotic thick limit | standard in astrophysics and cores |
| ray tracing / characteristics | integrate along rays | excellent for beams and lasers |

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Interfaces jump, and the opaque limit is a surface problem**: emissivity/reflectivity conditions
  give the classical view-factor and radiosity networks of thermal engineering.
* **The diffusion limit is a theorem — and a warning.** A scheme accurate at $\tau\sim1$ need not
  converge to the diffusion limit as $\tau\to\infty$: **asymptotic-preserving** schemes are required,
  and their absence lets energy leak through thick shields.
* **Neutron criticality is a non-self-adjoint eigenvalue problem** for $k_{\rm eff}$; multiplication
  breaks symmetry, so specialised solvers are needed.
* **Kirchhoff's law** (emissivity $=$ absorptivity) is detailed balance — the radiative face of the
  [fluctuation–dissipation theorem](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md).

## L4 · Numerics

* **Angular discretisation is the hard part**: ray effects are the classic $S_N$ artifact; remedies
  include richer quadrature, rotated quadratures, and Monte Carlo hybrids.
* **Asymptotic preservation** is the design criterion: accurate in thin media *and* in the diffusion
  limit without resolving either.
* **Radiation hydrodynamics is stiff** (opacity ↔ temperature ↔ field): implicit coupled solves are
  standard ([splitting & coupling](../07-numerics/05-operator-splitting-and-multiphysics-coupling.md)).
* **Benchmarks**: the Marshak wave, plane-parallel albedo, and standard neutron benchmarks (Godiva,
  C5G7) ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Exoplanet and fusion-plasma radiation; multigroup opacity tables; hybrid
  Monte Carlo–deterministic solvers; radiative transfer in turbulence.

## Checks

1. What single feature distinguishes the RTE from every other equation here? *(Angular non-locality
   through in-scattering.)*
2. When may radiation be treated as conduction? *(Optically thick — the Rosseland limit.)*
3. Why do naive schemes fail in thick media? *(They do not preserve the diffusion limit.)*

## See also

[Conduction](../03-microscopic/03-conduction.md) ·
[The Boltzmann equation](01-boltzmann-equation.md) ·
[Plasma & astrophysics](../08-bridges/05-plasma-and-astrophysics.md) ·
[Absorption & interfacial transfer](../04-interaction/08-absorption-dissolution-and-interfacial-transfer.md)

# Lattice-Boltzmann & particle kinetics


# Lattice-Boltzmann & particle kinetics

> **One-line picture.** Instead of discretising the continuum equations, discretise the *kinetic*
> ones: let populations stream on a lattice and collide locally. Continuum behaviour emerges, and
> boundaries become trivial.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Boltzmann equation](01-boltzmann-equation.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [particle & Monte Carlo methods](../07-numerics/09-particle-and-monte-carlo-methods.md),
> [machine learning & operator learning](../07-numerics/11-machine-learning-and-operator-learning.md)

## L1 · The idea

The lattice-Boltzmann method (LBM) keeps a small set of velocity directions per grid node, with
populations $f_i$ (density of molecules moving in direction $i$). Each step is two lines:

$$\text{stream: } f_i(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{c}_i\Delta t)=f_i^*(\mathbf{x}),\qquad
\text{collide: } f_i^*=f_i-\frac{\Delta t}{\tau}\left(f_i-f_i^{\rm eq}\right),$$

with $f^{\rm eq}$ a discretised Maxwellian. Density and momentum are the moments
$\rho=\sum_if_i$, $\rho\mathbf{u}=\sum_i\mathbf{c}_if_i$ — so **conservation is exact by
construction**. In the limit of small $\Delta t$ and small $\mathrm{Ma}$, a Chapman–Enskog expansion
recovers the incompressible Navier–Stokes equations with viscosity $\nu=c_s^2(\tau-\Delta t/2)$.

Why it became popular: purely local operations (ideal for GPUs), simple handling of complex
geometry and porous media, natural multiphase and thermal extensions, and kinetic fidelity that lets
it reach $\mathrm{Kn}\sim0.1$.

## L2 · The particle/kinetic zoo

| Method | Discretises | Best for | Weakness |
|---|---|---|---|
| **LBM** | velocity space on a lattice | incompressible flow, porous media, multiphase | low $\mathrm{Ma}$; boundary and compressibility limits |
| **DSMC** | molecules directly | rarefied gas, $\mathrm{Kn}\gtrsim0.1$ | noise; cost scales with collisions |
| **SPH** | field values at particles | free-surface flow, astrophysics, impacts | consistency at boundaries; artificial viscosity |
| **PIC** | distribution via macroparticles | plasmas, kinetic transport | noise; energy conservation |
| **vortex / blob methods** | vorticity in Lagrangian elements | wakes, high-$\mathrm{Re}$ external flow | viscous and boundary treatment |
| **DPD** | coarse-grained particles with soft forces | polymer and mesoscale fluids | parameters fitted, not derived |
| **Brownian dynamics** | overdamped stochastic particles | colloids, biomolecules | no hydrodynamics unless added |

The unifying theme: **the transport operator is implemented as motion**, so there is no numerical
diffusion from differencing a convective term — the price is statistical noise and a different kind
of consistency error.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Consistency via Chapman–Enskog**: LBM reproduces Navier–Stokes at second order in $\Delta x$ for
  small $\mathrm{Ma}$, with a *known* error structure (compressibility error $O(\mathrm{Ma}^2)$).
* **Exact conservation and positivity** of populations under many collision models (BGK with care,
  MRT/entropic models always), plus an H-theorem for entropic variants — which is why stability at
  high $\mathrm{Re}$ is achievable with the right collision operator.
* **Boundary conditions are kinetic**, so slip, thermal transpiration and contact angle emerge from
  wall rules instead of being imposed — a genuine advantage for micro- and multiphase flows.
* **Noise is a physical statement.** Particle methods approximate a distribution, so fluctuations are
  real and scale as $N^{-1/2}$; this makes them natural for problems where fluctuations *matter*
  ([stochastic PDEs](../06-beyond/07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md)).

## L4 · Numerics

* **Resolution rules**: LBM needs $\Delta t$ tied to $\Delta x$ (diffusive scaling) and $\mathrm{Ma}$
  small; DSMC needs cells smaller than the mean free path and enough particles per cell.
* **Coupling hierarchies**: hybrid continuum–kinetic solvers switch method by local $\mathrm{Kn}$;
  getting the interface conservative and noise-free is the hard part.
* **Verification**: LBM against Poiseuille and lid-driven cavity; DSMC against shock structure and
  Couette; SPH against hydrostatic and dam-break tests
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Performance**: LBM is bandwidth-bound and GPU-friendly; particle methods need sorting and
  neighbour lists, so spatial partitioning dominates the cost
  ([mesh, adaptivity & parallel](../07-numerics/08-mesh-adaptivity-moving-domains-and-parallel.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Learned collision operators ([ML & operator learning](../07-numerics/11-machine-learning-and-operator-learning.md));
  GPU-native hybrid solvers; LBM for multiphase flows with phase change; DSMC for hypersonic flight.

## Checks

1. How does a purely local algorithm produce a viscous continuum? *(Chapman–Enskog: the relaxation time
   τ sets the viscosity.)*
2. What is the main cost of particle methods? *(Noise: error decays only as $N^{-1/2}$.)*
3. Why does LBM handle complex geometry so well? *(Boundary conditions are local rules on populations;
   no body-fitted mesh is required.)*

## See also

[The Boltzmann equation](01-boltzmann-equation.md) ·
[Knudsen & rarefied transport](../03-microscopic/05-knudsen-and-rarefied-transport.md) ·
[Particle & Monte Carlo methods](../07-numerics/09-particle-and-monte-carlo-methods.md) ·
[Structure-preserving discretisations](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)

# Beyond classical PDEs


# Beyond classical PDEs

The classical framework of this atlas assumes: a local, smooth-enough PDE, with a unique solution that
depends continuously on the data. Every one of those assumptions has a productive failure mode, and this
category collects them. These are the notes to read when a problem refuses to fit.

| # | Note | The assumption it breaks |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | [Conservation laws & shocks](01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md) | smooth solutions exist; uniqueness of weak solutions |
| 2 | [Hamilton–Jacobi & viscosity solutions](02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md) | differentiability of solutions |
| 3 | [Nonlocal & fractional operators](03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md) | locality |
| 4 | [Memory, viscoelasticity & Cattaneo](04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md) | instantaneous constitutive response |
| 6 | [Nonlinear diffusion & porous medium](06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md) | infinite propagation speed; non-degenerate diffusivity |
| 7 | [Stochastic PDEs & random media](07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md) | determinism; smooth coefficients |
| 8 | [Homogenisation & upscaling](08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md) | that one scale is enough |
| 9 | [Free boundaries, level sets & phase fields](09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md) | that the domain is known |
| 10 | [Optimal transport & gradient flows](10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md) | that the right geometry is Euclidean |
| 11 | [Microcontinuum & generalized continua](11-microcontinuum-and-generalized-continua.md) | that a material point has no size |
| 12 | [Variational inequalities & hysteresis](12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md) | that behaviour is a function of the current state |
| 13 | [MHD & relativistic transport](13-mhd-and-relativistic-transport.md) | that the medium is unmagnetised and slow |

![**Figure E.** Characteristics, shocks and rarefactions: the picture that replaces "solutions are smooth"
once nonlinearity enters the flux.](conservation-law-shocks.pdf)

## How to use this category

Each note follows the same pattern: *state the classical assumption → show how it fails → give the
replacement theory → say what changes numerically*. If your problem has a sharp front and your solver
rings, start at [limiters](../07-numerics/04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md) but understand
[shocks](01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md). If a coefficient depends on the history, read
[memory](04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md). If your meshes disagree, read
[homogenisation](08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md).

> **The unifying discovery of this category** is that most of these generalisations are *projections or
> limits* of one another: eliminating a fast variable gives memory
> ([Mori–Zwanzig](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md)); eliminating a small scale gives
> homogenisation; letting the horizon shrink recovers the local operator; and changing the metric turns
> diffusion into transport along geodesics
> ([one mathematics of transport](../08-bridges/11-unifying-mathematics-of-transport.md)).

## Three more ways to leave the classical framework

| # | Note | The assumption it breaks |
|---|---|---|
| 14 | [Integrable systems & solitons](14-integrable-systems-and-solitons.md) | that nonlinear dispersive waves must smear or break: Lax pairs, inverse scattering as a nonlinear Fourier transform, soliton resolution |
| 15 | [Variational principles & Noether](15-variational-principles-and-noether.md) | that dynamics needs equations rather than a functional: action, symmetries, constraints, symplectic transport, GENERIC |
| 16 | [Delay & retarded transport](16-delay-and-retarded-transport.md) | that the state is instantaneous: delay equations as transport along a characteristic, infinite spectra, delay-induced oscillations and patterns |

These three, plus [optimal transport & gradient flows](10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md), are what
the [universality part](../11-universality/index.md) leans on when it claims that the same structures govern
minds, markets, algorithms and pure mathematics.


\newpage

# Conservation laws & shocks


# Conservation laws & shocks

> **One-line picture.** When the speed of transport depends on how much is being transported, waves can
> catch each other up. When they do, the solution jumps — and the equation must be reinterpreted.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [advection](../02-bulk/01-advection.md),
> [PDE taxonomy](../01-foundations/04-pde-taxonomy-and-boundary-conditions.md) · **Leads to.**
> [Hamilton–Jacobi & viscosity solutions](02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md),
> [limiters, TVD & WENO](../07-numerics/04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md)

![**Figure 23.** Left: a nonlinear flux function; the chord slope is the shock speed. Middle: characteristics collide (shock) or fan out (rarefaction). Right: the weak solutions, with the entropy condition choosing the physical one.](conservation-law-shocks.pdf)

## L1 · Why a shock is not a pathology

For $\partial_tc+\partial_xf(c)=0$ with nonlinear $f$ (Burgers' $f=\frac12c^2$, traffic $f=c(1-c)$),
the solution is constant along characteristics $\dot x=f'(c)$. A *faster* characteristic behind a
*slower* one will overtake it, and two values want to exist at the same place. That is a shock — not an
error, but the traffic jam you have experienced.

Real examples: gas dynamics, traffic (Lighthill–Whitham–Richards), two-phase flow in porous media
(Buckley–Leverett, the basis of oil-recovery models), chromatography, sedimentation (Kynch), flood
waves, and reactive transport with nonlinear isotherms.

## L2 · Weak solutions and the two conditions

Beyond the breaking time the classical solution fails, so reinterpret the PDE as the *integral*
balance law, which permits jumps. A mass balance on a shrinking control volume gives the
**Rankine–Hugoniot** condition (the chord slope is the shock speed):

$$s=\frac{f(c_R)-f(c_L)}{c_R-c_L}.$$

But the integral form admits several weak solutions (including non-physical expansion shocks). The
physical one is chosen by an **entropy condition**: Lax's $f'(c_L)>s>f'(c_R)$, or a vanishing-viscosity
regularisation $c_t+f(c)_x=\varepsilon c_{xx}$ as $\varepsilon\to0$, or a convex entropy inequality
$\partial_t\eta+\partial_xq\le0$. **Microscopic viscosity — the effect we threw away — is what selects
the answer.**

| Structure | Condition | Shape |
|---|---|---|
| shock | characteristics converge | jump satisfying entropy condition |
| rarefaction | characteristics diverge | smooth fan |
| contact | equal speeds across | jump in a passive quantity |
| non-convex flux | chord / equal-area rule | mixes rarefactions and shocks |

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Uniqueness needs the entropy condition.** Without it weak solutions are non-unique: a
  well-posed-looking PDE is ill-posed until the selection principle is added.
* **Regularisations agree.** Vanishing viscosity, relaxation and kinetic limits all pick the same
  entropy solution under mild conditions — several unrelated derivations reach one answer.
* **Systems are richer.** For Euler, shallow water or MHD, genuinely nonlinear and linearly degenerate
  families behave differently, and non-classical (undercompressive) shocks appear in special regimes.
* **Long-time behaviour self-organises into $N$-waves**: for scalar laws the large-time solution is
  fixed by the area under the initial data, not its shape. Shocks are the attractors of nonlinear
  transport.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Flux (conservative) form is mandatory**; otherwise the telescoping integral balance is lost and the
  shock speed is wrong.
* **Monotone first-order schemes converge to the entropy solution**; high-order schemes need
  [TVD/ENO/WENO limiters](../07-numerics/04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md), because Godunov's theorem
  forbids linear monotone schemes above first order.
* **Riemann solvers** (Roe, HLLC, AUSM) work face-by-face; approximate solvers need entropy fixes to
  avoid expansion shocks.
* **Benchmarks**: Sod and Lax shock tubes, Buckley–Leverett, LWR with a bottleneck, dam break
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** UQ for hyperbolic systems; shocks in random media
  ([stochastic PDEs](07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md)); learned limiters; compositional flow.

## Checks

1. Shock speed for Burgers with $c_L=1$, $c_R=-1$? *( $s=\frac12(-1-1)/(-2)=1$: moves right.)*
2. Why is an entropy condition needed at all? *(Weak solutions are non-unique; it rules out expansion
   shocks.)*
3. Why does a non-conservative scheme get the shock speed wrong? *(It violates the discrete integral
   balance, hence Rankine–Hugoniot.)*

## See also

[Hamilton–Jacobi & viscosity solutions](02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md) ·
[Limiters, TVD & WENO](../07-numerics/04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md) ·
[Nonlinear diffusion & porous medium](06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md) ·
[Networks, society & traffic](../08-bridges/09-networks-society-and-traffic.md)

# Hamilton–Jacobi & viscosity solutions


# Hamilton–Jacobi & viscosity solutions

> **One-line picture.** Describe a moving front by its arrival time and you get a first-order
> nonlinear equation whose solutions develop corners. Those corners are physical, and the theory of
> *viscosity solutions* is what makes them legal.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [conservation laws & shocks](01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [free boundaries & phase field](09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md),
> [images & signals](../08-bridges/08-images-and-signals.md)

## L1 · Fronts, arrival times, and the eikonal equation

Track a front moving with speed $F$ along its normal (a flame, a wave, an etching front, a
crystallisation front). Its arrival time $T(\mathbf{x})$ obeys the **eikonal equation**

$$F|\nabla T|=1,\qquad T=0\ \text{on the initial front},$$

a first-order nonlinear PDE. For a seismic wave, $|\nabla T|=1/c(\mathbf{x})$ gives first-arrival
travel times; for a robot, the same equation gives the distance-to-goal function — the reason
Hamilton–Jacobi equations are the language of optimal control and path planning.

Solutions develop discontinuities in the gradient (corners) whenever characteristics cross — behind a
concave corner, or where fronts from different sources collide. Classical differentiability fails, and
so does naive differentiation.

## L2 · The general form, and the viscosity-solution idea

The general first-order equation is $H(\mathbf{x},\nabla u)=0$; evolution versions are
$\partial_tu+H(\mathbf{x},\nabla u)=0$. Two complementary constructions resolve the corners:

* **Vanishing viscosity:** solve $u_t+H(\nabla u)=\varepsilon\nabla^2u$ and let $\varepsilon\to0$. The
  limit is the physically correct (and unique) solution — the same mechanism that selects entropy
  solutions of conservation laws.
* **Lax–Oleinik / Hopf–Lax formulas:** the solution is given by an infimum (shortest path, cheapest
  control) over trajectories — a variational representation, which is why HJ = dynamic programming.

The definition of a **viscosity solution** is beautifully minimal: a continuous $u$ is a viscosity
subsolution if every smooth test function touching it from above satisfies $H(\mathbf{x},\nabla\phi)\le0$,
and the reverse for supersolutions. Corners become testable objects, and the comparison principle
(uniqueness) follows. Caution: "viscosity solution" is a *definition*, not a numerical scheme — adding
a small $\varepsilon$ in a code does not automatically give the right answer unless it is consistent
with the limit.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Existence and uniqueness via comparison.** Under coercivity and convexity in $\nabla u$, viscosity
  solutions exist, are unique, and are *Lipschitz* — the corners are controlled.
* **Crandall–Lions theory** connects HJ equations to monotone (max-plus) semigroups, giving exact
  nonlinear superposition: solutions are obtained by infimal convolution, the nonlinear analogue of the
  Green's function convolution in [the spectral view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md).
* **Level-set connection.** The level sets of a viscosity solution solve a *geometric* equation of
  motion: the interface moves with normal velocity $F$. This is why level-set methods for
  [moving boundaries](09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md) work at all, including
  topological changes.
* **Optimal control duality.** The same object is the value function of a deterministic control problem
  (via Pontryagin/HJB), which is why the equation appears in robotics, economics and
  [mean-field games](../08-bridges/02-finance-and-economics.md).

## L4 · Numerics

* **Monotone schemes are mandatory.** Godunov-type or upwind discretisations of HJ equations converge to
  the viscosity solution; higher-order accurate non-monotone schemes converge to something else. This
  is the direct analogue of Godunov's theorem for conservation laws.
* **Fast marching (first-order, Dijkstra-like) and fast sweeping** solve the eikonal equation in
  $O(N\log N)$; they are the workhorses of seismology, computer graphics and medical imaging.
* **Level-set methods** discretise the geometry in higher dimension and handle merging/splitting
  fronts that are hopeless in arrival-time form (where a single $T$ cannot represent two fronts).
* **Benchmarks**: the eikonal on analytic media (constant and linear velocity), the Zeldovich–Frank-
  Kamenetskii flame front, and shape-from-shading in image processing
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** HJ on graphs and networks ([networks & traffic](../08-bridges/09-networks-society-and-traffic.md));
  mean-field games; second-order HJ from diffusions (Wasserstein gradient flows); machine-learned
  value functions with monotonicity constraints.

## Checks

1. Why do corners appear in arrival-time solutions? *(Characteristics cross where fronts from different
   directions meet; the gradient jumps.)*
2. What selects the correct solution among many? *(Vanishing viscosity, equivalently comparison: the
   viscosity solution.)*
3. Why must discretisations be monotone? *(Non-monotone schemes converge to non-solutions — the HJ
   analogue of Godunov's theorem.)*

## See also

[Conservation laws & shocks](01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md) ·
[Free boundaries, level sets & phase field](09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md) ·
[Images & signals](../08-bridges/08-images-and-signals.md) ·
[Networks, society & traffic](../08-bridges/09-networks-society-and-traffic.md)
