# Nonlocal & fractional operators


# Nonlocal & fractional operators

> **One-line picture.** Replace the Laplacian — which only listens to infinitesimal neighbours — by an
> integral over a neighbourhood, and transport acquires long jumps, long memory and new boundary
> physics.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [anomalous diffusion](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [microcontinuum continua](11-microcontinuum-and-generalized-continua.md),
> [stochastic PDEs](07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md)

## L1 · Why nonlocality, and what it looks like

Local PDEs assume that what happens at a point depends only on derivatives *at* that point. For many
media this is false:

* **Space-fractional** operators replace $\nabla^2$ by $(-\Delta)^{\beta/2}$, an integral over jumps of
  length $r$ weighted like $r^{-d-\beta}$: infinite second moment, Lévy statistics.
* **Time-fractional** operators replace $\partial_t$ by Caputo derivatives $D_t^{\alpha}$: a power-law
  memory kernel, subdiffusion, non-exponential relaxation.
* **Nonlocal diffusion** $\partial_tc=\int K(\mathbf x,\mathbf y)[c(\mathbf y)-c(\mathbf x)]\,\mathrm d\mathbf y$
  is the general form; compactly supported kernels give finite-range coupling.
* **Peridynamics** applies the same idea to solids: force is a nonlocal functional over a horizon
  $\delta$, which removes the crack-tip singularity and lets cracks nucleate and branch naturally.

## L2 · The operators, side by side

| Operator | Definition | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| fractional Laplacian | $(-\Delta)^{\beta/2}u\propto\text{p.v.}\!\int\frac{u(x)-u(y)}{\|x-y\|^{d+\beta}}\mathrm dy$ | $\beta=2$ local; $\beta<2$ jumps |
| Caputo derivative | ${}^CD_t^{\alpha}u=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_0^t\frac{u'(s)}{(t-s)^{\alpha}}\mathrm ds$ | power-law memory, Mittag-Leffler decay |
| nonlocal diffusion | integral with kernel $K$ | tunable coupling range |
| peridynamic operator | $\int_{\mathcal H}\mathbf f(\mathbf u'-\mathbf u)\,\mathrm dV'$ | crack-friendly, finite horizon |
| tempered kernel | $e^{-\lambda r}r^{-d-\beta}$ | heavy tails with exponential truncation (physical) |

A master-form transport equation is then $\partial_t^{\alpha}c=D\nabla^{\beta}c+f$, with orders fitted
from measured MSD exponents ([anomalous diffusion](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md)).

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Boundary conditions change.** A fractional operator needs data *outside* the domain (a volume
  constraint); a local no-flux condition alone is meaningless. This is the biggest practical source of
  modelling error.
* **Spectra change.** Fractional eigenvalues scale as $k^{\beta}$ rather than $k^2$, so every
  relaxation-time and spectral-gap estimate in this atlas is modified.
* **Variational structure survives.** $(-\Delta)^{\beta/2}$ is the gradient of a convex functional, so
  energy methods, maximum principles and entropy estimates hold in modified form — which is why
  fractional problems are tractable, not merely exotic.
* **Limits are meaningful.** $\beta\to2$ and horizon $\delta\to0$ recover the classical equations, which
  is how nonlocal models are validated.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Dense matrices are fatal** ($O(N^2)$ memory): use Toeplitz structure, FFT for uniform constant
  coefficients, fast multipole for peridynamics, convolution quadrature for time-fractional operators.
* **Heterogeneous coefficients break FFT**; then hierarchical matrices or asymptotically compatible
  schemes are needed — the latter converge to the *local* limit as the horizon shrinks with the grid.
* **Monte Carlo sidesteps the matrices**: simulate the underlying jump process
  ([particle methods](../07-numerics/09-particle-and-monte-carlo-methods.md)).
* **Benchmarks**: fractional-Gaussian profiles, Barenblatt-type solutions, peridynamic crack benchmarks
  (Kalthoff–Winkler), Mittag-Leffler relaxation
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Tempered space–time fractional models; engineering-scale peridynamics; nonlocal
  phase-field and damage models; learning kernels from data.

## Checks

1. What changes about boundary conditions? *(Data over a neighbourhood outside the domain is required.)*
2. Why does the fractional spectrum go as $k^\beta$? *(Scale invariance of the kernel.)*
3. What does "asymptotically compatible" mean? *(Refining the grid while shrinking the horizon
   converges to the local equation.)*

## See also

[Anomalous diffusion](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md) ·
[Microcontinuum & generalized continua](11-microcontinuum-and-generalized-continua.md) ·
[Stochastic PDEs & random media](07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md) ·
[Particle & Monte Carlo methods](../07-numerics/09-particle-and-monte-carlo-methods.md)

\newpage

# Memory, viscoelasticity & Cattaneo


# Memory, viscoelasticity & Cattaneo

> **One-line picture.** Instantaneous constitutive laws are an approximation. Give the flux a
> *relaxation time* and it lags the force — which restores finite propagation speed, produces
> viscoelastic memory, and changes the type of the equation.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [linear response & memory](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md),
> [non-Newtonian rheology](../02-bulk/09-non-newtonian-rheology.md) · **Leads to.**
> [nonlocal operators](03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md),
> [free boundaries & phase field](09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md)

## L1 · The lag, and why it matters

Fourier's and Fick's laws assume the flux responds to the gradient *instantaneously*. Physically that
cannot be exactly true: it would imply infinite propagation speed. The minimal fix is a relaxation
time $\tau$ — the **Maxwell–Cattaneo** law

$$\tau\,\partial_t\mathbf J+\mathbf J=-D\nabla c .$$

Eliminating the flux between this and the balance law gives the **telegrapher's equation**

$$\tau\,\partial_t^2c+\partial_tc=D\nabla^2c,$$

which is *hyperbolic* for short times (waves at speed $c_0=\sqrt{D/\tau}$) and *parabolic* for long
times (ordinary diffusion). The same equation describes voltage on a lossy transmission line, random
walks with persistence, and phonon heat transport ("second sound" observed in solids at low
temperature).

For materials, the same idea becomes a **hereditary constitutive law**:
$\boldsymbol\tau(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{t}G(t-t')\dot{\boldsymbol\gamma}(t')\,\mathrm{d}t'$, with $G$ the
relaxation modulus. Viscoelasticity is transport with memory.

## L2 · Models and their signatures

| Model | Law | Signature |
|---|---|---|
| Maxwell | $\lambda\dot{\boldsymbol\tau}+\boldsymbol\tau=2\mu\mathbf D$ | stress relaxes exponentially; normal stresses |
| Kelvin–Voigt | $\boldsymbol\tau=2\mu\mathbf D+2G\boldsymbol\varepsilon$ | creep, no stress relaxation |
| Jeffreys / Oldroyd-B | solvent + polymer + relaxation | realistic polymer melts; die swell |
| generalised Maxwell (Prony) | sum of exponentials | any measured $G(t)$ to engineering accuracy |
| fractional Maxwell | $\lambda^{\alpha}D_t^{\alpha}\boldsymbol\tau+\boldsymbol\tau=\cdots$ | broad spectrum (power law) |
| Maxwell–Cattaneo | $\tau\partial_t\mathbf J+\mathbf J=-D\nabla c$ | finite-speed diffusion, damped waves |

Test vocabulary you will meet: **creep** (strain under constant stress), **stress relaxation**
(stress under constant strain), and **dynamic moduli** $G'(\omega)$ (storage) and $G''(\omega)$ (loss),
coupled by Kramers–Kronig because of causality.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Type change and its consequences.** The telegraph equation is hyperbolic with damping; the
  solution has a *sharp front* at $x=c_0t$ and smooths only behind it. As $\tau\to0$ (stiff relaxation
  limit) the solution converges to the diffusion solution — but *not uniformly* near $t=0$, which is
  exactly where the hyperbolic correction matters (ultrafast laser heating, ballistic phonons).
* **Well-posedness needs care.** Naive hyperbolic heat conduction can produce negative temperatures
  (it lacks a maximum principle); extended thermodynamics adds second-order terms to restore
  positivity — the classical example of a higher-order closure that is *worse*, not better.
* **Memory implies nonlocal equations in time.** Power-law kernels turn into fractional derivatives,
  giving Mittag-Leffler relaxation, ageing, and non-exponential creep. In the frequency domain, memory
  is just frequency-dependent moduli, and causality ties real and imaginary parts together.
* **Objectivity again.** For flowing materials, the stress must be advected with an objective
  derivative ([material derivative](../01-foundations/02-material-derivative.md)); the corotational
  terms are not decoration.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Convolution costs.** Hereditary integrals are $O(N^2)$ in time; use sum-of-exponentials
  (Prony) approximations to convert them into auxiliary ODEs — this makes memory *local in time* again
  and is the standard industrial trick.
* **Hyperbolic diffusion schemes** need upwinding and limiters (they are hyperbolic!) and stiff
  relaxation handling when $\tau$ is small; asymptotic-preserving schemes transition smoothly to the
  parabolic limit.
* **Consistency with equilibrium.** Any hyperbolic model must reproduce Fourier's law in the limit of
  slow variation; check by comparing against the classical solution on long time and length scales.
* **Benchmarks**: Cattaneo heat pulse (front speed and amplitude), stress relaxation ($G(t)$) fits,
  Prony series accuracy, and Maxwell-model oscillatory shear
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Ultrafast and nanoscale heat transport (phonon hydrodynamics, Guyer–Krumhansl);
  biological tissue poroviscoelasticity; memory kernels learned from rheological data.

## Checks

1. What does the relaxation time $\tau$ do to the equation's type? *(Makes it hyperbolic at short times;
   a wave speed $\sqrt{D/\tau}$ appears.)*
2. Why does the hyperbolic model converge to diffusion only non-uniformly? *(A boundary layer in time
   near $t=0$: the wave front.)*
3. How can a power-law memory kernel be simulated efficiently? *(Sum-of-exponentials approximation
   converts it into a few auxiliary differential equations.)*

## See also

[Linear response & memory](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md) ·
[Nonlocal & fractional operators](03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md) ·
[Non-Newtonian rheology](../02-bulk/09-non-newtonian-rheology.md) ·
[Nonlinear diffusion & porous medium](06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md)

# Nonlinear diffusion & the porous medium equation


# Nonlinear diffusion & the porous medium equation

> **One-line picture.** Make the diffusivity depend on the concentration and diffusion stops being
> infinitely fast: it acquires a sharp edge and behaves partly like a wave.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [free boundaries & phase field](09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md),
> [optimal transport & gradient flows](10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md)

## L1 · Three ways for diffusion to be nonlinear

$$\partial_tc=\nabla\cdot\big(D(c)\nabla c\big)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\partial_tc=\nabla\cdot\big(mc^{m-1}\nabla c\big)=\nabla^2(c^m).$$

* **Degenerate diffusion** — $D(c)\to0$ as $c\to0$: this is the **porous medium equation**
  ($\partial_tc=\Delta c^m$, $m>1$). Gas flowing in soil, groundwater in an unconfined aquifer, heat
  radiation in a plasma, population dispersal with crowding.
* **Fast diffusion** — $D(c)\to\infty$ as $c\to0$ ($m<1$): solutions can vanish in finite time;
  used in plasma physics and for thin films.
* **$p$-Laplacian / non-Newtonian filtration** — $D\propto|\nabla c|^{p-2}$: shear-thinning flow in
  porous media, image processing (total-variation denoising), glaciology.

The headline difference from linear diffusion: **finite speed of propagation**. A compactly supported
initial blob stays compactly supported, with a genuinely moving boundary — an interface you can
specify and track ([free boundaries](09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md)).

## L2 · The similarity solution and its consequences

The porous-medium equation has the Barenblatt–Pattle solution
$c(\mathbf x,t)=t^{-\alpha}\big[1-\big(r/t^{\beta}\big)^2\big]_+^{\,1/(m-1)}$ with
$\alpha=d/(d(m-1)+2)$ and $\beta=\alpha/d$. The consequences are all visible in that formula:

| Feature | Linear diffusion | Porous medium ($m>1$) |
|---|---|---|
| support | infinite for $t>0$ | compact, moving front |
| front speed | infinite | finite, $\propto t^{\beta}$ |
| central value | $\propto t^{-d/2}$ | $\propto t^{-\alpha}$ (slower decay for $m>1$) |
| long-time state | Gaussian | Barenblatt profile |
| comparison principle | yes | yes (with a modulus) |

Barenblatt profiles also appear as the long-time attractor for *any* compactly supported data — the
nonlinear analogue of the central limit theorem for these flows, and the reason they are the natural
test cases for nonlinear diffusion codes.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Well-posedness by parabolic regularisation.** The equation is degenerate, so solutions are only
  Hölder continuous; existence follows from "regularised" equations ($D\geq\varepsilon$) plus compactness
  arguments (Aronson, Caffarelli–Vázquez).
* **Speed of propagation is finite and computable.** The support expands like $t^{1/(d(m-1)+2)}$ —
  derived from the similarity solution and confirmed by the theory of the free boundary.
* **Entropy and gradient-flow structure.** PME is the Wasserstein gradient flow of the internal energy
  $\frac{1}{m-1}\int c^m$; displacement convexity gives contraction and uniqueness — the cleanest
  demonstration that the "right" geometry depends on the physics
  ([optimal transport](10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md)).
* **Nonlinearity can destroy uniqueness.** For certain $p$-Laplacian-type problems with measure data,
  non-uniqueness is genuine: the maximum principle and comparison arguments do not simply carry over.
* **Interfaces can be non-smooth.** Solutions of the PME have interfaces whose derivatives may blow up
  at the edge; a genuine contact-line analysis is required (relevant to wetting and to
  Hele-Shaw flow).

## L4 · Numerics

* **Track the front or resolve it.** Uniform grids waste effort on the empty region around the support;
  moving meshes, level sets or front-tracking give far better accuracy per grid point
  ([moving meshes](../07-numerics/08-mesh-adaptivity-moving-domains-and-parallel.md)).
* **Preserve positivity and the support.** Schemes that create small positive values outside the support
  destroy the finite-speed property; use positivity-preserving, entropy-stable discretisations.
* **Nonlinear iterations.** Newton may struggle at the front; use damped Newton, Picard, or
  JKO/backward-Euler minimisation, which is monotone
  ([nonlinear solvers](../07-numerics/07-nonlinear-solvers-and-continuation.md)).
* **Benchmarks**: Barenblatt profile convergence, the "waiting time" problem for a blob on a slope,
  and the Hele–Shaw analogue ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Nonlinear diffusion on manifolds and networks; degenerate diffusion with reactions
  (combustion, tumour growth); nonlocal PME; rigorous long-time asymptotics.

## Checks

1. Why does the porous-medium equation propagate at finite speed? *($D\to0$ at the edge, so the edge
   cannot move until material arrives — mathematically, degeneracy at $c=0$.)*
2. What is the long-time attractor for compact data? *(The Barenblatt–Pattle profile.)*
3. Which functional makes PME a gradient flow, and in which metric? *(Internal energy
   $\frac{1}{m-1}\int c^m$, in the Wasserstein $W_2$ metric.)*

## See also

[Diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md) ·
[Free boundaries, level sets & phase field](09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md) ·
[Optimal transport & gradient flows](10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md) ·
[Images & signals](../08-bridges/08-images-and-signals.md)

# Stochastic PDEs & random media


# Stochastic PDEs & random media

> **One-line picture.** Two kinds of randomness: the medium's coefficients may be random fields, or the
> dynamics may be noise-driven. Both change the answer in ways that averaging coefficients cannot
> capture.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [random walks & Langevin](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [homogenisation & upscaling](08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md),
> [inverse problems & data assimilation](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)

## L1 · Noise in the equation

* **Additive noise** — a fluctuating source (thermal, biological, observational).
* **Multiplicative noise** — the coefficient depends on the state, so the equation is not invariant
  under a change of variables and the **Itô and Stratonovich interpretations differ by a drift term**.
  State which you mean: the difference is physical, not philosophical.
* **Random coefficients (random media)** — $K(\mathbf x)$ or $D(\mathbf x)$ is a random field (e.g.
  log-normal hydraulic conductivity). The solution is random even with no time-dependent noise.

The central question for random media is what an effective coefficient *means*. It is defined by the
requirement that the **ensemble average** spreads diffusively, and it is generally **not** the average
of $K$: for log-normal fields the effective conductivity involves a geometric-type mean with variance
corrections, and the corrections grow with the medium's variability.

## L2 · Phenomena that exist only with randomness

Randomness does not merely add error bars; it creates **new scaling laws**.

* **KPZ surface growth**: $\partial_th=\nu\nabla^2h+\frac\lambda2|\nabla h|^2+\eta$ has universal
  exponents $1/3$ and $2/3$ — neither diffusion nor ballistic, and shared by flames, interfaces and
  bacterial colonies.
* **Stochastic advection–dispersion** with random $\mathbf u$ or $D$ gives non-Fickian plumes and
  heavy-tailed arrival times: the correct model is
  [anomalous transport](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md), not a mis-measured $D$.
* **Dean's equation** shows hydrodynamic fluctuations generate long-range spatial correlations in
  density — fluctuations you might otherwise dismiss as noise.
* **Fluctuation–dissipation** ties the noise amplitude to the dissipative coefficient: you cannot
  choose temperature and diffusivity independently
  ([Langevin](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md)).

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Rough noise needs renormalisation.** Space–time white noise cannot be multiplied pointwise;
  nonlinear SPDEs such as KPZ require regularity structures/paracontrolled calculus to be well-defined.
  A modern analysis frontier with real physical content.
* **Itô vs Stratonovich** differ by a correction term of the form $\frac12\partial_cD$. Derive the
  convention from the physical limit (often Stratonovich for limits of physical processes; Itô for
  discrete-time and financial models), then stay consistent throughout the code.
* **Stochastic homogenisation is a theorem.** For divergence-form operators with stationary ergodic
  coefficients an effective tensor exists, but computing it requires the random *corrector* field — the
  stochastic counterpart of the deterministic cell problem
  ([homogenisation](08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md)).
* **Uncertainty in the answer is not uncertainty in the medium.** Averaged output is dominated by rare
  high-conductivity paths (percolation) or by rare low-conductivity barriers, so first-order
  perturbation theory can be qualitatively wrong.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Monte Carlo over realisations** is the baseline; multi-level and quasi-Monte Carlo reduce the
  $N^{-1/2}$ cost ([particle methods](../07-numerics/09-particle-and-monte-carlo-methods.md)).
* **Declare strong or weak convergence**: trajectory-accurate (strong) needs Itô–Taylor/Milstein; statistics
  (weak) needs cheaper schemes.
* **Random-field generation matters**: Gaussian fields by FFT or Karhunen–Loève; if the correlation
  length is under-resolved, the effective coefficient you measure is wrong.
* **Benchmarks**: 1-D log-normal aquifer arrival times, KPZ exponent recovery, and Itô/Stratonovich
  consistency tests ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Uncertainty quantification for transport prediction; data assimilation in random
  media; score-based generative models as reverse-time SPDEs
  ([ML & data](../08-bridges/07-machine-learning-and-data.md)).

## Checks

1. Why is the effective conductivity not the arithmetic mean? *(You average fluxes, not coefficients:
   harmonic/geometric-type means appear, with variance corrections.)*
2. What is the practical consequence of the Itô/Stratonovich choice? *(A drift correction that changes
   the mean transport.)*
3. Why is white-in-time nonlinear noise hard to define? *(Pointwise products of rough distributions are
   undefined; renormalisation is needed.)*

## See also

[Random walks, Langevin & response](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md) ·
[Homogenisation & upscaling](08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md) ·
[Anomalous diffusion](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md) ·
[Inverse problems & data assimilation](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)

# Homogenisation & upscaling


# Homogenisation & upscaling

> **One-line picture.** If the microstructure is far finer than the question, replace it with one
> effective coefficient — computed from a *cell problem*, not from an average.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [porous media & Darcy](../02-bulk/08-porous-media-and-darcy.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [subsidence & poroelasticity](../04-interaction/13-subsidence-and-poroelasticity.md),
> [stochastic PDEs](07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md)

## L1 · The idea and the small parameter

Rock, laminates, fabrics, battery electrodes: structure at scale $\ell\ll L$. Upscaling finds a
device-scale equation whose solution is the *average* of the true solution. The expansion parameter is
$\epsilon=\ell/L$.

* **Two-scale asymptotics** postulates $c(\mathbf x,\mathbf y)$ with $\mathbf y=\mathbf x/\epsilon$. The
  leading orders force the solution to be $\mathbf y$-independent; the next order gives the **cell
  problem** on a periodic cell and, from its solvability, the effective coefficient.
* **Volume averaging** averages the pore-scale equations over a representative elementary volume and
  closes them by assuming deviations are proportional to the macroscopic gradient.

For diffusion this yields $D_{\rm eff}$ from
$\nabla_{\mathbf y}\!\cdot\!\big(D(\mathbf y)(\mathbf e_i+\nabla_{\mathbf y}\chi_i)\big)=0$ with
periodic boundary conditions and corrector fields $\chi_i$. The same machinery yields Darcy's
permeability tensor, effective elastic moduli, and effective reaction rates inside reactive zones.

## L2 · What gets upscaled

| Micro-structure | Effective law | How |
|---|---|---|
| straight pores | Fick with $D_{\rm eff}=D\theta/\tau$ | tortuosity $\tau$, porosity $\theta$ |
| layered media | vector/tensor diffusion | arithmetic along layers, harmonic across |
| periodic geometry | tensor coefficient | cell problem |
| random log-normal media | corrected coefficient | stochastic homogenisation |
| fractures + matrix | two-domain (mobile/immobile) | exchange coefficient, hence memory |

**Double porosity** is the instructive case: when a fast pathway (fractures) coexists with a slow one
(matrix), the upscaled model is not a single advection–dispersion equation but a two-domain system with
an exchange term — exactly the memory structure that
[Mori–Zwanzig](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md) predicts when a slow subscale is
eliminated.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Convergence needs separation and ergodicity**, not merely "small": periodic homogenisation is a
  theorem, and stochastic homogenisation needs stationarity; without these, no effective coefficient
  exists.
* **Effective coefficients are not averages.** Across barriers the harmonic-type mean governs; along
  channels the arithmetic-type mean does. Confusing the two is the classic upscaling error.
* **Eliminating a subscale creates structure**: memory kernels, exchange fluxes and (with randomness)
  noise — not just a coefficient.
* **Validity is asymptotic in the *question*.** Upscaled models answer large-scale questions; the
  preasymptotic regime (transition between scales) is precisely where models and field data disagree.
* **Below the RVE size or a percolation threshold, no effective property exists** — properties
  fluctuate with the sample, which is why laboratory cores and field values often "disagree".

## L4 · Numerics

* **Compute correctors, not averages**, and validate with analytical bounds (Wiener, Hashin–Shtrikman).
* **Asymptotically compatible schemes** are mandatory for nonlocal/peridynamic upscaling
  ([nonlocal operators](03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md)).
* **FE² (two-scale FE)** nests micro-solves inside macro elements: accurate, expensive, and an active
  target for surrogate and learned models
  ([ML & operator learning](../07-numerics/11-machine-learning-and-operator-learning.md)).
* **Benchmarks**: layered-media conductivity, periodic cell problems with analytic answers, and
  double-porosity breakthrough against a fine-scale reference
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Preasymptotic upscaling; upscaling with reactions and feedback that change the
  microstructure; upscaled geomechanics and multiphase flow.

## Checks

1. Why can an effective coefficient exceed the arithmetic mean of the constituents? *(Channel-like
   microstructure: fast paths dominate.)*
2. What does eliminating a slow subscale introduce? *(Memory/exchange terms, and noise in random media.)*
3. When does no effective property exist? *(Below the RVE size, or below a percolation threshold.)*

## See also

[Porous media & Darcy](../02-bulk/08-porous-media-and-darcy.md) ·
[Shear dispersion (Taylor–Aris)](../02-bulk/06-shear-dispersion-taylor-aris.md) ·
[Stochastic PDEs & random media](07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md) ·
[Linear response & memory](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md)

# Free boundaries, level sets & phase fields


# Free boundaries, level sets & phase fields

> **One-line picture.** When the location of a boundary is an *output* rather than an input, you must
> either track it, or embed it in a function that merges and splits without complaining.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [phase change & Stefan](../04-interaction/09-phase-change-and-stefan.md),
> [Hamilton–Jacobi & viscosity solutions](02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md) · **Leads to.**
> [accretion & surface growth](../04-interaction/11-accretion-ablation-and-surface-growth.md)

## L1 · The problem and the three philosophies

Free boundaries appear throughout this atlas: melting fronts, corrosion surfaces, Hele-Shaw fingers,
crack tips, tumour boundaries, dendrites, dewetting films, and the support edge of nonlinear diffusion.

| Approach | Interface is | Strengths | Weaknesses |
|---|---|---|---|
| front tracking | explicit markers moved with physics | sharpest accuracy, exact jumps | fails on topology change; remeshing |
| level set | zero contour of $\phi(\mathbf x,t)$ | merging/splitting free; natural for HJ motion | reinitialisation, volume loss, thin-interface care |
| phase field | diffuse, width $\varepsilon$ | variational, energy-based, fully coupled | width tied to grid; curvature errors at low resolution |

## L2 · The equations

* **Level set:** $\partial_t\phi+V|\nabla\phi|=0$ — a Hamilton–Jacobi equation; use upwind/ENO schemes
  and reinitialise by solving $|\nabla\phi|=1$ in a narrow band. Curvature flow $V=-\kappa$ is the
  paradigm case.
* **Allen–Cahn (non-conserved):** $\partial_t\phi=-M(-\kappa^2\nabla^2\phi+f'(\phi))$, an $L^2$ gradient
  flow of a double-well free energy.
* **Cahn–Hilliard (conserved):** $\partial_tc=\nabla\!\cdot\!(M\nabla\mu)$,
  $\mu=f'(c)-\kappa^2\nabla^2c$ — an $H^{-1}$ gradient flow, giving spinodal decomposition and
  coarsening.
* **Mullins–Sekerka:** the sharp-interface limit. Diffusion drives long-wavelength growth, surface
  energy stabilises short wavelengths, and the balance selects the pattern scale — the reason dendrite
  spacing is what it is.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Variational structure**: Allen–Cahn and Cahn–Hilliard are gradient flows, so free energy decreases
  monotonically — the property a numerical scheme should preserve
  ([entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md)).
* **Γ-convergence**: as $\varepsilon\to0$ the diffuse energy converges to a perimeter functional and
  minimisers converge to minimisers. Phase fields are therefore *models*, not just numerical tricks.
* **Maximum principles differ**: Allen–Cahn has one; Cahn–Hilliard (fourth order) does not, so discrete
  solutions can leave $[-1,1]$ unless positivity-preserving schemes are used.
* **Surface energy is what makes the problem well-posed**: with zero surface tension the Mullins–Sekerka
  instability is ill-posed at short wavelengths.
* **Topology is not preserved** by level sets and phase fields — a feature (coalescence, branching) and
  a hazard if you expected one connected interface.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Level sets**: high-order upwinding (WENO), narrow-band reinitialisation, sub-cell interface
  reconstruction for accurate fluxes and volume.
* **Phase fields**: resolve $\varepsilon$ with 4–8 cells; use convex splitting or energy-stable schemes,
  or coarsening statistics come out wrong.
* **Volume conservation** is the standard failure mode of level sets and naive phase fields.
* **Benchmarks**: Zalesak's disk, bubble merger and Rayleigh–Taylor, Cahn–Hilliard coarsening
  exponents, diffuse-interface Stefan
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Coupled flow–phase field–elasticity for fracture; peridynamic damage; learned interface
  kinetics; large-scale multiphase flow with phase change.

## Checks

1. Which method handles a merging droplet with no special code? *(Level set or phase field.)*
2. What sets the dendrite spacing? *(Balance of diffusive growth and surface-energy stabilisation.)*
3. Why tie the phase-field width to the grid? *(Otherwise the effective surface energy and mobility are
   resolution-dependent.)*

## See also

[Phase change & Stefan](../04-interaction/09-phase-change-and-stefan.md) ·
[Hamilton–Jacobi & viscosity solutions](02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md) ·
[Nonlinear diffusion & porous medium](06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md) ·
[Structure-preserving discretisations](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)

# Optimal transport & gradient flows


# Optimal transport & gradient flows

> **One-line picture.** Ask "what is the cheapest way to move one density into another?" and you get a
> *distance* between distributions. In that distance, diffusion is simply rolling downhill.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [one mathematics of transport](../08-bridges/11-unifying-mathematics-of-transport.md),
> [machine learning & data](../08-bridges/07-machine-learning-and-data.md)

![**Figure 24.** Two ways to change a density. Along the optimal (Brenier) map, mass moves coherently and the entropy is *convex* in the interpolation parameter — the essence of displacement convexity and of the JKO scheme for diffusion.](optimal-transport-vs-diffusion.pdf)

## L1 · The distance, and the map

The **Wasserstein distance** $W_2(\rho_0,\rho_1)$ is the square root of the minimal cost of moving mass
with cost $=$ distance². In 1-D the optimal map is explicit — sort the quantiles and match them
(monotone rearrangement). In higher dimensions it is the gradient of a convex function (Brenier).

Two facts make this much more than a curiosity:

* **Transport is a continuity equation**: any motion of mass obeys
  $\partial_t\rho+\nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf v)=0$, and $W_2$ measures the cheapest such velocity field.
  Benamou–Brenier turned this into a fluid-dynamical formulation of optimal transport.
* **Diffusion is the steepest descent of entropy in $W_2$.** Heat flow, Fokker–Planck, the porous-medium
  equation and aggregation–diffusion models are all $W_2$ gradient flows — unifying transport phenomena,
  nonlinear PDEs and optimisation.

## L2 · The JKO scheme

Time-discretise the flow by minimising

$$\rho^{n+1}=\arg\min_\rho\left[\frac{1}{2\tau}W_2^2(\rho,\rho^n)+F(\rho)\right],$$

the **Jordan–Kinderlehrer–Otto** scheme: one implicit step of *any* $W_2$ gradient flow. Variational, so
it inherits existence, uniqueness and stability — and it is a practical method ("minimise, don't march").

| Functional $F$ | Gradient flow | Physics |
|---|---|---|
| entropy $\int\rho\ln\rho$ | heat, Fokker–Planck | diffusion, sampling |
| internal energy $\frac{1}{m-1}\int\rho^m$ | porous medium | degenerate diffusion, fronts |
| interaction $+$ entropy | aggregation–diffusion | chemotaxis, swarming |
| potential $+$ entropy | Fokker–Planck with $V$ | barrier crossing |

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Displacement convexity.** If $F$ is convex along Wasserstein geodesics, the flow contracts in $W_2$
  and JKO converges; quantitative constants give explicit decay rates. The strongest general tool for
  nonlinear diffusion.
* **Contraction is stability.** Metric contraction is exactly what makes a scheme stable, which is why
  JKO-type methods are unconditionally stable and monotone in energy.
* **The metric encodes the physics:** $W_2\to$ diffusion, $L^2\to$ Allen–Cahn, $H^{-1}\to$ Cahn–Hilliard.
  There is no "correct" metric in the abstract, only the physics.
* **Connections**: Otto calculus (Riemannian structure on densities), Schrödinger bridges
  (entropy-regularised transport — the basis of modern generative sampling), and optimal transport in
  economics and machine learning.

## L4 · Numerics

* **1-D is easy**: monotone rearrangement gives exact geodesics — an excellent diagnostic and a building
  block in higher dimensions.
* **Implicit gradient-flow schemes** (backward Euler, convex splitting, JKO) are stable at large steps and
  energy-monotone; explicit schemes usually are not.
* **Entropic regularisation (Sinkhorn)** turns transport into soft matrix scaling — why OT is now standard
  in machine learning ([ML & data](../08-bridges/07-machine-learning-and-data.md)).
* **Benchmarks**: heat-flow decay versus JKO minimisation, Barenblatt profiles from JKO steps, Sinkhorn
  against exact 1-D transport ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Transport-based UQ; flow matching and score-based generative models; OT on graphs;
  non-convex and non-smooth gradient flows.

## Checks

1. Why is diffusion a "transport"? *($\partial_t\rho+\nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf v)=0$ with
   $\mathbf v=-\nabla\ln\rho$.)*
2. What is JKO in one sentence? *(Implicit minimisation of free energy plus a Wasserstein penalty.)*
3. Why does the metric change the equation? *(Each metric defines a different steepest descent.)*

## See also

[Entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md) ·
[Nonlinear diffusion & porous medium](06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md) ·
[Machine learning & data](../08-bridges/07-machine-learning-and-data.md) ·
[One mathematics of transport](../08-bridges/11-unifying-mathematics-of-transport.md)

\newpage

# Microcontinuum & generalized continua


# Microcontinuum & generalized continua

> **One-line picture.** Classical continua assume a point has no size. When the microstructure is
> comparable to the gradients, give the point internal degrees of freedom — or accept that your model
> has no length scale and cannot reproduce size effects.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [nonlocal & fractional operators](03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [variational inequalities & hysteresis](12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md),
> [structure-preserving discretisations](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)

## L1 · Why classical continua run out of information

A classical elastic solid has **no intrinsic length scale**: a 1 mm beam and a 1 m beam of the same
material behave identically up to scaling. Experiments disagree at small scales — thin wires are
relatively stiffer in torsion, waves become dispersive near the micro-wavelength, shear bands have a
finite width. Classical averaging erased the scale information. Three ways to put it back:

* **Internal degrees of freedom** — each point carries an independent rotation (micropolar/Cosserat) or
  micro-deformation, giving couple stresses and a length $\ell$.
* **Gradient enrichment** — the energy depends on strain *gradients*, introducing higher-order terms
  (strain-gradient elasticity, gradient plasticity).
* **Nonlocal averaging** — stress depends on the neighbourhood's strain (Eringen, peridynamics): the
  solid-mechanics version of [nonlocal diffusion](03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md).

## L2 · Observable signatures

| Effect | Classical | Generalized continuum |
|---|---|---|
| torsion of thin wires | size-independent | stiffer as the diameter shrinks |
| bending stiffness | fixed $EI$ | raised by the length $\ell$ |
| wave propagation | non-dispersive | dispersive near micro-wavelengths |
| shear band width | zero (ill-posed) | finite, set by $\ell$ |
| crack-tip stress | singular | regularised and finite |
| strain localisation | mesh-dependent | mesh-objective |

The last two rows are the practical payoff: a length scale converts an ill-posed localisation problem
into a well-posed one, which is why gradient and nonlocal models dominate damage, plasticity and
fracture modelling.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Higher order $\Rightarrow$ more boundary conditions.** Gradient and couple-stress models are fourth
  order, needing e.g. prescribed micro-rotation or normal strain gradient. Their boundary layers are
  *physical*.
* **The length scale is what restores ellipticity.** Without it the acoustic tensor loses positivity,
  localisation solutions become non-unique, and results depend on the mesh.
* **Variational origin.** Gradient models are derived from an energy functional, so they inherit
  hyperelastic consistency and can be discretised in a structure-preserving way.
* **Nonlocal elasticity is not "smoother elasticity"**: reciprocity and Saint-Venant principles change,
  and the nonlocal length must be calibrated from dispersion or size-effect data.

## L4 · Numerics

* **$C^1$ elements** (Hermite, Argyris) or **mixed formulations** with the gradient as an independent
  variable; the mixed route is usually more robust.
* **Mesh-objectivity tests** are the verification standard: refine and confirm that localisation width
  converges to the physical $\ell$ rather than to zero.
* **Coupling with transport** appears in microstructured poroelasticity, thermal gradients in
  composites, and damage–diffusion coupling in electrodes
  ([poroelasticity](../04-interaction/13-subsidence-and-poroelasticity.md)).
* **Benchmarks**: torsion/bending size effects, dispersion curves for microstructured beams, localisation
  width convergence ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Engineering-scale peridynamic fatigue; gradient models calibrated by molecular dynamics;
  learned micromorphic constitutive laws.

## Checks

1. Why can a classical continuum not predict size effects? *(It is scale-invariant — no length scale.)*
2. What does a length parameter do to localisation? *(Restores ellipticity: unique, mesh-objective
   solutions.)*
3. Why extra boundary conditions? *(Fourth-order operators need two conditions per boundary.)*

## See also

[Nonlocal & fractional operators](03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md) ·
[Subsidence & poroelasticity](../04-interaction/13-subsidence-and-poroelasticity.md) ·
[Structure-preserving discretisations](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md) ·
[Engineering devices](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md)

# Variational inequalities & hysteresis


# Variational inequalities & hysteresis

> **One-line picture.** Sometimes the physics is an inequality, not an equation: flow stops below a
> threshold, contact only pushes, and a path once taken is partly remembered.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md),
> [non-Newtonian rheology](../02-bulk/09-non-newtonian-rheology.md) · **Leads to.**
> [microcontinuum continua](11-microcontinuum-and-generalized-continua.md),
> [engineering devices](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md)

## L1 · The problem class

Many transport problems are *inequalities*: you minimise energy subject to a constraint, and the
constraint is active in some unknown region that must be found as part of the solution.

* **Obstacle problem**: a membrane pressed by a rigid body; the contact region is unknown.
* **Contact and friction**: Coulomb friction means the tangential stress is bounded — slip occurs only
  when the bound is reached (and stops when it is not). The set of sliding points is an output.
* **Bingham/yield-stress flow**: below $\tau_y$ the material does not flow (unyielded plug).
* **Coating, infiltration, saturation**: flow switches on/off as drying or wetting fronts pass.
* **Phase transitions with hysteresis**: a "switching" state that depends on the loading direction
  (magnetic, ferroelectric, shape-memory, adsorption hysteresis).

All are of the form: minimise energy, subject to an inequality constraint, with an unknown active set.
The mathematical machinery is **complementarity**: for each point, either the constraint is slack and
the equation holds, or the constraint is active and the inequality holds — never both violations.

## L2 · What the mathematics provides

| Structure | Continuous form | Discrete analogue |
|---|---|---|
| obstacle | $u\ge\psi$, $-\Delta u-f\ge0$, $(u-\psi)(-\Delta u-f)=0$ | linear complementarity problem |
| contact | normal gap $\ge0$, pressure $\ge0$, product $=0$ | active-set or semismooth Newton |
| Coulomb friction | $\|\mathbf t\|\le\mu p_n$, slip only if equality | return-mapping, proximal splitting |
| yield stress | $\|\boldsymbol\tau\|\le\tau_y$ or rate $=0$ | augmented Lagrangian |
| hysteresis | rate-independent memory operator | Preisach/play operators, sweeping processes |

Hysteresis deserves a separate sentence: a **rate-independent** memory means the response depends on the
*sequence and extrema* of the driving, not on the speed. Quantitatively this needs operators
(Preisach, Prandtl–Ishlinskii, play/hysterons) rather than differential equations in time — a genuinely
different mathematical structure from everything else in this atlas.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Existence via convexity.** Variational inequalities inherit existence and uniqueness from convex
  minimisation over a closed convex set. Non-convex versions (damage, plasticity with softening) may
  lose uniqueness — which is physical (localisation), not a defect of the theory.
* **Regularity is limited by the free set.** Solutions are typically only Lipschitz/Hölder across the
  boundary of the active set: the active-set boundary behaves like a free boundary
  ([free boundaries](09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md)).
* **Monotone operators and damping.** Many quasi-static problems with hysteresis are *dissipative*, and
  this gives stability in the sense of maximal monotone operators (sweeping processes).
* **Hysteresis breaks the state-function assumption.** Constitutive laws must carry internal variables
  whose evolution is constrained by inequalities, so the material state is not a function of the current
  strain (or concentration) alone.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Semismooth Newton** (with active-set detection) and **primal–dual active-set methods** are the
  standard solvers; they converge superlinearly when implemented carefully
  ([nonlinear solvers](../07-numerics/07-nonlinear-solvers-and-continuation.md)).
* **Regularisation** (bi-viscosity, penalty, smoothed friction) makes problems differentiable but biases
  the answer; report the regularisation parameter and check sensitivity.
* **Augmented Lagrangians / proximal splitting** (ADMM) handle contact and yield-stress flow robustly and
  keep the constraint exactly satisfied.
* **Benchmark cases**: the obstacle problem with a known obstacle, Bingham flow in a channel (plug
  width), and classical friction contact tests
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Coupled contact with transport (fractured reservoirs, drying/wetting hysteresis in
  soils); Preisach-type models calibrated from data; sweeping-process theory for rate-independent
  systems; non-smooth optimisation at scale.

## Checks

1. What does complementarity mean concretely for a contact problem? *(Either the surfaces are separated
   with zero pressure, or they touch with positive pressure — never negative gaps or pressures.)*
2. Why is hysteresis not a differential equation in time? *(It is rate-independent: the response depends
   on the loading *path*, not on its speed.)*
3. Why must yield-stress solvers find an active set? *(The unyielded region is unknown and must be
   determined as part of the solution.)*

## See also

[Non-Newtonian rheology](../02-bulk/09-non-newtonian-rheology.md) ·
[Free boundaries, level sets & phase field](09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md) ·
[Nonlinear solvers & continuation](../07-numerics/07-nonlinear-solvers-and-continuation.md) ·
[Engineering devices](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md)

\newpage

# MHD & relativistic transport


# MHD & relativistic transport

> **One-line picture.** Couple transport to electromagnetism or relativity and new constraints appear:
> the field freezes into the fluid, and time becomes a coordinate you may not neglect.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [incompressible flow](../02-bulk/03-incompressible-flow-vorticity-and-pressure.md),
> [BGK & Vlasov](../05-kinetic/02-bgk-vlasov-and-plasma-kinetics.md) · **Leads to.**
> [plasma & astrophysics](../08-bridges/05-plasma-and-astrophysics.md)

## L1 · Magnetohydrodynamics

Navier–Stokes plus Maxwell, with momentum gaining the Lorentz force and the field being transported by
the flow:

$$\partial_t\mathbf B=\nabla\times(\mathbf u\times\mathbf B)+\eta\nabla^2\mathbf B,\qquad
\mathbf J=\frac{1}{\mu_0}\nabla\times\mathbf B .$$

* **Ideal MHD ($\eta\to0$): Alfvén's frozen-flux theorem.** Field lines move with the fluid; magnetic
  tension acts like a stiff spring. Consequences: Alfvén waves, magnetosonic shocks, and the
  impossibility of changing field *topology* by smooth motion.
* **Resistive MHD: reconnection.** Lines can break and rejoin, releasing magnetic energy — solar flares,
  coronal heating, tokamak sawteeth. Classical (Sweet–Parker) rates are too slow; fast reconnection
  (Petschek, plasmoid-mediated) remains an active research area.

The **dynamo problem**: how does a rotating, convecting conductor sustain a field against resistive
decay? Answered for the geodynamo and solar dynamo mainly by simulation plus mean-field closures — and
those closures are exactly the eddy-viscosity-style approximations of
[Mori–Zwanzig](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md).

## L2 · Relativistic and astrophysical highlights

| Setting | Transport feature |
|---|---|
| accretion disc | turbulent stress (an $\alpha$-disc closure) moves matter inward, energy outward |
| relativistic fluid | pressure contributes inertia; heat flux and bulk viscosity are frame-dependent |
| cosmic rays | energy-dependent $D(E)$ in magnetic turbulence produces spectral hardening |
| photon diffusion (Rosseland in a moving medium) | radiative viscosity and damping |
| neutrino transport | sets neutron-star cooling and nucleosynthesis |

Two lessons transfer back to ordinary engineering: **anisotropic transport** (magnetised media conduct
far better along field lines than across — structurally like layered porous media), and **closure by
mean-field theory**, with the same scientific status as engineering turbulence models.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Frozen flux** is a sharp statement about what ideal MHD cannot do; topology changes require
  non-ideal terms.
* **A divergence constraint**: $\nabla\cdot\mathbf B=0$ must be enforced (constrained transport,
  projection, or divergence cleaning) or solutions drift — the magnetic analogue of
  incompressibility.
* **Richer discontinuities**: slow, intermediate (Alfvén) and fast shocks plus rotational
  discontinuities, so MHD Riemann solvers are considerably more intricate than gas-dynamic ones.
* **Relativistic causality forbids naive constitutive laws.** Fourier's law with one temperature is
  acausal and unstable in relativistic fluids; causal (Israel–Stewart) or kinetic treatments are
  required — the same failure mode as
  [hyperbolic heat conduction](04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md).

## L4 · Numerics

* **Constrained transport** keeps $\nabla\cdot\mathbf B$ at machine zero; projection is costlier and less
  robust. HLLD-type solvers plus GLM cleaning are the standard toolkit, and naive central schemes fail
  at low plasma $\beta$.
* **Dynamos need extremely long integrations** (resistive times are huge): implicit/exponential
  integrators and strict conservation are mandatory.
* **Relativistic codes** struggle with conservative-to-primitive inversion (a fragile root-find) and with
  neutrino/radiation coupling.
* **Benchmarks**: Orszag–Tang vortex, Brio–Wu shock tube, Christensen dynamo benchmarks, relativistic
  shock tubes ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Plasmoid-mediated reconnection; planetary and stellar dynamos; GR-MHD for mergers;
  kinetic corrections where MHD fails.

## Checks

1. What does frozen flux forbid, and what restores topology change? *(It forbids topology change;
   resistivity/reconnection restores it.)*
2. Why is the divergence constraint like incompressibility? *(Both constrain a vector field and require
   an extra enforcement mechanism.)*
3. Why does Fourier's law fail relativistically? *(It is acausal: signals would exceed $c$.)*

## See also

[BGK & Vlasov](../05-kinetic/02-bgk-vlasov-and-plasma-kinetics.md) ·
[Plasma & astrophysics](../08-bridges/05-plasma-and-astrophysics.md) ·
[Memory, viscoelasticity & Cattaneo](04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md) ·
[Structure-preserving discretisations](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)

# Integrable systems & solitons


# Integrable systems & solitons

> **One-line picture.** Some nonlinear dispersive equations are secretly linear: change variables to
> *scattering data* and the nonlinear flow becomes a rigid translation. The transport is real — it just
> happens in spectral space.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Higher order & dispersive](../01-foundations/11-higher-order-and-dispersive.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Variational principles & Noether](15-variational-principles-and-noether.md),
> [Why everything flows](../11-universality/01-why-everything-flows.md)

## L1 · The observation that started it (1834, and again in 1965)

Scott Russell watched a solitary wave travel a canal for miles without changing shape. A century later
Zabusky and Kruskal simulated the Korteweg–de Vries equation, saw two such humps pass *through* each
other and emerge unchanged (apart from a small phase shift), and called them **solitons**. Since then the
same objects have been found in optical fibres, plasmas, Bose–Einstein condensates, lattices, Josephson
junctions and DNA models — and the mathematics behind them turned out to be one of the deepest in the
subject.

The engine is a *balance*: nonlinearity steepens a wave, dispersion spreads it, and the two can hold
each other in equilibrium — a soliton is a nonlinear wave whose steepening exactly cancels its spreading.

## L2 · Integrability: what it means, and how it works

**Lax pair.** A nonlinear equation $u_t=K[u]$ is integrable if it is the compatibility condition of two
*linear* problems, $L\psi=\lambda\psi$ and $\psi_t=A\psi$, i.e.

$$L_t=[A,L].$$

Because the evolution is a similarity transformation, **the spectrum of $L$ is time-independent** — the
eigenvalues are integrals of the motion. For KdV, $L=-\partial_x^2+u$ is a Schrödinger operator, so the
"quantum mechanics of a potential" governs water waves.

**Inverse scattering transform (IST)** — a nonlinear Fourier transform in three steps:

1. **Direct scattering**: send a wave packet through the potential $u(x,0)$, record the *scattering
   data*: reflection coefficient $R(k)$ and bound-state eigenvalues/norming constants.
2. **Evolve**: the scattering data obey *linear, constant-velocity* equations —
   $R(k,t)=R(k,0)e^{8ik^3t}$, eigenvalues fixed. This is **transport in spectral space**, and it is the
   entire nonlinear dynamics in disguise.
3. **Inverse scattering**: reconstruct $u(x,t)$ from the evolved data by solving a linear
   Riemann–Hilbert/Gel'fand–Levitan problem.

| Equation | Physical setting | Soliton content |
|---|---|---|
| KdV $u_t+6uu_x+u_{xxx}=0$ | shallow water, ion-acoustic plasma, lattices | $N$-solitons, elastic collisions with phase shifts |
| NLS $i\psi_t+\psi_{xx}+\|\psi\|^2\psi=0$ | fibre optics, BEC, deep water | bright/dark solitons, breathers, rogue waves |
| sine-Gordon $u_{tt}-u_{xx}+\sin u=0$ | Josephson junctions, crystal dislocations | kinks, antikinks, breathers |
| Toda lattice | nonlinear springs | discrete solitons |
| KP / Davey–Stewartson | 2-D water waves | line solitons, lumps, higher-dimensional structure |

**Why "infinite conservation laws" follows**: the spectrum is fixed, so every functional of the
scattering data is conserved; expanding in $k$ generates an infinite hierarchy (mass, momentum, energy,
and infinitely many more). This is also why integrable equations are so valuable numerically: they come
with an unlimited supply of exact checks.

**Beyond the first example.** The same circle of ideas gives Riemann–Hilbert problems, the
KP/Toda hierarchies, finite-gap (algebro-geometric) solutions on Riemann surfaces, $\tau$-functions, and
the asymptotic theory of universality classes in random matrix theory — the same equations resurface in
the statistics of growth interfaces and of eigenvalue fluctuations.

## L3 · What integrability guarantees

* **Solitons are *stable*, and variationally so.** For NLS, a soliton is the minimiser of the energy at
  fixed mass — orbital stability follows from that convexity, not from luck. This is the same mechanism
  that stabilises vortices, skyrmions and coherent structures generally.
* **Collisions are elastic with computable phase shifts.** Integrable systems exchange momentum but not
  "identity"; generic systems interact inelastically and chaotically. Two solitons emerging unchanged is
  therefore a *diagnostic* of integrability.
* **Soliton resolution**: for generic initial data, the solution decomposes as $t\to\infty$ into a finite
  number of solitons plus a decaying dispersive tail. This is the nonlinear analogue of a spectral
  decomposition or of the central limit theorem — a complete classification of long-time behaviour, and
  it is *proved* for KdV, NLS and many relatives.
* **Chaos is absent.** Integrable flows live on tori, have zero Lyapunov exponents, and have as many
  conserved quantities as degrees of freedom (Liouville). Perturb them and KAM theory keeps most tori —
  but allows exponentially slow *Arnold diffusion* between them: transport in action space, with an
  astronomical but finite rate.
* **Integrability is fragile but not isolated.** A generic perturbation destroys it, yet the nearby
  structure (tori, slow diffusion, coherent structures) persists — which is why solitons are observed in
  real water, real fibres and real plasmas rather than only in exactly solvable models.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Dissipation destroys the physics.** A first- or second-order upwind scheme turns a soliton into a
  spreading blob within a few widths. Integrable and dispersive problems demand spectral or high-order
  compact schemes with almost no numerical damping
  ([numerical diffusion & dispersion](../07-numerics/03-numerical-diffusion-and-dispersion-error.md)).
* **Splitting is exact where it can be.** For KdV/NLS, integrate the linear dispersive part exactly in
  Fourier space and the nonlinear part exactly in physical space, composed by Strang splitting: second
  order, cheap, and it preserves the invariants to high accuracy
  ([splitting & coupling](../07-numerics/05-operator-splitting-and-multiphysics-coupling.md)).
* **Conservation is your error metric.** Mass, momentum, energy and (for integrable cases) higher
  invariants are known exactly: monitor them instead of guessing. Soliton *positions* after many
  collisions are the sensitive test.
* **Long-time phase accuracy** matters more than local accuracy: soliton speeds are the physical
  observables, so a scheme with a small phase error will pass short tests and fail long ones.
* **Benchmarks**: the Zabusky–Kruskal recurrence experiment; the two-soliton collision phase shift; NLS
  bright solitons, breathers and Peregrine rogue waves; focusing blow-up at critical mass
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).

## Checks

1. What does $L_t=[A,L]$ buy you? *(Time-independence of the spectrum of $L$ — infinitely many conserved
   quantities, and linear evolution of the scattering data.)*
2. Why is "a soliton emerges unchanged from a collision" a signature of integrability? *(Elastic
   collisions with only phase shifts do not occur in generic nonlinear systems.)*
3. What is soliton resolution? *(The rigorous statement that generic solutions separate into solitons plus
   a decaying tail — a complete long-time classification.)*

## See also

[Higher order & dispersive](../01-foundations/11-higher-order-and-dispersive.md) ·
[Variational principles & Noether](15-variational-principles-and-noether.md) ·
[Conservation laws & shocks](01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md) ·
[Optimal transport & gradient flows](10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md)

# Variational principles & Noether


# Variational principles & Noether

> **One-line picture.** If a system minimises something, its equations are the Euler–Lagrange equations,
> its symmetries become conservation laws, and its flow is an *incompressible transport* in phase space.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Integrable systems](14-integrable-systems-and-solitons.md),
> [Why everything flows](../11-universality/01-why-everything-flows.md)

## L1 · Everything from a functional

Two complementary ways to write physics, both of which are transport statements:

* **Dissipative (gradient flow):** $\partial_tu=-\mathcal{M}\,\delta F/\delta u$ — downhill on a
  functional in a chosen metric ([entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md)).
* **Conservative (action principle):** the path taken makes the action $S=\int L\,\mathrm{d}t$ stationary;
  the resulting Euler–Lagrange equations are the equations of motion. In field theory the action is a
  spacetime integral and the equations are PDEs: waves, elasticity, Maxwell, Einstein, Schrödinger,
  Yang–Mills.

The same PDE can be read either way, which is why variational thinking explains *why* particular terms
appear: a conservation law is a symmetry, and a constitutive law is a variational derivative.

## L2 · Noether and the structure theorems

**Noether's theorem.** Each continuous symmetry of the action gives a conservation law:

| Symmetry | Conserved quantity | Transport content |
|---|---|---|
| time translation | energy | flux balance for energy |
| space translation | momentum | momentum flux tensor |
| rotation | angular momentum | vorticity/circulation theorems |
| boost (Galilean, Lorentz) | centre-of-mass velocity, energy–momentum | relativistic transport constraints |
| phase (gauge, $U(1)$) | charge, probability | charge conservation = continuity equation |
| scaling | virial theorem | exponents of similarity solutions |
| internal (non-abelian gauge) | colour/weak isospin currents | **Noether's second theorem**: identities ⇒ constraints (e.g. Bianchi ⇒ $\nabla_\mu T^{\mu\nu}=0$) |

**Hamiltonian form and incompressible transport.** Writing the field as $(q,p)$ with a Hamiltonian $H$,
the flow is $\dot q=\partial H/\partial p$, $\dot p=-\partial H/\partial q$: a **symplectic**
(shear-like) velocity field in phase space. **Liouville's theorem** says the flow preserves phase-space
volume — literally *incompressible transport*, so the phase-space density satisfies
$\partial_tf+\{f,H\}=0$, the collisionless Boltzmann equation. Transport in phase space is thus not an
analogy: it is a continuity equation with a divergence-free velocity.

**The dissipative counterpart.** Onsager's principle of least dissipation, the principle of minimum
entropy production, and the **GENERIC** structure
$\dot z=L\,\delta E/\delta z+M\,\delta S/\delta z$ (a reversible co-symmetric part $L$ plus a
symmetric, positive-semidefinite dissipative part $M$) give a single framework in which the
Hamiltonian and the gradient-flow pictures coexist. That is also where Onsager's reciprocal relations
came from ([constitutive relations](../01-foundations/03-constitutive-relations.md)).

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Symmetry ⟹ conservation, and broken symmetry ⟹ sources.** A conservation law holds *because* nothing
  breaks the symmetry; switch to a rotating frame and Coriolis "appears"; break spatial uniformity and a
  force appears. Every source term in this atlas can be read as a broken symmetry — which is a useful
  diagnostic when a term looks arbitrary in an equation.
* **Gauge invariance *is* a continuity equation.** The $U(1)$ symmetry of quantum mechanics gives
  $\partial_t|\psi|^2+\nabla\!\cdot\!\mathbf{j}=0$ exactly: quantum probability obeys a transport law with
  a conserved current. Magnetohydrodynamics, superconductivity and the Standard Model inherit the same
  structure — charge transport is not an analogy, it is Noether's theorem.
* **Constraints are Noether's second theorem.** Gauge/nonlocal symmetries produce *identities*
  (Bianchi identities) rather than conserved charges. That is why general relativity has its Hamiltonian
  and momentum constraints, why Maxwell has $\nabla\!\cdot\!\mathbf{B}=0$, and why pressure in
  incompressible flow is a Lagrange multiplier — all the same phenomenon
  ([incompressible flow](../02-bulk/03-incompressible-flow-vorticity-and-pressure.md)).
* **Stability is convexity.** The second variation of the action/functional decides stability; loss of
  positive-definiteness is a *unified* instability mechanism (Rayleigh–Bénard is loss of energy
  convexity; Turing is loss of stability of a uniform state; shearing instabilities are loss of
  definiteness of the energy in a moving frame).
* **Classical mechanics is the geometric-optics limit of wave mechanics.** The action satisfies a
  Hamilton–Jacobi equation, rays are its characteristics ([Hamilton–Jacobi](02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md)),
  and the Feynman weight $\exp(iS/\hbar)$ is the transport of amplitudes — the WKB/vanishing-$\hbar$ limit
  turns wave transport into ray transport, caustics and all.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Symplectic/variational integrators conserve the structure, not just the solution.** Derived from a
  *discrete* action principle, they obey a **discrete Noether theorem** (exact momentum and angular
  momentum) and show *bounded* energy error instead of secular drift — the reason molecular dynamics,
  celestial mechanics and accelerator physics use them at all
  ([structure-preserving discretisations](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)).
* **Casimir-preserving schemes** keep the invariants of the underlying Poisson bracket (e.g. vorticity
  integrals in 2-D Euler), which is what keeps long simulations of geophysical flows physical.
* **Hamiltonian Monte Carlo** is transport in phase space used as an algorithm: simulate the
  Hamiltonian flow with a symplectic integrator and accept/reject. Its efficiency in modern
  probabilistic machine learning *depends* on the symplectic structure
  ([machine learning & data](../08-bridges/07-machine-learning-and-data.md)).
* **Dissipative counterparts** should be discretely dissipative: entropy-stable schemes mirror GENERIC
  (reversible + dissipative split) and give unconditional nonlinear stability.
* **Benchmarks**: energy drift, symplectic versus non-symplectic, over $10^6$ steps (pendulum, Kepler,
  Fermi–Pasta–Ulam–Tsingou recurrence); discrete-Noether momentum checks; Casimir conservation;
  HMC acceptance rates versus step size.

## Checks

1. Why does Liouville's theorem make collisionless kinetic theory a transport equation?
   *($\{f,H\}=\nabla\!\cdot\!(f\,\mathbf{v})$ with $\nabla\!\cdot\!\mathbf{v}=0$ in phase space:
   incompressible flow.)*
2. What does Noether's *second* theorem produce? *(Identities — constraints like Bianchi,
   $\nabla\!\cdot\!\mathbf{B}=0$, incompressibility — rather than conserved charges.)*
3. What do variational integrators conserve exactly? *(Momentum and angular momentum (discrete Noether);
   energy is conserved to bounded oscillation rather than drifting.)*

## See also

[Entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md) ·
[Integrable systems & solitons](14-integrable-systems-and-solitons.md) ·
[Hamilton–Jacobi & viscosity solutions](02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md) ·
[Structure-preserving discretisations](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md) ·
[Mathematics itself](../11-universality/08-mathematics-itself.md)

# Delay & retarded transport


# Delay & retarded transport

> **One-line picture.** A delay is not a new ingredient: plug flow with a transit time *is* a delay, and
> every delay differential equation is a transport equation whose history has been integrated out. The
> cost of removing the spatial dimension is that the state becomes a function, not a number.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Memory, viscoelasticity & Cattaneo](04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md),
> [Material derivative](../01-foundations/02-material-derivative.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Cognitive science & mind](../11-universality/03-cognitive-science-and-mind.md),
> [Social behaviour, economics & language](../11-universality/04-social-behaviour-economics-language.md)

## L1 · Why lags are everywhere

Signals, materials and organisms take time to respond: an axon conducts in milliseconds, a gene
transcribes in minutes, a driver reacts in ~1 s, an epidemic incubates for days, an immune system
responds in weeks. Model any of them with an instantaneous response and you will miss the qualitative
behaviour — which is, remarkably often, *oscillation*.

Delays are the most economical generator of oscillation known: a simple negative feedback with a lag
produces ringing, and enough lag produces chaos. That single mechanism underlies neural rhythms, laser
pulsations, traffic waves, physiological cycles and control-system instability.

## L2 · The structure, and the identity that makes it transport

A scalar delay differential equation (DDE) is $\dot u=f(u(t),u(t-\tau))$; with spatial transport it
becomes a **delay PDE**, $\partial_tu+\mathbf{u}\!\cdot\!\nabla u=f(u,u_\tau)$. The key observation is
that a delay *is* a translation along a characteristic:

$$\text{plug flow: } u(t-\tau)=u(x-u_0\tau,\,t)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\underbrace{\partial_tu+u_0\partial_xu=f(u,u(x-L,t))}_{\text{transport with a spatial shift}} .$$

So a DDE is a transport equation in disguise: the "memory" of the lumped model is the spatial profile
that the advection equation carries. Two consequences:

* **Memory is transport with the space removed.** A single delay is a delta memory kernel; a
  distribution of delays is a general memory kernel; and a power-law kernel gives a fractional
  derivative ([memory & viscoelasticity](04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md)). DDEs, Volterra
  equations, fractional equations and transport-with-drift are four notations for one structure.
* **Distributed delays = transport in an internal coordinate.** Maturation delays, age-structured
  populations and transit-time distributions obey genuine transport equations in *age* or *transit time*:

$$\partial_tn+\partial_an=D\partial_a^2n-\mu(a)n,\qquad n(0,t)=\int\beta(a)n(a,t)\,\mathrm da
\qquad\text{(a McKendrick–von Foerster equation).}$$

That is why the same mathematics describes epidemiology (SEIR with incubation), cell biology (cell-cycle
models), pharmacokinetics (transit compartments) and chemical reactors (residence-time distributions).

| Model | Lag | Behaviour |
|---|---|---|
| Mackey–Glass (haematopoiesis) | blood-cell maturation | period-doubling route to chaos |
| delayed logistic / Wright | reproduction | Hopf oscillation, long-period cycles |
| optimal-velocity traffic model | driver reaction time | stop-and-go waves, jam propagation |
| neural field with axonal delay | conduction velocity | travelling waves, spindle rhythms, epileptiform waves |
| laser with optical feedback | cavity round trip | pulsations, low-dimensional chaos |
| machining / control loops | sensing + actuation | chatter, instability |
| drift-diffusion with delayed evidence | decision lag | oscillations in choice dynamics |

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **The state is a function, not a number.** A DDE needs a *history* $u(\theta)$, $\theta\in[-\tau,0]$, as
  initial datum; solutions exist by the **method of steps**, and the flow is a semigroup — but on the
  space of histories $C([-\tau,0])$, which is infinite-dimensional. Every intuitive statement about
  "state" must be re-read accordingly.
* **The spectrum is infinitely rich.** Linearising gives a *transcendental* characteristic equation
  (e.g. $\lambda=a+be^{-\lambda\tau}=0$), so there are **infinitely many eigenvalues**, finitely many in
  any right half-plane. For scalar linear DDEs they can be written exactly with the **Lambert $W$
  function**, $\lambda=a+\tfrac1\tau W(b\tau e^{-a\tau})$. Stability is decided by the rightmost
  eigenvalue — and by how it moves with $\tau$.
* **Delay destabilises, and creates stability pockets.** Negative feedback plus lag gives a Hopf
  bifurcation: oscillation appears from nothing. As $\tau$ increases further the pair can *re-stabilise*
  and destabilise again — stability switches that are impossible in any finite-dimensional ODE system, and
  are the origin of physiological and engineering cycling.
* **Delay can select a wavelength.** In a transported system with a delayed boundary condition, resonance
  occurs when the delay matches a spatial period ($L=u_0\tau$): delay imposes a *length scale*, creating
  patterns where the instantaneous model has none. This is delay-induced pattern formation — distinct from
  [Turing's mechanism](../04-interaction/04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md), though it can cooperate
  with it.
* **Causality decides well-posedness.** *Retarded* equations (delay in the lower-order terms) are
  well-posed with a history; *neutral* equations need more regularity and can be delicate; *advanced*
  equations (the state depending on the future) are ill-posed for the initial-value problem — the
  anti-causal case, and the reason you cannot simply reverse an advection with delay.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Method of steps** for small $\tau$; **continuous Runge–Kutta** with dense output for moderate delays;
  **pseudospectral collocation in the history interval** (Chebyshev nodes) for high accuracy and spectra
  — this converts the DDE into an ODE system plus coupling conditions, and is the standard production
  approach.
* **Expect spurious eigenvalues.** Discretising an infinite-dimensional spectrum always invents some:
  refine and re-check that the rightmost eigenvalues converge before trusting a stability verdict.
* **Delay PDEs need characteristic-aware treatment.** The delayed term is a history *along the
  characteristic*; store and interpolate the history with high order, or you will inject dissipation and
  artificial dispersion into a hyperbolic problem.
* **Estimating $\tau$ from data is possible** — delay-embedding (Takens' theorem) reconstructs the
  dynamics from a single time series; mutual information, autocorrelation and wavelet methods give
  practical estimates. This is the entry point to data-driven dynamics and to
  [cognitive time series](../11-universality/03-cognitive-science-and-mind.md).
* **Benchmarks**: delayed logistic equation (Hopf at $\tau=\pi/2$); Mackey–Glass period doubling to chaos;
  delayed optimal-velocity traffic waves; Lang–Kobayashi laser dynamics; neural-field travelling waves
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).

## Checks

1. Why is a DDE "just transport"? *(Plug flow makes $u(t-\tau)=u(x-u_0\tau,t)$: the delay is a spatial
   shift, and the memory of the lumped model is the profile the advection equation carries.)*
2. What makes the spectrum infinite, and how do you solve it exactly for a scalar linear DDE? *(The
   $e^{-\lambda\tau}$ term; via the Lambert $W$ function.)*
3. Give a behaviour a delayed system has that no finite-dimensional ODE can. *(Stability switches as
   $\tau$ grows: an infinite sequence of stable and unstable intervals.)*

# Numerics & inversion


# Numerics & inversion

Everything in this atlas eventually meets a computer. This category is the discipline of that meeting:
stability before accuracy, verification before validation, and uncertainty before reporting.

| # | Note | The one thing it gives you |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | [Discretisation, stability & convergence](01-discretization-consistency-stability-convergence.md) | the three questions, and the family tree of schemes |
| 2 | [CFL, stiffness & time integration](02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md) | why explicit steps are limited, implicit steps are not, and what stiffness costs |
| 3 | [Numerical diffusion & dispersion](03-numerical-diffusion-and-dispersion-error.md) | the two errors every scheme commits, and how to measure them |
| 4 | [Limiters, TVD & WENO](04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md) | sharp fronts without ringing; beating Godunov's barrier |
| 5 | [Operator splitting & multiphysics coupling](05-operator-splitting-and-multiphysics-coupling.md) | solving coupled physics piecewise without introducing instability |
| 6 | [Linear solvers & preconditioning](06-linear-solvers-and-preconditioning.md) | the elliptic solve inside every implicit step |
| 7 | [Nonlinear solvers & continuation](07-nonlinear-solvers-and-continuation.md) | Newton, its failure modes, and how homotopy rescues them |
| 8 | [Mesh, adaptivity & parallel decomposition](08-mesh-adaptivity-moving-domains-and-parallel.md) | putting resolution where the action is; splitting the work |
| 9 | [Particle & Monte Carlo methods](09-particle-and-monte-carlo-methods.md) | when to stop meshing and start walking |
| 10 | [Inverse problems & data assimilation](10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md) | getting coefficients out of data, with honest uncertainty |
| 11 | [Machine learning & operator learning](11-machine-learning-and-operator-learning.md) | where learning helps, and the failures that matter |
| 12 | [Verification, validation & benchmarks](12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md) | manufactured solutions, convergence rates, and the standard test cases |
| 13 | [Structure-preserving discretisations](13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md) | schemes that respect conservation, positivity and entropy by construction |

![**Figure F.** Stability and the Courant condition: forward Euler lives in a small disc while backward
Euler covers the whole stable half-plane — which is why stiff transport must be implicit.](stability-regions.pdf)

## The order to read them in

1. **Understand the operator** ([discretisation](01-discretization-consistency-stability-convergence.md))
   and your regime ([dimensionless numbers](../01-foundations/05-dimensionless-numbers.md)).
2. **Pick the time treatment** ([CFL & stiffness](02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md)) and the
   spatial scheme ([limiters](04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md) if sharp features exist).
3. **Make the algebra work** ([linear](06-linear-solvers-and-preconditioning.md) and
   [nonlinear solvers](07-nonlinear-solvers-and-continuation.md)).
4. **Make it scale** ([mesh & parallel](08-mesh-adaptivity-moving-domains-and-parallel.md)).
5. **Prove it** ([V&V](12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)) before believing any physics.
6. **Then invert** ([inverse problems](10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)) — remembering that
   inversion inherits every weakness of the forward solver.

> **The one habit that prevents most disasters:** separate *stability* from *accuracy*. A stable scheme can
> be wrong by a factor of two; an accurate scheme can be unstable and produce nothing. Diagnose which
> problem you have before changing anything.
