# Discretisation, stability & convergence


# Discretisation, stability & convergence

> **One-line picture.** Replace derivatives by differences (or fluxes, or basis functions), then prove
> three things: the discrete equations approximate the continuous ones, small errors do not grow, and
> therefore the answer converges.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [PDE taxonomy](../01-foundations/04-pde-taxonomy-and-boundary-conditions.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [CFL & stiffness](02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md),
> [numerical diffusion](03-numerical-diffusion-and-dispersion-error.md),
> [structure-preserving schemes](13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)

## L1 · The logic

Three words govern everything:

* **Consistency** — the discretisation reproduces the PDE as $\Delta x,\Delta t\to0$ (check with a Taylor
  expansion; the leading leftover terms are the *truncation error*, which also tells you the *order*).
* **Stability** — errors do not grow without bound. Test by von Neumann analysis (eigenvalues of the
  amplification matrix, $|G(k)|\le1$), the energy method, or boundedness of the discrete semigroup.
* **Convergence** — the discrete solution approaches the true one. The **Lax–Richtmyer equivalence
  theorem** ties it together: *consistency $+$ stability $\iff$ convergence* for linear problems.

**Modified equations** are the practitioner's shortcut: Taylor-expand the scheme *as if it were exact*
and read off which PDE it actually solves. This is where "numerical viscosity" comes from — the
leading error term often looks exactly like a diffusion term, with a coefficient you did not choose.

## L2 · The families, and what each is good at

| Family | Conserves? | Geometry | Strengths | Weaknesses |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| finite difference | not automatically | structured grids | simplest, high order possible | geometry, conservation |
| finite volume | **exactly** (flux form) | any (unstructured) | conservation, shocks, multiphase | reconstruction needed for accuracy |
| finite element | exactly (weak form) | any, adaptive | elliptic problems, error theory | monotonicity not automatic |
| spectral | yes (globally) | simple domains | exponential accuracy for smooth data | Gibbs for discontinuities |
| spectral element / DG | yes | any | high order + geometry + hp-adaptivity | complexity, CFL for explicit DG |
| lattice-Boltzmann / particle | exactly (by construction) | particle-based | complex geometry, kinetic regimes | noise, low-Mach limits |
| neural / operator learning | not guaranteed | mesh-free | speed after training | accuracy guarantees, extrapolation |

The rule of thumb that runs through this atlas: **discretise the conserved form and let the fluxes
enforce conservation**; then add monotonicity/positivity constraints where the physics demands them.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Order and error estimates.** For a consistent scheme of order $p$ in space and $q$ in time, the error
  scales as $O(\Delta x^p+\Delta t^q)$ *provided* the solution is smooth enough — and provided stability
  holds. The smoothness clause is where high-order methods fail (see
  [limiters](04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md)).
* **Stability is a property of the operator, not the grid.** Non-normal operators (advection with
  strong shear, MHD at low $\beta$) can be *asymptotically* but not *transiently* stable: eigenvalues are
  misleading. Use the pseudospectrum or the energy method.
* **the discrete maximum principle.** Monotone stencils (M-matrices) guarantee positivity and bounds;
  this is a nonlinear property that a linear stability analysis cannot see.
* **Verification needs more than convergence.** Check the *rate* by refinement studies (grid convergence
  index), and check conservation and symmetry properties that the scheme should preserve
  ([V&V](12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).

## L4 · Practicalities

* **Choose the discretisation from the operator's type**, not from taste: elliptic $\to$ FEM/FV with
  multigrid; parabolic $\to$ implicit stepping; hyperbolic $\to$ conservative FV with limiters; kinetic
  $\to$ particle/discrete-velocity methods.
* **Order reduction** is common: boundary closures, variable coefficients and nonlinearity can knock a
  nominally fourth-order scheme down to second. Measure the observed rate.
* **Conservation in practice**: sum the fluxes across the whole domain and compare with the change of
  mass; a mismatch means a bug, not "numerical diffusion".
* **Represent the geometry honestly**: body-fitted, cut-cell, immersed boundary or overset grids each
  trade accuracy against complexity.
* **Benchmarks**: manufactured solutions (the only test with a known answer), the lid-driven cavity,
  Taylor–Green vortex, and shock tubes
  ([V&V](12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).

## Checks

1. Which two conditions give convergence for linear problems? *(Consistency and stability — Lax–Richtmyer.)*
2. Why can eigenvalues of the amplification matrix be dangerously misleading? *(Non-normal operators can
   grow transiently while all eigenvalues are stable.)*
3. What is the modified equation of first-order upwind advection? *(Advection plus a diffusion term with
   $D_{\rm num}=\frac{U\Delta x}{2}(1-\mathrm{CFL})$.)*

## See also

[CFL, stiffness & time integration](02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md) ·
[Numerical diffusion & dispersion](03-numerical-diffusion-and-dispersion-error.md) ·
[Verification, validation & benchmarks](12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md) ·
[Structure-preserving discretisations](13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)

# CFL, stiffness & time integration


# CFL, stiffness & time integration

> **One-line picture.** An explicit step may not let information jump more than one cell; an implicit
> step may, but it must solve a system. Stiffness is the gap between the fastest and slowest
> timescales — and it decides which of the two you can afford.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [discretisation & stability](01-discretization-consistency-stability-convergence.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [operator splitting & coupling](05-operator-splitting-and-multiphysics-coupling.md),
> [linear solvers](06-linear-solvers-and-preconditioning.md)

![**Figure 25.** Stability regions and the Courant condition. Forward Euler is confined to a disc;
backward Euler covers the entire left half-plane. Stiff transport pushes the eigenvalues far left, which
is why explicit methods are impossible there and implicit methods are cheap in stability terms —
though not in cost.](stability-regions.pdf)

## L1 · The two limits

* **Advection (hyperbolic):** the CFL condition $|U|\Delta t/\Delta x\le1$ (in $d$ dimensions roughly
  $\le1/\sqrt d$ for multidimensional explicit schemes). It is a *domain-of-influence* statement: the
  numerical solution may not use information from farther than a wave can travel in one step.
* **Diffusion (parabolic):** $\Delta t\le\Delta x^2/(2D)$. This is far harsher: halving $\Delta x$
  quadruples the number of steps, and in $d$ dimensions the time step scales as $\Delta x^2/d$.
* **Reaction:** the fastest rate constant sets an explicit limit, $\Delta t\lesssim1/k_{\max}$, which can
  be nanoseconds in combustion while the flame takes milliseconds to evolve.

**Stiffness ratio** $\lambda_{\max}/\lambda_{\min}$ measures the separation. Stiff systems are not
unstable *per se*, but explicit schemes pay the fastest scale to resolve the slowest — a ratio that can
reach $10^{12}$ in reactive transport.

## L2 · The schemes, and what each buys

| Scheme | Order | Stability | Best for |
|---|---|---|---|
| forward Euler | 1 | $|1+\lambda\Delta t|\le1$ | cheap, non-stiff, adjoint-ish |
| backward Euler | 1 | L-stable (no limit) | stiff, unconditionally stable, dissipative |
| Crank–Nicolson | 2 | A-stable, not L-stable | second order; can ring on stiff transients |
| explicit RK4 | 4 | limited region | accuracy for non-stiff smooth problems |
| IMEX (e.g. RK-IMEX) | 2–3 | implicit stiff part, explicit transport | advection–diffusion–reaction |
| BDF2/BDF3 | 2–3 | stiffly stable, L-stable variants | long-time stiff simulation |
| exponential integrators | 2–4 | exact for linear stiff part | reaction–diffusion, oscillatory stiff |
| Rosenbrock | 2–4 | L-stable | stiff with moderate nonlinearity |

The decisive distinction is **A-stability vs L-stability**: A-stable schemes control growth; L-stable
schemes also damp the fastest modes to zero, which is what you want when the fast mode is irrelevant
physics (stiff chemistry) — Crank–Nicolson's failure to do so is the famous source of ringing.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Dahlquist's barriers.** No explicit linear multistep method is A-stable above order 2; A-stable
  linear multistep methods cannot exceed order 2 unless they sacrifice something (BDF up to 6 is
  stiffly stable rather than A-stable). This is why high-order stiff integration goes to implicit
  Runge–Kutta or exponential methods.
* **Order reduction.** For stiff problems the *observed* order of implicit schemes often drops, because
  the error constants are amplified by the stiff eigenvalues; measure the rate, do not assume it.
* **Stability ≠ accuracy.** An explicit scheme below its stability limit may still be wildly inaccurate
  in phase (see [numerical dispersion](03-numerical-diffusion-and-dispersion-error.md)). Separating the
  two questions is the most valuable habit in numerical transport.
* **Adaptive stepping needs a proper error estimate** (embedded pairs, or step doubling) and a *relative*
  tolerance: concentrations spanning decades need per-species scaling.

## L4 · Practicalities

* **Choose by the dimensionless groups**: diffusion-dominated $\to$ implicit; advection-dominated $\to$
  explicit with a CFL-based step; reactive $\to$ implicit chemistry (per-cell, cheaply vectorised);
  mixed $\to$ IMEX.
* **Never let the CFL number silently exceed 1** in an explicit advection step; it is a stability
  violation, not an accuracy warning.
* **Adaptive initial steps** matter in transient problems; start small and let the controller grow.
* **Report the CFL and diffusion numbers** with every result — they are as important as the grid size.
* **Benchmarks**: linear advection and diffusion with analytic solutions, the ROBER stiff kinetics
  problem, and step-size convergence studies
  ([V&V](12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).

## Checks

1. Why is the diffusive limit quadratic in $\Delta x$ while the advective limit is linear? *(Diffusion
   propagates as $\sqrt{Dt}$; resolving it requires $\Delta t\sim\Delta x^2/D$.)*
2. What extra property does L-stability give over A-stability? *(Damping of fast modes — no ringing on
   stiff transients.)*
3. Why can an accurate scheme still be useless? *(Phase errors make wave speeds wrong even when
   amplitudes are right.)*

[Numerical diffusion & dispersion](03-numerical-diffusion-and-dispersion-error.md) ·

# Numerical diffusion & dispersion


# Numerical diffusion & dispersion

> **One-line picture.** A discretised advection operator is a *slightly different* operator. The
> difference always shows up as extra smoothing (diffusion) or wrong wave speed (dispersion) — or both.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [CFL & stiffness](02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md),
> [spectral view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [limiters, TVD & WENO](04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md)

![**Figure 26.** The error portrait. Left: after two periodic crossings, upwind advection smears the pulse while Lax–Wendroff rings. Middle: the amplification factor $|G(k)|$ — above one means unconditional instability. Right: the phase portrait — a lag means every wave travels at the wrong speed.](numerical-error-portrait.pdf)

## L1 · Where the errors come from

Take first-order upwind for $\partial_tc+U\partial_xc=0$ and Taylor-expand as if the scheme were exact.
The **modified equation** is

$$\partial_tc+U\partial_xc=D_{\rm num}\partial_x^2c+\cdots,\qquad
D_{\rm num}=\frac{U\Delta x}{2}(1-\mathrm{CFL}) .$$

So upwinding is *advection plus diffusion* — the scheme solves the wrong PDE, albeit one that converges
to the right one as $\Delta x\to0$. Lax–Wendroff, by contrast, is exact to second order and therefore has
no leading diffusion term: what remains is *dispersion*, $\partial_x^3c$ terms that make waves travel at
slightly wrong speeds, producing oscillations behind sharp features.

| Scheme | Leading error | Symptom | CFL behaviour |
|---|---|---|---|
| upwind (1st) | $D_{\rm num}\partial_{xx}$ | smears fronts | $D_{\rm num}\to0$ as CFL $\to1$ |
| Lax–Friedrichs (1st) | large diffusion | very diffuse | — |
| Lax–Wendroff (2nd) | dispersive $\partial_{xxx}$ | wiggles/ringing | worst at CFL $=0.5$ |
| beam-warming (2nd upwind) | dispersive | asymmetric ringing | one-sided |
| implicit upwind (1st) | very large $D_{\rm num}$ | heavy smearing | $D_{\rm num}$ grows with CFL |
| spectral | exponential (smooth data) | Gibbs at discontinuities | — |

## L2 · The measurement framework

Work in Fourier space: the discrete amplification factor $G_{\rm num}(k)$ should equal $e^{-ikU\Delta t}$.
Two diagnostics follow:

* **Amplitude error** $1-|G|$: positive $=$ numerical diffusion. Integrate it over a step to get the
  "effective viscosity" of your scheme — a number you can compare against the physical $D$ (the ratio is
  what decides whether your scheme is acceptable at your Péclet number).
* **Phase error** $\arg G+ikU\Delta t$: nonzero $=$ numerical dispersion. It makes every wave travel at
  the wrong speed, which matters disproportionately when many waves interact (turbulence, acoustics).

A useful rule: *first-order schemes are accurate when the physical diffusion dominates*; when the
physical $\mathrm{Pe}$ is large, you must either use higher order with limiters, or accept that you are
simulating a diffusive problem you did not intend.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Godunov's order barrier.** A linear, monotone scheme is at most first order. Monotonicity
  (no new extrema, positivity) and second-order accuracy are mutually exclusive without nonlinearity —
  hence [limiters](04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md).
* **Dissipation and dispersion are not free choices.** Any consistent scheme has an error operator whose
  leading term is one of these two forms; you choose the balance, not whether it appears.
* **Total variation.** TVD schemes prevent new extrema in space but may still be only first order at
  extrema (the classic limiter clipping problem at local maxima) — a second-order accurate *linear* TVD
  scheme does not exist.
* **Time-stepping contributes too.** Implicit schemes add their own damping (backward Euler is very
  diffusive); Crank–Nicolson has a weak phase error at large $\Delta t$. Analyse space and time errors
  together via $G_{\rm full}(k,\Delta t)$.

## L4 · Practicalities

* **Report the observed effective viscosity/dispersion** of your scheme, not just the grid size. Compare
  it to the physical coefficients: if $D_{\rm num}\gtrsim D$, you are simulating the scheme.
* **Mesh refinement studies must reveal the asymptotic order**; if order drops, find the culprit
  (boundaries, limiters, source terms, time splitting).
* **Do not compare against an analytic solution on a scaled grid without checking the CFL**; the error
  changes qualitatively with CFL (upwind is best at CFL $\to1$).
* **Diagnostics to keep in your workflow**: amplitude and phase portraits per scheme, and a convergence
  plot with error norms ($L^1$ to expose smearing, $L^\infty$ to expose ringing).
* **Benchmarks**: the Gaussian advection test (wrapped, with an exact answer), the Zalesak disk, and
  linear-advection convergence studies ([V&V](12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).

## Checks

1. What is the numerical diffusivity of first-order upwind, and when does it vanish? *($\frac{U\Delta
   x}{2}(1-\mathrm{CFL})$; it vanishes as CFL $\to1$.)*
2. Why does reducing $\Delta x$ not always reduce ringing? *(Second-order schemes have dispersive error
   that scales as $\Delta x^2$ but persists — and sharp features keep exciting it.)*
3. Why are $L^1$ and $L^\infty$ errors both needed? *($L^1$ measures smearing, $L^\infty$ exposes
   oscillations.)*

## See also

[CFL, stiffness & time integration](02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md) ·
[Limiters, TVD & WENO](04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md) ·
[The spectral view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md) ·
[Advection](../02-bulk/01-advection.md)

# Limiters, TVD & WENO


# Limiters, TVD & WENO

> **One-line picture.** Second-order accuracy creates new maxima; monotonicity forbids them. The escape
> is to make the scheme *nonlinear*: switch to first order exactly where a front needs it.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [numerical diffusion & dispersion](03-numerical-diffusion-and-dispersion-error.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [structure-preserving schemes](13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md),
> [conservation laws & shocks](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md)

## L1 · The idea

Godunov's theorem says no linear monotone scheme exceeds first order. So build a nonlinear one: compute a
high-order flux, then **limit** it by comparing it with lower-order fluxes at the same face. Where the
solution is smooth, the high-order flux is used and you get accuracy; where it is sharply varying, the
limiter pulls the scheme back toward monotone first order, and no new extrema appear.

## L2 · The machinery

* **TVD (total variation diminishing):** $\mathrm{TV}(c^{n+1})\le\mathrm{TV}(c^n)$ with
  $\mathrm{TV}=\sum_i|c_{i+1}-c_i|$. Sufficient for monotonicity and no new extrema.
* **MUSCL / slope limiters:** reconstruct cell values with slopes, then limit the slope (minmod,
  superbee, van Leer, MC). One function per face, cheap and robust.
* **Flux limiters:** write the flux as a blend $\phi(r)$ of first- and second-order fluxes, with
  $r$ the ratio of consecutive gradients; TVD requires $\phi$ in a specific region (Sweby diagram).
* **ENO/WENO:** use the smoothest of several candidate stencils (ENO) or a weighted combination (WENO),
  giving high order in smooth regions and essentially no oscillation at discontinuities.
* **Positivity-preserving and maximum-principle-preserving limiters** extend the same logic to keep
  concentrations non-negative and bounded — essential for reactive transport.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Second-order TVD is impossible** for a *linear* scheme; TVD methods are nonlinear by construction.
* **Order loss at extrema.** TVD schemes clip smooth extrema, degrading local accuracy to first order
  there; WENO recovers full order in smooth regions including extrema, at higher cost.
* **Convergence to the entropy solution.** Provided the scheme is conservative and consistent, TVD/ENO
  schemes converge to the weak entropy solution of the conservation law
  ([shocks](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md)).
* **Positivity is separate from monotonicity** and must be checked independently when source terms or
  diffusion are present.

## L4 · Practicalities

* **Choose the limiter by symptom**: minmod (diffusive but robust), van Leer/MC (balanced),
  superbee (sharp but can clip), WENO5 (smooth flows with occasional sharpness).
* **Limiters interact badly with implicit solvers** (nonsmooth functions frustrate Newton): use active-set
  detection or frozen limiters during the nonlinear iteration
  ([nonlinear solvers](07-nonlinear-solvers-and-continuation.md)).
* **Do not silence physical oscillations.** Oscillations in the *exact* solution (dispersive waves) must
  not be removed by a limiter — that is over-limiting, and it changes the physics.
* **Benchmarks**: Zalesak disk, Sod shock tube, the double Mach reflection, and tracer advection with
  positivity requirements ([V&V](12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).

## Checks

1. Which theorem forces limiters to be nonlinear? *(Godunov's order barrier.)*
2. What is the price of TVD at smooth extrema? *(Order reduction to first order there.)*

## See also

[Conservation laws & shocks](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md) ·
[Numerical diffusion & dispersion](03-numerical-diffusion-and-dispersion-error.md) ·
[Structure-preserving discretisations](13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)

\newpage

# Operator splitting & multiphysics coupling


# Operator splitting & multiphysics coupling

> **One-line picture.** Complex transport problems are sums of simple operators. Solve them in turn and
> you get a scheme that is only as good as the *commutator* you threw away.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [CFL & stiffness](02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [inverse problems](10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md),
> [subsidence & poroelasticity](../04-interaction/13-subsidence-and-poroelasticity.md)

## L1 · Why split, and what it costs

Reaction–diffusion–advection, flow–geomechanics, radiation–hydrodynamics: each subproblem has its own
best method. Splitting lets you use them — but introduces an error proportional to the commutator
$[A,B]=AB-BA$ of the operators.

| Splitting | Steps | Order | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lie–Trotter | $A$ then $B$ | 1 | simplest, cheapest |
| Strang | $A/2$, $B$, $A/2$ | 2 | standard for reaction–diffusion |
| IMEX | implicit stiff part, explicit transport | 2–3 | avoids splitting stiff chemistry |
| exponential | exact linear part + nonlinear corrector | 2–4 | good for stiff linear operators |

The commutator error matters exactly when both operators are fast — which is the physically interesting
regime. Diagnostics: compare a split scheme with a fully coupled solve on a test problem, and refine
$\Delta t$ to see whether the splitting error dominates.

## L2 · Coupling strategies for multiphysics

* **Monolithic (simultaneous):** solve all fields together in one Newton loop. Robust, but requires a
  coupled Jacobian and a coupled preconditioner.
* **Partitioned / staggered (sequential):** solve each physics in turn with data exchange. Modular and
  cheap, but may become *unstable* even when the continuous system is stable — the added-mass and
  added-stiffness instabilities of fluid–structure and poroelastic coupling are the classic examples.
* **Fixed-stress / drained- undrained splits** for poroelasticity: expose a parameter that makes the
  staggered scheme unconditionally stable while retaining accuracy.
* **Iterative coupling:** repeat the staggered step until convergence within the time step; one
  iteration ("loose coupling") is cheapest and least accurate.

| Coupling | Cost per step | Stability | Use when |
|---|---|---|---|
| monolithic | high | best | strong coupling, strong nonlinearity |
| fixed-stress / stabilised split | moderate | very good | poroelasticity (industry standard) |
| loose partitioned | low | conditional | weak coupling, exploratory work |
| IMEX in time | moderate | good | stiff reactions + transport |

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Splitting order** follows from the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff expansion: Strang is second order because
  the leading commutator term cancels by symmetry.
* **Conservation can be lost.** Splitting can break exact conservation laws unless each substep is
  conservative *and* the split is designed to preserve the invariant — a frequent silent bug in
  climate and reactive-transport codes.
* **Partitioned instability is a mathematical fact**, not an implementation bug: the coupling operator's
  spectrum can move into the unstable half-plane even though both subsystems are individually stable.
* **Positivity.** Splitting positive substeps does not guarantee positive results unless the substeps
  commute appropriately; check with the reaction–diffusion pair.

## L4 · Practicalities

* **Keep the splitting error below the discretisation error** and verify by refining in time only.
* **Prefer IMEX over splitting** for stiff reactions: it avoids the first-order splitting error that
  plagues fast-chemistry problems.
* **Stabilise partitioned schemes structurally** (fixed-stress, Robin transmission, Aitken relaxation)
  rather than by tuning under-relaxation parameters.
* **Benchmarks**: Mandel and Terzaghi problems (poroelasticity), reaction–diffusion with an analytic
  solution, and fluid–structure benchmarks (Turek–Hron)
  ([V&V](12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Adaptive split/non-split switching; learned coupling operators; error estimators that
  include the splitting error.

## Checks

1. Why is Strang splitting second order when Lie–Trotter is first? *(Symmetry cancels the leading
   commutator term.)*
2. When does a staggered scheme fail while both physics are stable? *(Strong coupling — added-mass or
   added-stiffness instability.)*

## See also

[CFL & time integration](02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md) ·
[Subsidence & poroelasticity](../04-interaction/13-subsidence-and-poroelasticity.md) ·
[Reaction kinetics & sinks](../04-interaction/01-reaction-kinetics-and-sinks.md) ·
[Structure-preserving discretisations](13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)

\newpage

# Linear solvers & preconditioning


# Linear solvers & preconditioning

> **One-line picture.** Every implicit time step hides an elliptic problem whose solution is *global*:
> a change anywhere affects everywhere. Solving it quickly is the difference between a usable code and
> an unusable one.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [CFL & time integration](02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [nonlinear solvers & continuation](07-nonlinear-solvers-and-continuation.md),
> [mesh, adaptivity & parallel](08-mesh-adaptivity-moving-domains-and-parallel.md)

## L1 · What is being solved, and why it is hard

Implicit transport steps produce $A\mathbf x=\mathbf b$ with $A$ sparse but ill-conditioned. Sources of
difficulty:

* **Anisotropy**: a dispersion tensor with $\alpha_L/\alpha_T\sim100$ makes the operator very anisotropic;
  pointwise smoothers fail, and convergence degrades with the anisotropy squared.
* **High contrast**: permeability or conductivity spanning $10^6$ across cells (layers, fractures)
  destroys simple preconditioners.
* **Convection dominance**: the matrix loses symmetry (and often positive definiteness), so CG is out.
* **Coupling**: multiphysics adds blocks (flow–geomechanics) with very different scales.

## L2 · The toolbox

| Method | Requires | Use for |
|---|---|---|
| CG | symmetric positive definite | diffusion/elliptic, preconditioned |
| GMRES (restarted) | nonsymmetric | advection–diffusion, coupled blocks |
| BiCGSTAB / IDR | nonsymmetric | cheaper per iteration alternative to GMRES |
| multigrid (geometric) | structured hierarchy | elliptic, optimal $O(N)$ |
| algebraic multigrid (AMG) | matrix only | unstructured, high-contrast coefficients |
| domain decomposition (Schwarz) | partition | parallel scaling, adds coarse space |
| FFT / spectral | periodic, constant coefficients | ideal reference, fast Poisson |
| direct sparse (LU) | $O(N^{1.5})$ fill | small 2-D, ill-conditioned, robustness |

**Preconditioning is the whole game.** Practical rules: use AMG for elliptic blocks, incomplete LU or
line-relaxation for anisotropic advection–diffusion, block preconditioners (SIMPLE, Schur complement
approximations) for coupled flow, and always monitor the *true residual*.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Krylov convergence depends on the spectrum**, not the size: clustered eigenvalues converge in a few
  iterations. Preconditioning is the art of clustering.
* **Optimal complexity**: multigrid converges in a grid-independent number of iterations for ideal
  elliptic problems — the only method that does.
* **Eigenvalue-based judgement fails for non-normal operators**: transient growth is real, and
  convergence can stall even with nice eigenvalues. Monitor residuals, not spectra.
* **Saddle-point systems** (incompressible flow, mixed formulations) need block preconditioners;
  naive diagonal preconditioning ignores the physics of the Schur complement and converges slowly.

## L4 · Practicalities

* **Start with a good discretisation**: a conservative, well-scaled operator is easier to solve than a
  badly scaled one. Non-dimensionalise variables before assembly.
* **Solve to a tolerance that matters**: inexact solves within a Newton iteration should be tightened as
  Newton converges (Eisenstat–Walker), otherwise you waste effort or lose quadratic convergence.
* **Monitor**: iterations per solve, true residual history, and the condition estimate. A change in
  iteration counts is often the first sign of a modelling or meshing bug.
* **Parallelism**: strong scaling is limited by preconditioner scalability; multigrid coarse solves and
  domain-decomposition coarse spaces are the usual bottlenecks.
* **Benchmarks**: 2-D/3-D Poisson with high contrast, the SPE10 permeability field, and lid-driven
  cavity at increasing Reynolds number ([V&V](12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).

## Checks

1. Why is CG inappropriate for advection-dominated transport? *(The matrix is nonsymmetric.)*
2. Why does geometric multigrid beat Krylov methods on elliptic problems? *(Grid-independent iteration
   count: $O(N)$ total work.)*

## See also

[CFL & stiffness](02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md) ·
[Mesh, adaptivity & parallel decomposition](08-mesh-adaptivity-moving-domains-and-parallel.md) ·
[Structure-preserving discretisations](13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)

\newpage

# Nonlinear solvers & continuation


# Nonlinear solvers & continuation

> **One-line picture.** Nonlinear transport problems are solved by repeatedly solving linear ones — and
> the difference between a working code and a failing one is usually the *globalisation*, not the speed.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [linear solvers](06-linear-solvers-and-preconditioning.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [variational inequalities & hysteresis](../06-beyond/12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md),
> [inverse problems](10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)

## L1 · Newton and its family

For $\mathbf F(\mathbf u)=0$, Newton solves $\mathbf J(\mathbf u^k)\,\delta=-\mathbf F(\mathbf u^k)$ and
updates $\mathbf u^{k+1}=\mathbf u^k+\delta$: quadratic convergence near a solution, fragile far from one.

| Variant | Idea | When to use |
|---|---|---|
| Newton | full Jacobian | fast, smooth problems, good initial guess |
| Newton–Krylov (JFNK) | Jacobian-free matrix–vector products | Jacobian impractical to form |
| damped Newton / line search | shrink the step if the residual does not drop | near-singular or strongly nonlinear |
| trust region | bound the step, adapt the model | ill-conditioned, unreliable Jacobians |
| Picard / fixed-point | iterate on the linearised operator | mildly nonlinear, cheap, linear convergence |
| pseudo-transient continuation | add a time term to drive to steady state | steady solves with bad initial guesses |
| Gauss–Newton / Levenberg | nonlinear least squares | inverse problems, data fitting |

## L2 · Continuation: make the problem easy first

Instead of solving the hard problem directly, solve a sequence of easier ones:

$$\mathbf F(\mathbf u,\lambda)=0\quad\text{with }\lambda:0\to1\ \text{in steps}
\qquad(\text{parameter or homotopy continuation}).$$

Applications: ramping the Rayleigh number in convection, ramping reaction rates from zero, ramping
nonlinear diffusion from the linear case, or a "natural continuation" in a pseudo-time variable. If the
solution branch folds (saddle-node bifurcation), pseudospectral arc-length continuation follows the
branch around the fold — essential for ignition/extinction problems
([kinetics](../04-interaction/01-reaction-kinetics-and-sinks.md)).

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Local quadratic convergence** for smooth $\mathbf F$ and nonsingular $\mathbf J$; superlinear for
  quasi-Newton. Convergence *radius* is problem-specific and shrinks with nonlinearity.
* **Inexact Newton** retains superlinear convergence if the linear tolerance is tightened with the
  residual norm (Eisenstat–Walker) — the practical basis of JFNK.
* **Continuation exists when the solution manifold does not fold**; at folds, the Jacobian is singular and
  you need arc-length parameterisation. Distinguishing a fold from a *failure to converge* is a key skill.
* **Discrete schemes can break Newton.** Limiters, phase changes and complementarity constraints make
  $\mathbf F$ non-differentiable; use semismooth Newton, active-set strategies, or smooth regularisation.

## L4 · Practicalities

* **Non-dimensionalise and scale unknowns** before assembling residuals: badly scaled variables destroy
  line searches.
* **Always monitor the residual norm, its reduction per iteration and the Jacobian conditioning**.
  Divergence with a decreasing residual usually means a bad linear tolerance; oscillation means a bad
  Jacobian or a genuinely non-differentiable scheme.
* **Warm-start** from the previous time step; for steady problems use pseudo-transient continuation with
  CFL-type ramping (a trick imported from aerodynamics).
* **Benchmarks**: Bratu problem, lid-driven cavity at increasing Reynolds number, and reactive
  steady-state ignition curves ([V&V](12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Automatic differentiation for exact Jacobians; learned preconditioners; robust solvers
  for non-smooth multiphysics.

## Checks

1. Why is JFNK attractive for multiphysics? *(No analytic Jacobian needed: matrix–vector products suffice,
   and preconditioners can be physics-based approximations.)*
2. What does a fold mean for continuation? *(The Jacobian is singular; you must parameterise by
   arc-length to track the branch.)*

## See also

[Linear solvers & preconditioning](06-linear-solvers-and-preconditioning.md) ·
[Variational inequalities & hysteresis](../06-beyond/12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md) ·
[Inverse problems & data assimilation](10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)

\newpage

# Mesh, adaptivity & parallel decomposition


# Mesh, adaptivity & parallel decomposition

> **One-line picture.** Transport lives in thin regions: fronts, boundary layers, interfaces, plumes.
> Resolution must follow the action — and the work must be split so that the splits look seamless.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [discretisation & stability](01-discretization-consistency-stability-convergence.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [particle & Monte Carlo methods](09-particle-and-monte-carlo-methods.md),
> [structure-preserving discretisations](13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)

## L1 · Where the difficulty goes

* **Boundary layers** need wall-normal clustering ($10^{2}$–$10^{4}$ grid stretch), not uniform refinement.
* **Shock and reaction fronts** are $O(1)$–$O(10)$ cells thick regardless of domain size: adaptive
  refinement (AMR) or moving meshes are the only affordable answers.
* **Interfaces** (phase change, free surfaces, faults) need either body-fitted meshes (accurate, hard to
  remesh) or embedded methods (easy, with cut-cell stability problems).
* **Multiphase plume fronts** in groundwater can require refinement where nothing is happening yet,
  because the plume will arrive later: error estimators must be predictive, not reactive.

## L2 · The techniques

| Technique | Idea | Prerequisite |
|---|---|---|
| $h$-refinement (AMR) | split cells flagged by an error indicator | hierarchical mesh (quadtree/octree) + hanging-node handling |
| $p$-refinement | raise the polynomial degree locally | high-order element library |
| $r$-adaptivity / moving mesh | move nodes toward the action | mesh motion PDE + remapping |
| ALE | mesh moves with the interface, bulk stays Eulerian | conservative remapping between meshes |
| immersed boundary | fixed grid, forces/blocks applied near the interface | stable cut-cell or regularisation |
| overset / Chimera | multiple overlapping grids | interpolation in the overlap region |

**Error indicators** are the heart of adaptivity: gradient- or curvature-based (cheap), residual-based
(justified for FEM), goal-oriented/adjoint-based (targets a specific output quantity — the most
powerful and most expensive). For transport, the interesting outputs (breakthrough time, peak
concentration) are *not* well targeted by gradient indicators, so adjoint methods pay off.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Convergence and optimality**: adaptive FEM converges at the optimal rate for the true error when the
  indicator is equivalent to the error (reliability and efficiency); this theory exists for elliptic
  problems and is partly open for transport-dominated cases.
* **Conservation across refinement interfaces** requires flux matching and careful hanging-node
  treatment, or mass leaks. Refinement conservatively and test it by integrating a tracer.
* **Remapping must be conservative and monotone** (donor-cell, or high-order with limiters); otherwise
  moving meshes destroy positivity and mass — a classic ALE failure.
* **Parallel partition quality** determines cost: load balance, communication volume and the halo depth
  (which must match the stencil width) are the knobs.

## L4 · Practicalities

* **Domain decomposition (MPI)** with halo/ghost cells: exchange before each stencil evaluation; use
  non-blocking sends and combine messages to reduce latency. For a 5-point stencil, one layer of ghosts
  suffices; a WENO5 stencil needs three.
* **Partition once, communicate often**: use graph partitioners (METIS/ParMETIS) and keep partitions
  fixed unless load changes.
* **Time-step synchronisation** is the hidden cost of local refinement in explicit schemes: refined
  regions need smaller $\Delta t$ (sub-cycling) or the whole simulation slows down.
* **Always test parallel correctness**: identical results on 1, 2 and 8 ranks (bitwise if possible),
  conservation across partitions, and weak/strong scaling curves.
* **Benchmarks**: the SPE10 field for AMG/AMR, lid-driven cavity with AMR, and shock diffraction
  ([V&V](12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).

## Checks

1. What must be exchanged at partition boundaries, and how wide? *(Ghost/halo values, as wide as the
   stencil requires.)*
2. Why does local refinement slow explicit simulations? *(The time step follows the smallest cell,
   unless sub-cycling is implemented.)*

## See also

[Linear solvers & preconditioning](06-linear-solvers-and-preconditioning.md) ·
[Accretion & surface growth](../04-interaction/11-accretion-ablation-and-surface-growth.md) ·
[Structure-preserving discretisations](13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)

\newpage

# Particle & Monte Carlo methods


# Particle & Monte Carlo methods

> **One-line picture.** Represent the solution by particles that move — transport becomes motion, and
> the error becomes statistical.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [discretisation & stability](01-discretization-consistency-stability-convergence.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [lattice-Boltzmann & particle kinetics](../05-kinetic/06-lattice-boltzmann-and-particle-kinetics.md),
> [inverse problems](10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)

## L1 · Why particles

Particle methods win when (i) the dimensionality is high, (ii) the domain moves or changes topology,
(iii) the physics is naturally stochastic, or (iv) advection dominates so strongly that a mesh-based
convective term would be pure numerical diffusion. They lose when you need high accuracy in a low-dimensional
smooth problem, or when you need exact conservation.

| Method | Particle represents | Transport implemented by |
|---|---|---|
| random walk / Brownian dynamics | probability mass | random displacements + drift |
| particle tracking | fluid parcels / tracer mass | integrating $\dot{\mathbf x}=\mathbf u+\text{noise}$ |
| SPH | field values at moving nodes | kernel interpolation, symmetric forces |
| DSMC | molecules | ballistic flight + stochastic collisions |
| PIC | phase-space distribution | pushing particles, depositing moments to a grid |
| MCMC | samples of a distribution | rejection/acceptance moves |
| multi-level MC | nested sample sets | averaging across mesh levels |

## L2 · The error law and how to beat it

The central fact: Monte Carlo error decays as $N^{-1/2}$ *independently of dimension* — the reason
particles are competitive in $d\ge4$ and hopeless in $d=1$ (where a spectral method gains orders of
magnitude more per unit work).

Ways to do better:

* **Variance reduction**: antithetic variates, control variates, importance sampling, stratification.
* **Quasi-Monte Carlo**: low-discrepancy sequences give nearly $O(N^{-1})$ for smooth integrands.
* **Multi-level Monte Carlo**: compute most samples on coarse grids, few on fine — reduces cost for a
  given error by orders of magnitude in random-media problems.
* **Hybridisation**: use particles only where they are needed (near shocks or interfaces) and a
  continuum solver elsewhere, with a conservative, low-noise interface.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Convergence in distribution, not pointwise**: particle methods approximate weak solutions; individual
  trajectories are not the answer, statistics are.
* **Consistency of the stochastic process with the PDE**: random walks with the right drift and variance
  converge to the corresponding Fokker–Planck solution (the basis of Monte Carlo diffusion solvers).
* **Conservation is statistical, not exact**: mass is conserved only in the mean, so long simulations need
  occasional resampling or weight control.
* **Stiffness appears as rare events**: escape and reaction events are exponentially unlikely, so the
  relevant statistics cannot be obtained by brute force
  ([Fokker–Planck & Kramers](../05-kinetic/03-fokker-planck-and-kramers.md)).

## L4 · Practicalities

* **Report the statistical error bar**, and use block averaging or autocorrelation analysis for
  time-correlated samples.
* **Seed and sample-size reproducibility** matter: specify the RNG and the number of realisations, and
  use common random numbers for comparisons between model variants.
* **Neighbour search dominates the cost** in SPH/DSMC/molecular dynamics: cell lists, Verlet lists and
  spatial sorting are essential ([parallel decomposition](08-mesh-adaptivity-moving-domains-and-parallel.md)).
* **Benchmarks**: the random-walk diffusion constant, DSMC shock structure, SPH dam break, and MCMC
  sampling of a known distribution ([V&V](12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Coupling to learned proposals (MCMC with neural surrogates); particle methods for
  nonlocal and fractional operators; differentiable particle simulations.

## Checks

1. Why do particle methods beat grid methods in high dimensions? *(The error is dimension-independent.)*
2. Why must you report a statistical error bar? *(The estimator is random: $N^{-1/2}$ with a prefactor
   that depends on variance, not just on $N$.)*

## See also

[Lattice-Boltzmann & particle kinetics](../05-kinetic/06-lattice-boltzmann-and-particle-kinetics.md) ·
[Stochastic PDEs & random media](../06-beyond/07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md) ·
[Anomalous diffusion](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md)

\newpage

# Inverse problems & data assimilation


# Inverse problems & data assimilation

> **One-line picture.** Forward problems smooth; inverse problems amplify. Every measurement noise
> becomes a reconstruction artefact unless you regularise — and every claim needs an uncertainty.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [PDE taxonomy & well-posedness](../01-foundations/04-pde-taxonomy-and-boundary-conditions.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [machine learning & operator learning](11-machine-learning-and-operator-learning.md),
> [measurement & experiments](../10-appendix/05-measurement-and-experiments.md)

## L1 · Why inversion is hard

Determining $D(\mathbf x)$, a source term, or an initial condition from concentration measurements is
typically **ill-posed**: small data errors produce large parameter errors, many parameter fields explain
the data equally well, and the solution may not depend continuously on the data. Classic demonstration:
the backward heat equation, which is the Fourier face of ill-posedness
([spectral view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md)).

The practical consequences in transport are well known: fitting a dispersion coefficient to a
preasymptotic plume gives a length-dependent "coefficient"; fitting a Soret coefficient without
controlling for Dufour gives a mixture.

## L2 · The main approaches

| Approach | Formulation | Typical use |
|---|---|---|
| Tikhonov / ridge | $\min\|A\mathbf m-\mathbf d\|^2+\lambda\|\mathbf m-\mathbf m_0\|^2$ | smooth parameter fields |
| total variation | $\ell^1$ of gradients | sharp interfaces (layered media) |
| truncated SVD / L-curve | discard small singular directions | diagnosing resolution |
| adjoint gradient descent | compute $\nabla_{\mathbf m}J$ by solving one adjoint PDE | large-scale inversion, 4D-Var |
| ensemble Kalman (EnKF) | propagate an ensemble, update with data | sequential data assimilation |
| particle filter | weighted samples of the posterior | strongly nonlinear dynamics |
| Bayesian MCMC / variational inference | posterior $p(\mathbf m\vert \mathbf d)$ | full uncertainty quantification |
| deep-learning surrogates | amortise the forward solve | real-time control, repeating inversions |

**Adjoint methods** deserve emphasis: one linear solve of the adjoint equation gives the gradient of the
objective with respect to *all* parameters — the reason industrial reservoir and atmospheric inversions
are feasible at all. They also expose the same numerical hazards as the forward problem (see below).

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Regularisation restores well-posedness**, and the choice of $\lambda$ encodes prior information: it is
  a modelling decision, not a numerical knob.
* **Resolution analysis is possible and necessary**: the resolution matrix $R$ (how much of each true
  parameter appears in the estimate) and the model covariance tell you what the data can and cannot
  resolve. Report them.
* **Adjoints invert the *discrete* equations, not the PDE**: a nonconservative forward scheme produces a
  biased gradient. Code the adjoint of the code, and verify gradients against finite differences.
* **Uncertainty quantification needs a prior and a likelihood**: point estimates without intervals are
  not scientific claims, especially for exponential sensitivities (Arrhenius, Kramers escape).

## L4 · Practicalities

* **Always verify the gradient** (Taylor test / finite-difference check) before running a large
  optimisation; gradient errors are the most common cause of "the inversion does not work".
* **Choose the regulariser by the physics**: layered media and fractures need sparsity-promoting
  penalties, not smoothness.
* **Multi-scale and gradient-based coarse-to-fine strategies** beat brute-force inversion for
  heterogeneous fields.
* **Report predictive uncertainty** by propagating the posterior through the forward model, not by
  quoting the parameter uncertainty alone.
* **Benchmarks**: the 1-D tracer column inversion, the Henry problem parameter identification, and
  standard 4D-Var/EnKF testbeds (Lorenz-63/96)
  ([V&V](12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Physics-informed and operator-learning surrogates inside inversion loops; inversion of
  unstable systems ("inverting the arrow of time" with learned priors); joint inversion of coupled
  physics (heat, mass, mechanics).

## Checks

1. Why does fitting a Fickian $D$ to a preasymptotic plume give a length-dependent coefficient? *(The
   model is wrong in that regime: the fit absorbs the model error into the parameter.)*
2. What does one adjoint solve give you? *(The gradient of the objective with respect to all parameters.)*
3. Why must the adjoint match the discrete forward scheme? *(Otherwise the computed gradient is not the
   gradient of the discrete objective, and optimisation stalls.)*

## See also

[Measurement & experiments](../10-appendix/05-measurement-and-experiments.md) ·
[Machine learning & operator learning](11-machine-learning-and-operator-learning.md) ·
[Sorption & retardation](../04-interaction/07-sorption-and-retardation.md) ·
[Wells, injection & breakthrough](../04-interaction/12-wells-injection-efflux-and-breakthrough.md)

\newpage

# Machine learning & operator learning


# Machine learning & operator learning

> **One-line picture.** Learning can approximate solution *operators*, accelerate expensive sub-models, or
> discover closures — but transport has structural properties (conservation, positivity, causality) that
> a naive network will happily violate.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [inverse problems & data assimilation](10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [machine learning & data bridges](../08-bridges/07-machine-learning-and-data.md),
> [verification & benchmarks](12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)

## L1 · Four distinct uses (do not blur them)

| Use | What is learned | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Surrogate / emulator** | map from parameters to outputs | replace a reservoir simulator in an optimisation loop |
| **Operator learning** | map from *functions* to functions (PDE solution operators) | DeepONet, Fourier Neural Operator (FNO), graph neural operators |
| **Physics-informed solving** | the solution itself, constrained by residuals | PINNs for inverse problems with sparse data |
| **Closure / subgrid modelling** | unresolved terms from resolved ones | turbulence, subgrid dispersion, kinetic closures |

These have very different accuracy requirements: surrogates need interpolation accuracy, operator learners
need generalisation across parameter families, closures need *structural* consistency (conservation,
realisability) — the hardest bar.

## L2 · What works, and what does not

**Works well:**
* Spectral-bias-free representations for smooth operator families (FNO-type models) on regular grids.
* Acceleration of repeated many-query problems: design optimisation, UQ, control, real-time forecasting.
* Learned closures *with* hard physical constraints: divergence-free architectures, conservative
  parameterisations, or learning the *correction* to a trusted closure.

**Works poorly (or requires care):**
* Extrapolation to unseen regimes (a network trained at $\mathrm{Re}=10^3$ does not know turbulence at
  $10^6$).
* Translation-dominated advection: generic architectures struggle with the exact translation symmetry
  that characteristics-based methods give for free
  ([advection](../02-bulk/01-advection.md)).
* Long-horizon rollouts: small errors compound; energy can grow or decay spuriously unless the model is
  structurally stable.
* Sharp fronts and shocks: spectral bias smooths what must remain discontinuous — combine with limiters
  or characteristic-aware layers ([limiters](04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md)).

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees (and does not)

* **Universal approximation is not generalisation.** Approximating on the training distribution says
  nothing about behaviour outside it; error bounds for operator learning exist but require assumptions on
  the operator family.
* **Symmetry and conservation must be built in.** Equivariant/graph architectures and constrained losses
  help; hard constraints (projection to divergence-free fields, conservative flux forms) help more.
* **PINNs are an inverse-problem tool**, not a replacement for solvers: they shine with sparse data and
  unknown parameters, and struggle with stiff, multiscale or high-frequency transport unless the
  architecture encodes the asymptotics.
* **Data assimilation meets learning**: learned models act as priors/regularisers in inversion, which is
  why the same "residual + data" loss appears in both communities
  ([inverse problems](10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)).

## L4 · Practicalities

* **Judge by extrapolation, stability and conservation**, not by offline test error: report long rollouts,
  mass/energy drift, and performance on a held-out physical regime.
* **Structure-preserving hybridisation** is the pragmatic route: keep the trusted solver, learn the
  expensive or under-resolved piece, and enforce the invariants explicitly.
* **Data efficiency**: use simulation-generated data, exploit symmetries, and start from a numerical
  baseline so the network learns a correction rather than the whole physics.
* **Benchmarks**: Burgers, Darcy flow with random coefficients, Navier–Stokes at moderate $\mathrm{Re}$,
  and turbulence closures with reported *a posteriori* metrics
  ([V&V](12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Learned closures that provably respect the second law; operator learning on unstructured
  meshes and spheres; differentiable simulation; foundation models for multiphysics.

## Checks

1. Why do generic networks struggle with advection? *(Exact translation symmetry is hard to represent;
   characteristics-aware designs or spectral layers are needed.)*
2. What must a learned turbulence closure preserve to be usable? *(Conservation, realisability and
   correct scaling — otherwise long simulations drift.)*

## See also

[Machine learning & data](../08-bridges/07-machine-learning-and-data.md) ·
[Inverse problems & data assimilation](10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md) ·
[Operator & equation catalogue](../10-appendix/03-operator-and-equation-catalog.md)

\newpage

# Verification, validation & benchmarks


# Verification, validation & benchmarks

> **One-line picture.** Verification asks "am I solving the equations right?"; validation asks "am I
> solving the right equations?". They are different questions with different tools, and confusing them
> wastes years.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [discretisation & stability](01-discretization-consistency-stability-convergence.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [measurement & experiments](../10-appendix/05-measurement-and-experiments.md)

## L1 · The two questions

* **Code verification**: does the implementation solve the discrete equations it claims to solve? Tools:
  unit tests of operators, **method of manufactured solutions (MMS)** — pick an analytic solution,
  substitute it into the PDE to obtain a source term, and check the *observed order of accuracy* under
  refinement.
* **Solution verification**: how large is the numerical error in this particular run? Tools: grid
  convergence index (GCI, Roache), Richardson extrapolation, and a posteriori error estimators. Report
  the uncertainty, not just the answer.
* **Validation**: do the equations (with their empirical parameters) reproduce measured reality, within
  stated uncertainties? Tools: comparison with experiments, calibration, and acceptance criteria defined
  *before* running.
* **Calibration vs validation**: fitting parameters to data and then "validating" on the same data proves
  nothing. Hold out data.

## L2 · Benchmarks you can rely on

| Physics | Benchmark | What it tests |
|---|---|---|
| advection | Gaussian advection, Zalesak disk | dissipation, dispersion, symmetry |
| shocks | Sod, Lax, double Mach reflection | conservation, entropy, limiters |
| convection | Rayleigh–Bénard onset and $\mathrm{Nu}(\mathrm{Ra})$ | coupling, buoyancy, resolution |
| porous flow | Henry, McWhorter–Sunada, SPE10 | density coupling, heterogeneity, upscaling |
| poroelasticity | Mandel, Terzaghi, Barry–Mercer | coupling stability, consolidation |
| phase change | Stefan similarity, gallium melting | free-boundary accuracy, latent heat |
| turbulence | channel at $\mathrm{Re}_\tau\approx180$, decaying isotropic | spectra, wall laws |
| kinetic | Couette/DSMC across $\mathrm{Kn}$, shock structure | kinetic limits, slip |
| radiation | Marshak wave, plane-parallel albedo | asymptotic preservation |
| reactive transport | column breakthrough with retardation | sorption, dispersion, kinetics |

The rule for using a benchmark: state the metric, the tolerance and the acceptance criterion *first*;
otherwise "looks fine" replaces verification.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Observed order of accuracy** in $h$ (or better, GCI) is the single most informative diagnostic: it
  detects bugs, boundary-closure errors, and order reduction from nonlinearity or limiters.
* **Conservation audits** are cheap and decisive: integrate fluxes over the boundary and compare with the
  stored mass change to machine precision (up to boundary-condition error).
* **Symmetry and invariance tests** (translation, rotation, Galilean invariance) detect subtle indexing
  and discretisation errors that convergence studies can miss.
* **Sensitivity to parameters must be reported**: a validation claim without parameter uncertainties is
  not a validation.

## L4 · Practicalities

* **Automate**: a test suite that runs quick MMS and conservation checks on every commit catches the
  majority of real bugs.
* **Keep the "digital twin" honest**: log solver tolerances, CFL numbers, iteration counts and error
  indicators with every result so that anomalies can be diagnosed later.
* **Distinguish code, solution and model error** explicitly in reports; reviewers will ask.
* **Cross-code comparison** (different codes, same benchmark) is the strongest verification available
  when no analytic solution exists.
* **Frontiers.** Automated verification and formal methods; uncertainty-aware validation criteria (model
  form error); benchmarks for learned surrogates and closures.

## Checks

1. What is the difference between verification and validation? *(Solving the equations right versus
   solving the right equations.)*
2. Why is MMS the gold standard for code verification? *(It provides an exact solution for arbitrary
   operators, so the observed order can be measured for every term.)*

## See also

[Discretisation & stability](01-discretization-consistency-stability-convergence.md) ·
[Measurement & experiments](../10-appendix/05-measurement-and-experiments.md) ·
[Benchmarks across the atlas](../10-appendix/04-key-derivations.md)

\newpage

# Structure-preserving discretisations


# Structure-preserving discretisations

> **One-line picture.** Instead of hoping a scheme behaves, engineer it so the discrete equations satisfy
> the *theorems*: conservation, a maximum principle, an entropy inequality.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [discretisation & stability](01-discretization-consistency-stability-convergence.md),
> [entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [limiters, TVD & WENO](04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md),
> [linear solvers](06-linear-solvers-and-preconditioning.md)

## L1 · What "structure" means here

A conservative PDE has invariants and inequalities. Structure-preserving (or *mimetic*, *compatible*,
*geometric*) discretisations reproduce them exactly on the discrete level:

| Structure | Discrete requirement | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| conservation | flux form with one flux per face | telescoping sums: exact mass balance |
| duality | discrete divergence $=$ negative adjoint of gradient | symmetric matrices, correct null space |
| maximum principle | monotone stencil (M-matrix) | positivity, boundedness |
| entropy inequality | discrete entropy flux/ dissipation | nonlinear stability |
| energy/kinetic-energy conservation | skew-symmetric convection | honest turbulence statistics |
| symplecticity / time reversal | symplectic or reversible integrators | long-time fidelity in Hamiltonian limits |
| curl/div identities | discrete de Rham complex | $\nabla\!\cdot\!\nabla\times=0$ exactly (MHD, EM) |
| well-balancedness | hydrostatic states preserved | lakes at rest, stratified flow, gravity balances |

## L2 · How it is achieved

* **Finite volume** gives conservation automatically; add limiters for positivity
  ([limiters](04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md)), and well-balanced source discretisations for gravity.
* **Mixed/hybrid FEM** uses compatible element pairs so that the discrete gradient, divergence and curl
  are adjoints — the reason staggered (MAC) grids work so well for incompressible flow.
* **Summation-by-parts (SBP) + SAT** operators provide discrete integration by parts with weak boundary
  conditions, giving provable stability; add entropy-conservative fluxes for nonlinear stability.
* **Convex splitting and JKO-type schemes** make gradient flows energy-monotone
  ([optimal transport](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md)).
* **Symplectic integrators** preserve the Hamiltonian structure of the reversible part, which is why
  long-time molecular dynamics does not drift.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Discrete conservation is exact**, not asymptotic: mass errors are zero to round-off, which is the only
  standard acceptable for climate, reservoir and reactive transport.
* **Entropy stability implies nonlinear stability**: with a discrete entropy inequality you get a bound
  independent of the grid, without any linearisation.
* **Positivity and bounds come from monotonicity**, which is a nonlinear property that linear stability
  analysis cannot see.
* **Compatibility prevents spurious modes**: exactly satisfying discrete vector identities removes the
  checkerboard pressures and divergence drifts of naive discretisations.
* **The cost is design complexity**: structure-preserving schemes are harder to implement, may limit
  attainable order, and often need special boundary treatments.

## L4 · Practicalities

* **Start from the invariants you care about** and choose the scheme to preserve them: mass first,
  then positivity, then entropy.
* **Test the structure directly**: run conservation and bound checks as unit tests, not as
  post-hoc analysis.
* **Combine structures**: SBP + limiters + well-balanced sources is the modern recipe for geophysical
  transport.
* **Beware of mixed-order errors:** preserving a structure at second order while using a fourth-order
  interior flux requires matched interface treatments.
* **Benchmarks**: hydrostatic balance tests, conservation audits over long integrations, posivity tests
  for reactive transport, and entropy production checks in turbulent flows
  ([V&V](12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Entropy-stable schemes for MHD and relativistic flows; compatible discretisations on
  unstructured/polyhedral meshes; structure-preserving learned discretisations.

## Checks

1. Why does a flux-form finite-volume scheme conserve exactly? *(Summed face fluxes telescope; interior
   fluxes cancel while boundary fluxes match the stored mass change.)*
2. What does the discrete de Rham complex prevent? *(Spurious modes and divergence/curl drift in
   Maxwell/MHD and incompressible flow.)*

## See also

[Linear solvers & preconditioning](06-linear-solvers-and-preconditioning.md) ·
[Limiters, TVD & WENO](04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md) ·
[Entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md) ·
[Optimal transport & gradient flows](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md)

# Bridges to other fields


# Bridges to other fields

This is why the atlas exists: transport theory is not one field's tool, it is a common language. Each note
here follows the same recipe — *name the conserved quantity and the flux law, then say what the field
calls them and what goes wrong there*.

| # | Note | Where it lands |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | [Heat–mass–momentum analogy & circuit models](01-analogy-and-circuit-models.md) | engineering practice; resistance networks; Chilton–Colburn |
| 2 | [Finance & economics](02-finance-and-economics.md) | Black–Scholes as backward diffusion; mean-field games |
| 3 | [Biology & medicine](03-biology-and-medicine.md) | oxygen limits, morphogens, tumours, drug delivery, nerves |
| 4 | [Geoscience & climate](04-geoscience-and-climate.md) | mantle convection, aquifers, sea ice, radiative–convective balance |
| 5 | [Plasma & astrophysics](05-plasma-and-astrophysics.md) | kinetic and MHD transport; accretion; cosmic rays |
| 6 | [Engineering devices](06-engineering-devices.md) | batteries, reactors, heat exchangers, chips, additive manufacturing |
| 7 | [Machine learning & data](07-machine-learning-and-data.md) | diffusion models, graph diffusion, optimal transport in ML |
| 8 | [Images & signals](08-images-and-signals.md) | blurring as heat flow, anisotropic diffusion, level-set segmentation |
| 9 | [Networks, society & traffic](09-networks-society-and-traffic.md) | traffic jams, epidemics, opinion spread, PageRank |
| 10 | [Quantum & condensed matter](10-quantum-and-condensed-matter.md) | ballistic phonons, hydrodynamic electrons, second sound |
| 11 | [One mathematics of transport](11-unifying-mathematics-of-transport.md) | the closing argument: it is all one subject |

![**Figure G.** The concept map of the whole atlas. Follow the colours to see which level a topic first
becomes useful, and follow the arrows to see which ideas are prerequisites for which.](concept-map.pdf)

## What transfers, and what does not

**Transfers beautifully:**
* The balance law and the divergence theorem — universal.
* Dimensionless analysis and similarity solutions — universal.
* The structure of the equations (elliptic/parabolic/hyperbolic, local/nonlocal) — universal.
* Numerical methods, stability theory and inversion — universal.
* Entropy/energy arguments — universal *provided* you identify the right functional and metric.

**Does not transfer automatically:**
* Constitutive laws (a "diffusion coefficient" in finance is not measured by a diaphragm cell).
* Boundary conditions (a Dirichlet condition is a modelling claim about a real interface).
* Conservation (in social and financial systems, quantities are only conserved by construction).
* Values of parameters: the dimensionless numbers may be astronomically different.

> **A useful test before using an analogy:** can you write down what is conserved, what drives it, and
> what the boundary does — in the new field's own terms? If not, the analogy is decorative.

\newpage

# Heat–mass–momentum analogy & circuit models


# Heat–mass–momentum analogy & circuit models

> **One-line picture.** Fourier, Fick and Newton are the same equation with different words. Once you
> see that, every linear steady transport problem becomes a resistor network.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [constitutive relations](../01-foundations/03-constitutive-relations.md),
> [boundary layers](../02-bulk/04-boundary-layers.md)

![**Figure 27.** Left: heat, mass and momentum side by side — one flux law, three names. Right: the circuit view, where series and parallel rules solve transport problems by inspection.](transport-analogy.pdf)

## L1 · The analogy

$$\text{flux}=-\text{(property)}\times\nabla\text{(potential)}$$

| Transport | Flux | Potential | Property | Dimensionless flux |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| heat | $\mathbf q$ | $T$ | $k$, $\alpha$ | $\mathrm{Nu}$ |
| mass | $\mathbf J$ | $c$ | $D$ | $\mathrm{Sh}$ |
| momentum | $\boldsymbol\tau$ | $u$ | $\mu$, $\nu$ | $C_f$ |

The analogy becomes quantitative through **Chilton–Colburn**: for the same geometry and flow, the
dimensionless transfer coefficients are equal up to a weak power of the ratio of diffusivities:

$$j_H=\frac{\mathrm{Nu}}{\mathrm{Re}\,\mathrm{Pr}^{1/3}}\approx j_D=\frac{\mathrm{Sh}}{\mathrm{Re}\,\mathrm{Sc}^{1/3}}\approx \frac{C_f}{2},$$

valid when $\mathrm{Pr}$, $\mathrm{Sc}\sim O(1)$ and there is no form drag or blowing. Consequences:
measure one coefficient, predict the other two; calibrate a heat-transfer experiment and use it for a
drying or dissolution problem.

Where the analogy fails is as instructive as where it works: separated flow and form drag (momentum
transfer is dominated by pressure, not skin friction), transpiration/blowing (mass transfer at the wall
modifies the boundary layer), and very large $\mathrm{Pr}$ or $\mathrm{Sc}$ (different layer thicknesses).

## L2 · Resistance networks and bond graphs

Steady linear transport is circuit analysis:

| Domain | "Ohm's law" | Resistance |
|---|---|---|
| thermal | $q=\Delta T/R$ | $L/(kA)$; interface $1/(hA)$ |
| electrical | $I=\Delta V/R$ | $L/(\sigma A)$ |
| hydraulic | $Q=\Delta p/R$ | $8\mu L/(\pi r^4)$ (Hagen–Poiseuille) |
| diffusive mass | $\dot m=\Delta c/R$ | $L/(DA)$ |

Series adds resistances, parallel adds conductances, and transient problems gain capacitances
($C=\rho c_pV$ for thermal, $\theta V$ for sorptive mass storage), giving first-order time constants
$\tau=RC$. This is why chemical engineers draw networks rather than solve PDEs, and why
finite-volume codes and bond graphs look structurally identical.

## L3 · Consequences worth remembering

* **The bottleneck wins**: total resistance is dominated by the largest element — usually the
  interfacial film or contact resistance, not the bulk. That is why one clean surface beats a better
  material.
* **Dimensionless transfer coefficients are transferable**: the same correlation works for a heated pipe,
  an electrode and a dissolving tablet when the geometry is similar.
* **The analogy gives order-of-magnitude estimates in minutes**, which is what you need before choosing
  a mesh.

## L4 · Numerics

* **Wall functions and correlations are the lumped models** of the network view; replacing them with
  resolved simulation is expensive and sometimes unnecessary.
* **Verification by analogy**: if your 2-D conjugate heat-transfer solver gives a $\mathrm{Nu}$ far from
  the correlation for the same $\mathrm{Re}$ and $\mathrm{Pr}$, suspect the boundary layer resolution or
  an interface error.

## Checks

1. Why is $j_H\approx j_D$ useful, and when does it fail? *(It lets one measurement predict both heat and
   mass transfer; it fails with form drag, surface blowing and extreme $\mathrm{Pr}/\mathrm{Sc}$.)*
2. Which resistance usually dominates in a heat exchanger? *(The films, not the wall — unless fouling
   adds more.)*

## See also

[Boundary layers](../02-bulk/04-boundary-layers.md) ·
[Conduction](../03-microscopic/03-conduction.md) ·
[Engineering devices](06-engineering-devices.md) ·
[Constitutive relations](../01-foundations/03-constitutive-relations.md)

# Transport in finance & economics


# Transport in finance & economics

> **One-line picture.** A price is a transported quantity: it drifts and diffuses, and its expectation can
> be computed by solving the same equations as heat flow, backwards in time.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [random walks & Langevin](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md),
> [optimal transport](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md)

## L1 · The same equations in a new costume

Model an asset price as a stochastic process $\mathrm{d}S=\mu S\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma S\,\mathrm{d}W$. The
value $V(S,t)$ of a contingent claim satisfies, by Itô's lemma plus no-arbitrage,

$$\partial_tV+\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\partial_{SS}V+rS\partial_SV-rV=0,$$

the **Black–Scholes equation**: a *backward* parabolic PDE, mathematically the diffusion equation with
drift, run backwards in time. Its Green function is the lognormal kernel; the Feynman–Kac formula turns
expectations into PDE solutions, which is why Monte Carlo and finite differences are interchangeable
pricing tools.

Correspondences worth memorising:

| Transport object | Financial object |
|---|---|
| diffusion coefficient | volatility $\sigma^2$ |
| advection velocity | risk-free drift under the risk-neutral measure |
| initial condition | terminal payoff (hence backward in time) |
| boundary condition | payoff at barriers / asymptotics in $S$ |
| backward Kolmogorov | pricing PDE (Feynman–Kac) |
| Fokker–Planck (forward) | density of prices / risk-neutral distribution |
| local vol / Dupire | state-dependent diffusivity fitted to option prices |
| optimal transport | model-independent bounds, calibration, risk measures |
| Wasserstein gradient flow | mean-field games, agent-based equilibrium dynamics |

**Dupire's equation** is the inverse-problem face: given option prices for all strikes and maturities,
recover $\sigma(S,t)$ — a coefficient-identification problem, structurally identical to inferring
$D(c)$ from breakthrough curves ([inverse problems](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)).

## L2 · Optimal transport and mean-field games

Two modern bridges:

* **Model-independent pricing**: optimal transport gives bounds on derivative prices consistent with
  observed marginal distributions (Martingale optimal transport) — the geometry of moving mass
  constrained by no-arbitrage.
* **Mean-field games**: when many interacting agents each optimise while reacting to the aggregate
  distribution, the equilibrium is a *coupled system* of a forward Fokker–Planck (transport of the
  population) and a backward Hamilton–Jacobi equation (optimal control). This is precisely the
  forward/backward structure of optimal control in transport
  ([Hamilton–Jacobi](../06-beyond/02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md)), and it is now used for
  traffic, epidemics, energy markets and crowd dynamics.

## L3 · Where the analogy is genuinely useful

* **Numerical methods transfer**: implicit schemes, spectral methods, and rare-event simulation were all
  developed in PDE transport and are standard in derivative pricing.
* **Structural insight transfers**: the absence of arbitrage is a *monotonicity/maximum-principle*
  statement; a pricing PDE that violates the maximum principle allows arbitrage in the model.
* **Inverse problems are the same species**: calibration is ill-posed, needs regularisation, and produces
  parameters that are unidentifiable in some directions — exactly as in aquifer parameter estimation.
* **Caveat**: financial models have *no physical conservation law* and no measured microscale; the
  analogy is mathematical, not physical, and the validity of a model is judged by market behaviour and
  stability, not by a second law.

## L4 · Practice

* Sharp price fronts (digital options, barriers, American exercise) reintroduce everything from
  [limiters](../07-numerics/04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md): oscillation and smearing near kinks, and the need
  for free-boundary ([obstacle](../06-beyond/12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md)) methods for
  American options.
* Long-horizon and high-dimensional problems push toward [Monte Carlo and particle methods](../07-numerics/09-particle-and-monte-carlo-methods.md)
  and, recently, [operator learning](../07-numerics/11-machine-learning-and-operator-learning.md).

## Checks

1. Why is Black–Scholes a backward equation? *(It is a conditional expectation of a terminal payoff.)*
2. What is the transport analogue of volatility? *(A state-dependent diffusion coefficient — the same
   object as $D(\mathbf x)$ in a heterogeneous medium.)*

## See also

[Random walks, Langevin & response](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md) ·
[Optimal transport & gradient flows](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md) ·
[Hamilton–Jacobi & viscosity solutions](../06-beyond/02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md) ·
[Inverse problems & data assimilation](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)
