# Transport in biology & medicine


# Transport in biology & medicine

> **One-line picture.** Life is a fight against diffusion: too slow to feed a cell body, exactly right
> for signalling, and fatal when it fails.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md),
> [reaction–diffusion & Turing](../04-interaction/04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md)

## L1 · Diffusion limits the size of life

Oxygen must reach a cell interior by diffusion when no vessels are present. With
$D\approx2\times10^{-9}$ m²/s and a need for ~1 s of supply, the accessible radius is roughly
$r\sim\sqrt{Dt}\approx50$–$200$ µm. That is the scale of a capillary spacing, of an avascular tumour's
necrotic core, and of the reason insects can breathe by tubes (tracheae) but not grow large like
vertebrates. **Diffusion sets the plumbing.**

The same scaling appears in drug delivery (diffusion of a drug out of a polymer implant, or into a
biofilm), in tissue engineering (how thick a scaffold can be), and in pharmacokinetics (compartmental
models are just lumped resistance–capacitance networks —
[circuit models](01-analogy-and-circuit-models.md)).

## L2 · Four biological transport mechanisms worth knowing

| Mechanism | Equations | Role |
|---|---|---|
| signalling by morphogens | reaction–diffusion, Turing patterns | patterning of digits, hair, stripes; robustness requires gradients of ligands/receptors |
| chemotaxis | Keller–Segel | immune recruitment, bacterial foraging, slime-mould aggregation |
| nerve conduction | cable equation (telegrapher-like) | action potentials as travelling waves in excitable media |
| electrochemical transport | Nernst–Planck + Poisson | membrane potentials, ion channels, kidneys, nerve, salt balance |
| tissue mechanics coupling | poroelasticity + transport | cartilage, bone remodelling, interstitial flow, oedema |
| blood–tissue exchange | convection–reaction in vessels + diffusion | oxygen/glucose delivery, drug distribution |

**Travelling waves in excitable media** are the reason cardiac tissue has a refractory period: fronts
become spiral waves, and spiral breakup is fibrillation. The mathematics is identical to the
[reaction–diffusion fronts](../04-interaction/05-fronts-and-travelling-waves.md) of combustion.

## L3 · Transport reasoning that changes decisions

* **Diffusion limitation predicts hypoxic cores** in tumours and hence which therapies (or which sizes of
  spheroid) are realistic.
* **Microscale Péclet numbers govern drug penetration**: in tumour interstitium, slow interstitial flow
  plus binding gives non-Fickian, retarded transport —
  [sorption & retardation](../04-interaction/07-sorption-and-retardation.md).
* **Reaction–diffusion models without parameters are useless**: the same equations with different
  rate constants give spots, stripes or travelling waves — the parameters must be estimated
  ([inverse problems](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)).
* **Anomalous diffusion is the norm inside cells** (crowding, cytoskeleton, molecular motors): measured
  exponents $\alpha<1$, ergodicity breaking, and subtle dependence on probe size
  ([anomalous diffusion](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md)).

## L4 · Practice

* **Do not measure a biological coefficient with a Fickian model if the transport is anomalous** — you
  will get a length- and time-dependent number.
* **Couple the mechanics**: growth, swelling and transport are coupled through poroelasticity; ignoring
  it produces wrong drug release and wrong tumour-induced stress predictions.
* **Use dimensionless groups for scale-up**: oxygen penetration depth, Thiele modulus (reaction vs
  diffusion in a biofilm), and Péclet numbers in vessels decide the regime.
* **Frontiers.** Single-molecule and single-cell transport in living tissue; multiscale models from
  channels to organs; mechano-transport coupling in disease; learned constitutive laws for tissue.

## Checks

1. Why can a cell not be 1 cm across without a vascular supply? *(Oxygen diffusion over 1 cm would take
   hours-to-days.)*
2. Why is the cable equation "telegrapher-like"? *(It carries a diffusive current along the axon plus a
   membrane capacitance, giving wave propagation with damping.)*

## See also

[Reaction–diffusion & Turing](../04-interaction/04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md) ·
[Chemotaxis & Keller–Segel](../04-interaction/06-chemotaxis-and-keller-segel.md) ·
[Charged & electrochemical transport](../03-microscopic/07-charged-and-electrochemical-transport.md) ·
[Anomalous diffusion](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md)

\newpage

# Transport in geoscience & climate


# Transport in geoscience & climate

> **One-line picture.** Earth is a set of coupled transport problems spanning 18 orders of magnitude in
> length, with $\mathrm{Ra}$, $\mathrm{Re}$ and $\mathrm{Pe}$ far beyond anything a laboratory can reach.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [convection & buoyancy](../02-bulk/02-convection-and-buoyancy.md),
> [porous media & Darcy](../02-bulk/08-porous-media-and-darcy.md)

## L1 · Five transport systems that make a planet

| System | Dominant transport | Characteristic numbers |
|---|---|---|
| mantle convection | solid-state creep ($\mu_{\rm eff}\sim10^{21}$ Pa·s) | $\mathrm{Ra}\sim10^{7}$–$10^{9}$, $\mathrm{Pr}\to\infty$ |
| outer core | magnetohydrodynamic convection | $\mathrm{Rm}\sim10^{3}$ (dynamo action) |
| groundwater & aquifers | Darcy flow + dispersion + sorption | $\mathrm{Pe}>10^{4}$, $\mathrm{Da}$ from $10^{-3}$ to $10^{2}$ |
| atmosphere & ocean | advection, radiation, phase change | $\mathrm{Re}\sim10^{8}$–$10^{9}$, $\mathrm{Ra}$ huge, $\mathrm{Le}\neq1$ |
| cryosphere (ice sheets, sea ice, permafrost) | nonlinear viscous flow + phase change | Stefan numbers, Glen's flow law $n\approx3$ |

## L2 · What transport theory contributes

* **Mantle convection is Rayleigh–Bénard at infinite Prandtl number** with temperature-dependent
  viscosity: an instability whose pattern (plumes versus sheets) is a *transport* question, and whose
  time scale (hundreds of millions of years) is set by the diffusion of heat through the lithosphere.
* **Groundwater**: the same Darcy/ADE machinery as engineering, with the added twist that heterogeneity
  is multiscale and often gives non-Fickian (heavy-tailed) breakthrough. Upscaling of random fields is a
  core geoscience activity ([homogenisation](../06-beyond/08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md),
  [stochastic PDEs](../06-beyond/07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md)).
* **Climate**: radiative transfer (Planck, absorption bands, scattering) plus convection sets the
  radiative-convective equilibrium profile; the atmosphere is a heat engine transporting energy from the
  tropics poleward by eddies, and the ocean transports heat by a slow thermohaline circulation. Both are
  advection–diffusion problems with strongly scale-dependent eddy diffusivities
  ([turbulence](../02-bulk/05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md)).
* **Ice**: creep of ice is a nonlinear (Glen) viscous flow, so ice sheets are *nonlinear* transport
  problems whose surface evolves as a free boundary
  ([nonlinear diffusion](../06-beyond/06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md)).
* **Permafrost and gas hydrates** combine phase change, flow and heat transport with strong feedbacks
  ([phase change & Stefan](../04-interaction/09-phase-change-and-stefan.md)).

## L3 · Transport thinking that reshapes predictions

* **Eddy diffusivity is the crux of climate prediction.** The transport of heat by unresolved eddies must
  be parameterised; failures here dominate uncertainty in climate sensitivity.
* **Groundwater models are only as good as their heterogeneity**. Effective dispersion coefficients are
  asymptotic quantities, often not reached at field scales — so "non-Fickian" is the norm, not an anomaly
  ([shear dispersion](../02-bulk/06-shear-dispersion-taylor-aris.md)).
* **Subsidence and induced seismicity** show that transport changes the solid: coupling flow with
  poroelasticity is mandatory for aquifer and reservoir management
  ([subsidence](../04-interaction/13-subsidence-and-poroelasticity.md)).
* **CO₂ storage** requires coupled multi-phase flow, dissolution, geochemistry and geomechanics — a
  pointed example of multiphysics coupling stability
  ([splitting & coupling](../07-numerics/05-operator-splitting-and-multiphysics-coupling.md)).

## L4 · Practice

* **Scale analysis before simulation**: for a 1000 km basin, $1000$ Myr, $10^{-6}$ m²/s hydraulic
  diffusivity, the transport time is set by diffusion and not by any assumed velocity.
* **Never trust a single realisation** for heterogeneous media: ensembles and UQ are required
  ([stochastic PDEs](../06-beyond/07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md)).
* **Conservation audits** are non-negotiable at climate scale: mass and energy budgets must close to
  machine precision over century-scale integrations
  ([structure-preserving schemes](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)).

## Checks

1. Why is mantle convection a Rayleigh–Bénard problem with an enormous Prandtl number? *(Inertia is
   negligible compared to viscous forces in a creeping solid — the momentum equation is essentially
   balance-dominated.)*
2. Why is "the" dispersion coefficient not a material property in an aquifer? *(Heterogeneity is
   multiscale; dispersion is an asymptotic, scale-dependent quantity.)*

## See also

[Convection & buoyancy](../02-bulk/02-convection-and-buoyancy.md) ·
[Porous media & Darcy](../02-bulk/08-porous-media-and-darcy.md) ·
[Subsidence & poroelasticity](../04-interaction/13-subsidence-and-poroelasticity.md) ·
[Homogenisation & upscaling](../06-beyond/08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md)

# Transport in plasma & astrophysics


# Transport in plasma & astrophysics

> **One-line picture.** Space is not empty and stars are not solid: astrophysical transport is kinetics,
> radiation and MHD competing on scales where the mean free path can exceed the system.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [BGK & Vlasov](../05-kinetic/02-bgk-vlasov-and-plasma-kinetics.md),
> [radiative transfer](../05-kinetic/04-radiative-transfer.md)

## L1 · The transport modes, and when each applies

| Environment | Mean free path vs size | Description needed |
|---|---|---|
| solar wind, corona | $\gg$ size along field lines | [Vlasov](../05-kinetic/02-bgk-vlasov-and-plasma-kinetics.md), kinetic instabilities |
| magnetosphere | comparable | hybrid kinetic–fluid, reconnection |
| tokamak core | small across field, huge along it | anisotropic (Braginskii) fluid + gyrokinetics |
| accretion disc (ISM) | small | MHD with turbulent stress closures |
| stellar interior | very small | radiation + convection (mixing-length closures) |
| supernova remnant | shock-dominated | MHD shocks, particle acceleration |
| neutron star, merger | quantum/general-relativistic | relativistic MHD, neutrino transport |

The organising principle is the same as everywhere in this atlas: **compare a mean free path (or
collision time) with the system size (or time) and choose the description accordingly.** The
astrophysical twist is that the comparison often comes out "neither", which is why hybrid and
asymptotic-preserving methods dominate the field.

## L2 · Three transport problems that organise the subject

* **Accretion discs.** Angular momentum must be transported outward for matter to spiral inward. Micro
  viscosity is far too small, so the transport is turbulent and described by a stress closure
  ($\alpha$-disc, or magnetorotational-instability-driven MHD turbulence). This is *exactly* an
  eddy-viscosity problem ([turbulence](../02-bulk/05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md)) with the same
  scientific status (and the same caveats) as engineering closures.
* **Cosmic-ray transport.** Charged particles scatter on magnetic turbulence, diffusing with an
  energy-dependent $D(E)$; the advection–diffusion equation in *momentum* space becomes transport in
  phase space, producing spectral features observed at Earth.
* **Radiative transport in stars and atmospheres.** Photon diffusion in the interior (Rosseland limit) and
  line transfer in atmospheres (full RTE) determine stellar structure and the spectra we interpret —
  and in supernovae and neutron-star mergers the neutrino version determines the explosion and the
  elements produced ([radiative transfer](../05-kinetic/04-radiative-transfer.md)).

## L3 · Ideas that transfer back to engineering

* **Anisotropy is the rule**: transport coefficients along and across magnetic field lines differ by
  orders of magnitude, so tensor coefficients dominate the physics — as in layered porous media.
* **Nonlocal transport**: photon and neutrino transport, and non-local heat flux in laser plasmas, cannot
  be represented by a local gradient law at all.
* **Kinetic dissipation without collisions**: Landau damping shows that "dissipation" can be phase-space
  transport ([BGK & Vlasov](../05-kinetic/02-bgk-vlasov-and-plasma-kinetics.md)).
* **Instability-driven transport**: most astrophysical transport coefficients are set by instabilities
  rather than molecular properties, so they must be *computed*, not looked up.

## L4 · Practice

* **Never use a continuum code outside its mean-free-path validity**, especially across shocks and in low
  density plasma: the kinetic correction dominates the shock structure.
* **Conserve and constrain**: divergence cleaning for magnetic fields, exact conservation for
  relativistic solvers, and careful treatment of the conservative-to-primitive inversion.
* **Benchmarks**: Landau damping rates, the Orszag–Tang vortex, Blondin reconnection tests, and
  relativistic shock tubes ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).
* **Frontiers.** Kinetic turbulence in the solar wind; reconnection in relativistic plasmas;
  neutrino transport in mergers; dynamo action across planetary and stellar regimes.

## Checks

1. Why is accretion-disc transport a closure problem? *(Micro viscosity is far too weak to move angular
   momentum; turbulent stress must be modelled.)*
2. What determines whether you need kinetics or MHD? *(The mean free path and gyro-radius compared with
   the gradient scale lengths.)*

## See also

[BGK & Vlasov / plasma kinetics](../05-kinetic/02-bgk-vlasov-and-plasma-kinetics.md) ·
[Radiation & neutron transport](../05-kinetic/04-radiative-transfer.md) ·
[MHD & relativistic transport](../06-beyond/13-mhd-and-relativistic-transport.md) ·
[Turbulence & eddy diffusivity](../02-bulk/05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md)

\newpage

# Transport in engineering devices


# Transport in engineering devices

> **One-line picture.** Every device that works is a solved transport problem; every device that fails
> usually violates a transport assumption (a hot spot, a depleted electrode, a fouled surface).
>
> **Prerequisites.** [analogy & circuit models](01-analogy-and-circuit-models.md),
> [reaction kinetics](../04-interaction/01-reaction-kinetics-and-sinks.md)

## L1 · Six workhorses and their transport cores

| Device | Transport core | Failure mode if neglected |
|---|---|---|
| lithium-ion battery | P2D/Newman model: Nernst–Planck + porous-electrode kinetics | lithium plating, thermal runaway, capacity fade |
| fuel cell / electrolyser | two-phase flow in porous electrodes + membrane transport | flooding, dry-out, hot spots |
| chemical reactor | residence-time distribution + reaction + heat transfer | runaway, hot spots, poor selectivity |
| heat exchanger | convection correlations + fouling + two-phase boiling | underperformance (film resistances dominate) |
| semiconductor chip | drift–diffusion + phonon transport + contact resistance | electromigration, thermal throttling |
| membrane separation | solution–diffusion + concentration polarisation | flux decline, fouling, selectivity loss |
| additive manufacturing | melt-pool Marangoni flow + solidification + residual stress | porosity, distortion, residual stress |
| catalytic converter | pore diffusion + surface kinetics (Thiele modulus) | light-off delay, poisoning |

## L2 · Dimensionless groups are the design language

* **Thiele modulus** $\phi=L\sqrt{k/D}$: reaction versus diffusion in a catalyst pellet — sets whether the
  pellet is used efficiently (effectiveness factor $\eta=\tanh\phi/\phi$).
* **Biot number** $\mathrm{Bi}=hL/k$: whether a body is isothermal or has internal gradients, i.e. whether
  a lumped model is valid.
* **Fourier number** $\mathrm{Fo}=\alpha t/L^2$: thermal penetration in transient processes (brazing,
  quenching, batteries at high rate).
* **Damköhler and Péclet**: whether a reactor is transport-limited or kinetics-limited
  ([dimensionless cards](../10-appendix/02-dimensionless-number-cards.md)).
* **Sherwood/Nusselt correlations**: the standard route from geometry to transfer coefficient, with the
  analogy to momentum ([heat–mass–momentum analogy](01-analogy-and-circuit-models.md)).

## L3 · Failure modes as transport pathologies

* **Thermal runaway** in a battery is a positive feedback: temperature raises reaction rate (Arrhenius),
  which raises heat release, which raises temperature — and the coupling is stiff, requiring implicit,
  fully coupled simulation ([stiffness](../07-numerics/02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md)).
* **Concentration polarisation** at a membrane or electrode is a boundary-layer phenomenon: neglecting it
  over-predicts current limits and under-predicts voltage losses.
* **Hot spots in catalysis** appear where pore diffusion and reaction conspire; the same Thiele analysis
  that predicts pellet efficiency predicts ignition.
* **Fouling and scaling** are slow, history-dependent surface processes with hysteresis
  ([accretion & surface growth](../04-interaction/11-accretion-ablation-and-surface-growth.md)).

## L4 · Practice

* **Start with a network model** (resistances, capacitances, Thiele/Biot/Fourier), then simulate only
  where the lumped model fails. Most device engineering is won at this stage.
* **Couple multiphysics implicitly** where feedback is strong (batteries, combustion, phase change) and
  use stabilised partitioned schemes where it is weak
  ([splitting & coupling](../07-numerics/05-operator-splitting-and-multiphysics-coupling.md)).
* **Validate against standard protocols**: C-rate cycling for batteries, GHSV and conversion/selectivity
  for reactors, LMTD/NTU and $\epsilon$-NTU data for heat exchangers.
* **Use the verification suite routinely**: manufactured solutions plus device-level benchmarks
  ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).

## Checks

1. Why does the Thiele modulus determine catalyst utilisation? *(It compares reaction rate to pore
   diffusion; large $\phi$ confines reaction to a thin outer shell.)*
2. When is a lumped-capacitance model valid? *(Small Biot number: internal gradients negligible.)*

## See also

[Heat–mass–momentum analogy & circuits](01-analogy-and-circuit-models.md) ·
[Conduction](../03-microscopic/03-conduction.md) ·
[Absorption & interfacial transfer](../04-interaction/08-absorption-dissolution-and-interfacial-transfer.md) ·
[Structure-preserving discretisations](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)

\newpage

# Transport in machine learning & data


# Transport in machine learning & data

> **One-line picture.** Modern generative models *are* transport equations solved backwards in time; and
> graph diffusion is the same Laplacian you met in heat conduction.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [random walks & Langevin](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md),
> [optimal transport](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md)

## L1 · Four places where transport theory is the theory

| ML object | Transport equation behind it | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Diffusion model (DDPM, score-based) | forward noising SDE + reverse-time Fokker–Planck | sampling = solving an SDE backwards; score $=\nabla\log p$ |
| Flow matching / continuous normalising flows | continuity equation $\partial_t\rho+\nabla\!\cdot(\rho\mathbf v)=0$ | learn a velocity field that transports a prior into data |
| Graph neural networks / PageRank | graph Laplacian diffusion, random walk with teleport | smoothing, ranking, consensus; heat kernel on graphs |
| Optimal transport in ML | Wasserstein distance, Sinkhorn | distribution comparison, domain adaptation, generative loss |

The key mathematical statements: the **Fokker–Planck equation** governs how a probability density evolves
under noise; running it backwards requires the *score* $\nabla\log p_t$, which is what a network learns;
and the reverse-time SDE is a legitimate transport equation in exactly the sense of this atlas
([Fokker–Planck & Kramers](../05-kinetic/03-fokker-planck-and-kramers.md)).

## L2 · Why the atlas's concepts keep appearing

* **Entropy and free energy**: variational inference minimises a Kullback–Leibler divergence, i.e. a
  relative entropy — the same functional that drives diffusion
  ([entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md)).
* **JKO and Wasserstein**: sampling schemes can be written as Wasserstein gradient flows of a free energy,
  including Langevin dynamics and its accelerated variants.
* **Stability and stiffness**: training diffusion models means integrating stiff SDEs; discretisation
  choices (Euler–Maruyama vs higher order) visibly change sample quality
  ([SDE integrators](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md)).
* **Conservation and structure**: flow-based models are *exactly* continuous transports (invertible), and
  their architecture must be divergence-free-friendly to control the density change — the same
  compatibility requirement as incompressible-flow discretisations
  ([structure-preserving](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)).

## L3 · The reverse direction: ML inside transport modelling

* **Operator learning** accelerates repeated PDE solves ([operator learning](../07-numerics/11-machine-learning-and-operator-learning.md)).
* **Learned closures** for turbulence and subgrid dispersion — with the caveat that they must respect
  conservation and realisability.
* **Surrogates inside inversion and data assimilation**, replacing expensive forward solves
  ([inverse problems](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)).
* **Graph diffusion** for network transport: the same Laplacian governs heat on meshes, diffusion maps for
  manifold learning, and influence propagation in social networks
  ([networks, society & traffic](09-networks-society-and-traffic.md)).

## L4 · Practice and pitfalls

* **Never evaluate a generative model without a metric tied to the transport**: probability-flow ODE
  residuals, Wasserstein distances between sample and data distributions, and mode coverage.
* **Respect the dimensional analysis**: noise schedules and step sizes behave like diffusion numbers
  ($\Delta t$ vs $\Delta x^2/D$); rescaling data changes what "stable step" means.
* **Watch for the physics-free failure mode**: a generative model can produce density where the
  conservation law forbids it. Structural constraints must be enforced, not hoped for.
* **Benchmarks**: analytic Gaussian transports (exact Wasserstein/entropy), Lorenz-96 assimilation, and
  Darcy-flow operator benchmarks ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).

## Checks

1. What does a diffusion model learn, in transport language? *(The score, hence the drift of the
   reverse-time Fokker–Planck flow that transports noise into data.)*
2. Which functional does both variational inference and heat flow minimise? *(Relative entropy.)*

## See also

[Fokker–Planck & Kramers](../05-kinetic/03-fokker-planck-and-kramers.md) ·
[Optimal transport & gradient flows](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md) ·
[Machine learning & operator learning](../07-numerics/11-machine-learning-and-operator-learning.md) ·
[Networks, society & traffic](09-networks-society-and-traffic.md)

\newpage

# Transport in images & signals


# Transport in images & signals

> **One-line picture.** Every classic image-processing operation is a PDE in disguise: smoothing is heat
> flow, edge preservation is nonlinear diffusion, and segmentation is a moving front.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md),
> [nonlinear diffusion](../06-beyond/06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md)

## L1 · The dictionary

| Image operation | PDE | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Gaussian blur | heat equation $\partial_tu=\Delta u$ | scale $t$ is the blur radius squared |
| anisotropic diffusion (Perona–Malik) | $\partial_tu=\nabla\!\cdot\!\big(g(\|\nabla u\|)\nabla u\big)$ | diffusivity falls where gradients are large |
| denoising by total variation | $\partial_tu=\nabla\!\cdot\!\big(\nabla u/\|\nabla u\|\big)$ | $L^1$ of gradients; keeps edges, makes staircases |
| shock filter | $\partial_tu=-\|\nabla u\|\,F(D^2u)$ | Hamilton–Jacobi: sharpens edges |
| inpainting | diffusion with a constraint on the known region | obstacle/complementarity structure |
| segmentation / active contours | level-set front motion | curve evolution, topology change free |
| optical flow | advection–diffusion for the flow field | brightness constancy is an advection constraint |
| scale space / structure tensor | anisotropic heat kernel on the image | multiscale analysis by diffusion time |
| diffusion maps / manifold learning | heat kernel on a data graph | eigenfunctions of the graph Laplacian |

Two bridges are deeper than they look: **the heat equation's semigroup is the multiscale representation of
an image** (Gaussian pyramids are diffusion in time), and **the graph Laplacian of a data cloud converges
to the Laplace–Beltrami operator of the underlying manifold** — which is why diffusion-based methods are
legitimate geometry, not heuristics.

## L2 · Why nonlinearity is essential

Linear diffusion smooths edges as fast as noise — unacceptable for images. Perona–Malik's insight was to
make the diffusivity depend on the local gradient: strongly diffusive in flat regions, nearly zero across
edges. The result behaves like anisotropic diffusion along level lines, and it is a
[nonlinear diffusion](../06-beyond/06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md) equation with all the
attendant theory: well-posedness requires regularisation (the equation as originally proposed can be
backward-diffusive for steep gradients), and total-variation flow is the degenerate limit that keeps edges
while removing oscillation.

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Maximum principle and scale-space axioms** justify Gaussian blur as *the* linear smoothing (Koenderink:
  causality, isotropy and linearity force the heat equation).
* **TV flow is a gradient flow** of total variation and has a well-developed theory (with staircasing as a
  known artifact) — a rare case where an image-processing heuristic has complete analysis.
* **Shock filters and level-set segmentation are viscosity solutions** of Hamilton–Jacobi equations, so
  monotone numerics are required for stability
  ([Hamilton–Jacobi](../06-beyond/02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md)).
* **Inpainting is a free-boundary/complementarity problem**: the solution must satisfy the PDE where data
  is missing and match the data where it exists ([variational inequalities](../06-beyond/12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md)).

## L4 · Practice

* **Choose the scheme by the structure you want**: explicit with small steps for accuracy, operator
  splitting for speed (splitting diffusion from fidelity), and AOS/implicit schemes for unconditional
  stability ([splitting](../07-numerics/05-operator-splitting-and-multiphysics-coupling.md)).
* **Do not over-smooth**: every diffusion-based method trades noise against detail in a way that is exactly
  quantified by its diffusion coefficient — measure it.
* **For PDE-based vision, verify with synthetic ground truth**: Gaussian blur with a known $\sigma$, TV
  denoising of piecewise constants, and level-set motion of a circle.

## Checks

1. Why is Gaussian blur the "right" linear smoother? *(Scale-space axioms force the heat equation.)*
2. What goes wrong with unregularised Perona–Malik? *(It can be backward-diffusive for large gradients:
   ill-posed, noise-amplifying.)*

## See also

[Nonlinear diffusion & porous medium](../06-beyond/06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md) ·
[Hamilton–Jacobi & viscosity solutions](../06-beyond/02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md) ·
[The spectral view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md) ·
[Machine learning & data](07-machine-learning-and-data.md)

\newpage

# Transport in networks, society & traffic


# Transport in networks, society & traffic

> **One-line picture.** Put the transport equation on a graph and you get traffic jams, opinion spread,
> epidemics, queues and web ranking — the same conservation law with a new geometry.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [conservation laws & shocks](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md),
> [diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md)

## L1 · Four transport problems with non-physical "stuff"

| System | Conserved quantity | Flux law | Character |
|---|---|---|---|
| road traffic | cars per metre | $f(\rho)=\rho v(\rho)$, typically concave | shocks: jams propagate *backwards* |
| epidemics | susceptible/infected counts | contact rate × mixing | reaction–diffusion, fronts |
| opinion / innovation | agents holding a view | imitation, switching | diffusion-like, with thresholds |
| queues and packets | jobs | service capacity, routing | conservation + discrete rules |
| PageRank / influence | probability mass | random walk with teleportation | heat kernel on a graph |

**Traffic** is the most beautiful case: the Lighthill–Whitham–Richards (LWR) model is a *scalar
conservation law* $\partial_t\rho+\partial_x f(\rho)=0$ with concave flux. Its shock speed is the chord
slope, which is *negative* when density is high — hence jams travel upstream while cars move downstream.
Adding a bottleneck or a red light produces rarefactions and shocks, and the
Godunov/Riemann machinery of [shock theory](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md) becomes a
traffic simulator. Second-order models (Payne–Whitham) add momentum and can produce phantom jams and
instability — the traffic analogue of an ill-posed fluid model.

## L2 · Graphs as the geometry

On a network, the Laplacian $L=D-A$ replaces $\nabla^2$: heat conduction, random walks, consensus
dynamics and PageRank are all $\dot{\mathbf u}=-L\mathbf u$ plus external terms. Consequences:

* **Spectral gap = mixing time**: the second eigenvalue of the graph Laplacian controls how fast diffusion
  homogenises, exactly as in the continuum
  ([spectral view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md)).
* **Communities are bottlenecks**: small cuts slow diffusion, which is the basis of spectral clustering.
* **Epidemics**: SIR on networks is reaction–diffusion with the contact graph as geometry; the epidemic
  threshold is a spectral radius condition, and spatial spread is a
  [front](../04-interaction/05-fronts-and-travelling-waves.md) in a network metric.
* **Optimal routing/evacuation** is a flow problem with capacity constraints — a
  [variational inequality](../06-beyond/12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md) (Wardrop
  equilibrium: no driver can improve by switching route).

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Shocks in traffic are unavoidable** for concave flux: any deceleration region steepens into a jam; the
  entropy condition selects the jam solution rather than an "expansion jam".
* **Conservation on networks is subtle**: at junctions you must specify a *distribution* rule, and it
  must respect capacity and non-negativity; naive averaging loses cars.
* **Discrete versus continuum**: continuum models are limits of stochastic agent models, and the limit can
  produce different behaviour (finite-size noise drives jams) — a transport analogue of
  [fluctuation effects](../06-beyond/07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md).
* **Ranking is diffusion**: PageRank is the stationary distribution of a random walk with restarts, i.e.
  the equilibrium of a transport process with a source term.

## L4 · Practice

* **Validate traffic models against fundamental-diagram data** ($\rho$ vs flux from loop detectors), not
  against single-vehicle anecdotes: the flux curve *is* the constitutive law.
* **Benchmarks**: Riemann problems for LWR with a bottleneck, ring-road instability thresholds, SIR front
  speeds, and PageRank convergence on standard graphs.
* **Beware of policy conclusions from an unvalidated flux function**: the shape of $f(\rho)$ determines
  whether jams form at all.
* **Frontiers.** Coupled traffic + emissions + routing; heterogeneous behaviour with human factors;
  mean-field games for selfish routing; epidemic control as an optimal-transport problem.

## Checks

1. Why do traffic jams travel backwards? *(Shock speed is negative when the chord slope of $f(\rho)$ on the
   dense side is negative — the wave moves upstream even though cars move downstream.)*
2. What sets the mixing time on a graph? *(The spectral gap of the graph Laplacian.)*

## See also

[Conservation laws & shocks](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md) ·
[Fronts & travelling waves](../04-interaction/05-fronts-and-travelling-waves.md) ·
[Machine learning & data](07-machine-learning-and-data.md) ·
[Variational inequalities & hysteresis](../06-beyond/12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md)

\newpage

# Transport in quantum & condensed matter


# Transport in quantum & condensed matter

> **One-line picture.** The same transport equations, with quantum carriers: what changes is that the
> carriers can be ballistic, coherent, and honest about their statistics.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [conduction](../03-microscopic/03-conduction.md),
> [the Boltzmann equation](../05-kinetic/01-boltzmann-equation.md)

## L1 · Five carriers, five transport stories

| Carrier | Transport quantity | Equation | Regime notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| phonon | heat | Boltzmann transport equation (BTE) | ballistic when $\ell\gg L$: Fourier's law fails |
| electron | charge and heat | drift–diffusion; BTE; Wiedemann–Franz | quantum coherence in mesoscopic devices |
| magnon | spin/heat | spin diffusion; magnon BTE | spin caloritronics |
| exciton / polariton | energy | diffusion + recombination | organic photovoltaics |
| superfluid/condensate | mass, heat | two-fluid model | second sound: heat as a wave |

Condensed matter is where the *limits* of classical transport are visible and controllable:

* **Ballistic transport** (nanowires, graphene, point contacts): resistance is set by geometry and
  quantum reflection, not by a bulk coefficient. Fourier's law is replaced by the Landauer picture.
* **Second sound**: in pure crystals at low temperature, heat propagates as a wave — the long-predicted
  [Maxwell–Cattaneo](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md) regime, now routinely measured.
* **Hydrodynamic electron flow**: in very clean conductors, electrons behave as a viscous fluid, with
  viscous dissipation and Poiseuille-like profiles — quantum transport becoming
  [Navier–Stokes](../02-bulk/03-incompressible-flow-vorticity-and-pressure.md).
* **Quantum walks and Wigner transport**: the Wigner function obeys a kinetic equation whose diffusion
  limit is classical, but whose coherence terms have no classical analogue.

## L2 · The general structure

The transport coefficients of a quantum system are **Kubo formulas**: equilibrium correlation functions
of current operators, exactly the [Green–Kubo relations](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md)
with quantum correlators. This single structure explains:

* why thermal and electrical conductivity are linked (Wiedemann–Franz), and where that link fails;
* why transport is *dissipative from a unitary theory* only after coarse-graining — the quantum version of
  the H-theorem's fine print;
* why low-dimensional systems can have anomalously conducting or insulating behaviours (integrable
  systems, many-body localisation) that no classical diffusion equation reproduces.

## L3 · What is genuinely different

* **Quantisation of transport**: conductance can be quantised ($2e^2/h$ per channel), so "coefficient" is
  no longer a continuous material property.
* **Coherence**: interference between paths (Aharonov–Bohm, weak localisation) is transport behaviour with
  no classical counterpart.
* **Statistics matter**: fermions and bosons obey different distribution functions; the classical Fick law
  is the degenerate limit.
* **Long memory and non-Markovian dynamics** are the norm at short times: the memory kernel language of
  [Mori–Zwanzig](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md) is native to this field.

## L4 · Practice

* **Check the mean free path against the device** before using any bulk coefficient
  ([Knudsen-style reasoning](../03-microscopic/05-knudsen-and-rarefied-transport.md)).
* **Benchmarks**: Debye-model thermal conductivity, Landauer conductance of a point contact, second-sound
  pulse speeds, and Kubo-formula consistency with the fluctuation–dissipation theorem.
* **Frontiers.** Hydrodynamic electron flow in graphene and Weyl metals; phonon hydrodynamics; spin
  caloritronics; quantum thermal machines; transport in strongly correlated systems where quasiparticles
  fail.

## Checks

1. Why does Fourier's law fail in a nanowire? *(The phonon mean free path exceeds the wire length: transport
   is ballistic.)*
2. What is the quantum analogue of Green–Kubo? *(Kubo formulas: equilibrium current correlation functions,
   with quantum statistics.)*

## See also

[Conduction](../03-microscopic/03-conduction.md) ·
[The Boltzmann equation](../05-kinetic/01-boltzmann-equation.md) ·
[Linear response & memory](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md) ·
[Memory, viscoelasticity & Cattaneo](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md)

# One mathematics of transport


# One mathematics of transport

> **One-line picture.** Every equation in this atlas is a continuity equation for something. Add a
> constitutive law, a metric and an entropy, and you have specified which *kind* of transport you mean.
>
> **Prerequisites.** the whole atlas, but especially
> [balance laws](../01-foundations/01-balance-laws-and-flux.md),
> [entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md),
> [optimal transport](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md)

## L1 · The common skeleton

**Level 1 — the balance law.** $\partial_tc+\nabla\cdot\mathbf J=f$. Exact, universal, geometry-free.

**Level 2 — the constitutive law.** $\mathbf J$ as a function of the state: Fick, Fourier, Newton, Darcy,
Ohm, Maxwell–Stefan, kinetic moments, or a memory integral. This step is where physics, empirical
parameters and *all* the error enter.

**Level 3 — the structure.** The same balance law with different structures gives qualitatively different
phenomena:

| Structure | Equation class | Behaviour |
|---|---|---|
| dissipation $+$ gradient law | parabolic | smoothing, maximum principle, entropy up |
| transport $+$ nonlinear flux | hyperbolic | shocks, entropy condition, finite speed |
| transport $+$ elliptic constraint | mixed | boundary layers, projection methods |
| nonlocal kernel | integro-differential | jumps, long range, new boundary conditions |
| memory kernel | Volterra / fractional | relaxation, ageing, non-exponential decay |
| variational inequality | complementarity | switching, active sets, hysteresis |
| stochastic | SDE/SPDE | fluctuation, scaling laws, rare events |

## L2 · The unifying objects

* **The continuity equation on measure space.** $\partial_t\mu+\nabla\cdot(\mu\mathbf v)=0$ makes sense for
  densities, dirac masses, empirical measures and graph measures alike — which is why discrete, continuum
  and stochastic transport share theorems.
* **Semigroups and generators.** Each transport equation defines a semigroup $e^{t\mathcal L}$; the
  generator $\mathcal L$ is the operator, and its spectral properties (spectral gap, decay rates) give the
  long-time behaviour ([spectral view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md)).
* **Entropy and free energy.** A Lyapunov functional supplies stability, uniqueness and the large-time
  limit. Gradient-flow structure in the *right metric* is what makes the entropy decrease meaningful, and
  the metric is the physics: $L^2$, $H^{-1}$, or Wasserstein
  ([optimal transport](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md)).
* **Green–Kubo and Mori–Zwanzig.** Microscopic transport coefficients are equilibrium correlations;
  eliminating fast variables produces memory kernels and noise, which is why every "effective" model is an
  approximation of a projection ([linear response](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md)).
* **Asymptotic limits.** Kinetic → fluid (Chapman–Enskog), local → nonlocal (horizon → 0), stochastic →
  deterministic (law of large numbers), hyperbolic → parabolic (relaxation → 0). Most of the apparent
  diversity in this atlas collapses into a handful of such limits.

## L3 · The recurring trade-off

Everything practical in transport is the same trade:

$$\text{resolution} \;\longleftrightarrow\; \text{closure} \;\longleftrightarrow\; \text{cost}.$$

* Resolve the small scales: DNS, molecular dynamics, pore-scale simulation, direct kinetic solvers.
* Close them: eddy diffusivity, upscaled coefficients, effective reactions, memory kernels, learned
  closures.
* Pay the price somewhere: cost, accuracy, generality, or the validity range.

The dimensionless numbers tell you which branch you are on; the entropy inequality tells you which
closures are legal; and verification tells you whether you implemented it correctly.

## L4 · How to use this atlas

1. **Identify the conserved quantity**, then the flux law — that fixes the equation.
2. **Compute the dimensionless numbers** to learn which term dominates
   ([cards](../10-appendix/02-dimensionless-number-cards.md)).
3. **Find the structure** (parabolic, hyperbolic, nonlocal, variational, stochastic) to know which theorems
   and methods apply ([operator catalogue](../10-appendix/03-operator-and-equation-catalog.md)).
4. **Choose the scheme from the structure + regime**, not from habit
   ([discretisation](../07-numerics/01-discretization-consistency-stability-convergence.md)).
5. **Verify, then validate** ([V&V](../07-numerics/12-verification-validation-and-benchmarks.md)).

## Checks

1. Name the three levels of the skeleton and say which one carries the physics. *(Balance law — universal;
   constitutive law — physics and error; structure — which theorems apply.)*
2. Why can the same equation be diffusion in one metric and transport in another? *(Because "steepest
   descent" depends on how you measure distance between states.)*

## See also

[Balance laws & flux](../01-foundations/01-balance-laws-and-flux.md) ·
[Entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md) ·
[Optimal transport & gradient flows](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md) ·
[Operator & equation catalogue](../10-appendix/03-operator-and-equation-catalog.md)

\newpage

# Words that collide


# Words that collide

Transport phenomena is a subject assembled from several disciplines that independently invented the same
mathematics and then gave it incompatible names. This short category dismantles the most expensive
collisions — the ones that change the physics if you guess wrong.

| Note | The collision |
|---|---|
| [1 · Dispersion](01-dispersion.md) | four meanings: porous-media spreading, Taylor shear dispersion, wave dispersion, numerical phase error |
| [2 · Convection](02-convection.md) | heat-transfer conversation, buoyancy-driven flow, meteorological vertical transport, and "the convective term" (which is advection) |
| [3 · Diffusion & conduction](03-diffusion-and-conduction.md) | one equation, six names (diffusion, conduction, viscous diffusion, migration, relaxation, pseudo-diffusion) |
| [4 · Absorption, sinks & flux](04-absorption-and-sink-lingo.md) | absorption vs adsorption, source vs sink, physical vs numerical flux, Robin vs Cauchy vs outflow |

## The general rule

> Before writing an equation, ask three questions: **what is conserved?**, **what is the flux law?**, and
> **what is happening on the boundary?** Almost every terminological collision dissolves into one of
> these three, and almost every modelling error is a wrong answer to one of them.

Two further habits that prevent most confusion:

* **Name coefficients by their function**, not their symbol: "$D$ has units m²/s and relates mass flux to
  a concentration gradient" — then check that your symbol carries the same meaning in the paper you are
  reading.
* **Say which frame and which form** you are using — Lagrangian or Eulerian, conservative or advective.
  Half of all disagreements about "advection versus convection" are really disagreements about those two
  choices ([material derivative](../01-foundations/02-material-derivative.md)).

## See also

[Balance laws & flux](../01-foundations/01-balance-laws-and-flux.md) ·
[Constitutive relations](../01-foundations/03-constitutive-relations.md) ·
[Boundary conditions](../01-foundations/04-pde-taxonomy-and-boundary-conditions.md) ·
[Operator & equation catalogue](../10-appendix/03-operator-and-equation-catalog.md)

# Words that collide 1 — dispersion


# "Dispersion" means at least four different things

> **Why this note exists.** Almost every confusion between a hydrodynamicist, a hydrogeologist, a
> spectroscopist and a numerical analyst starts with this word.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [advection](../02-bulk/01-advection.md),
> [shear dispersion](../02-bulk/06-shear-dispersion-taylor-aris.md)

## The four meanings

| Meaning | Domain | What it is | Where it lives here |
|---|---|---|---|
| **mechanical / hydrodynamic dispersion** | porous media, groundwater | spreading caused by velocity variation in pores and flow paths; a tensor, $\alpha_L\gg\alpha_T$ | [Darcy flow](../02-bulk/08-porous-media-and-darcy.md) |
| **shear (Taylor–Aris) dispersion** | pipe and channel flow | spreading from a velocity profile coupled to cross-stream diffusion; $D_{\rm eff}=D+a^2U^2/48D$ | [Taylor–Aris](../02-bulk/06-shear-dispersion-taylor-aris.md) |
| **wave dispersion** | waves and optics | oscillation frequency depends on wavenumber, $\omega=\omega(k)$; different wavelengths travel at different speeds | [spectral view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md) |
| **numerical dispersion** | computation | phase error of a discretisation: waves travel at the wrong speed, producing oscillations | [numerical errors](../07-numerics/03-numerical-diffusion-and-dispersion-error.md) |

Two more minor usages: **statistical dispersion** (variance/spread of any distribution) and **material
dispersion** (a medium supporting multiple wave modes, as in acoustics).

## How to keep them apart

* **Is the spreading caused by a velocity field?** If yes, it is mechanical/hydrodynamic or shear
  dispersion (transport spreading). If the *shape of a wave* changes speed with wavelength, it is wave
  dispersion.
* **Does it vanish when the flow stops?** Mechanical dispersion does; molecular diffusion does not; wave
  dispersion depends on the medium, not the flow.
* **Is it physical or numerical?** Numerical dispersion is an artifact of the scheme and disappears under
  refinement (with the right scheme); the others do not.
* **Is it a tensor or a scalar?** Hydrodynamic dispersion is anisotropic and aligned with the flow;
  numerical dispersion is aligned with the grid. Both look like diffusion; only one is real.

## The trap

> A plume that spreads ten times faster than molecular diffusion is not "diffusion" in the Fickian
> material-property sense — its coefficient depends on the velocity, the geometry and (in porous media)
> the observation scale. Reporting it as a material constant is the single most common error in
> subsurface transport.

## See also

[Shear dispersion (Taylor–Aris)](../02-bulk/06-shear-dispersion-taylor-aris.md) ·
[Porous media & Darcy](../02-bulk/08-porous-media-and-darcy.md) ·
[Numerical diffusion & dispersion](../07-numerics/03-numerical-diffusion-and-dispersion-error.md) ·
[The spectral view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md)

\newpage

# Words that collide 2 — convection


# "Convection" is three words wearing one coat

> **Why this note exists.** In heat transfer, fluid mechanics and geophysics, "convection" implies
> different mathematics — and "convective term" means something different again.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [advection](../02-bulk/01-advection.md),
> [convection & buoyancy](../02-bulk/02-convection-and-buoyancy.md)

## The usages

| Usage | Meaning | Mathematics |
|---|---|---|
| **heat-transfer engineer** | combined advection + conduction, often with a correlation: $q=h\Delta T$ | a *modelled* boundary flux, $h$ from $\mathrm{Nu}$ correlations |
| **fluid dynamicist** | buoyancy-driven flow (natural convection); forced or mixed convection by analogy | coupled momentum + energy, $\mathrm{Ra}$, $\mathrm{Gr}$, $\mathrm{Ri}$ |
| **meteorologist** | vertical transport by bulk air motion (including dry convection) | advection with a velocity field, often parameterised |
| **mathematician** | sometimes the whole material derivative, $\mathrm{D}/\mathrm{D}t$ | the transport operator itself |
| **geophysicist** | mantle convection: creeping (infinite-$\mathrm{Pr}$) flow | Stokes + energy equations |

The specific term that causes most trouble: **"the convective term"** in the Navier–Stokes equations,
$\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u}$, is *advection* in the strict sense — the fluid carrying its own
momentum. It is nonlinear self-transport, and calling it "convection" invites confusion with buoyancy.

## The clean distinctions

* **Advection** — passive transport by a velocity field. **Your leaf.**
* **Convection** — transport *with* the motion, where the motion is generated by the transported quantity
  (buoyancy, surface tension) or by an external forcing that makes the transfer "convective" in the
  engineering sense. **The heat making its own wind.**
* **Conduction** — transport with no bulk motion at all
  ([words that collide 3](03-diffusion-and-conduction.md)).
* **Convection vs advection in a model**: if you supply $\mathbf u$ and never solve for it, say
  *advection*; if $\mathbf u$ depends on the transported field, say *convection*.

## The trap

> "Forced convection" is advection with a known velocity field plus conduction. If your code contains a
> prescribed velocity, calling the term "convection" is harmless — but claiming your model *predicts*
> convective enhancement is not, because the velocity came from a correlation, not from the physics.

## See also

[Advection](../02-bulk/01-advection.md) ·
[Convection & buoyancy](../02-bulk/02-convection-and-buoyancy.md) ·
[Heat–mass–momentum analogy](../08-bridges/01-analogy-and-circuit-models.md) ·
[Material derivative](../01-foundations/02-material-derivative.md)

# Words that collide 3 — diffusion & conduction


# "Diffusion" and "conduction" are one equation with different carriers

> **Why this note exists.** The mathematics is identical (a gradient-driven flux), the physics is not,
> and the vocabulary splits into at least six words for one idea.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md),
> [conduction](../03-microscopic/03-conduction.md)

## One equation, six names

| Word | Carriers | Driving gradient | Coefficient | Field |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **diffusion** (Fick) | molecules, ions, vacancies | concentration | $D$ | chemistry, biology, materials |
| **conduction** (Fourier) | phonons, electrons | temperature | $k$, $\alpha$ | heat transfer |
| **viscous diffusion** | momentum through collisions | velocity | $\nu$ | fluid mechanics |
| **migration / drift** | charged species in a field | electric potential | mobility $u$ | electrochemistry |
| **relaxation** | any excess | — | relaxation time $\tau$ | kinetic theory, rheology |
| **pseudo-diffusion** | quantised carriers | chemical potential | $D$ from Kubo | condensed matter |

Where the difference is real:

* **The conservation law differs.** Heat diffusion conserves *energy* and includes a heat capacity
  ($\alpha=k/\rho c_p$); mass diffusion conserves *mass* with $D$ alone. Mixing the two formulas is the
  most common sign of a physicist writing biology or vice versa.
* **The temperature dependence is opposite.** $D_{\rm liquid}$ falls as viscosity rises with cooling;
  $k$ of a solid generally rises with electron/phonon transport but falls with phonon–phonon scattering.
* **Drift is not diffusion.** Migration in an electric field has a *linear* dependence on the field,
  not on its gradient; it is advection in potential space, and its coupling to diffusion is fixed by the
  Einstein relation ([charged transport](../03-microscopic/07-charged-and-electrochemical-transport.md)).
* **Anisotropy shows up differently.** Laminated rock has tensor diffusion; layered composites have
  tensor conduction; in both cases the effective coefficients along and across the layering differ.

## The trap

> Because the equations are identical, a solved problem in one field is a solved problem in the other
> — *provided* the boundary conditions map. Value conditions map to value conditions, flux conditions to
> flux conditions, but a convective (Robin) condition maps to a *different* physical situation than you
> intended. Always translate the boundary conditions explicitly.

## Two more confusions worth naming

* **"Diffusion" as a whole model**: first-order decay, dispersion, or any mixing process is often called
  diffusion colloquially. Only the second-order gradient law is diffusion proper.
* **"Conduction" in electronics**: Ohm's law, ballistic conduction and quantum conduction are different
  regimes of the same word ([quantum & condensed matter](../08-bridges/10-quantum-and-condensed-matter.md)).

## See also

[Diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md) ·
[Conduction](../03-microscopic/03-conduction.md) ·
[Charged & electrochemical transport](../03-microscopic/07-charged-and-electrochemical-transport.md) ·
[Words that collide 1 — dispersion](01-dispersion.md)

\newpage

# Words that collide 4 — absorption, sinks & flux lingo


# Absorption, sinks, flux and boundary lingo

> **Why this note exists.** These words appear in every transport paper and mean different things in
> adjoining fields. Misusing them changes the physics — often silently.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [sorption](../04-interaction/07-sorption-and-retardation.md),
> [boundary conditions](../01-foundations/04-pde-taxonomy-and-boundary-conditions.md)

## The collisions

| Word | Meaning A | Meaning B (and C) |
|---|---|---|
| **absorption** | uptake into the *bulk* of another phase (CO₂ into water) | in optics/radiative transfer, conversion of radiative energy into internal energy |
| **adsorption** | attachment to a *surface* | — |
| **sink** | volumetric removal term ($f<0$) | a well in a flow field; an absorbing boundary |
| **source** | volumetric addition ($f>0$) | an inflow boundary; a point injection |
| **flux** | physical flux, $\mathbf J$ (per area per time) | **numerical flux** at a cell face (a reconstruction, not a physical quantity) |
| **Danckwerts condition** | zero-gradient outflow | sometimes loosely used for any outflow treatment |
| **Robin / third kind** | a relation between flux and state, $-k\partial_nT=h(T-T_\infty)$ | "convective boundary condition" (which is a *model of a film*, not a fluid-dynamic fact) |
| **Cauchy condition** | state **and** flux prescribed | often conflated with Robin |
| **outflow / efflux** | mass leaving the domain | "outflow boundary", which in some codes means *inflow allowed where the flow reverses* |

## Why the distinctions matter

* **Absorption is a volumetric process; adsorption is interfacial.** A model that treats an interfacial
  process as a volumetric sink cannot represent saturation, competition or surface area — and gets the
  wrong scaling with particle size.
* **A source term is not a boundary condition.** Injecting "1 kg/s" via a boundary flux and via a
  volumetric source differ in where the mass appears: near the boundary (with a boundary layer) or in the
  bulk. Choose the one that matches the physics.
* **Numerical flux is not physical flux.** Comparing "fluxes" from a report and from a code often mixes
  these; only the physical flux has units of transported quantity per area per time, and only it appears
  in a mass balance.
* **Robin is a closure, not a law.** The heat-transfer coefficient $h$ contains the boundary layer; the
  same $h$ cannot be reused at a different $\mathrm{Re}$, $\mathrm{Pr}$ or geometry without a correlation.

## The trap

> "Loss to the walls" can be modelled as a sink, a boundary flux, or a Robin condition, and the three give
> *different* answers: which one is right depends on whether the transport resistance is in the bulk or in
> a surface layer. Ask "where is the resistance?" before writing the term.

## See also

[Absorption, dissolution & interfacial transfer](../04-interaction/08-absorption-dissolution-and-interfacial-transfer.md) ·
[Sorption & retardation](../04-interaction/07-sorption-and-retardation.md) ·
[Boundary conditions](../01-foundations/04-pde-taxonomy-and-boundary-conditions.md) ·
[Reaction kinetics & sinks](../04-interaction/01-reaction-kinetics-and-sinks.md)

# Symbol glossary


# Symbol glossary

> **How to read this.** Symbols clash across disciplines — the point of this table is to flag the
> dangerous ones explicitly. Units are SI unless stated.

## Fields and states

| Symbol | Meaning | Units | Watch out |
|---|---|---|---|
| $c$ | concentration (mass or moles per volume) | kg/m³, mol/m³ | in kinetics $c$ may be dimensionless saturation |
| $C$ | dimensionless concentration | — | sometimes heat capacity |
| $T$ | temperature | K | in plasma physics $T$ is often in eV |
| $p$ | pressure | Pa | in poroelasticity $p$ is *pore* pressure |
| $\rho$ | density | kg/m³ | $\rho_b$ = bulk density of a porous medium |
| $\theta$ | porosity / void fraction | — | also used for temperature in heat-transfer texts |
| $\phi$ | potential; volume fraction; level-set function | V or — | triple use, always define |
| $S$ | saturation; source term; storativity | —, kg/(m³s), — | in hydrogeology $S$ = storativity |
| $u,v,w$ | velocity components | m/s | $u$ may also be a generic unknown |

## Transport coefficients

| Symbol | Meaning | Units | Watch out |
|---|---|---|---|
| $D$ | diffusion coefficient | m²/s | $D_{\rm eff}$, $D_L$, $D_T$, $D_K$ variants |
| $k$ | thermal conductivity | W/(m·K) | **also** a reaction rate constant |
| $\alpha$ | thermal diffusivity $k/\rho c_p$ | m²/s | **also** dispersivity, and the fractional order in $D_t^\alpha$ |
| $\nu$ | kinematic viscosity | m²/s | sometimes $\mu/\rho$, sometimes a wave frequency |
| $\mu$ | dynamic viscosity | Pa·s | **also** chemical potential |
| $K$ | permeability | m² | **also** a kinetic constant, and hydraulic conductivity when multiplied by $\rho g/\mu$ |
| $K_d$ | distribution coefficient (sorption) | m³/kg | dimensionless variants exist |
| $L$ | length scale | m | be explicit: which length? |
| $\lambda$ | mean free path; relaxation time; eigenvalue | m, s, 1/s | overused — define it every time |

## Operators and dimensionless numbers

| Symbol | Meaning | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| $\mathrm{D}/\mathrm{D}t$ | material derivative | $\partial_t+\mathbf u\cdot\nabla$ |
| $\nabla\cdot$ | divergence | flux balance |
| $\nabla^2$, $\Delta$ | Laplacian | $\Delta$ also means a *change* |
| $(-\Delta)^{\beta/2}$ | fractional Laplacian | nonlocal, $\beta=2$ local |
| $D_t^{\alpha}$ | Caputo fractional time derivative | memory |
| $\mathrm{Pe},\mathrm{Re},\mathrm{Ra},\mathrm{Da},\mathrm{Kn},\mathrm{Nu},\mathrm{Sh},\mathrm{Pr},\mathrm{Sc}$ | see [cards](02-dimensionless-number-cards.md) | each has more than one definition in the wild |
| $\varepsilon$ | dissipation rate; interface width; small parameter | m²/s³, m, — |
| $\eta$ | Kolmogorov scale; efficiency | m, — | also dynamic viscosity in some texts! |
| $\tau$ | stress; relaxation time | Pa, s | same symbol, different tensorial order |

## The five most dangerous clashes

1. **$\mu$**: viscosity (fluid mechanics) vs chemical potential (thermodynamics).
2. **$k$**: thermal conductivity vs reaction rate constant.
3. **$\alpha$**: thermal diffusivity vs dispersivity vs fractional order vs volume fraction (rare).
4. **$K$**: permeability vs equilibrium constant vs bulk modulus in $\mathrm{K}$-$ \varepsilon$.
5. **$\eta$**: Kolmogorov scale vs efficiency vs dynamic viscosity (in older German literature).

> **Habit worth forming.** Every note in this atlas states the units at least once. If you are reading a
> paper and a coefficient's units are not stated, that is not a stylistic quirk — it is a modelling
> ambiguity you will have to resolve before you can compare anything.

## See also

[Constitutive relations](../01-foundations/03-constitutive-relations.md) ·
[Dimensionless number cards](02-dimensionless-number-cards.md) ·
[Operator & equation catalogue](03-operator-and-equation-catalog.md)

# Dimensionless number cards


# Dimensionless number cards

> Each card: **definition → what it compares → what happens at the extremes → where to read more.**
> Definitions vary between communities (especially $\mathrm{Da}$ and $\mathrm{Bi}$); always state yours.

## Transport ratios

**Péclet** $\mathrm{Pe}=UL/D$ — advection vs diffusion. $\ll1$: diffusion wins, smooth fields; $\gg1$: sharp
fronts, boundary layers. Groundwater ${>}10^{4}$, cells ${<}10^{-2}$.
→ [dimensionless numbers](../01-foundations/05-dimensionless-numbers.md)

**Reynolds** $\mathrm{Re}=UL/\nu$ — inertia vs viscosity. ${<}1$: creeping, reversible; $10^{3}$–$10^{5}$:
turbulent transition; ${>}10^{6}$: fully turbulent. Microbe ${<}1$, river $10^{6}$, climate $10^{9}$.
→ [turbulence](../02-bulk/05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md)

**Rayleigh** $\mathrm{Ra}=g\beta\Delta TL^3/(\nu\alpha)$ — buoyancy vs dissipation. ${<}1708$: conduction
only; $10^{3}$–$10^{6}$: steady rolls; $10^{8}$–$10^{10}$: turbulence ($\mathrm{Nu}\sim\mathrm{Ra}^{0.3}$);
mantle $\sim10^{8}$, Sun $\sim10^{22}$.
→ [convection](../02-bulk/02-convection-and-buoyancy.md)

**Damköhler** $\mathrm{Da}=kL^2/D$ (diffusion-based) or $kL/U$ (advection-based) — transport vs reaction.
$\ll1$: transport-limited, chemistry space-filling; $\gg1$: reaction-limited, thin sheets.
→ [reaction kinetics](../04-interaction/01-reaction-kinetics-and-sinks.md)

**Knudsen** $\mathrm{Kn}=\lambda/L$ — mean free path vs device. ${<}0.01$: continuum; $0.01$–$0.1$: slip;
$0.1$–$10$: transition; ${>}10$: free molecular.
→ [Knudsen transport](../03-microscopic/05-knudsen-and-rarefied-transport.md)

**Nusselt** $\mathrm{Nu}=hL/k$ — total heat transfer vs pure conduction. $1$: no advection; $100$:
vigorous convection. Correlated as $\mathrm{Nu}=C\,\mathrm{Re}^m\mathrm{Pr}^n$ in forced flow.
→ [boundary layers](../02-bulk/04-boundary-layers.md)

**Sherwood** $\mathrm{Sh}=h_mL/D$ — mass analogue of $\mathrm{Nu}$; **Chilton–Colburn** links
$j_D\approx j_H\approx C_f/2$.
→ [analogy](../08-bridges/01-analogy-and-circuit-models.md)

**Prandtl / Schmidt / Lewis** $\mathrm{Pr}=\nu/\alpha$, $\mathrm{Sc}=\nu/D$, $\mathrm{Le}=\mathrm{Sc}/\mathrm{Pr}$
— relative thicknesses of momentum, thermal and mass boundary layers. Gases: $\mathrm{Pr}\approx0.7$,
$\mathrm{Sc}\approx1$; water: $\mathrm{Pr}\approx7$, $\mathrm{Sc}\approx1000$ (liquid), $\mathrm{Sc}\approx600$
for salt.
→ [boundary layers](../02-bulk/04-boundary-layers.md)

## Interface and device numbers

**Biot** $\mathrm{Bi}=hL/k_{\rm solid}$ — external vs internal resistance. ${<}0.1$: lumped capacitance
valid; ${>}10$: strong internal gradients.
→ [engineering devices](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md)

**Fourier** $\mathrm{Fo}=\alpha t/L^2$ — dimensionless time for conduction (transient processes).
**Stefan** $\mathrm{St}=c_p\Delta T/L_{\rm latent}$ — sensible vs latent heat in phase change; small
$\mathrm{St}$ means latent heat dominates and interfaces crawl as $\sqrt{t}$.
→ [phase change](../04-interaction/09-phase-change-and-stefan.md)

**Brinkman** $\mathrm{Br}=\mu U^2/(k\Delta T)$ and **Eckert** $\mathrm{Ec}=U^2/(c_p\Delta T)$ — viscous
dissipation vs conduction: the number that decides whether high-speed or polymer flow self-heats.
→ [viscous dissipation](../04-interaction/14-viscous-dissipation-and-heating.md)

**Thiele modulus** $\phi=L\sqrt{k/D}$ — reaction vs pore diffusion; effectiveness factor
$\eta=\tanh\phi/\phi$.
→ [engineering devices](../08-bridges/06-engineering-devices.md)

**Grashof / Richardson** $\mathrm{Gr}=g\beta\Delta TL^3/\nu^2$, $\mathrm{Ri}=\mathrm{Gr}/\mathrm{Re}^2$ —
natural vs forced convection: $\mathrm{Ri}\lesssim0.1$ forced, $\gtrsim10$ natural.
**Marangoni** $\mathrm{Ma}=\sigma_T\Delta TL/(\mu\alpha)$ — surface-tension vs viscous forces; dominates in
small systems. → [convection](../02-bulk/02-convection-and-buoyancy.md)

**Mach** $U/c_s$ — compressibility (${>}0.3$ matters, ${>}1$ shocks); **Froude** $U/\sqrt{gL}$ —
gravity waves; **Weber** $\rho U^2L/\sigma$ — inertia vs surface tension (drop breakup);
**Capillary** $\mu U/\sigma$; **Bond** $\Delta\rho gL^2/\sigma$ — gravity vs surface tension.
→ [advection](../02-bulk/01-advection.md), [free boundaries](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md)

**Weissenberg / Deborah** $\mathrm{Wi}=\lambda\dot\gamma$, $\mathrm{De}=\lambda/t_{\rm obs}$ — elastic
memory vs flow time; ${>}1$ elasticity dominates.
→ [non-Newtonian rheology](../02-bulk/09-non-newtonian-rheology.md)

**Stefan number, Jakob, Rayleigh–Darcy** appear in porous-media phase change;
$\mathrm{Ra}_{\rm Darcy}=g\beta\Delta TKL/(\nu\alpha)$ is the porous analogue of $\mathrm{Ra}$.
→ [Darcy flow](../02-bulk/08-porous-media-and-darcy.md)

## More cards: fluid, interface, plasma and quantum

| Number | Definition | Reading |
|---|---|---|
| Hartmann $\mathrm{Ha}$ | $B_0L\sqrt{\sigma/\mu}$ | magnetic braking: $\gg1$ ⟹ Hartmann layers, flattened (quasi-2-D) flow |
| Stuart $\mathrm{N}$ | $\sigma B_0^2L/(\rho U)$ | magnetic vs inertial forces — MHD turbulence suppressor |
| Reynolds magnetic $\mathrm{Rm}$ | $UL/\eta$ | $\gg1$: field frozen in, dynamo action possible; $\ll1$: field diffuses |
| Lundquist $\mathrm{Lu}$ | $B_0L\sqrt{\mu_0\rho}/\eta$ | Alfvénic vs resistive time: reconnection regime |
| Plasma beta $\beta$ | $2\mu_0p/B^2$ | $\ll1$: magnetically dominated (corona); $\gg1$: pressure dominated (core) |
| Hall parameter | $\omega_c\tau$ | magnetisation of carriers — Hall effect, magnetoresistance |
| Womersley $\alpha$ | $R\sqrt{\omega/\nu}$ | pulsatile flow: $\gg1$ ⟹ plug-like with thin Stokes layer (aorta) |
| Strouhal $\mathrm{St}$ | $fL/U$ | vortex shedding frequency; acoustics of jets |
| Bond $\mathrm{Bo}$ | $\Delta\rho gL^2/\sigma$ | gravity vs surface tension: drops, bubbles, capillary length |
| Capillary $\mathrm{Ca}$ | $\mu U/\sigma$ | viscous vs capillary: coating, displacement in porous media |
| Ohnesorge $\mathrm{Oh}$ | $\mu/\sqrt{\rho\sigma L}$ | atomisation regime maps |
| Morton $\mathrm{Mo}$ | $g\mu^4/(\rho\sigma^3)$ | bubble shape regime (with $\mathrm{Eo}$, $\mathrm{Re}$) |
| Jakob $\mathrm{Ja}$ | $\rho c_p\Delta T/(\rho_vh_{fg})$ | boiling/condensation; $\gg1$ ⟹ fast evaporation |
| Rayleigh–Darcy $\mathrm{Ra}_{\rm D}$ | $g\beta\Delta TKL/(\nu\alpha)$ | porous-medium convection threshold (${>}4\pi^2$) |
| Zeldovich $\mathrm{Ze}$ | $E_a\Delta T/(RT^2)$ | combustion sensitivity; $\gg1$ ⟹ thin flame sheets |
| Damköhler II/III | $kC_0L/U$ etc. | competing definitions: state which (heat release vs mixing) |
| Bodenstein $\mathrm{Bo}_{\rm ch}$ | $uL/D_{\rm ax}$ | reactor back-mixing (plug-flow limit $\to\infty$) |
| Thiele $\phi$ | $L\sqrt{k/D}$ | catalyst effectiveness $\eta=\tanh\phi/\phi$ |
| Graetz $\mathrm{Gz}$ | $\mathrm{Re}\,\mathrm{Pr}\,D/L$ | developing thermal profiles in ducts; entrance length |
| Stokes number (aerosol) $\mathrm{Stk}$ | $\tau_pU/L$ | particle inertia: $\gg1$ ⟹ impaction, $\ll1$ ⟹ follows flow |
| Knudsen diffusion ratio | $D_K/D_{\rm mol}$ | which diffusion regime a pore is in |
| Boltzmann number $\mathrm{Bo}_{\rm rad}$ | $\rho c_pU/(\sigma T^3)$ | when radiation matters in a flow |
| Photon optical depth $\tau$ | $\int\kappa\,\mathrm ds$ | thin vs thick radiative transfer |
| Batchelor number | $\nu/D$ in a scalar cascade | scalar dissipation scales |
| Deborah / Weissenberg | $\lambda/t_{\rm obs}$, $\lambda\dot\gamma$ | viscoelasticity onset |
| Péclet (thermal) | $UL/\alpha$ | advection vs conduction (distinct from mass $\mathrm{Pe}$) |
| Nusselt (film) / Biot (fluid) | $hL/k_{\rm solid}$ | which resistance controls: external film or internal conduction |
| Marangoni, Rayleigh, Grashof, Richardson | see above | surface vs buoyancy vs forced convection |
| Reynolds (porous) $\mathrm{Re}_{\rm pore}$ | $\rho qd/\mu$ | Forchheimer onset ($\gtrsim1{-}10$) |
| Magnetic Reynolds, Hall, Lundquist | see above | the MHD triad: dynamo, magnetisation, reconnection |
| Quantum: de Broglie ratio | $\lambda_{\rm dB}/L$ | when quantum transport corrections matter |
| Thermal de Broglie | $\lambda_{\rm dB}/d$ | quantum degeneracy of a gas (BEC threshold) |
| Knudsen (kinetic) | $\lambda/L$ | the master number for leaving the continuum |

> [!tip] How to use these
> Two habits: **(1)** always state which definition you mean when a number has several (Damköhler,
> Biot, Reynolds); **(2)** convert your number into a *ratio of timescales* — that is what makes it
> interpretable, and it is how you spot the regime where your assumption (continuum, mean field, dilute,
> Newtonian) quietly fails.

# Operator & equation catalogue


# Operator & equation catalogue

> Every equation in this atlas is a balance law plus one of these operators. Find your operator, get the
> type, the numerics and the physics.

| Operator / equation | Order & type | Local? | Notes, numerics | Explained in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| advection $\mathbf u\cdot\nabla c$ | 1st, hyperbolic | yes | upwind/limiter, CFL, numerical diffusion | [Advection](../02-bulk/01-advection.md) |
| conservative transport $\nabla\cdot(\mathbf u c)$ | 1st, hyperbolic | yes | finite-volume flux form | [Balance laws](../01-foundations/01-balance-laws-and-flux.md) |
| diffusion $\nabla\cdot(D\nabla c)$ | 2nd, parabolic | yes | $\Delta t\le\Delta x^2/2D$ explicit; implicit preferred | [Diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md) |
| conduction $\nabla\cdot(k\nabla T)$ | 2nd, parabolic | yes | interface flux continuity | [Conduction](../03-microscopic/03-conduction.md) |
| Darcy flux $-(K/\mu)\nabla p$ | flux law | yes | conservative, high-contrast solvers | [Porous media](../02-bulk/08-porous-media-and-darcy.md) |
| incompressibility $\nabla\cdot\mathbf u=0$ | constraint + Poisson | no (elliptic) | projection, staggered grids | [Vorticity & pressure](../02-bulk/03-incompressible-flow-vorticity-and-pressure.md) |
| reaction $R(c)$ | 0th (algebraic) | yes | stiff; implicit; positivity | [Kinetics](../04-interaction/01-reaction-kinetics-and-sinks.md) |
| nonlinear conservation law $\partial_tc+\partial_xf(c)=0$ | 1st, hyperbolic | yes | shocks, entropy condition, TVD/WENO | [Shocks](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md) |
| Hamilton–Jacobi $\partial_tu+H(\nabla u)=0$ | 1st, nonlinear | yes | monotone schemes, fast marching | [Hamilton–Jacobi](../06-beyond/02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md) |
| nonlinear diffusion $\nabla\cdot(mc^{m-1}\nabla c)$ | 2nd, degenerate parabolic | yes | finite speed, Barenblatt, free boundary | [Nonlinear diffusion](../06-beyond/06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md) |
| $p$-Laplacian $\nabla\cdot(\|\nabla c\|^{p-2}\nabla c)$ | 2nd, quasilinear | yes | TV denoising, non-Newtonian flow | [Images & signals](../08-bridges/08-images-and-signals.md) |
| Cahn–Hilliard $\nabla\cdot(M\nabla(f'-\kappa^2\nabla^2c))$ | 4th, parabolic | yes | needs $C^1$ or mixed methods | [Free boundaries](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md) |
| telegraph / Cattaneo $\tau c_{tt}+c_t=Dc_{xx}$ | 2nd, hyperbolic-damped | yes | finite speed, stiff relaxation | [Memory & viscoelasticity](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md) |
| fractional time $D_t^{\alpha}c=D\nabla^2c$ | non-integer | no (memory) | convolution quadrature, sum-of-exponentials | [Nonlocal & fractional](../06-beyond/03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md) |
| fractional space $(-\Delta)^{\beta/2}c$ | non-integer | **no** | dense/Toeplitz/FFT; needs volume BCs | same note |
| nonlocal diffusion $\int K(x,y)[c(y)-c(x)]\mathrm dy$ | integral | **no** | asymptotically compatible schemes | same note |
| memory $\int M(t-s)\mathbf X(s)\mathrm ds$ | Volterra | no (time) | Prony/sum-of-exponentials | [Linear response](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md) |
| Fokker–Planck | 2nd, parabolic | yes | conservative form, positivity | [Fokker–Planck](../05-kinetic/03-fokker-planck-and-kramers.md) |
| Boltzmann | kinetic, $2d$-dim phase space | collision nonlocal in $\mathbf v$ | DSMC, discrete velocity | [Boltzmann](../05-kinetic/01-boltzmann-equation.md) |
| Vlasov | kinetic, self-consistent field | field non-local | PIC, semi-Lagrangian | [BGK & Vlasov](../05-kinetic/02-bgk-vlasov-and-plasma-kinetics.md) |
| radiative transfer $\Omega\cdot\nabla I=-\kappa I+j$ | 1st in space, integral in angle | **no (angle)** | $S_N$, Monte Carlo, asymptotic-preserving | [Radiative transfer](../05-kinetic/04-radiative-transfer.md) |
| stochastic PDE | stochastic | — | strong vs weak convergence | [Stochastic PDEs](../06-beyond/07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md) |
| variational inequality | constraint | yes | semismooth Newton, active sets | [Variational inequalities](../06-beyond/12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md) |
| gradient flow $\partial_t\rho=-\mathcal M\,\delta F/\delta\rho$ | depends on metric | yes | energy-stable schemes, JKO | [Optimal transport](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md) |
| MHD induction | mixed hyperbolic/parabolic | yes | constrained transport, HLLD | [MHD](../06-beyond/13-mhd-and-relativistic-transport.md) |

## Quick classification guide

* **Order** fixes the number of boundary conditions and the stencil width.
* **Type** (elliptic/parabolic/hyperbolic) fixes the boundary-data requirements and the numerical family.
* **Locality** decides sparse versus dense/hierarchical algebra.
* **Constraints** ($\nabla\cdot\mathbf u=0$, $\nabla\cdot\mathbf B=0$, inequalities) need separate
  enforcement and produce saddle-point structure.
* **Linearity** decides whether you solve once or iterate.

## Higher order, systems and non-PDE forms

The table above is the classical catalogue; these are the entries that make it general
([general classification](../01-foundations/10-general-pde-classification.md),
[higher order](../01-foundations/11-higher-order-and-dispersive.md)).

| Operator / equation | Order & type | Local? | Notes, numerics | Explained in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| principal symbol $\sigma_L(\mathbf x,\mathbf k)$ | *any* order, any $d$ | — | classification = roots of the symbol; test your *discrete* symbol against it | [general classification](../01-foundations/10-general-pde-classification.md) |
| first-order symmetric system $u_t+\sum_jA_ju_{x_j}=Bu$ | 1st system, symmetric hyperbolic | yes | energy estimates; finite speed; goundation of GR/MHD/elasticity codes | same note |
| constrained hyperbolic system | 1st/2nd system + constraints | yes | projection/constrained transport/constraint damping | [variational principles & Noether](../06-beyond/15-variational-principles-and-noether.md) |
| dispersive $u_t+u_{xxx}=0$ (Airy/KdV) | 3rd, dispersive | yes | spectral or high-order; low-order dissipation destroys solitons | [integrating solitons](../06-beyond/14-integrable-systems-and-solitons.md) |
| hyper-diffusive $u_t=-a_4\partial_x^4u$, $a_4>0$ | 4th, parabolic | yes | stabilising; $\Delta t\sim\Delta x^4$; hyperviscosity must be reported | [higher order](../01-foundations/11-higher-order-and-dispersive.md) |
| Kuramoto–Sivashinsky | 4th, unstable band | yes | spatiotemporal chaos; attractor dimension grows with size | same note |
| thin-film / lubrication | 4th (often degenerate) | yes | free surface, finite-wavelength dewetting | [free boundaries](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md) |
| phase-field crystal | 6th, pattern-forming | yes | crystalline order; narrow unstable band | [higher order](../01-foundations/11-higher-order-and-dispersive.md) |
| strain-gradient / micropolar | 4th, elliptic (mixed form) | yes | two BCs per edge; mesh-objective localisation | [microcontinuum](../06-beyond/11-microcontinuum-and-generalized-continua.md) |
| delay PDE / retarded functional equation | hyperbolic + history | yes | store & interpolate history; characteristic-aware schemes | [delay & retarded transport](../06-beyond/16-delay-and-retarded-transport.md) |
| Volterra / hereditary integral | time-nonlocal | no (time) | sum-of-exponentials, convolution quadrature | [memory & viscoelasticity](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md) |
| master equation on a graph/lattice | discrete state space | edges | sparse exponentials, kinetic Monte Carlo, Gillespie | [why everything flows](../11-universality/01-why-everything-flows.md) |
| Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman (control) | 1st, nonlinear, viscosity | yes | monotone schemes; value iteration = discrete HJ | [algorithms](../11-universality/05-computation-and-algorithms.md) |
| mean-field game system | HJB $+$ Fokker–Planck coupled | yes (both) | fixed-point iteration; monotonicity for uniqueness | [finance & economics](../08-bridges/02-finance-and-economics.md) |
| Ricci / mean-curvature / harmonic-map flow | 2nd, parabolic in geometry | yes (geo) | Lyapunov monotonicity as a numerical diagnostic | [mathematics itself](../11-universality/08-mathematics-itself.md) |
| minimum-cost flow, assignment, EMD | discrete optimal transport | graph | network simplex; the exact sibling of Sinkhorn | same note |
| Markov category / probabilistic program | compositional (no derivatives) | — | semantics, not numerics | [information & inference](../11-universality/07-information-statistics-and-inference.md) |
