# Key derivations


# Key derivations

Each section states the starting point, the one decisive step, and the result. If you can reproduce these
ten, you can follow every note in the atlas.

## 1 · Fick's law from a random walk
Start from the master equation $p_{n+1}(x)=\frac12p_n(x-a)+\frac12p_n(x+a)$. Taylor expand both sides to
second order: $\partial_tp=\frac{a^2}{2\tau}\partial_x^2p$, so $D=a^2/2\tau$ and
$\langle x^2\rangle=2Dt$. **Decisive step:** the flux is the *difference* of the two hopping fluxes,
$-D\partial_xp$. → [Diffusion](../03-microscopic/01-diffusion.md)

## 2 · The diffusion Green function
Solve $\partial_tc=D\partial_x^2c$, $c(x,0)=M\delta(x)$ by Fourier transform:
$\hat c(k,t)=Me^{-Dk^2t}$, invert to get the Gaussian with variance $2Dt$. **Decisive step:** in Fourier
space diffusion is multiplication by $e^{-Dk^2t}$.
→ [Spectral view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md)

## 3 · The divergence theorem step of every balance law
Volume balance → divergence theorem → integrands equal (arbitrary volume) → local PDE. **Decisive step:**
the domain is arbitrary, so the integrand must vanish pointwise. This is where jump conditions come from
when smoothness fails. → [Balance laws](../01-foundations/01-balance-laws-and-flux.md)

## 4 · Reynolds transport theorem
Differentiate a moving-volume integral, use $\dot{\mathbf x}=\mathbf u$ and the divergence theorem:
$\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm dt}\int_Vc\,\mathrm dV=\int_V[\partial_tc+\nabla\cdot(c\mathbf u)]\mathrm dV$.
**Decisive step:** the boundary moves with the material, so a surface term appears.
→ [Material derivative](../01-foundations/02-material-derivative.md)

## 5 · Einstein's 1905 argument
Balance the diffusive flux $-D\nabla c$ against the drift flux $c\,\mu_{\rm mob}\mathbf F$ at equilibrium,
where $c\propto e^{-V/k_BT}$; matching gives $D=\mu_{\rm mob}k_BT$. With Stokes drag
$\mu_{\rm mob}=1/(6\pi\mu a)$, $D=k_BT/(6\pi\mu a)$.
→ [Brownian motion](../03-microscopic/02-brownian-motion-and-einstein-relations.md)

## 6 · Taylor–Aris dispersion
Decompose the velocity as mean + fluctuation, and the concentration into a cross-sectional mean plus a
deviation. Solve the deviation to leading order in the radial diffusion time, then average: the mean
satisfies a 1-D advection–diffusion equation with
$D_{\rm eff}=D+a^2U^2/48D$. **Decisive step:** the *solvability condition* of the cell problem (the mean
of the deviation equation must vanish). Removes the ambiguity and identifies the effective diffusivity.
→ [Shear dispersion](../02-bulk/06-shear-dispersion-taylor-aris.md)

## 7 · Rayleigh–Bénard onset
Linearise the Boussinesq equations about the conduction state, eliminate pressure and velocity to get a
single equation for the temperature perturbation, and impose stress-free or no-slip boundaries. The
smallest eigenvalue over allowed wavenumbers gives $\mathrm{Ra}_c=1708$ with $k_c\approx3.12$ (rigid).
**Decisive step:** the eigenvalue problem is *linear*, so the onset is sharp.
→ [Convection](../02-bulk/02-convection-and-buoyancy.md)

## 8 · Stefan similarity solution
Seek $T=f(\eta)$ with $\eta=x/\sqrt{\alpha t}$ and an interface at $s=2\lambda\sqrt{\alpha t}$. The heat
equation becomes an ODE; the Stefan condition at the interface becomes the transcendental equation for
$\lambda$. **Decisive step:** the similarity ansatz simultaneously satisfies the PDE and the free-boundary
condition only for this scaling. → [Phase change](../04-interaction/09-phase-change-and-stefan.md)

## 9 · Barenblatt–Pattle profile for the porous medium equation
For $\partial_tc=\Delta c^m$ seek a self-similar compactly supported solution
$c=t^{-\alpha}f(r t^{-\beta})$ with $\alpha=d/(d(m-1)+2)$; the ODE for $f$ is solvable and yields the
profile that attracts all compact data. **Decisive step:** mass conservation fixes the exponents, and
degeneracy at $c=0$ produces the moving edge.
→ [Nonlinear diffusion](../06-beyond/06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md)

## 10 · JKO: heat flow as steepest descent of entropy
Show that $\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm dt}\int\rho\ln\rho=-\int\|\nabla\ln\rho\|^2\rho$, and that minimising
$F+\frac{1}{2\tau}W_2^2$ over $\rho$ with $F=\int\rho\ln\rho$ recovers one implicit heat step. **Decisive
step:** expanding $W_2^2$ for small displacements turns the Wasserstein gradient into $\nabla\cdot(\rho\nabla
\delta F/\delta\rho)$. → [Optimal transport](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md)

## Five more derivations (higher order, geometry, stochastic, collective)

These extend the list to the material added in the extended atlas — the general classification, higher orders,
dimensions, collective phenomena and inference.

## 11 · Classification from the principal symbol
Given an operator of order $m$, keep only the highest derivatives, substitute $\partial\to i\mathbf k$ and study
the polynomial $\sigma_L(\mathbf x,\mathbf k)$. **Decisive step:** type is a property of the *symbol*, not of the
equation's lower-order terms — which is why the same classification covers any order, any dimension and any
system. → [general classification](../01-foundations/10-general-pde-classification.md)

## 12 · Parity and sign rules for higher-order operators
For $u_t=a_m\partial_x^mu$, the dispersion relation is $\omega=a_mi^{m+1}k^m$. **Decisive step:** read off the
parity — odd $m$ gives real $\omega$ (dispersion, no growth), even $m$ gives imaginary $\omega$ (dissipation),
and the *sign* of an even-order coefficient decides smoothing versus short-wave blow-up (the Lax–Mizohata
condition). → [higher order & dispersive](../01-foundations/11-higher-order-and-dispersive.md)

## 13 · Green function and first-passage distribution for drift–diffusion
Solve $\partial_tp=-\mu\partial_xp+\frac{\sigma^2}{2}\partial_x^2p$ on $(-\theta,\theta)$ with absorbing
boundaries; the first-passage density follows from the flux at the boundary. **Decisive step:** reaction times
are *boundary fluxes* of a diffusion — the same calculation for chemical reactions (Kramers), decision making
(DDM) and psychophysics. → [cognitive science & mind](../11-universality/03-cognitive-science-and-mind.md)

## 14 · Fisher metric from invariance (statement and use)
Require a Riemannian metric on the space of distributions that is invariant under sufficient statistics; the
unique answer (up to scale) is $g_{ij}=\mathbb E[\partial_i\ln p\,\partial_j\ln p]$. **Decisive step:** the
geometry is *forced*, so natural gradient is not a heuristic but the invariant steepest-descent direction.
→ [information, statistics & inference](../11-universality/07-information-statistics-and-inference.md)

## 15 · Small-noise limit of Fokker–Planck gives Hamilton–Jacobi
Insert $p\sim e^{-S/\varepsilon}$ into $\partial_tp=-\partial_x(bp)+\frac\varepsilon2\partial_x^2p$ and keep the
leading order in $\varepsilon$: you get $\partial_tS+H(\partial_xS)=0$ with $H=b\,p$. **Decisive step:** the
classical action is the rate function of a rare event — which is why WKB, large deviations (Sanov,
Freidlin–Wentzell) and optimal control share formulas.
→ [information, statistics & inference](../11-universality/07-information-statistics-and-inference.md)

## The habits these ten derivations teach

1. **Always find the solvability condition** — it is usually where the physics enters (items 6, 9).
2. **Non-dimensionalise early** — the dimensionless numbers fall out of the algebra, not from memory
   (items 7, 8).
3. **Get the equilibrium right first** — a diffusive flux must be expressible as a drift of a Boltzmann
   factor (item 5), otherwise your constitutive law violates the second law.
4. **Check conservation at every step** — the local form is a derived convenience; the integral statement
   is the physics (item 3).

# Measurement & experiments


# Measurement & experiments

> **One-line picture.** Nature never reports a coefficient; it reports a signal. Every measurement is an
> inverse problem in disguise.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [inverse problems & data assimilation](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)

## L1 · The catalogue

| Quantity | Method | What is measured | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| diffusion coefficient $D$ (liquid) | Taylor dispersion in a capillary, diaphragm cell | axial spreading | extremely precise; needs a well-characterised profile |
| $D$ (polymer, colloid) | dynamic light scattering, PFG-NMR, single-particle tracking | fluctuation correlation / displacement | DLS gives $D$ and size; SPT reveals anomalies |
| $D$ in porous media | column tracer tests, push–pull tests | breakthrough curves | inversion for $D$, $v$, retardation, kinetics |
| thermal conductivity $k$ | steady-state guard method, laser flash (LFA), 3$\omega$, thermoreflectance | temperature response | thin films need thermoreflectance; interface conductance separately |
| viscosity $\mu$ | capillary, rotational, falling ball | stress–strain-rate relation | non-Newtonian fluids need a full flow curve |
| permeability $K$ | core flooding, well tests (Theis analysis) | flow rate / drawdown | scale-dependent: core vs field values differ |
| reaction rate constants | batch reactors, plug-flow reactors, TGA/DSC | concentration vs time | Arrhenius fits require several temperatures |
| dispersion/dispersivity | column and field tracer tests | plume moments / breakthrough shape | often scale-dependent: report the length |
| velocity fields | PIV/PTV, LDV, ultrasonic | instantaneous velocity | PIV resolves structures; LDV gives point accuracy |
| concentration fields | PLIF, absorption spectroscopy, Raman | planar/point concentration | PLIF needs calibration for laser sheet non-uniformity |
| phase change | DSC/TGA, neutron/X-ray imaging, thermocouples | latent heat, front position | front tracking needs imaging |
| surface phenomena | tensiometry, ellipsometry, QCM | surface tension, film thickness, adsorption | contact-angle hysteresis is common |

## L2 · The four rules of transport measurement

1. **A measurement is a convolution.** You measure the response of your apparatus, not the material:
   finite injection widths, sensor dynamics and boundary effects all smear the signal. Deconvolve, or
   design the experiment so the instrument's response is much shorter than the physics.
2. **Fit the model you actually have.** Extracting $D$ from a plume assumes Fickian behaviour; if the
   transport is anomalous, the fitted $D$ drifts with scale. Report the fitting window and test for scale
   dependence.
3. **Report uncertainty and sensitivity together.** Transport coefficients often enter exponentially
   (Arrhenius, Kramers), so a 10% coefficient error can be a factor-of-two rate error.
4. **Design to break degeneracy.** Velocity, dispersion, retardation and kinetic sorption all shift or
   spread a breakthrough curve; use multiple observation points, multiple flow rates, and non-sorbing
   tracers to separate them
   ([sorption & retardation](../04-interaction/07-sorption-and-retardation.md)).

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Identifiability is a property of the experiment, not the data volume.** More samples do not fix a
  structurally degenerate design; the resolution matrix must be studied before the campaign
  ([inverse problems](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)).
* **Moment analysis is robust and assumption-light**: the first two moments of a breakthrough curve give
  velocity and dispersion without fitting any functional form — the recommended first pass.
* **Frequency-domain measurements trade time for resolution**: periodic forcing (modulated
  thermoreflectance, oscillatory shear) measures the *frequency-dependent* coefficient $D(\omega)$,
  $\eta(\omega)$ directly, exposing memory that a single steady number hides
  ([linear response & memory](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md)).
* **Well-designed experiments measure ratios**, not absolutes: the ratio of dispersion to diffusion, of
  $\mathrm{Sh}$ to $\mathrm{Nu}$, or of two thermal conductivities cancels apparatus errors.

## L4 · Practice

* **Calibrate the apparatus with a known sample** before measuring an unknown one, and report the
  calibration uncertainty separately from the measurement spread.
* **Check the dimensionless groups of the apparatus** (Péclet of the test section, Biot number of the
  sample, $\mathrm{Kn}$ of the gas) so the experiment sits in the intended regime.
* **Cross-validate with an independent method** — thermoreflectance against laser flash, PFG-NMR against
  DLS — because systematic errors are method-specific.
* **Publish the raw signal and the fit window**, not only the coefficient: reproducibility depends on the
  former.

## Checks

1. Why is a tracer test an inverse problem? *(You measure concentration histories, not coefficients; the
   parameters come from fitting or moment analysis.)*
2. Why is a frequency-domain measurement more informative than a steady one? *(It resolves the memory
   kernel/frequency dependence, not just a single lumped value.)*

# History, etymology & open problems


# History, etymology & open problems

> Knowing the *sequence* makes ideas memorable, the *etymology* makes them intuitive, and the *open
> problems* keep you honest about what is settled.

## A transport timeline

| Year | Person | Contribution |
|---|---|---|
| 1822 | Fourier | *Théorie analytique de la chaleur*: Fourier series + the heat equation |
| 1827 | Brown | pollen jitter, unexplained for 78 years |
| 1828/1845 | Navier / Stokes | momentum transport with viscous stress |
| 1855 | Fick | diffusion equation, by analogy with Fourier |
| 1856 | Darcy | linear flow law for water through sand |
| 1857 | Clausius | mean free path: kinetic theory begins |
| 1872 | Boltzmann | kinetic equation and the H-theorem |
| 1904 | Prandtl | boundary-layer theory |
| 1905 | Einstein / Smoluchowski | Brownian motion; Stokes–Einstein relation |
| 1931 | Onsager | reciprocal relations for coupled transport |
| 1934 | Zeldovich, Frank-Kamenetskii, Semenov | combustion fronts, thermal explosion |
| 1937 | Fisher; KPP | reaction–diffusion fronts and their speed |
| 1941 | Kolmogorov | turbulence cascade and the $-5/3$ spectrum |
| 1948 | Cattaneo, Vernotte | finite-speed heat conduction |
| 1951/1957 | Green / Kubo | transport coefficients from equilibrium correlations |
| 1952 | Turing | diffusion-driven pattern formation |
| 1953–56 | Taylor, Aris | shear dispersion in tubes |
| 1955–56 | Lighthill, Whitham, Richards | traffic flow as a conservation law |
| 1958 | Cahn–Hilliard | spinodal decomposition |
| 1960/1965 | Mori / Zwanzig | memory kernels and projection operators |
| 1967 | Strang | second-order operator splitting |
| 1970 | Keller–Segel | chemotaxis |
| 1974/1998 | Benamou–Brenier / JKO | optimal transport and gradient flows |
| 1986–88 | Harten–Osher–Engquist / Shu–Osher | ENO and WENO schemes |
| 1990s | (many) | lattice-Boltzmann, DSMC and kinetic solvers mainstream |
| 2019+ | machine learning | neural operators, PINNs, learned closures |

## Etymology: the words carry the pictures

| Word | Origin | Literal image |
|---|---|---|
| advection | *advehere* "carry to" | a cart carrying goods |
| convection | *convehere* "carry together" | a crowd moving as one body |
| diffusion | *diffundere* "pour apart" | wine spreading on a table |
| conduction | *conducere* "lead together" | a bucket brigade of energy |
| dispersion | *dispergere* "scatter" | seed thrown by a sower |
| dissipation | *dissipare* "squander" | money scattered to the winds |
| radiation | *radius* "spoke, ray" | rays leaving a source |
| efflux / influx | *fluere* "flow" | flowing out / flowing in |
| adsorption | *ad-* + *sorbere* "suck to" | a sponge grabbing molecules |
| absorption | *ab-* + *sorbere* "suck away" | swallowed into the bulk |
| accretion | *accrescere* "grow to" | a snowball rolling downhill |
| subsidence | *subsidere* "sit below" | the ground taking a seat |
| viscoelastic | *viscus* "mistletoe glue" | a material that remembers |

The Latin prefixes do the physical work — *ad-* to, *con-* together, *dis-* apart, *sub-* below,
*ab-* away — which is why four roots (*vehere*, *fundere*, *sorbere*, *fluere*) generate the entire
vocabulary.

## Open problems (a working list)

1. **3-D Navier–Stokes regularity** (Millennium Prize): does smooth data stay smooth forever?
2. **Turbulence closures with guarantees**: accurate *and* provably realisable and conservative.
3. **Anomalous transport as a predictive theory**: parameters from medium statistics, not fits.
4. **High-Weissenberg viscoelastic simulation**: separating physical from numerical instability.
5. **Reactive turbulent transport at scale**: combustion and subsurface reactive mixing remain hard.
6. **Long-time statistics of gradient flows**: coarsening, metastability and ageing need new methods.
7. **Structure-preserving learned models**: closures that respect entropy, positivity and conservation.
8. **UQ for multiphysics**: coupling error and model-form error propagating through long integrations.
9. **Nonlocal-to-local limits**: asymptotically compatible discretisations for general kernels.
10. **Rare events and extremes**: escape, nucleation, fracture and failure beyond heuristics.

# Universality


# Universality: the same mathematics everywhere

This part of the atlas makes the strong claim explicit and then *tests* it, domain by domain:
**almost every model of change we build is a flow of a density — and flows of densities are that one
balance law, its generators, its functionals and its metrics.** The claim is about mathematics, not
about the world: it says that *if* you can name what is being redistributed, the rest of the atlas
applies. What it does not say is written down just as carefully in the last note.

| # | Note | What it settles |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | [Why everything flows](01-why-everything-flows.md) | the five universal structures (continuity, generators, functionals, metrics, optimality) and exactly when each applies |
| 2 | [The decision tree & lookup](02-decision-tree-and-lookup.md) | your object → its transport form. A worked translation table with ~60 entries |
| 3 | [Cognitive science & mind](03-cognitive-science-and-mind.md) | evidence accumulation, predictive coding, free energy, neural fields, memory decay, semantic diffusion |
| 4 | [Social behaviour, economics & language](04-social-behaviour-economics-language.md) | opinion dynamics, wealth distributions, innovation diffusion, segregation, language change |
| 5 | [Computation & algorithms](05-computation-and-algorithms.md) | Dijkstra as Hamilton–Jacobi, PageRank as diffusion, SGD as a Fokker–Planck flow, sampling as transport |
| 6 | [Physics, chemistry & field theory](06-physics-chemistry-and-field-theory.md) | relativity, quantum mechanics, quantum field theory, the renormalisation group as coarse-graining |
| 7 | [Information, statistics & inference](07-information-statistics-and-inference.md) | Fisher geometry, natural gradient, large deviations, Bayesian updating as transport of measures |
| 8 | [Mathematics itself](08-mathematics-itself.md) | Ricci flow, mean-curvature flow, Markov categories — transport as the connective tissue of mathematics |
| 9 | [Limits of the claim](09-limits-of-the-claim.md) | where the analogy is a theorem, where it is a model, and where it is only a metaphor |

![**Figure U1.** The concept map, including this part: the arrows are *prerequisites* — the same few
ideas feed every field.](concept-map.pdf)

## How to use this part

* If you have a **model** from another field, start at the [lookup table](02-decision-tree-and-lookup.md):
  find your object, read the transport form, follow the link.
* If you want the **argument**, read [why everything flows](01-why-everything-flows.md) — it is a
  theorem-by-theorem statement of what makes a system a transport system.
* If you are **sceptical** (you should be), read the [limits note](09-limits-of-the-claim.md) first. A
  claim that cannot say where it fails is not a claim, it is advertising.

> **The one-sentence version.** Any quantity you can count, whose changes can be attributed to movement
> across boundaries plus creation and destruction, is a density obeying the transport equation; and if
> the movement is driven by a gradient, a potential, an entropy, an optimality principle or a random walk,
> then the whole machinery of the atlas — classification, well-posedness, numerics, limits, universality
> classes — applies unchanged. What is *not* covered is the meaning of the quantity, the measurement of
> it, and the discreteness and semantics of the structures it lives on.

\newpage

# Why everything flows


# Why everything flows

> **One-line picture.** Five structures, and almost every quantitative model of change has at least one of
> them: a *balance* of a countable quantity, a *generator* of a random process, a *functional* that is
> being minimised, an *optimality* principle, or a *symmetry*. Each yields a transport equation, and the
> rest of the atlas applies.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Balance laws & flux](../01-foundations/01-balance-laws-and-flux.md),
> [Entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [The translation lookup](../11-universality/02-decision-tree-and-lookup.md),
> [Limits of the claim](../11-universality/09-limits-of-the-claim.md)

## L1 · The claim, stated so that it can be checked

The honest version of "everything can be described by this mathematics" is:

> **If you can name a quantity, and account for every change in it as movement across boundaries plus
> creation/destruction, then you have a transport model. If in addition the movement is driven by a
> random process, a potential, an entropy, an optimality principle or a symmetry, then you have one of
> the five standard *types* of transport model — with classification, well-posedness theory, error
> analysis and universality classes already worked out.**

That is a statement about *models*, not about the world, and it is decidable in each case: either you can
write the balance, the generator, the functional, the objective or the symmetry — or you cannot. When you
can, this atlas is your toolbox. When you cannot, [note 9](../11-universality/09-limits-of-the-claim.md)
says why.

The striking part is how often you can. Density of cars on a road, probability of a belief, calcium ions
in a spine, wealth in a population, sentences in a corpus, packets in a router, neurons in a field of
thousands, eigenvalues of a random matrix, energy in a climate model, information in a business
forecast — all of them have been *written down* in one of the five forms, and each time the mathematics
transferred wholesale.

## L2 · The five universal structures

| # | Structure | What you supply | What you get | Family |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Balance / continuity** | a countable quantity + local exchange | $\partial_t\mu+\nabla\!\cdot\!(\mu\mathbf v)=\sigma$ (measure-valued) | advection, dispersion, conservation laws, population balances |
| 2 | **Generator of a random process** | transition rules (jump, diffuse, or both) | forward equation for the law: master equation, Fokker–Planck, Kolmogorov | diffusion, queues, epidemics, chemical kinetics, RL, MCMC |
| 3 | **Functional to be minimised** | an energy/entropy + a metric | gradient flow; Lyapunov decay; stability | heat flow, Cahn–Hilliard, porous medium, ML training, variational inference |
| 4 | **Optimality / control** | a cost and dynamics | Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman (value) + dual density flow (MFG) | optimal control, RL, optimal transport, Schrödinger bridges, economics |
| 5 | **Symmetry** | invariance of the action/functional | conservation laws, constraints, reciprocity, exponents | Noether, gauge theory, scaling laws, Onsager |

Two *meta-structures* ride on top:

* **Coarse-graining (renormalisation).** Whenever you cannot resolve everything, you eliminate variables —
  and the price is memory and noise ([Mori–Zwanzig](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md)). Iterating that
  step gives the renormalisation group: fixed points of coarse-graining are **universality classes**, which
  is *why* a magnetic alloy, a fluid interface and certain bacteria colonies share exponents.
* **Information–entropy bookkeeping.** Any bounded observer of a stochastic system must lose information at
  a rate set by dissipation: fluctuation theorems (Jarzynski, Crooks) and Landauer's principle are the
  transport equations of *knowledge* about a system ([limits note](../11-universality/09-limits-of-the-claim.md)).

**Structure 1 is the widest, and the reason is trivial.** Suppose $\mu_t$ is any family of measures (or
densities, or counts) and suppose that the only way mass changes in a region is that it crosses the
boundary or is created inside. Then for every smooth test function $\varphi$,

$$\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int\varphi\,\mathrm d\mu_t
=\int\nabla\varphi\cdot\mathbf v\,\mathrm d\mu_t+\int\varphi\,\sigma\,\mathrm d\mu_t ,$$

which *is* the continuity equation in weak form. No smoothness, no differentiability, no grid, no
metric — Dirac masses, empirical distributions, graph measures and percolation clusters all qualify. This
is the single most useful sentence in the atlas: **if your quantity is countable and moves locally, you
already have a transport equation** — you have only to identify the velocity (or the jump kernel) and the
source.

## L3 · What is a theorem, what is a model, what is a heuristic

Being precise here is what separates a real universality claim from wishful thinking.

| Structure | Status | Conditions, caveats |
|---|---|---|
| 1 · balance ⟹ continuity (weak form) | **theorem** | exact for measure-valued solutions; identifying $\mathbf v$ (or a jump kernel) is the modelling step. Pure jumps give *nonlocal* transport, not an advection equation |
| 2 · Markov process ⟹ forward equation | **theorem** (Feller semigroups) | the law's equation is the dual of the generator: diffusion ⟹ parabolic, jumps ⟹ master equation, Lévy ⟹ fractional, mixtures ⟹ Liouville + reset (PDMP). *Kramers–Moyal* shows Fokker–Planck is exact only for continuous paths with finite first two moments |
| 3 · functional ⟹ gradient flow | **theorem *if* the structure exists** | needs a Lyapunov functional *and* a metric; De Giorgi/JKO/$\Gamma$-convergence give existence, contraction, convergence. Many systems are *not* gradient flows; the general split is reversible + dissipative (GENERIC/metriplectic) |
| 4 · optimality ⟹ HJB (+ dual flow) | **theorem** under regularity | dynamic programming for deterministic and stochastic control (viscosity solutions); mean-field games need monotonicity for uniqueness; optimal transport is the static dual, Schrödinger bridges the entropic version |
| 5 · symmetry ⟹ conservation | **theorem** (Noether I & II) | conservation laws; *constraints* from the second theorem; reciprocity (Onsager) and exponents (dimensional analysis + RG) |
| meta · coarse-graining ⟹ RG | **heuristic in general, theorem in specific models** | fixed points of coarse-graining explain universality classes; exponents come with corrections, and mean-field theory is exact only above the upper critical dimension |
| meta · information loss | **theorem** (fluctuation theorems, Landauer) | dissipation sets the rate at which an observer loses information about a stochastic system |

**Three silent assumptions to check every single time.**

1. **Range of influence.** Local PDE, nonlocal kernel, or network edges? This choice is not cosmetic: it
   changes the boundary data, the well-posedness theory and the solver
   ([nonlocal operators](../06-beyond/03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md)).
2. **Is your state Markovian?** If the future depends on the past beyond the current state, the density you
   wrote is incomplete: either enlarge the state (include age, history, internal variables) or carry
   memory — which converts a diffusion into a Volterra/fractional problem
   ([memory](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md),
   [delay](../06-beyond/16-delay-and-retarded-transport.md)).
3. **Is the measured density the modelled density?** Observation has its own transport (sampling bias,
   aggregation bias, instrument response). Skipping this step is what makes many social-science and
   epidemiological fits look better — or worse — than they are
   ([inverse problems](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)).

**And two honest warnings.**

* **Non-identifiability.** Different microscopic mechanisms can generate the *same* macroscopic transport
  (same drift, same diffusion). Transport equations are therefore excellent for prediction and poor for
  mechanism — unless you design experiments that separate them
  ([measurement](../10-appendix/05-measurement-and-experiments.md)).
* **Mean-field breakdown.** Every transport model assumes fluctuations average out. In finite populations,
  low copy numbers and small systems they do not, and you must return to the stochastic description. This is
  the exact analogue of the **Knudsen number**: the ratio of the microscopic correlation scale to the system
  scale, and the criterion for whether the continuum description is even defined.

## L4 · How to perform the translation (a checklist you can run)

1. **Name the quantity** with units, and say whether it is extensive (total), per-capita, or relative.
2. **Write the balance**: what crosses boundaries, and what is created or destroyed? If you cannot, there is
   no transport model — this is [balance laws & flux](../01-foundations/01-balance-laws-and-flux.md) and nothing more.
3. **Identify the driver**: gradient (constitutive law), randomness (generator), optimality (control),
   symmetry (conservation), or memory (kernel / fractional derivative).
4. **Identify the geometry**: Euclidean, Wasserstein, Fisher–Rao, graph, manifold. The metric decides which
   functional decreases and which numerical method is stable.
5. **Non-dimensionalise**, find the regime, classify the operator
   ([general classification](../01-foundations/10-general-pde-classification.md)).
6. **Find the invariants and the Lyapunov functional** — or prove that none exists; then look for oscillatory
   or limit-cycle behaviour and expect phase error to dominate your numerics.
7. **Choose numerics from the structure**, not from habit
   ([discretisation](../07-numerics/01-discretization-consistency-stability-convergence.md)).
8. **State the validity range**, including the mean-field/Knudsen condition from L3, and say what you would do
   if it failed.

> [!warning] Trap
> A successful translation is not a proof of mechanism. It buys you decades of mathematics — stability,
> well-posedness, error estimates, universality classes — but it never tells you that the underlying
> mechanism *is* a diffusive flow. Two models with the same transport behaviour are indistinguishable from
> macroscopic data alone, and that is an identifiability statement, not a mystery.

## Checks

1. When does the law of a Markov chain satisfy a *diffusion* equation? *(Only when paths are continuous with
   finite first two moments; otherwise it satisfies a master/kinetic equation — jumps, memory and fractional
   operators in general.)*
2. Which structure do a Hamiltonian system and a Brusselator oscillator have? *(Neither is a gradient flow:
   the first is volume-preserving transport (Liouville), the second has limit cycles and no Lyapunov
   functional — so no monotone functional and no automatic contraction.)*
3. What is the social-science analogue of the Knudsen number? *(System size relative to the interaction
   correlation length: with few agents per scale, mean-field descriptions fail and agent-based stochastic
   simulation is required.)*
4. Why are universality exponents usually not theorems? *(They need renormalisation-group arguments or limit
   theorems, available for specific models; in general they are asymptotic hypotheses with corrections.)*

# The decision tree & lookup


# The decision tree & lookup

> **One-line picture.** Find your object on the left, read the transport form on the right, follow the link.
> If your object is not here, the [checklist](01-why-everything-flows.md) will get you there in eight steps.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Why everything flows](01-why-everything-flows.md) ·
> **Leads to.** the domain notes of this part, and every note they link to

## Step 1 · The three-question decision tree

```
        Is there a countable quantity whose changes you can attribute
        to crossing a boundary + creation/destruction?
                          │
            ┌── no ───────┴─────── yes ──────────────────────────────┐
            │                                                       │
   Not (yet) a transport problem.                        What drives the movement?
   Try: enlarge the state,                          ┌──────────┬──────────┬─────────┐
   change the observable,                        gradient    random     optimal  symmetry
   or read the limits note.                          │          │          │        │
                                                     ▼          ▼          ▼        ▼
                                              constitutive  generator   HJB +    Noether
                                              law ⟹ PDE     ⟹ master / dual     ⟹ conserved
                                              (Fick,        FP /       density    quantities,
                                              Fourier,      kinetic    flow       constraints
                                              Darcy, …)                (MFG, OT)
                                                     │          │          │        │
                                                     └──────────┴──────────┴────────┘
                                                                  │
                            classify the operator (order, type, dimension),
                            non-dimensionalise, find the invariants/Lyapunov functional,
                            choose the numerics, and state the validity range.
```

## Step 2 · The lookup table

### Physics and engineering

| Your object | Transport form | Note |
|---|---|---|
| heat, solute, momentum in a continuum | advection–diffusion; Navier–Stokes | [diffusion](../01-foundations/01-balance-laws-and-flux.md) |
| a shock, a traffic jam, a flood wave | scalar conservation law + entropy condition | [shocks](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md) |
| a flame, an epidemic front, an invader | reaction–diffusion front, speed $2\sqrt{Dk}$ | [fronts](../04-interaction/05-fronts-and-travelling-waves.md) |
| a phase boundary (melting, freezing) | Stefan free boundary | [phase change](../04-interaction/09-phase-change-and-stefan.md) |
| a rough growing interface | KPZ / stochastic growth | [SPDEs](../06-beyond/07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md) |
| light or neutrons in a medium | radiative transfer (integro-differential) | [radiative transfer](../05-kinetic/04-radiative-transfer.md) |
| a rarefied gas, a micro-device | Boltzmann/kinetic; DSMC | [Boltzmann](../05-kinetic/01-boltzmann-equation.md) |
| a plasma, a fusion edge | Vlasov + Maxwell; gyrokinetics | [plasma kinetics](../05-kinetic/02-bgk-vlasov-and-plasma-kinetics.md) |
| a fibre-optic pulse, a rogue wave | nonlinear Schrödinger (integrable) | [solitons](../06-beyond/14-integrable-systems-and-solitons.md) |
| a suspension, a granular material | kinetic theory of granular gases; nonlocal rheology | [rheology](../02-bulk/09-non-newtonian-rheology.md) |
| spacetime itself | second-order constrained hyperbolic system | [MHD & relativity](../06-beyond/13-mhd-and-relativistic-transport.md) |
| quantum probability | continuity equation for $\lvert\psi\rvert^2$; Wigner transport | [quantum matter](../08-bridges/10-quantum-and-condensed-matter.md) |
| a quantum field | propagators (Green functions); RG flow of couplings | [mathematics itself](08-mathematics-itself.md) |
| turbulence | stochastic cascade + closure problem | [turbulence](../02-bulk/05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md) |
| a climate model | advection–diffusion on a rotating sphere + radiation | [geoscience](../08-bridges/04-geoscience-and-climate.md) |
| ice sheets, glaciers | nonlinear (Glen) viscous flow, free surface | [nonlinear diffusion](../06-beyond/06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md) |
| an electrochemical cell | porous-electrode Nernst–Planck + porous flow | [charged transport](../03-microscopic/07-charged-and-electrochemical-transport.md) |
| a composite material | homogenised tensor transport | [homogenisation](../06-beyond/08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md) |
| a crack, a damage zone | nonlocal (peridynamic) mechanics | [microcontinuum](../06-beyond/11-microcontinuum-and-generalized-continua.md) |

### Life, mind and behaviour

| Your object | Transport form | Note |
|---|---|---|
| a chemical reaction network | master equation / mass-action ODE | [kinetics](../04-interaction/01-reaction-kinetics-and-sinks.md) |
| a gene network with bursting | piecewise-deterministic Markov process (Liouville + reset) | [why everything flows](01-why-everything-flows.md) |
| transport inside a cell | crowding-limited anomalous diffusion | [anomalous diffusion](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md) |
| embryonic patterning | reaction–diffusion / Turing | [Turing patterns](../04-interaction/04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md) |
| bacterial chemotaxis, immune recruitment | Keller–Segel; blow-up threshold $8\pi/\chi$ | [chemotaxis](../04-interaction/06-chemotaxis-and-keller-segel.md) |
| a neuron's membrane potential | cable equation; excitable medium; conduction delay | [delay](../06-beyond/16-delay-and-retarded-transport.md) |
| neural population activity | neural-field equations; mean-field density of spiking networks | [cognitive science](03-cognitive-science-and-mind.md) |
| perceptual decision making | drift–diffusion; first-passage time distributions | [cognitive science](03-cognitive-science-and-mind.md) |
| predictive coding, active inference | variational free-energy descent (KL gradient flow) | [cognitive science](03-cognitive-science-and-mind.md) |
| working memory, attractors | Hopfield energy descent (gradient flow on a spin glass) | [cognitive science](03-cognitive-science-and-mind.md) |
| forgetting curves | power-law relaxation / fractional diffusion | [cognitive science](03-cognitive-science-and-mind.md) |
| priming, spreading activation | diffusion on a semantic graph | [cognitive science](03-cognitive-science-and-mind.md) |
| synaptic plasticity, learning | stochastic gradient descent → Fokker–Planck | [computation](05-computation-and-algorithms.md) |
| an epidemic | SIR-type reaction–diffusion; network transport | [networks & traffic](../08-bridges/09-networks-society-and-traffic.md) |
| ecological invasion | Fisher–KPP front (pulled speed) | [fronts](../04-interaction/05-fronts-and-travelling-waves.md) |
| foraging and search | Lévy flights (superdiffusion) | [anomalous diffusion](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md) |
| tumour growth and drug delivery | reaction–diffusion + poroelasticity + binding | [biology & medicine](../08-bridges/03-biology-and-medicine.md) |

### Society, economics and language

| Your object | Transport form | Note |
|---|---|---|
| opinion dynamics | nonlinear diffusion / bounded-confidence transport | [social science](04-social-behaviour-economics-language.md) |
| segregation | energy descent on a lattice (flip dynamics) | [social science](04-social-behaviour-economics-language.md) |
| innovation diffusion | logistic/Bass growth ⟹ reaction–diffusion in space | [social science](04-social-behaviour-economics-language.md) |
| wealth and income distributions | Fokker–Planck with multiplicative noise ⟹ power laws | [social science](04-social-behaviour-economics-language.md) |
| market prices | drift–diffusion (backward Kolmogorov) | [finance](../08-bridges/02-finance-and-economics.md) |
| strategic interaction of many agents | mean-field games (HJB + Fokker–Planck) | [finance](../08-bridges/02-finance-and-economics.md) |
| language change, dialect diffusion | advection–diffusion + Wright–Fisher noise | [social science](04-social-behaviour-economics-language.md) |
| word-frequency distributions | Yule–Simon preferential attachment ⟹ power law | [social science](04-social-behaviour-economics-language.md) |
| traffic | conservation law with concave flux | [networks & traffic](../08-bridges/09-networks-society-and-traffic.md) |
| crowd dynamics | nonlocal pedestrian transport; mean-field games | [social science](04-social-behaviour-economics-language.md) |
| information and rumours | reaction–diffusion on contact networks | [networks & traffic](../08-bridges/09-networks-society-and-traffic.md) |

### Computation, information and mathematics

| Your object | Transport form | Note |
|---|---|---|
| shortest paths, A\*, travel-time fields | eikonal / Hamilton–Jacobi; fast marching | [computation](05-computation-and-algorithms.md) |
| PageRank, influence, consensus | diffusion on a graph (graph Laplacian) | [computation](05-computation-and-algorithms.md) |
| gradient-descent training | gradient flow; with noise ⟹ Fokker–Planck | [computation](05-computation-and-algorithms.md) |
| Monte Carlo, HMC sampling | transport of measures in phase space | [computation](05-computation-and-algorithms.md) |
| diffusion models, flow matching | reverse-time SDE / continuity equation | [machine learning & data](../08-bridges/07-machine-learning-and-data.md) |
| Bayesian updating | transport of a measure (a change of measure) | [information & inference](07-information-statistics-and-inference.md) |
| estimators, Cramér–Rao bounds | gradient flow in Fisher–Rao geometry (natural gradient) | [information & inference](07-information-statistics-and-inference.md) |
| rare events, large deviations | WKB/Hamilton–Jacobi rate function (Sanov, Freidlin–Wentzell) | [information & inference](07-information-statistics-and-inference.md) |
| Ricci flow, mean-curvature flow | geometric diffusion of a metric or interface | [mathematics itself](08-mathematics-itself.md) |
| Markov kernels, probabilistic programming | Markov category: transport maps as morphisms | [mathematics itself](08-mathematics-itself.md) |
| eigenvalue statistics, random matrices | integrable hierarchies, determinantal processes | [solitons](../06-beyond/14-integrable-systems-and-solitons.md) |
| theta functions, modular forms | heat kernels on lattices and their symmetries | [mathematics itself](08-mathematics-itself.md) |

## Step 3 · What is *not* covered

The table is large, but it is not universal. Conspicuously absent: meaning, semantics, purpose,
measurement, and the discrete structure of language and games. Those are treated honestly in
[limits of the claim](09-limits-of-the-claim.md). The short version: transport theory is a theory of
**redistribution**, so it speaks only about quantities that can be counted and moved — and about the
consequences of the constraints that counting and moving impose.

> [!tip] How to use this page
> Do not read the table top to bottom. Find *your* object, note the equation family, then jump straight
> to that family's note and read only its L1 and L2 rungs. If the fit is wrong, go up one level to
> [the five structures](01-why-everything-flows.md) and re-derive the translation yourself — that is
> faster than browsing sixty rows.

## Checks

1. Which row covers "the number of users of an app"? *(Innovation diffusion: logistic growth, plus
   reaction–diffusion if geography matters.)*
2. Which row covers training a neural network with a noisy gradient? *(SGD as a Fokker–Planck/Langevin
   flow — the same family as Kramers escape and diffusion models.)*
3. Why does "shortest path" belong in the same table as heat conduction? *(Both are fields of the same
   transport structure — arrival times (eikonal/HJ) versus densities (diffusion); Dijkstra is a discrete
   Hamilton–Jacobi solver.)*
4. What would you do if your object is a *decision* rather than a quantity? *(Model the decision
   variable's distribution or its evidence accumulation; if the object has no distribution, you are outside
   the claim — see the limits note.)*

# Cognitive science & mind


# Cognitive science & mind

> **One-line picture.** A brain is not a set of instantaneous wires: it accumulates noisy evidence over
> time, spreads activity across tissue, diffuses chemicals through nanometre gaps, and holds *distributions*
> over hypotheses. Every one of those is a transport process — and in several cases the equation was fitted
> to neural or behavioural data long before anyone called it transport.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Fokker–Planck & Kramers](../05-kinetic/03-fokker-planck-and-kramers.md),
> [Delay & retarded transport](../06-beyond/16-delay-and-retarded-transport.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Social behaviour, economics & language](04-social-behaviour-economics-language.md),
> [Information, statistics & inference](07-information-statistics-and-inference.md)

## L1 · Why the mind is a transport system

Four independent reasons, in increasing order of literality:

1. **Decisions take time and vary.** Responses are distributed, not deterministic, and reaction-time
   *distributions* have a characteristic right-skewed shape. The canonical explanation is noisy evidence
   accumulating until it crosses a threshold — a first-passage problem for a diffusion.
2. **Activity is spatially extended.** Cortex is a sheet with short-range excitation, longer-range inhibition
   and conduction delays; its coarse-grained activity obeys integro-differential **neural-field** equations
   whose solutions include travelling waves, persistent bumps and patterns.
3. **Signalling molecules physically diffuse.** Neurotransmitter across a 20 nm cleft, calcium in a spine,
   IP₃ through astrocytic networks, potassium in extracellular space: ordinary diffusion–reaction problems
   with measurable coefficients. Cortical spreading depression — the slow chemical wave behind migraine aura —
   is a textbook reaction–diffusion wave.
4. **Beliefs are distributions.** Under uncertainty the evolving object is a measure over hypotheses;
   Bayesian updating, variational inference and message passing are transport of that measure, often with an
   explicit *optimality* structure (minimise surprise or expected cost).

## L2 · The canonical models, with their equations

### 1 · Evidence accumulation: the drift–diffusion model

$$\mathrm dx=\mu\,\mathrm dt+\sigma\,\mathrm dW,\qquad \text{decide when }x=\pm\theta,\qquad
\partial_tp=-\mu\,\partial_xp+\tfrac{\sigma^2}{2}\partial_x^2p .$$

The decision variable's density obeys a Fokker–Planck equation with absorbing boundaries; the observable is
the **first-passage-time distribution** (inverse-Gaussian-like), which predicts both the shape of
reaction-time distributions and the *error-RT relation*. The parameters have psychological names: drift $\mu$
= evidence quality; boundary $\theta$ = caution, so the **speed–accuracy trade-off** is literally a boundary
value; starting point = bias; and the **non-decision time** is exactly a
[delay](../06-beyond/16-delay-and-retarded-transport.md). This is the most quantitatively successful model
family in cognitive psychology — and it is a transport model.

### 2 · Why this is also statistically optimal

Accumulating **log-likelihood ratios** and stopping at a threshold is Wald's sequential probability ratio
test: optimal in the sense of minimising expected sample size for a given error rate. So the psychological
model is the control-theoretic *optimum* of an inference problem — the same intersection of transport and
optimality that appears in mean-field games ([finance & economics](../08-bridges/02-finance-and-economics.md)).

**Attention enters as precision.** Raising precision lowers $\sigma$: in this formalism, paying attention is
literally *reducing the diffusion coefficient* of the decision variable, and it sharpens the effective
drift-to-noise ratio in exactly the way signal-detection theory's $d'$ describes.

### 3 · Predictive coding and the free-energy principle

Variational inference replaces an intractable posterior with the closest tractable one,
$q^*=\arg\min_q\mathrm{KL}\big(q\,\|\,p(\cdot\mid\text{data})\big)$; gradient descent on that functional is a
gradient flow ([entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md)).
Predictive coding implements it as precision-weighted prediction-error dynamics between hierarchical levels;
**active inference** adds actions, turning the problem into stochastic optimal control with a
Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman structure ([Hamilton–Jacobi](../06-beyond/02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md)).

> [!warning] Trap
> The *mathematics* of variational free energy is standard and rigorous; the claim that brains literally
> implement it is a **modelling hypothesis**, not a theorem. It is attractive because one functional
> unifies perception, action and learning — but its predictions are often under-determined, rival accounts
> (reinforcement learning, Bayesian sampling, direct posterior coding) make overlapping predictions, and
> distinguishing them experimentally is an open problem. Treat it as a framework that generates models.

### 4 · Neural fields, delayed loops and brain rhythms

Continuum descriptions of cortex (Amari, Wilson–Cowan) read

$$\partial_tu(\mathbf x,t)=-u+\int w(\mathbf x-\mathbf y)\,S\big(u(\mathbf y,t-\tau)\big)\,\mathrm d\mathbf y+I(\mathbf x,t),$$

an integro-differential transport equation with a **delay built into the kernel**. Its solution repertoire
covers a lot of neuroscience:

* **Persistent bumps** — working memory as a stationary (or slowly drifting) state of a transport equation.
* **Travelling pulses and waves** — propagation with conduction delay; delay selects wavelength and rhythm,
  the standard story for spindle- and alpha-band activity ([delay](../06-beyond/16-delay-and-retarded-transport.md)).
* **Turing-type patterns** — Ermentrout and Cowan's explanation of the *geometric* form of elementary visual
  hallucinations follows from the symmetries of a cortical pattern-forming instability: one of the most
  striking quantitative predictions of a reaction–diffusion model in neuroscience
  ([Turing patterns](../04-interaction/04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md)).
* **Excitable spiral waves** — the mathematical cousin of cardiac arrhythmias, used to model
  epileptiform activity ([fronts](../04-interaction/05-fronts-and-travelling-waves.md)).

And the reduction to a *density* is exact in a limit: for many weakly coupled spiking neurons, the population
density in (voltage, conductance) space satisfies a Fokker–Planck equation with the synaptic input entering
as drift and diffusion. The brain, at that level of description, *is* a transport system.

### 5 · Memory: attractors, decay, and the statistics of synapses

* **Retrieval as relaxation.** In Hopfield-type networks memories are minima of an energy functional and recall
  is a descent flow — the same gradient-flow structure as phase ordering, with metastable states, slow
  relaxation, and a storage capacity near $0.138N$ for random Hebbian patterns. Retrieval is transport in
  state space, governed by spin-glass mathematics.
* **Forgetting as fractional relaxation.** Empirical retention curves follow *power laws* (Wickelgren, Wixted)
  rather than exponentials — and power-law relaxation is the signature of memory kernels and fractional
  derivatives ([memory & viscoelasticity](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md)). A single
  exponential requires one time constant; memory has a broad spectrum, which is what a fractional law encodes.
* **Consolidation as two-compartment transport.** Fast (hippocampal) and slow (cortical) stores exchanging
  information is a two-reservoir transport problem in which the exchange rate and replay schedule set the
  retention statistics — the "complementary learning systems" picture in transport language.
* **Synapses as a distribution.** Plasticity rules are stochastic updates, so the *distribution* of synaptic
  weights evolves by a Fokker–Planck equation whose stationary state reproduces the observed heavy-tailed
  weight distributions. Learning and forgetting are drift and diffusion in weight space.

### 6 · Semantic networks: diffusion on a graph

Represent knowledge as a graph of concepts. Then "spreading activation" (Collins & Loftus) is *diffusion on a
graph*, with the graph Laplacian replacing $\nabla^2$; priming is pre-activation (a non-zero initial
condition), semantic distance is resistance (graph distance), and reaction times in word recognition scale
with how quickly activation arrives. The same operator gives the smoothing, the mixing time and the spectral
gap ([computation & algorithms](05-computation-and-algorithms.md)), so vocabulary-size effects, category
fluency and the shape of semantic spaces all inherit the mathematics of transport on networks.

This is where cognitive science meets [networks & traffic](../08-bridges/09-networks-society-and-traffic.md):
a concept network behaves like any other transport network — bottlenecks, hubs, spectral gaps — and the
statistical regularities of language (Zipf's law) come from preferential-attachment growth, i.e. from a
multiplicative stochastic transport in frequency space
([social behaviour, economics & language](04-social-behaviour-economics-language.md)).

### 7 · Cognitive maps: eigenmodes and the successor representation

Navigation provides two unusually crisp transport structures:

* **Grid cells as Laplacian eigenmodes.** Models in which grid-cell firing patterns emerge as *eigenvectors
  of the spatial transition matrix* (equivalently of a graph Laplacian on the environment) reproduce the
  observed hexagonal lattices. Grid fields are then literally the eigenmodes of diffusion on the environment —
  the same objects as the vibrational modes of a drum
  ([dimensions & geometry](../01-foundations/12-dimensions-geometry-and-manifolds.md)).
* **The successor representation.** The expected discounted future occupancy of states,
  $M=(I-\gamma T)^{-1}$, is the **resolvent** of the transition operator: a Green function for a discounted
  transport problem. Value learning and predictive maps then become algebra of transport operators, and
  planning becomes a transport computation rather than a tree search.

Navigation as optimal control adds the Hamilton–Jacobi branch: shortest-path and travel-time fields satisfy an
eikonal equation, so *both* hemispheres of the atlas — diffusion and optimality — describe what the same
circuitry appears to do ([Hamilton–Jacobi](../06-beyond/02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md)).

### 8 · Psychophysics: thresholds as first-passage statistics

Signal-detection theory says that discriminability is separation over noise, $d'=\Delta\mu/\sigma$. In the
diffusion picture that is a statement about two first-passage problems: hit and false-alarm rates are the
probabilities that a noisy accumulator with one drift or the other reaches a boundary first. Consequences that
are actually used: the psychometric function is a diffusion-to-threshold curve; just-noticeable differences
scale with the inverse square root of Fisher information
([information & inference](07-information-statistics-and-inference.md)); and response bias is a shift in the
starting point. Weber's law itself emerges when noise scales with stimulus magnitude — a *multiplicative*
noise model, the same mathematical feature that generates lognormal and power-law distributions elsewhere in
this atlas ([social behaviour, economics & language](04-social-behaviour-economics-language.md)).

Physical transport closes the loop at the small end: synaptic cleft diffusion (20–30 nm, microseconds), spine
calcium microdomains, and dendritic cable filtering are ordinary diffusion problems whose coefficients are
measured, and they set the time constants that appear as "non-decision time" in the behavioural models above.

## L3 · What is established, what is a framework, what is a hypothesis

Calibration matters more here than anywhere else in the atlas, because cognitive science attracts
over-claiming. The honest scorecard:

| Claim | Status |
|---|---|
| Reaction-time *distributions* from a noisy accumulator with absorbing boundaries | **quantitatively validated** across many tasks; neural ramping correlates (e.g. parietal/premotor build-up) |
| Log-likelihood accumulation is optimal (SPRT) | **theorem** (Wald), given known likelihoods and stable evidence statistics |
| Precision modulates effective diffusion (attention as $\sigma\downarrow$) | **well-supported modelling claim**, with direct experimental manipulations of noise |
| Neural fields generate waves, bumps and Turing patterns | **validated** where measured (wave speeds, persistent activity); the mapping of hallucination geometry to cortical coordinates is an interpretation, not a measurement |
| Spiking-network activity ⇒ exact Fokker–Planck in a limit | **theorem** under exchangeability and neglected correlations; finite-size effects and synchrony are the corrections |
| Free-energy/active-inference framework | **rigorous mathematics, hypothesis about brains** — predictions often under-determined |
| Hopfield capacity $\approx0.138N$ | **theorem** for random Hebbian patterns; modified for structured/correlated ones |
| Power-law forgetting implies fractional diffusion | **partly an inference**: power laws constrain the exponent, not the mechanism — mixtures of exponentials and lognormal spectra mimic them too |
| Grid fields as Laplacian eigenmodes | **one successful model among competitors** — it reproduces hexagonality but does not uniquely identify the circuit |
| Successor representation $=(I-\gamma T)^{-1}$ | **mathematical identity** for discounted occupancy; the cognitive claim is that brains compute it |

**Two structural warnings.**

* **Identifiability.** Several distinct mechanisms (different boundaries, collapsing thresholds, variable
  drift, non-decision time) can produce nearly identical reaction-time distributions. Unless the experiment
  separates them, the fitted parameters are not evidence about mechanism
  ([inverse problems](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)).
* **Mean-field breakdown.** The exact density equation needs many similar neurons and weak correlations. In
  small circuits, in synchronised bursts, or in pathological states, correlations are the physics — and you must
  simulate spiking networks or use fluctuation-corrected closures. This is the neural version of the
  [Knudsen number](01-why-everything-flows.md).

## L4 · Practice: how this is actually done

* **Fitting decision models.** Use the first-passage density for the likelihood (analytic for
  drift–diffusion, numeric else), hierarchical Bayesian estimation for group-level parameters, and — for quick
  work — quantile or "EZ" estimators. Standard pitfalls: right-censored and contaminated responses (model them
  with a mixture), confounding of non-decision time with boundary separation, and reporting fits without
  parameter-recovery checks.
* **Simulating SDEs**: Euler–Maruyama with small steps, or exact sampling for special cases; check the
  empirical first-passage distribution against theory before trusting parameter recovery
  ([random walks & Langevin](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md)).
* **Neural fields**: translation-invariant kernels make FFT convolution natural; delay terms require stored,
  interpolated history; travelling-wave stability is analysed with Evans functions; delay-induced rhythms come
  from pseudospectral analysis of the delayed spectrum
  ([delay](../06-beyond/16-delay-and-retarded-transport.md)).
* **Spiking-network densities**: solve the voltage Fokker–Planck equation with a refractory reset (a boundary
  condition that returns probability to the reset value), which conserves total probability exactly — a good
  test of any implementation. Validate the mean-field reduction against direct network simulations at several
  network sizes.
* **Latent dynamics from data**: state-space models (Kalman/particle filters), delay embedding for single
  series (Takens), dimensionality reduction as transport to a latent space. Remember that the *observed* density
  is not the latent one — observation transport must be modelled explicitly
  ([data assimilation](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)).
* **Benchmarks**: posterior-predictive checks on RT distributions (not just means); wave-speed and
  pattern-wavelength comparisons for neural fields; FP-versus-network firing-rate agreement; eigenvalue
  structure of grid fields; Hopfield capacity curves.

## Checks

1. In the drift–diffusion model, what psychological construct is the *boundary*? *(Caution / response
   threshold: it sets the speed–accuracy trade-off.)*
2. Why is "attention = increasing precision" a statement about a diffusion coefficient? *(Precision is inverse
   variance; lowering $\sigma$ raises $d'$ — attention changes the noise term of the accumulator.)*
3. What is the neural analogue of the Knudsen number? *(Network size relative to correlation length: small
   circuits and strong synchrony invalidate the mean-field density description.)*
4. Why does a power-law forgetting curve *not* prove fractional diffusion? *(Power laws are reproducible by
   other broad-spectrum mechanisms; they constrain the exponent, not the mechanism.)*

# Social behaviour, economics & language


# Social behaviour, economics & language

> **One-line picture.** Societies are made of agents, but the quantities we measure — opinions, wealth,
> adoption rates, word frequencies — are *distributions*, and distributions move. The mathematics is the same:
> drift and diffusion for the shape of a distribution, reaction–diffusion for its spatial spread.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Fokker–Planck & Kramers](../05-kinetic/03-fokker-planck-and-kramers.md),
> [Fronts & travelling waves](../04-interaction/05-fronts-and-travelling-waves.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Cognitive science & mind](03-cognitive-science-and-mind.md),
> [Information, statistics & inference](07-information-statistics-and-inference.md)

## L1 · Why the social sciences are transport problems

Two levels exist, and it matters which one you are on:

* **Agent level** — discrete, heterogeneous, strategic, often only finitely many. Nothing here is a PDE until
  you aggregate.
* **Population level** — the distribution of agents over states (opinions, wealth, locations, vocabulary). This
  *is* a density, and it evolves by transport whenever agents move, copy, switch or are created/destroyed.
  Aggregation is a limit theorem (law of large numbers / mean-field), valid when agents are many and weakly
  correlated — the social-science **Knudsen condition**.

The payoff is not decoration: transport theory predicts *distribution shapes* (power laws, lognormal tails),
*spreading speeds* (adoption fronts), and *stability* (consensus, polarisation, cycles) — quantities that
agent-level intuition gets wrong.

## L2 · The canonical models

**Opinion dynamics.** Continuous-opinion models give nonlinear diffusion,
$\partial_t\rho=\partial_x^2\!\big(D(\rho)\rho\big)$, where the diffusivity depends on how far opinions can
influence each other. Bounded confidence produces *compact support* and clustering — the mathematics of
[porous-medium-type degeneracy](../06-beyond/06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md) with a moving front,
which is why consensus, polarisation and fragmentation are *transport* outcomes rather than voting outcomes.

**Wealth and income.** A multiplicative random growth process, $\dot w=\mu w+\sigma w\,\xi$, has a
Fokker–Planck equation for the wealth density,

$$\partial_t\rho=-\partial_w(\mu w\rho)+\tfrac{\sigma^2}{2}\partial_w^2(w^2\rho),$$

whose stationary state is a power law (Pareto). This is the cleanest case in the social sciences where the
*observed distribution shape* is predicted by a transport coefficient: multiplicative noise ⟹ heavy tails,
additive noise ⟹ Gaussian. Kinetic exchange models reproduce the same families and explain why redistribution
changes the exponent rather than the mechanism.

**Innovation and behaviour diffusion.** Adoption among a population with imitation gives logistic (Bass)
growth; combined with spatial movement it gives
$\partial_tp=D\nabla^2p+kp(1-p)$ — Fisher–KPP, with front speed $2\sqrt{Dk}$
([fronts](../04-interaction/05-fronts-and-travelling-waves.md)). Consequences that matter: adoption waves have a
*measurable* speed, heterogeneous resistance slows the front (pulled-front sensitivity), and spatial structure
determines whether an innovation stalls.

**Segregation.** Schelling's model — mild individual preferences plus local moves — is an energy-descent
process on a lattice: agents flip to reduce local "unhappiness", so the aggregate dynamics is a
[gradient flow](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md) with a strong hysteresis loop.
The famous result (mild preferences ⟹ severe segregation) is a statement about a *non-convex* energy landscape
with metastable states and path dependence, not about individual prejudice.

**Language change and contact.** A variant spreading in a population obeys a reaction–diffusion equation with
**Wright–Fisher noise**: logistic selection plus spatial diffusion plus genetic drift gives dialect boundaries —
clines, isoglosses and wave-like propagation, with the noise term mattering precisely because speaker
populations are finite ([stochastic PDEs](../06-beyond/07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md)). Counting
vocabulary gives **Zipf's law**: preferential attachment (Yule–Simon) is multiplicative growth in frequency
space, whose stationary distribution is a power law with exponent near 1 — the same mechanism as wealth above,
applied to words.

**Networks and crowds.** Epidemics, rumours and crowd flow live on graphs and in space: SIR-type reaction
diffusion on contact networks, nonlocal pedestrian transport (a flux depending on a *neighbourhood* density —
[nonlocal operators](../06-beyond/03-nonlocal-and-fractional-operators.md)), and mean-field games for
strategic routing.

| Social object | Transport structure | Shape it predicts |
|---|---|---|
| opinions | nonlinear diffusion / degenerate | clustering, polarisation, consensus time |
| wealth, word frequencies | multiplicative-noise Fokker–Planck / Yule–Simon | power laws, lognormal cores |
| adoption, rumours | logistic + diffusion (Fisher–KPP) | fronts with speed $2\sqrt{Dk}$ |
| segregation | energy descent with hysteresis | sharp separating interfaces |
| dialects | reaction–diffusion + Wright–Fisher noise | clines, isoglosses, drift |
| cities and flows | gravity models, optimal transport | spatial interaction laws |

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees — and what it warns you about

* **Aggregation is a theorem with hypotheses.** Going from agents to a density needs many weakly correlated
  agents. Fault that condition and the fluid description fails *qualitatively*: finite populations add
  demographic noise (Wright–Fisher), small groups can lock into states the mean-field theory says are
  impossible, and a single influential actor can change a front (pulled fronts are driven by their leading
  edge — [fronts](../04-interaction/05-fronts-and-travelling-waves.md)).
* **Stationary shapes are diagnostic, not explanatory.** Pareto tails, Zipf exponents and lognormal cores each
  arise from several different mechanisms; the transport equation tells you *which families* are possible
  (multiplicative noise ⟹ heavy tails; additive ⟹ Gaussian) and what else must be true if that family holds
  (e.g. two-regime transport: lognormal body, power-law tail). It does not identify the mechanism from the shape
  alone — the social version of the non-identifiability warning
  ([inverse problems](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)).
* **Hysteresis and tipping points are structural.** Non-convex energies mean the same parameters support
  several stable states; policy changes may be reversible in the model but not in the trajectory. Where the
  transport equation has a saddle-node bifurcation, the system has a **tipping point**, and near it the
  linearised drift loses its restoring rate: *critical slowing down*. That yields testable early-warning
  statistics — rising variance, rising autocorrelation, longer recovery times — because the spectral gap is
  closing ([spectral view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md)).
* **Optimality and equilibrium are different theories.** Mean-field games pair an agent-level optimality
  principle (HJB) with a population-level transport equation (Fokker–Planck): the equilibrium is a *fixed point
  of the coupling*. Classic equilibria (Nash, competitive, Walrasian) are the static limits; the transport view
  adds the approach dynamics, its stability, and the possibility of cycles where the static theory sees none
  ([finance & economics](../08-bridges/02-finance-and-economics.md)).
* **Parameters are behavioural, so they move.** Unlike viscosity, an adoption rate or a dispersion coefficient
  responds to the policy applied to it — the transport coefficients are not constants of nature. Fit them on
  one regime and use them in another at your peril; this is the mathematical content of the Lucas critique.

## L4 · Practice

* **Estimate exponents honestly.** Log–log least squares is biased: use maximum likelihood with a
  Kolmogorov–Smirnov or Hill estimator, fit the *tail separately* from the body, and report confidence intervals
  by bootstrap. Distinguish "the data are heavy-tailed" from "the model is a power law".
* **Simulate agents and the continuum together.** Validate every mean-field reduction against agent-based
  simulation at several population sizes, and use the discrepancy to estimate the size of the fluctuation
  correction. Where they disagree, believe the agents.
* **Do not confuse expansion with relocation.** A spreading front and a hierarchical jump-diffusion can produce
  the same spatial adoption pattern: identifiability requires time-resolved data (which individuals adopted
  where), not just a final map — the geography version of the same warning as above.
* **Networks change the thresholds.** Epidemic and cascade thresholds depend on the graph's spectral radius,
  not on mean degree; use configuration models and real topologies, and check network version versus mean-field
  prediction ([networks & traffic](../08-bridges/09-networks-society-and-traffic.md)).
* **Early-warning indicators need discipline**: detrend, use rolling windows, correct for multiple comparisons,
  and report false-positive rates — slowing down is a genuine fingerprint, but not a prophecy.
* **Benchmarks**: Bass-model adoption curves and their spatial fronts; Zipf's law under a stated tokenization;
  Lorenz/Gini recomputed from fitted distributions; Schelling segregation curves; epidemic thresholds against
  network spectra.

## Checks

1. Why do multiplicative (not additive) fluctuations in wealth produce power-law tails? *(The Langevin equation $\dot w=\mu w+\sigma w\xi$ has a stationary Fokker–Planck solution $\rho\propto w^{-(1+2\mu/\sigma^2)}$ — a Pareto law determined by the drift-to-noise ratio.)*
2. What does a tipping point look like in the transport equation, and what is the observable warning?
   *(A saddle-node bifurcation of the drift; near it the linearised restoring rate vanishes — critical slowing
   down, with rising variance and autocorrelation.)*
3. What is the social analogue of the Knudsen number, and what happens when it is small? *(Population size
   relative to interaction correlation length; small populations add demographic noise, so the fluid/mean-field
   description fails and stochastic agent models are required.)*
4. Why can the same final map of adoption be produced by two different transport mechanisms? *(Contagious
   expansion (front) and hierarchical jump-diffusion are non-identifiable from end states alone.)*
