# Computation & algorithms


# Computation & algorithms

> **One-line picture.** Many of the algorithms you already use are discretisations of transport equations —
> shortest paths are arrival-time fields, PageRank is a stationary density, training is a gradient flow, and
> sampling is transport of a measure. Reading them that way gives you convergence rates, stability theory and
> intuition for free.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Hamilton–Jacobi & viscosity solutions](../06-beyond/02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md),
> [Optimal transport & gradient flows](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Information, statistics & inference](07-information-statistics-and-inference.md),
> [Machine learning & data](../08-bridges/07-machine-learning-and-data.md)

## L1 · Three identities worth memorising

1. **Dijkstra = Hamilton–Jacobi.** The distance-to-goal field $T(\mathbf x)$ satisfies the eikonal equation
   $|\nabla T|=1/c$. Dijkstra's algorithm is a *monotone upwind sweep* — the discrete version of fast marching —
   and its correctness proof is the discrete maximum principle. A\* adds an admissible heuristic, which is a
   viscosity subsolution bound guiding the sweep.
2. **PageRank = diffusion.** The stationary distribution of a random walk with teleportation is the resolvent
   of a transport operator, $\pi\propto(I-\alpha P)^{-1}\mathbf 1$: teleportation is a source term, the damping
   factor is a mean free path, and personalised PageRank is the Green function of that operator.
3. **Training = gradient flow.** Gradient descent is $\dot\theta=-\nabla L$; stochastic gradient descent adds
   noise, giving a **Fokker–Planck equation for the distribution of parameters**; and momentum methods are
   second-order ODEs — accelerated methods are damped oscillators with a specific damping schedule.

## L2 · The lookup, algorithm by algorithm

| Algorithm | Transport form | What you get from the analogy |
|---|---|---|
| Dijkstra, A\*, fast marching, level sets | eikonal / Hamilton–Jacobi | monotonicity ⟹ correctness; upwind schemes; low CFL |
| graph diffusion, label propagation, consensus | heat equation on a graph | mixing time $\sim1/\lambda_2$; Cheeger bottlenecks |
| PageRank, personalised PageRank, Katz centrality | resolvent $(I-\alpha P)^{-1}$ | damping ⟹ finite memory; Green-function interpretation |
| Jacobi / Gauss–Seidel / SOR / multigrid | smoothing iterations for an elliptic operator | why multigrid wins (it is a mode-dependent filter) |
| gradient descent, Newton, momentum, Adam | gradient flow (+ noise, + inertia) | Lyapunov descent, stability limits, step-size rules |
| SGD, Langevin, stochastic optimisation | Fokker–Planck / Langevin | stationary distributions, escape rates, noise scale |
| Metropolis, Gibbs, HMC, NUTS | transport of measures with a target invariant density | mixing times, spectral gaps, symplectic structure |
| score-based / diffusion models, flow matching | forward noising SDE + reverse-time SDE / continuity equation | exact sampling by solving a transport equation |
| variational inference, ELBO, Wasserstein VI | KL gradient flow (Fisher–Rao or Wasserstein) | geometry-appropriate optimisers; convergence rates |
| belief propagation, message passing, GNNs | discrete transport of messages on a factor graph | convergence as a diffusion on the factor graph |
| dynamic programming, value iteration, RL | Bellman = discrete Hamilton–Jacobi; continuous RL = HJB | value functions as arrival-time-like fields |
| replicator dynamics, evolutionary algorithms | transport in frequency space with Fisher metric | natural-gradient structure; ESS, Fisher's theorem |
| queueing networks (M/M/1, heavy traffic) | birth–death master equation ⟹ reflected Brownian motion | heavy-traffic limit is a transport approximation |
| optimal transport, Sinkhorn, Wasserstein GAN | Monge–Kantorovich + entropic regularisation | geometry of distributions; convex formulations |
| attention in transformers | a *nonlocal* smoothing/conditional-expectation operator (kernel or OT interpretations proposed) | caution: an interpretation, not an identity |

## L3 · What the transport reading actually gives you

* **Convergence rates become spectral statements.** Mixing time of a Markov chain $\sim1/\lambda_2$ of the
  graph Laplacian; the Cheeger inequality bounds it by bottlenecks; gradient-flow convergence follows from
  strong convexity, and *sampling* rates from log-Sobolev and Poincaré inequalities. So "convergence proofs" in
  optimisation and sampling are transport theorems in disguise
  ([entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md)).
* **Monotonicity gives correctness.** Monotone, consistent discretisations of Hamilton–Jacobi converge to the
  viscosity solution ([HJ](../06-beyond/02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md)) — which is exactly why
  Dijkstra-style sweeps are correct, why fast marching works, and why an *inadmissible* heuristic in A\* breaks
  optimality.
* **Stability limits are CFL conditions.** Step-size limits, momentum stability regions and "learning-rate
  warm-up" are the same phenomena as in explicit integration of stiff equations: the fast modes set the step,
  and the slow physics sets the timescale you care about
  ([CFL & stiffness](../07-numerics/02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md)).
* **Samplers are transport with an invariant measure.** MCMC/Langevin convergence is the statement that the
  empirical measure is transported into the target; HMC works *because* of symplectic structure, so changing
  the integrator changes the algorithm's validity
  ([variational principles & Noether](../06-beyond/15-variational-principles-and-noether.md)).
* **Honest caveat — no free lunch.** NP-hardness is combinatorial. Nothing in this note makes hard problems
  easy; the transport reading makes them *interpretable*, sometimes gives rates, and reliably supplies good
  diagnostics. When the continuous limit is poor (small graphs, few samples, strong heterogeneity), discrete
  structure dominates — the computational version of the
  [Knudsen condition](01-why-everything-flows.md).

## L4 · Practice

* **Design from the continuum limit, then verify at the discrete level.** Choose step sizes, dampings and
  scheduling from the continuum stability analysis, then confirm empirically — the scaling is usually right and
  the constants usually need calibration.
* **Preserve the structure you rely on**: flux-form/mass-conserving updates for diffusion solvers, symplectic
  integrators for HMC, monotone updates for arrival-time fields, doubly-stochastic transitions when you need
  uniform stationarity.
* **Use transport diagnostics in the loop**: effective spectral gap by power iteration; residual history for
  elliptic solvers; ESS and autocorrelation for samplers; energy/loss Lyapunov monitoring for training — a
  sudden loss spike is the discrete analogue of a violated energy inequality, not bad luck.
* **Implement with the right tools**: graph Laplacians via sparse Cholesky or AMG; fast marching on grids and
  meshes for eikonal problems; FFT for translation-invariant kernels and convolution quadrature for memory;
  sparse OT/Sinkhorn with entropic regularisation for distribution-valued objectives.
* **Benchmarks**: mixing times on graphs against $\lambda_2$ predictions; eikonal test media with analytic
  travel times; density estimation against exact 1-D transport; sampler accuracy versus solver step count;
  solver-versus-spectral-method comparisons on the same PDE.

## Checks

1. Why is Dijkstra's algorithm correct — in transport language? *(It is a monotone upwind scheme for the eikonal
   equation; monotone, consistent schemes converge to the viscosity solution.)*
2. What is the transport interpretation of PageRank's damping factor? *(A finite mean free path: the resolvent
   $(I-\alpha P)^{-1}$ truncates the walk's memory, giving a well-defined stationary density.)*
3. Why does momentum help, in equation language? *(It turns first-order descent into a damped second-order
   oscillator, whose damping schedule sets the convergence rate.)*
4. When does the continuous-limit intuition fail? *(Small graphs, few samples, strong heterogeneity — when
   discrete structure, not transport geometry, governs behaviour.)*

## See also

[Hamilton–Jacobi & viscosity solutions](../06-beyond/02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md) ·
[Optimal transport & gradient flows](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md) ·
[Information, statistics & inference](07-information-statistics-and-inference.md) ·
[Machine learning & data](../08-bridges/07-machine-learning-and-data.md) ·
[Networks, society & traffic](../08-bridges/09-networks-society-and-traffic.md)

# Physics, chemistry & field theory


# Physics, chemistry & field theory

> **One-line picture.** In physics the transport view is not an analogy: fields *are* transported, quantum
> probability *does* obey a continuity equation, statistical mechanics *is* transport in phase space, and
> the renormalisation group is coarse-graining in the sense of Mori–Zwanzig — flow in the space of
> parameters.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Variational principles & Noether](../06-beyond/15-variational-principles-and-noether.md),
> [The Boltzmann equation](../05-kinetic/01-boltzmann-equation.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Mathematics itself](08-mathematics-itself.md),
> [Plasma & astrophysics](../08-bridges/05-plasma-and-astrophysics.md)

## L1 · Four branches, one structure

* **Classical fields.** Elasticity, electromagnetism, gas dynamics and MHD are first- or second-order
  *systems*, symmetric-hyperbolic once you set them up properly; their well-posedness theory is the
  theory of constrained hyperbolic transport.
* **Quantum mechanics.** The Schrödinger equation gives an *exact* continuity equation for probability —
  $\partial_t|\psi|^2+\nabla\!\cdot\!\mathbf j=0$ — so quantum evolution is transport of a conserved density by a
  unitary generator. Decoherence and dissipation are then kinetics in phase space.
* **Statistical mechanics.** Liouville's theorem is incompressible transport; the BBGKY hierarchy is the closure
  problem; the Boltzmann equation is kinetic transport; hydrodynamics is its Chapman–Enskog limit.
* **Quantum field theory.** Fields propagate by Green functions ("propagators"), scattering is an inverse problem,
  and the renormalisation group is coarse-graining: **transport in coupling space**, with fixed points that define
  universality classes.

## L2 · The structures, itemised

| System | Transport structure | Key statement |
|---|---|---|
| wave, elastic, Maxwell | symmetric hyperbolic (Friedrichs) | finite speed; constraints propagate (Bianchi/divergence) |
| Euler, MHD | hyperbolic conservation laws | shocks, entropy conditions, Rankine–Hugoniot |
| Einstein's equations | constrained hyperbolic (ADM/BSSN) | **well-posedness proved this way** — why numerical relativity works |
| Schrödinger | unitary generator; continuity equation | probability conserved exactly; current $\mathbf j=(\hbar/m)\mathrm{Im}(\psi^*\nabla\psi)$ |
| Gross–Pitaevskii (BEC) | nonlinear Schrödinger | solitons, vortices, quantum turbulence |
| Wigner equation | kinetic transport in phase space | quantum analogue of Boltzmann; contains coherence + classical drift |
| open quantum system | Lindblad master equation; CPTP maps | positivity and trace preserved — a "maximum principle" for operators |
| Dirac, Klein–Gordon | first/second-order hyperbolic | propagators = Green functions; spin transport |
| Liouville, BBGKY | incompressible phase-space transport + hierarchy | closure problem (the same as turbulence) |
| Boltzmann, Fokker–Planck | kinetic transport | H-theorem; hydrodynamic limits |
| chemical master equation | master equation on the *composition lattice* | stochastic chemistry as transport of probability over molecule numbers |
| mass-action kinetics | reaction–diffusion | patterns, oscillations, bistability |
| QFT propagators | Green functions of field operators | scattering amplitudes from a wave equation |
| renormalisation group | coarse-graining flow in coupling space | fixed points ⟹ universality classes, critical exponents |
| lattice field theory | structure-preserving discretisation | continuum limit is a *measured* limit, like any PDE code |
| quantum walks | discrete transport on a graph | quantum speedups for mixing/transport problems |

## L3 · What this buys, and what it costs

* **Numerical relativity exists because of classification.** The ADM formulation is only weakly hyperbolic;
  strongly hyperbolic reformulations (BSSN and relatives) with constraint damping made long-time black-hole
  evolutions possible. This is the clearest example in physics where *choosing the right hyperbolic form* was
  the difference between a well-posed and an unusable problem — and where gauge choice (a foliation) changes the
  operator's type and stability.
* **Constraints are Noether identities, and they propagate.** Bianchi identities guarantee that if
  $\nabla_\mu T^{\mu\nu}=0$ and the constraints hold on one slice, they hold on later slices. Codes exploit this
  with projection or damping rather than re-solving them
  ([variational principles & Noether](../06-beyond/15-variational-principles-and-noether.md)).
* **Quantum conservation is exact.** Unitarity gives $\partial_t|\psi|^2+\nabla\!\cdot\!\mathbf j=0$; open systems
  replace it with completely positive trace-preserving maps, which are the operator-level analogue of a maximum
  principle: positivity and total probability are preserved by construction. Decoherence appears as *diffusion in
  phase space*, and the classical limit $\hbar\to0$ is a singular limit with boundary layers and caustics
  ([Hamilton–Jacobi](../06-beyond/02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md)).
* **The renormalisation group is coarse-graining, and couplings transport.** The beta function is a velocity
  field in the space of parameters; fixed points are stationary points; relevant directions are unstable
  eigendirections. Hence: effective transport coefficients *run with scale* — the same phenomenon as
  homogenisation's effective tensors ([homogenisation](../06-beyond/08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md)) and the
  same reason mean-field theory works above the upper critical dimension
  ([why everything flows](01-why-everything-flows.md)). Honest caveat: the RG is a framework; rigorous versions
  exist for specific lattice models and low-dimensional constructive field theory, while many applications are
  perturbative approximations with uncontrolled errors.
* **Chemical discreteness is a transport statement.** The chemical master equation is transport of probability on
  a *lattice of compositions* with jumps of $\pm1$ molecule; the system-size expansion turns it into a
  Fokker–Planck equation with $1/V$ noise (the chemical Langevin equation). So "concentrations obey
  reaction–diffusion" is a *limit*, with a computable correction — the chemistry version of the Knudsen number.

## L4 · Practice

* **Numerical relativity**: hyperbolic formulations with constraint damping, gauge drivers, apparent-horizon
  finders; verify with convergence tests and known spacetimes (Schwarzschild perturbations, binary black-hole
  waveform catalogues). Treat constraints as *conservation audits*, not as equations to re-solve every step.
* **Lattice field theory**: the continuum limit is measured by scaling the lattice spacing exactly as in a fluid
  code; discretisation artifacts (doubling, chiral symmetry breaking) require structure-preserving fermion
  discretisations. The **sign problem** at finite density remains the central obstacle — honest candidates
  include reweighting, complex Langevin, tensor networks and quantum simulation, none of them a general solution.
* **Quantum simulation of transport**: analogue and gate-based devices can implement Lindblad dynamics, quantum
  walks and spin transport; the interesting question is where they beat classical transport solvers, and that is
  an open, actively studied question rather than a guarantee.
* **Stochastic chemistry**: Gillespie's algorithm is an exact kinetic Monte Carlo for the master equation;
  $\tau$-leaping is a controlled approximation to the corresponding Fokker–Planck equation; stiff deterministic
  kinetics stay implicit ([time integration](../07-numerics/02-cfl-stiffness-and-time-integration.md)).
* **Benchmarks**: H-theorem monitoring in kinetic codes; Maxwell and elastic wave convergence; constraint
  convergence in GR; Gillespie versus exact master-equation solutions; RG predictions versus Monte Carlo critical
  exponents; lattice-spacing scaling studies.

## Checks

1. Why did numerical relativity require reformulating Einstein's equations? *(ADM is only weakly hyperbolic;
   strongly hyperbolic forms with constraint damping are well-posed and stable — classification decided
   feasibility.)*
2. What is the quantum-mechanical continuity equation, and what does it conserve? *($\partial_t|\psi|^2+\nabla\!\cdot\!\mathbf j=0$:
   total probability, exactly — the $U(1)$ Noether current.)*
3. What is the renormalisation group in transport language? *(Coarse-graining flow — a velocity field (beta
   function) in parameter space; fixed points are universality classes and couplings "run" with scale.)*
4. In what sense is a chemical reaction network a transport problem? *(Its master equation transports probability
   over the composition lattice by $\pm1$ jumps; the large-volume limit is a Fokker–Planck/Langevin
   approximation with $1/V$ noise.)*

## See also

[The Boltzmann equation](../05-kinetic/01-boltzmann-equation.md) ·
[Mathematics itself](08-mathematics-itself.md) ·
[Plasma & astrophysics](../08-bridges/05-plasma-and-astrophysics.md) ·
[Limits of the claim](09-limits-of-the-claim.md)

# Information, statistics & inference


# Information, statistics & inference

> **One-line picture.** Inference is transport of a *measure over hypotheses*: Bayes reweights it, filtering
> evolves it continuously, large deviations transport it into rare corners — and the space it moves in has a
> metric (Fisher–Rao) that is not a choice but a theorem.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Information & statistics bridge](../08-bridges/07-machine-learning-and-data.md),
> [Optimal transport & gradient flows](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Computation & algorithms](05-computation-and-algorithms.md),
> [Limits of the claim](09-limits-of-the-claim.md)

## L1 · Two transport pictures of inference

1. **The measure picture.** A belief state is a probability measure over hypotheses; updating it is a
   *transport*: conditioning reweights (a measure-theoretic operation), sequential updating composes, and in
   continuous time the conditional measure solves a stochastic transport equation. Filtering is therefore a PDE
   for a measure, with observation noise as a source and drift as information flow.
2. **The geometry picture.** The set of distributions is a Riemannian manifold whose metric — the
   **Fisher information** — is *forced* by invariance requirements (Čencov's theorem). Distance in that metric
   measures statistical distinguishability; steepest descent in it is the **natural gradient**; and information
   *is* a metric, not a mere number.

Both pictures are transport statements, and both are used in practice: the first in filtering and data
assimilation, the second in optimisation and in every modern variational-inference algorithm.

## L2 · The structures

| Object | Transport structure | Content |
|---|---|---|
| Bayes' rule | reweighting of a measure (a change of measure) | posterior $\propto$ likelihood $\times$ prior; composition of transport maps |
| sequential Bayesian updating | flow of a measure; in continuous time the Kushner–Stratonovich/Zakai equations | nonlinear filtering as a stochastic PDE for the posterior |
| Kalman filter | exact Gaussian transport of the conditional measure | Riccati equation = transport of the covariance |
| variational inference / ELBO | KL gradient flow (Wasserstein or Fisher–Rao) | free-energy descent; mode-seeking caveats |
| Fisher information | metric on the space of distributions | Čencov uniqueness; Cramér–Rao bound $\mathrm{Var}\ge1/I(\theta)$ |
| natural gradient | gradient flow in Fisher–Rao geometry | parameterisation-invariant learning (KFAC, Shampoo) |
| maximum entropy | variational principle with a constraint | exponential families; conjugate duality |
| large deviations (Sanov) | rare event probabilities $\sim e^{-N\,\mathrm{KL}}$ | empirical measures converge by transport into atypical sets |
| Freidlin–Wentzell | small-noise diffusion exit | the rate function solves a **Hamilton–Jacobi** equation (vanishing viscosity) |
| fluctuation theorems | exact identities for entropy production | Jarzynski, Crooks; free-energy estimation from non-equilibrium work |
| Landauer's principle | erasure cost of information | information is physical: deletion dissipates |
| rate–distortion | constrained transport of a source distribution | Shannon's trade-off; Blahut–Arimoto akin to Sinkhorn iterations |
| PAC-Bayes, information bottleneck | KL-penalised transport objectives | generalisation and compression as transport costs |

The deepest bridge here is **large deviations = Hamilton–Jacobi**: the small-noise limit of a Fokker–Planck
equation is a Hamilton–Jacobi equation for the rate function, so probability theory's rare-event asymptotics and
the eikonal/arrival-time calculus are the same mathematics
([Hamilton–Jacobi](../06-beyond/02-hamilton-jacobi-and-viscosity-solutions.md)). That is also why WKB and
semiclassical physics share formulas with statistics.

## L3 · What is a theorem, what is a model

* **The geometry is forced.** Čencov's theorem: the Fisher metric is the only Riemannian metric on the space of
  distributions (up to scale) invariant under sufficient statistics. So information geometry is not a stylistic
  choice, and "natural gradient" is *the* parameterisation-invariant descent direction.
* **Accuracy has a geometric bound.** Cramér–Rao: $\mathrm{Var}(\hat\theta)\ge1/I(\theta)$. Maximum-likelihood
  estimators attain it asymptotically under regularity — accuracy limits are statements about curvature of the
  statistical manifold.
* **Filtering: one exact case, one hard case.** For linear-Gaussian models the Kalman filter is *exactly* optimal
  and the Riccati equation transports the covariance; the general nonlinear filtering problem is
  infinite-dimensional and intractable, so practical filters are approximations with known failure modes
  (divergence, particle degeneracy, curse of dimensionality).
* **Rare events are Hamilton–Jacobi.** Sanov's theorem (empirical measures) and Freidlin–Wentzell (small-noise
  exits) give *exact exponential rates*, and their rate functions solve Hamilton–Jacobi equations. The corollary
  is the profound one: the "cost" of an atypical outcome is a Kullback–Leibler distance — information and
  probability unified by a variational problem.
* **Non-equilibrium identities are exact — and treacherous.** Jarzynski's and Crooks' theorems hold exactly for
  any protocol, but the exponential average is dominated by rare trajectories, so estimates have enormous
  variance unless the protocol is slow or carefully designed. There is a genuine theory here: **thermodynamic
  length** is a Riemannian metric on state space, and minimum-dissipation protocols are geodesics — optimal
  control and transport geometry in the same statement.
* **Bayesian asymptotics are transport statements.** Under regularity the posterior concentrates at the true
  parameter (Bernstein–von Mises); under *misspecification* it concentrates on the Kullback–Leibler projection
  onto the model class — inference transports belief to the nearest distribution in information geometry, which
  is why misspecified models still "converge", just to the wrong place.
* **Priors are measures, and measures are geometry.** A prior is a choice of volume element; Jeffreys' prior is
  the Fisher volume element (invariant), while naive flat priors are not invariant under reparameterisation.
  The Lindley paradox and prior-sensitivity debates are downstream of this — a modelling choice, stated plainly.

## L4 · Practice

* **Natural gradient**: compute Fisher–vector products, use KFAC/Shampoo-style block approximations, and remember
  that Adam is a diagonal heuristic rather than a true Fisher method. Damp the Fisher when it is singular, and
  say which Fisher (empirical vs true) you used.
* **Filtering**: below ~10 dimensions particle filters are fine; above, use ensemble methods with localisation,
  or variational data assimilation. Degeneracy and divergence are common enough to deserve diagnostics
  ([data assimilation](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)).
* **Variational inference**: $\mathrm{KL}(q\|p)$ is mode-seeking and underestimates variance; $\alpha$-divergences
  or Jeffreys-style objectives trade coverage; Wasserstein VI gives geometry-appropriate behaviour. Always report
  posterior *coverage*, not just point accuracy.
* **Rare events**: importance sampling with annealed proposals, splitting methods, forward flux sampling. The
  optimal exponential tilt is a KL projection (Csiszár's I-projection), so the numerics of rare events and the
  geometry of information are the same computation.
* **Free-energy estimation from non-equilibrium work**: use BAR/MBAR rather than plain Jarzynski, and design
  protocols with thermodynamic length in mind if you can.
* **Benchmarks**: Cramér–Rao versus empirical estimator variance; Kalman versus exact linear-Gaussian posteriors;
  Sanov rate versus simulated rare-event frequency; Jarzynski versus free-energy perturbation as a function of
  protocol length; posterior coverage tests.

## Checks

1. Why is the Fisher metric "the" geometry of statistical inference? *(Čencov's invariance theorem: it is the
   unique metric invariant under sufficient statistics.)*
2. What does the small-noise limit of a Fokker–Planck equation give, and why does that matter for statistics?
   *(A Hamilton–Jacobi equation for the rate function — the exact exponential cost of rare events, e.g. Sanov's
   $N\,\mathrm{KL}$.)*
3. Under model misspecification, to what does the posterior converge? *(To the KL projection of the truth onto
   the model class — the nearest distribution in information-geometric distance.)*
4. Why is Jarzynski's equality hard to use numerically? *(The exponential average is dominated by rare
   low-work trajectories; variance explodes for fast protocols — which is why optimal/geodesic protocols and
   BAR-type estimators exist.)*

## See also

[Computation & algorithms](05-computation-and-algorithms.md) ·

# Mathematics itself


# Mathematics itself

> **One-line picture.** Transport is not only *used by* mathematics — it *is* a large part of mathematics:
> geometric flows are heat equations for shapes, semigroups and Dirichlet forms are the general theory behind
> "every Markov process has a PDE", and category theory gives transport a compositional semantics.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Variational principles & Noether](../06-beyond/15-variational-principles-and-noether.md),
> [Optimal transport & gradient flows](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Limits of the claim](09-limits-of-the-claim.md)

## L1 · The claim for mathematics

Four independent bridges, each of which is a *research area* rather than a metaphor:

1. **Geometry is diffusion.** Curvature can be made to flow by a heat-type equation, and the resulting flows
   solve classification problems in topology. Shape, in this view, is a slowly relaxing field.
2. **Analysis is the theory of generators.** Hille–Yosida, Dirichlet forms and martingale problems give a
   bijection (under mild conditions) between well-behaved Markov processes and their generators: *every* such
   process has a transport equation, and *every* suitable operator generates one.
3. **Probability is composition of transports.** Markov kernels form a *symmetric monoidal category* with a
   copy/discard structure: conditioning, Bayesian inversion and independence become categorical operations, and
   this "Markov category" framework gives rigorous, compositional semantics to probabilistic programs and
   graphical models.
4. **Discrete geometry keeps Stokes' theorem exactly.** Discrete exterior calculus (DEC) builds operators on
   meshes so that $\nabla\!\cdot\!\nabla\times=0$, the divergence theorem and the de Rham complex hold
   *identically* — transport theory and discrete mathematics joined at the definition.

## L2 · The structures

| Mathematical object | Transport formulation | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Ricci flow $\partial_tg=-2\mathrm{Ric}$ | heat equation for the metric | Perelman's entropy monotonicity; Poincaré conjecture |
| mean-curvature flow | $L^2$ gradient flow of area | interface motion, image segmentation |
| harmonic map flow | gradient flow of Dirichlet energy | maps between manifolds |
| Yang–Mills / Lüscher gradient flow | gradient flow in gauge-field space | stochastic quantisation; used in lattice QCD |
| heat kernel | propagator of the Laplacian | recovers area, curvature, Euler characteristic |
| spectral geometry | eigenvalues of $\Delta_g$ | "can one hear the shape of a drum?"; isospectral manifolds |
| semigroups & generators | $e^{tL}$; Hille–Yosida | the general theory behind parabolic PDEs |
| Dirichlet forms | energy forms for Markov processes | symmetric Markov processes, Fukushima's theory |
| martingale problem | generator + law of a process | Stroock–Varadhan: PDE ↔ probability |
| gradient flows in metric spaces | $W_2$/$L^2$/$H^{-1}$ descent | Ambrosio–Gigli–Savaré unify ODE, PDE, optimisation |
| $\Gamma$-convergence | variational limits of functionals | why singular limits (sharp interfaces) are rigorous |
| optimal transport | Monge–Kantorovich problem | geometry of measures; Wasserstein space is a metric space |
| min-cost flow, assignment, EMD | discrete optimal transport | combinatorial optimisation *is* transport on graphs |
| Markov categories | Markov kernels as morphisms | compositional probability, Bayesian inversion |
| discrete exterior calculus | mimetic operators on meshes | exact discrete Stokes/divergence; structure preservation |
| information geometry | Fisher–Rao manifold | statistics as geometry (Čencov's theorem) |

## L3 · What the mathematics guarantees

* **Generators and processes are in bijection (up to technicalities).** Hille–Yosida gives well-posed parabolic
  evolution for a huge class of operators; Dirichlet forms characterise symmetric Markov processes (with the
  Beurling–Deny decomposition separating local diffusion from jumps). So "every Markov process has a transport
  equation, and most reasonable operators generate one" is a theorem, not a slogan — and the *type* of the
  generator (local vs jump, order of the symbol) classifies the transport
  ([general classification](../01-foundations/10-general-pde-classification.md)).
* **Geometric flows carry Lyapunov functionals.** Perelman's entropy is monotone under Ricci flow, exactly as the
  free energy is monotone under a gradient flow; monotonicity is what rules out bad singularities and gives
  long-time structure. Blow-up analysis near singularities uses *parabolic rescaling* — the same
  self-similarity technique as Stefan and Barenblatt
  ([key derivations](../10-appendix/04-key-derivations.md)).
* **Transport geometry is a genuine metric space.** Wasserstein space is geodesic; the Benamou–Brenier
  formulation makes optimal transport a fluid-dynamics problem; displacement convexity gives contraction.
  Combinatorial transport (min-cost flow, assignment) is the *graph* version, so the algorithms of combinatorial
  optimisation and of measure geometry are two implementations of one theory.
* **Categories give transport a semantics.** Markov categories make composition, conditioning, independence and
  almost-sure reasoning structural, so probabilistic programs and graphical models inherit a precise algebra —
  and Bayesian inversion becomes a categorical operation rather than an ad-hoc formula.
* **Discrete geometry can be exact.** In discrete exterior calculus, Stokes' theorem, the de Rham complex and the
  identities $\nabla\!\cdot\!\nabla\times=0$ hold identically on a mesh. This is the mathematical backbone of the
  structure-preserving discretisations used throughout the numerics part
  ([structure-preserving schemes](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md)).
* **Transport data under-determines geometry.** Heat-kernel asymptotics recover area, curvature and Euler
  characteristic, but isospectral non-isometric manifolds exist: "can one hear the shape of a drum?" has the
  answer *no*. Even in pure geometry, the transport description is not a complete invariant — a good emblem for
  the whole atlas ([limits of the claim](09-limits-of-the-claim.md)).

## L4 · Practice

* **Use mimetic/DEC discretisations** when exact discrete theorems matter; use finite elements when you need
  energy minimisation; use level sets or phase fields for mean-curvature flow.
* **Monitor the Lyapunov functional numerically** — Perelman entropy for Ricci flow, area for MCF, energy for
  harmonic maps: it is a diagnostic that transfers straight from transport theory into geometry codes.
* **Spectral numerics** answer geometric questions: compute Laplace eigenvalues and compare with known spectra
  (spheres, tori, hyperbolic surfaces) to test both the discretisation and the geometry.
* **Optimal-transport solvers** (Sinkhorn, network simplex, semi-discrete methods) are now standard tools in
  geometry, statistics and machine learning alike — 1-D and graph cases have exact algorithms worth using as
  tests.
* **Benchmarks**: mean-curvature flow of a circle (exact solution); eigenvalues on the sphere against
  $\ell(\ell+1)/R^2$; discrete Stokes tests on random meshes; Sinkhorn versus exact transport on graphs;
  conservation of symplectic/contact structures in geometric integrators.

## Checks

1. Which theorem turns "Markov process" into "transport equation" in general? *(Hille–Yosida/Dirichlet-form
   theory: the generator of a Feller semigroup, with local diffusion and jump parts classified by its symbol.)*
2. What plays the role of a Lyapunov functional in Ricci flow? *(Perelman's entropy — monotone, and the key to
   excluding bad singularities.)*
3. Why is combinatorial min-cost flow a transport problem? *(It is Monge–Kantorovich on a graph: capacities and
   costs replace densities and distances.)*
4. What does the existence of isospectral non-isometric manifolds say about transport descriptions?
   *(They under-determine geometry: even complete spectral/transport data need not fix the object.)*

[Variational principles & Noether](../06-beyond/15-variational-principles-and-noether.md) ·
[Structure-preserving discretisations](../07-numerics/13-structure-preserving-discretizations.md) ·

# Limits of the claim


# Limits of the claim

> **One-line picture.** The claim is that a balance law plus a driver generates *a* transport model for anything
> countable — not that the world is a PDE, not that such models are unique, and not that they capture meaning,
> purpose or agency. A claim that cannot state its limits is not a claim.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Why everything flows](01-why-everything-flows.md) ·
> **Leads to.** back to [the universality index](index.md) with clearer eyes

## L1 · What is *not* being claimed

* **Not a physical theory of everything.** Transport equations describe the *redistribution* of quantities; they
  do not explain why the quantities exist, what they mean, or which of them matter.
* **Not that everything *is* a PDE.** Many systems are discrete, combinatorial, semantic or strategic. What is
  universal is that one can often *write a transport model* for a distribution over their states — a modelling
  statement, not an identity.
* **Not uniqueness.** Different mechanisms commonly produce the same macroscopic transport: the map from
  mechanism to equation is not injective.
* **Not a substitute for measurement.** A density you cannot observe is a theoretical object; without an
  operational definition, "the density" can be unfalsifiable
  ([inverse problems](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)).
* **Not explanation.** Fitting a transport law predicts; it does not, by itself, explain. That gap is permanent
  and deserves a sentence in every paper.

## L2 · The gaps, itemised

| Gap | What it means | What can be done |
|---|---|---|
| **Discrete/combinatorial structure** | graphs, languages, games, proofs have structure that is not a density | transport *distributions over structures*; mean-field limits of random structures; accept combinatorial hardness |
| **Semantics and meaning** | Shannon information is not meaning; no balance law conserves sense | stay silent, or model meaning statistically (embeddings) while saying it is not conserved |
| **Purpose, norms, agency** | goals are not conserved quantities; "should" is not a drift term | use optimality/control structure for *rational* behaviour, and state that the objective is an assumption |
| **Non-measurable quantities** | no observable ⟹ no density | define the observable first; write the measurement model explicitly |
| **Emergence and information loss** | coarse-graining destroys information; memory and noise are the residue | Mori–Zwanzig-style corrections; never invert a reduced model and claim mechanism |
| **Non-identifiability** | many mechanisms, one transport law | design experiments that separate mechanisms; report a resolution analysis |
| **Mean-field breakdown** | fluctuations dominate in small/finite systems | agent-based or stochastic simulation; fluctuation-corrected closures |
| **Universality over-claiming** | power laws are not the only heavy tails: lognormal, stretched exponential and mixtures are common | fit tails properly; compare families; report model selection |
| **Formal limits** | no theory proves everything about itself; a universal *class of models* is not a *universal theory* | distinguish the toolbox (this atlas) from a theory of everything |
| **Ethics and intervention** | models of people change people; parameters are policy-dependent | give conditional predictions; be explicit about normativity; treat the Lucas critique as a modelling constraint |

## L3 · The four-question test

Before claiming that your problem "is a transport problem", answer these four:

1. **What is conserved** — or conserved up to stated sources and sinks? If nothing, stop.
2. **What is measured**, and how does the measured quantity relate to the modelled density?
3. **What is the geometry** — which metric makes the dynamics a descent, a flow, or a transport?
4. **Where does the continuum/mean-field assumption break**, and what is your fallback?

Answer all four and the machinery of this atlas applies: you may cite classification, well-posedness and numerical
results with confidence. Fail on (2) or (4) and you have a hypothesis, not a result — and the honest move is to
say so.

> [!warning] Trap
> Two seductive errors. **Over-extension**: "everything is diffusion", producing unfalsifiable papers.
> **Under-extension**: "it is only a metaphor", throwing away genuinely transferable theorems. The four-question
> test is the practical middle, and it costs five minutes.

## L4 · Working across fields without fooling yourself

* **Pre-register the mapping**: quantity, flux, driver, metric — written down *before* fitting.
* **State the identifiability plan**: which observation separates your mechanism from its rivals?
* **Test the mean-field condition empirically**: compare continuum predictions with agent or particle simulation
  at several sizes ([why everything flows](01-why-everything-flows.md)).
* **Report the mapping's failures**, not just its fits — failures are where the next model comes from.
* **Keep a falsification list**: what observation would make you abandon the transport description?
* **Separate the four claims in the write-up**: prediction, mechanism, meaning and policy have different evidence
  standards, and mixing them is the most common way good mathematics becomes bad science.

## Checks

1. Give a case where a transport model fits excellently but explains nothing. *(Any power-law tail: several
   mechanisms produce it, so the fit constrains the exponent, not the mechanism.)*
2. Why is "information is conserved" false? *(Information about a system is dissipated at a computable rate, and
   information is not meaning.)*
3. What is the difference between a universal *class of models* and a universal *theory*? *(A class can represent
   many systems while proving nothing about them; a theory makes falsifiable claims.)*
4. Which two questions most often expose over-claiming? *(What is measured? Where does the mean-field assumption
   break?)*

# Recurring mechanisms


# Recurring mechanisms: the shapes that keep coming back

Part [11](../11-universality/index.md) argued that the *same balance law* shows up everywhere. This
part is the other half of the argument: **the same nine mechanisms** ride on top of that law, and once
you can recognise them you can recognise the behaviour of a system you have never met.

They are not models. They are *verbs*: a system settles (**equilibrium**), resists a nudge
(**stability**), changes character when a knob turns (**bifurcation**), lets parts talk to each other
(**nonlinearity**), forgets its beginnings (**chaos**), organises itself at the edge (**criticality**),
looks the same at every zoom (**scaling**), is secretly another problem in disguise (**duality**), and
is pushed around by chance (**noise**).

| # | Mechanism | The question it answers | The signature |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | [Equilibria & critical points](01-equilibria-and-critical-points.md) | Where does it stop? | $\dot{x}=0$; a stationary profile; a balance of fluxes |
| 2 | [Linearisation & stability](02-linearization-and-stability.md) | Does it come back? | eigenvalues of a Jacobian; a growth-rate curve $\lambda(k)$ |
| 3 | [Bifurcation theory](03-bifurcation-theory.md) | When does its character change? | a branch appearing, splitting, or exchanging stability |
| 4 | [Nonlinearity & mode coupling](04-nonlinearity-and-mode-coupling.md) | How do parts talk? | harmonics, cascades, shocks, solitons |
| 5 | [Chaos & predictability](05-chaos-and-predictability.md) | How far can I look ahead? | a positive Lyapunov exponent; a strange attractor |
| 6 | [Phase transitions & criticality](06-phase-transitions-and-criticality.md) | Where does order appear, and how sharply? | diverging correlation length; power-law avalanches |
| 7 | [Scaling & self-similarity](07-scaling-and-self-similarity.md) | What is the shape, independent of size? | exponents; data collapse; $\pi$-groups |
| 8 | [Duality & transforms](08-duality-and-transforms.md) | Is this another problem in costume? | an exact map that swaps hard for easy |
| 9 | [Noise-induced phenomena](09-noise-induced-phenomena.md) | What does chance *do*? | escapes, resonances, noise-selected states |

![**Figure R1.** Two completely different mechanisms, drawn in one frame: the *landscape* view of
settling (left) and the same information as a one-dimensional flow (right). Almost every mechanism in
this part has a landscape version and a flow version.](energy-landscape-stability.pdf)

## How to use this part

* **Diagnostically.** You have a system that does something surprising. Walk the table top to bottom:
  find its equilibria, linearise, ask whether a parameter is near a bifurcation, ask whether the
  nonlinearity is mixing modes, ask whether a Lyapunov time has expired. Nine times in ten the surprise
  is one of these nine.
* **Constructively.** You want to *build* a model with a given behaviour. Go to the
  [intersections map](10-intersections-map.md): combining two mechanisms from this list is the standard
  way new phenomena are discovered — and the standard way papers get titles.
* **Sceptically.** Every mechanism here has a failure mode in which it is *asserted* rather than
  demonstrated: "it's a power law" (not necessarily critical), "it has a butterfly effect" (not
  necessarily chaotic), "it's self-organised criticality" (not necessarily self-organised). Each note
  carries its own suspicion list; the [bibliography](11-bibliography.md) marks which sources are
  primary, review, or popular.

> **The one-sentence version.** A transport system has a small repertoire of behaviours, and the
> mathematical tools for each are mature — so the usual reason a strange behaviour is misdiagnosed is
> not missing theory but a missing translation between the phenomenon and the mechanism.

## Relation to the rest of the atlas

These notes are deliberately *level-crossing*: each one starts as an L1 picture you could tell a
child, and ends at L4 with what a practitioner computes, the traps in the computation, and where the
theory is still open. Links point back to the concrete notes where the mechanism is doing actual work:
[convection](../02-bulk/02-convection-and-buoyancy.md) for bifurcation,
[Turing patterns](../04-interaction/04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md) for mode competition,
[turbulence](../02-bulk/05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md) for cascades,
[anomalous diffusion](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md) for scaling,
[stochastic PDEs](../06-beyond/07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md) for noise,
[delay](../06-beyond/16-delay-and-retarded-transport.md) for memory.
