# Equilibria and critical points


# Equilibria and critical points

> **One-line picture.** A system stops when everything that pushes it balances — and *how* it stops
> tells you what kind of system it is. A bathtub stops because nothing is coming in; a river never
> stops but its shape does; a laser stops pulsing but keeps pumping. Same word, three different
> mathematics.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Balance laws & flux](../01-foundations/01-balance-laws-and-flux.md),
> [Entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Linearisation & stability](02-linearization-and-stability.md),
> [Bifurcation theory](03-bifurcation-theory.md)

## L1 · The everyday version

Put a marble in a bowl. It rattles, then sits still at the bottom. Put it on a dome: it sits, but the
smallest breath sends it away. Both are equilibria — the marble is not moving — but only one is
*stable*, and the difference is the whole subject.

Three things called "equilibrium" must be kept apart, because collapsing them causes real confusion:

1. **Thermodynamic equilibrium** — nothing flows, anywhere. The system is maximally disordered: heat
   conduction stops when $T$ is uniform; diffusion stops when $c$ is uniform.
2. **A steady state / non-equilibrium steady state (NESS)** — the *state variables* stop changing, but
   fluxes continue. A river's depth profile is steady while water rushes past; a heated rod's profile
   is steady while heat flows through it; your body is steady while matter and energy stream through it.
3. **A balance of forces** — a mechanical equilibrium that may be neutral (oars, planets) rather than
   attractive.

The first is *static*, the second *dynamic* and alive, the third *neutrally* free. Most interesting
transport is of the second kind, which is why "equilibrium" is a poor name for it: the state is
stationary, not the substance. *Nothing about the fluid element persists; the pattern does.*

## L2 · What the equations say

Steady means the time derivative vanishes — and, just as importantly, so does the net of whatever else
changes the state.

| Setting | Stationary condition | What it looks like |
|---|---|---|
| ODE $\dot{x}=f(x)$ | $f(x)=0$ | a point; the marble's resting places |
| PDE $\partial_t u=\mathcal L u+\mathcal N(u)$ | $\mathcal L u+\mathcal N(u)=0$ | a *profile*: $\mathrm{d}P/\mathrm{d}x=0$ in flow, $\nabla^2T=0$ in conduction |
| Balance with sources | $\nabla\!\cdot\!\mathbf J=\sigma$ | a NESS with $\mathbf J\neq 0$: flux in, flux out, no accumulation |
| Variational | $F'(u)=0$ | a critical point of an energy or entropy functional |
| Discrete/network | mass-balance residual zero | a flow pattern on a graph (Kirchhoff, resistor networks, PageRank) |

Three consequences carry most of the practical weight:

* **A profile is a balance of two mechanisms.** In advection–diffusion the steady state is where
  transport *by* the flow is exactly undone by spreading: $\mathbf v\!\cdot\!\nabla c=D\nabla^2c$,
  giving exponential boundary layers of thickness $D/v$. In a boundary layer it is inertia against
  viscosity; in a Stefan problem, latent heat against conduction. Name the two competing terms and
  dimensional analysis guesses the profile.
* **Stability is a separate question from existence.** A stationary state exists whenever a balance
  can be struck; whether it is *observable* is decided by note
  [2](02-linearization-and-stability.md).
* **Steady state ≠ minimum energy.** A NESS is generally *not* an energy minimum: it minimises
  something else (entropy production, in the near-equilibrium regimes of Onsager; free energy in a
  moving frame for some flows), or nothing at all.

![**Figure R1.** The same stationary point seen two ways. Left: as a critical point of an energy
landscape $V(x)$ — the marble picture, where stability is curvature. Right: as the zero of the flow
field — where "arrows point in" is stability and "arrows point out" is instability. When a system has a
gradient structure, the two pictures are the same theorem.](energy-landscape-stability.pdf)

## L3 · Existence, multiplicity, and counting solutions

For a nonlinear problem "find the steady states" is a global question, and the tools are topological.

* **Variational existence.** If a functional is coercive and weakly lower semicontinuous, minimisers
  exist — that is how one proves existence of equilibrium profiles without solving anything. Convexity
  gives *uniqueness*; loss of convexity is where multiplicity begins.
* **Index and degree theory.** The Poincaré–Hopf theorem says the indices of the zeros of a vector
  field on a closed surface sum to its Euler characteristic, so zeros cannot simply vanish: they move,
  merge, or exit through the boundary. This is a *forecast*: it predicts the
  [bifurcations](03-bifurcation-theory.md) that must exist.
* **Morse theory.** The number of critical points of a smooth function is bounded below by the topology
  of the space it lives on. Multiple steady states are, in this sense, topological rather than
  accidental.
* **Spectral bridging.** Stationary states of $\partial_t u=\mathcal L u+\mathcal N(u)=0$ are fixed
  points of $\mathcal L^{-1}\mathcal N$: an equation on the spectrum, which is why the
  [spectral view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md) is the natural language.
* **Detailed balance.** When true equilibrium exists (no net flux), a stationary Markov distribution
  obeys $\pi_i w_{ij}=\pi_j w_{ji}$. Every departure from this — cyclic fluxes — is the signature of a
  NESS, and a precondition for life-like behaviour: a motor, a pump, a cell.

## L4 · Finding them in practice

1. **Newton–Krylov.** For discretised PDEs the Jacobian is sparse; matrix-free Krylov solves of
   $J\delta=-R$ plus continuation in the parameter is the workhorse. Near a bifurcation the Jacobian is
   singular *by design*, and you must switch to
   [arc-length continuation](../07-numerics/07-nonlinear-solvers-and-continuation.md).
2. **Time marching to a steady state.** Cheapest when a stable state is to be reached, useless when
   the state is unstable, and dangerous when the state is *metastable*: the run looks converged for a
   long time and then leaves.
3. **Spurious discretisation equilibria.** A coarse grid, a limiter or a large time step can create
   fixed points the continuum problem does not have, and destroy real ones. Refine one level and check
   that the state moves by less than an error bar.
4. **Diagnose the converged state.** Report residuals *and* fluxes: a residual of $10^{-9}$ around a
   vanishing flux balance can still be physically wrong.

Traps observed in practice: multiple steady states in the same geometry (high-Reynolds-number
lid-driven cavity flows settle into *different* patterns depending on how they were started);
hysteresis of convection onset; grid-dependent locking of a front; and "converged" profiles that are
converged only in a norm nobody printed.

\newpage

# Linearisation and stability


# Linearisation and stability

> **One-line picture.** Push a system a little and watch: if the push decays, the state is stable and
> you will see it; if it grows, the state is a mathematical ghost. Linearisation is the calculus of
> that one question — and it reaches astonishingly far, because "will the nudge grow?" is also "which
> wavelength will appear?".
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Equilibria & critical points](01-equilibria-and-critical-points.md),
> [Spectral & Green-function view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Bifurcation theory](03-bifurcation-theory.md),
> [Phase transitions & criticality](06-phase-transitions-and-criticality.md)

## L1 · The everyday version

A tall stack of coins either stands or it does not; the difference is not the height but whether a
wobble shrinks or amplifies. Everything in this note answers that question in advance:

* **stability** — wobbles die: the state is what you observe;
* **instability** — wobbles grow until something else takes over (the
  [bifurcation](03-bifurcation-theory.md) story, next note);
* **marginal** — wobbles persist: neutral, and the interesting case, because here the system might do
  either.

In an extended system the wobble is not a single number but a *shape*: a ripple with a wavelength. The
question becomes **which wavelengths grow**, which is why one stability calculation predicts stripes on
a fish, rolls in a heated pan, and jets in the atmosphere.

## L2 · The Jacobian, and the four portraits

Perturb $x\to x^{\ast}+\delta$ in $\dot{x}=f(x)$. Truncating the Taylor series gives the *linearisation*
$\dot\delta=J\delta$ with $J=\partial f/\partial x$ at $x^{\ast}$, whose solution is a sum of exponentials
$e^{\lambda_i t}$ over the eigenvalues of $J$:

* $\mathrm{Re}\,\lambda<0$ for all $i$ → stable (the perturbation dies);
* some $\mathrm{Re}\,\lambda>0$ → unstable (it grows);
* $\mathrm{Re}\,\lambda=0$ → marginal, and linear theory cannot decide: nonlinearity does.

![**Figure R2.** All of stability theory, locally, in four pictures. The pair of eigenvalues decides the
portrait: two real negatives (node), a complex pair with negative real part (spiral), a sign change
(saddle — the decisive case for escape), and a purely imaginary pair (centre — where a nudge neither
dies nor grows).](linear-stability-zoo.pdf)

| Portrait | Eigenvalues | Physical reading |
|---|---|---|
| **Node** | both real, negative | monotone return; relaxation to a steady state |
| **Spiral** | complex, negative real part | damped oscillation; a ringing that fades |
| **Saddle** | real, opposite signs | a watershed; escape over a barrier ([Kramers](09-noise-induced-phenomena.md)) |
| **Centre / marginal** | purely imaginary | a persistent mode — the seed of oscillation and of critical slowing |

For PDEs the same four cases are applied *mode by mode*. Substituting $u=\hat u\,e^{ikx+\lambda t}$
into $\partial_t u=\mathcal L u$ turns an operator into a function of $k$: the **dispersion relation**
$\lambda(k)$. Stability then becomes a picture rather than a number: stable if
$\mathrm{Re}\,\lambda(k)<0$ for every $k$, and the *most unstable* $k$ is the one you will see. Two
canonical shapes keep appearing:

* $\lambda(k)=-Dk^2$ (diffusion): monotonically worse for large $k$ — fine scales are always damped;
* a hump: stable at $k=0$, unstable over a band $k_1<k<k_2$. This is diffusion-driven
  ([Turing](../04-interaction/04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md)) instability, and it is how a
  perfectly uniform state decides on a wavelength — see the right-hand panel of
  [the pattern figure](../13-panta-rhei/02-beauty-and-the-flow.md).

## L3 · Beyond eigenvalues: five refinements that matter

1. **Energy (Lyapunov) methods.** Find a functional $E\ge0$ with $\dot E\le0$ and you have proved
   stability *without* linearising, hence beyond the small-perturbation regime. In fluids this is how
   nonlinear stability of shear flows is established; it usually gives a threshold *below* the spectral
   one — conservative, but rigorous.
2. **Non-normality and transient growth.** When the eigenvectors of $J$ are nearly parallel (shear
   flows, reaction networks), perturbations can grow enormously *before* eventually decaying: a
   transient amplification that awakens nonlinearity and bypasses the eigenvalue story entirely. It is
   why a pipe flow is linearly stable yet experimentally turbulent. Diagnose with pseudospectra, not
   eigenvalues alone.
3. **Absolute versus convective instability.** A flow can be stable to a disturbance that grows, but
   only while it is *advected away* — spatially amplifying noise. The distinction decides whether a jet
   radiates or amplifies, and is invisible if you only Fourier-transform in space.
4. **Centre manifolds and amplitude equations.** At marginality, project onto the slow modes: you get a
   small system — the normal form — whose coefficients decide the outcome. This is the bridge from
   linear theory to [bifurcation](03-bifurcation-theory.md) theory.
5. **Conditioning of the answer.** Eigenvalues of a non-normal matrix can be hypersensitive to
   parameters. "The growth rate is 0.01" may be a statement about your matrix, not about the physics.

## L4 · Computing stability without fooling yourself

* **Discretise, then ask.** Assemble the *discretised* Jacobian and compute its rightmost eigenvalues by
  shift-invert [Arnoldi/Krylov](../07-numerics/06-linear-solvers-and-preconditioning.md). Do not compute
  the whole spectrum: you want the edge of the right half-plane, so shift the problem there.
* **Beware spurious modes.** Boundary conditions live *inside* the discretisation; unstable eigenmodes
  that sit on two grid cells or hug the boundary are usually the grid apologising, not the physics.
  Refine and re-check.
* **Delay makes it transcendental** ($\lambda=ae^{-\lambda\tau}$): infinitely many eigenvalues, so the
  stability boundary must come from the characteristic equation or a Krylov approximation of the delay
  operator — see [delay & retarded transport](../06-beyond/16-delay-and-retarded-transport.md).
* **Nonlinear confirmation is mandatory.** An eigenvalue says the state *will* leave; a nonlinear run
  says *where to*. Report both, or you have told half the story.

\newpage

# Bifurcation theory


# Bifurcation theory

> **One-line picture.** Turn a knob slowly and nothing happens, nothing happens, then everything
> happens. Bifurcation theory is the bookkeeping of those moments: a state appears, vanishes, splits, or
> starts to oscillate — and it happens in only a handful of ways.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Linearisation & stability](02-linearization-and-stability.md),
> [Dimensionless numbers](../01-foundations/05-dimensionless-numbers.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Chaos & predictability](05-chaos-and-predictability.md),
> [Phase transitions & criticality](06-phase-transitions-and-criticality.md)

## L1 · The everyday version

A kettle is quiet, then sings, then roars: the *same* geometry, one knob (heat) turned. A thermostat
clicks on and off. A bowed string locks into a note above a critical bow speed. An epidemic either dies
out or takes off as one number crosses one. These are not gradual quantitative changes but changes of
*kind* — new behaviours appearing.

That is a bifurcation: the moment a stationary state loses its job and something else takes it. Because
the mathematics is local, the list is short: **four** elementary ways in continuous systems, plus the
cascade that leads to chaos.

## L2 · The four elementary bifurcations

![**Figure R3.** The elementary bifurcations, stable branches solid. *Saddle-node*: two states are born
together, or annihilate. *Transcritical*: two branches cross and exchange stability — the threshold of
an epidemic, a laser, a harvest. *Pitchfork*: one branch becomes two and the system must choose a side
— buckling, magnetisation, convection. *Hopf*: a fixed point hands over to a self-sustained cycle — the
origin of almost every oscillation, including the onset of your heartbeat.](bifurcation-diagrams.pdf)

| Bifurcation | Normal form | Where you meet it |
|---|---|---|
| **Saddle-node** | $\dot x=r+x^2$ | ignition/extinction, tipping points, [SNIC firing onset in neurons](../08-bridges/03-biology-and-medicine.md) |
| **Transcritical** | $\dot x=rx-x^2$ | epidemic thresholds, laser threshold, harvesting and growth limits |
| **Pitchfork** | $\dot x=rx-x^3$ | [Rayleigh–Bénard onset](../02-bulk/02-convection-and-buoyancy.md), buckling, ferromagnets, symmetry breaking |
| **Hopf** | amplitude² $\propto r-r_c$ | [oscillating chemical reactions](../04-interaction/03-autocatalysis-and-oscillations.md), thermoacoustics, vocal folds, thermo-solutal convection |

Two lessons that generalise to every case:

* **Stability changes hands at the crossing.** You do not need the nonlinearity to find *where* a
  bifurcation is; you need it to find *what* happens next.
* **The direction of the branch decides whether the transition is gentle or catastrophic.** A
  *supercritical* Hopf turns on a small oscillation that grows smoothly; a *subcritical* one jumps, and
  the jump comes with hysteresis — the system remembers how you arrived.

## L3 · Universality, and the reasons to be careful

* **Normal forms.** Near a bifurcation the details are irrelevant: every saddle-node looks like
  $r+x^2$ after rescaling. That is a theorem, not an analogy, and it is the reason this note is short.
* **Symmetry forks in two.** A pitchfork requires a symmetry (mirror, translation). Break it and the
  fork unfolds into a saddle-node plus a continuous branch — which is why imperfect experiments never
  see a clean fork, and why "imperfection sensitivity" is a real design concern.
* **The cascade.** Period doubling $1\to2\to4\to8\cdots$ accumulates geometrically at the Feigenbaum
  rate $\delta\approx4.669$ and ends in chaos — a route whose universality is exact and measurable
  ([logistic map](05-chaos-and-predictability.md)). Intermittency and quasi-periodic routes
  (Ruelle–Takens) are the other standard roads.
* **Early-warning signals.** As a bifurcation approaches, the restoring force weakens, so recovery from
  small kicks slows (**critical slowing down**), fluctuations grow, and the state "flickers" between
  competing attractors. These are measurable leading indicators — used on ecosystems, climate
  subsystems, lasers, power grids — and they are honest only with the caveats of note
  [6](06-phase-transitions-and-criticality.md).
* **Catastrophe theory and its critique.** Thom's classification of elementary catastrophes (fold,
  cusp, swallowtail, butterfly, …) gives the geometry of *all* low-dimensional gradient-like
  multi-stability, and it inspired genuine work (window design; Dalí's late paintings; models of sudden
  behavioural shifts). But the well-known critiques — that applied catastrophe theory claimed more than
  its hypotheses allowed, and that many of its "explanations" were unfalsifiable descriptions rather
  than predictions — still stand (see the [bibliography](11-bibliography.md)). The defensible version is
  local and testable: identify the order parameter, measure the stable branches and the fold locations,
  then predict something not yet observed.

## L4 · Working with bifurcations

1. **Continuation is the tool.** Solve with
   [Newton plus arc-length continuation](../07-numerics/07-nonlinear-solvers-and-continuation.md) so
   that branches can be traced *through* folds, where the parameter cannot serve as the marching
   variable.
2. **Track the spectrum.** Monitor $\max\mathrm{Re}\,\lambda$ along a branch: it crosses zero exactly at
   the bifurcation, and *how* it crosses identifies the type for free — a real eigenvalue through zero
   (saddle-node, pitchfork) or a complex pair (Hopf).
3. **Validate against theory.** At a supercritical Hopf the amplitude should scale like
   $\sqrt{r-r_c}$ and the period should vary smoothly away from onset; at period doubling the ratio of
   successive parameter gaps should approach $4.6692$. Both are checks you can print.
4. **Traps.** Near a fold, the state is hypersensitive to model error: a 5% error in a parameter can
   move the predicted tipping point by far more than 5%, so quoting one without an uncertainty band is
   meaningless. And beware of a "cusp" in one-dimensional data — a fold in a badly chosen projection is
   the most common false positive in the literature.

\newpage

# Nonlinearity and mode coupling


# Nonlinearity and mode coupling

> **One-line picture.** Linear systems add; nonlinear systems **talk**. When two motions interact, they
> create new motions at new scales — and that single fact generates harmonics, turbulence, shocks,
> solitons, patterns and chaos. Nonlinearity is not a difficulty; it is a *mechanism for transfer*.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Constitutive relations](../01-foundations/03-constitutive-relations.md),
> [Higher order & dispersive](../01-foundations/11-higher-order-and-dispersive.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Chaos & predictability](05-chaos-and-predictability.md),
> [Scaling & self-similarity](07-scaling-and-self-similarity.md)

## L1 · The everyday version

Play two notes on a violin: you hear two notes, plus a faint extra tone that neither string produced.
Push two storm waves into each other and they do not pass through unchanged — one may break. Ask a
loudspeaker for more volume and it distorts: new frequencies appear. Add a second chemical that
reacts with the first and the smooth pattern that was forming becomes spots.

In linear systems effects add and pass through each other. In nonlinear systems they **interact**, and
the interaction has three everyday signatures:

1. **New components appear** — harmonics (2×, 3×), subharmonics, combination tones.
2. **Everything saturates or dies** — growth is capped, thresholds appear, runaway ends in a new state.
3. **Energy travels between scales** — a smooth, large-scale motion can feed small-scale roughness
   (the [turbulent cascade](../02-bulk/05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md)), and a small-scale
   irregularity can organise the large scale (pattern formation).

## L2 · Where nonlinearity comes from, and what it does

| Source | Example | Immediate consequence |
|---|---|---|
| State-dependent transport coefficient | $k(T)$, $\mu(\dot\gamma)$, $D(c)$ | a linear PDE becomes a nonlinear one: [porous-medium flow](../06-beyond/06-nonlinear-diffusion-and-porous-medium.md) |
| Bilinear reaction | $u\,v$, $u\,v^2$ | excitability, [oscillation](../04-interaction/03-autocatalysis-and-oscillations.md), Turing patterns |
| Inertial advection | $\mathbf v\!\cdot\!\nabla\mathbf v$ | steepening → [shocks](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md); turbulence |
| Feedback and saturation | growth $\dot x=rx(1-x/K)$ | finite carrying capacity; Hopf and limit cycles |
| Thresholds and switches | relay, valve, ignition | multi-stability, [hysteresis](../06-beyond/12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md) |
| Geometric nonlinearity | large deformations, moving boundaries | [free boundaries](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md) behave nonlinearly even for linear physics |

Modes couple when three of them satisfy a **resonance condition**: $k_1\pm k_2=k_3$ *and*
$\omega_1\pm\omega_2=\omega_3$. Everything familiar then follows:

* **Harmonic generation** — one mode feeds its multiples; the spectrum widens.
* **The cascade** — a range of resonances passes energy from large to small scales, producing the
  power-law spectra of turbulence and wave turbulence.
* **Shock formation** — the "fast parts" of a wave catch up the slow parts, so a smooth ramp steepens
  into a jump; only diffusion can round it off, and the whole problem needs a weak-solution framework
  with an entropy condition.
* **Solitons** — when steepening is balanced by *dispersion* instead of dissipation, the hump neither
  breaks nor spreads: a stable, particle-like wave
  ([integrable systems](../06-beyond/14-integrable-systems-and-solitons.md)).
* **Pattern selection** — a band of wavelengths is unstable but only one or a few survive competition;
  which one depends on nonlinear saturation, not on the linear growth rate alone.

![**Figure R4.** Mode coupling made visible. The left panel is a real solution of a two-species
reaction–diffusion system: an initially smooth, seeded field grows into spots and stripes of a
preferred size. The right panel is the reason: only a *band* of wavenumbers grows, and the growth-rate
curve has a maximum. Nonlinearity sets the final amplitude and arbitrates the competition between the
surviving modes.](reaction-diffusion-art.pdf)

![**Animation A4.** A smooth wave steepening into a shock. Nothing is added and nothing
removed: the fast part of the wave catches up the slow part, and at $t=1$ the solution stops
being differentiable. This is nonlinearity with no escape clause.](anim-shock-formation.png)

## L3 · The weakly nonlinear toolkit

When the failure of superposition is *small*, perturbation theory turns a hard PDE into a small set of
equations for the amplitudes:

1. **Rescale** with the small amplitude $\varepsilon$: $u=\varepsilon u_1+\varepsilon^2u_2+\cdots$.
2. **Solve order by order.** At first order you recover the linear problem; at second order the
   nonlinearity acts as a *source* at exactly the resonance conditions.
3. **Impose solvability** (Fredholm alternative: the source must be orthogonal to the adjoint
   homogeneous mode) and out pop **amplitude equations**:
   * pattern amplitude $A$: $\dot A=\mu A-g|A|^2A$ — the Landau equation; $g>0$ gives a gentle
     (supercritical) onset, $g<0$ a jump (subcritical);
   * three-wave interactions in wave turbulence, and the Zakharov equations for strong wave coupling;
   * the nonlinear Schrödinger and Korteweg–de Vries equations as *universal* envelopes, integrable by
     inverse scattering, with solitons as the elementary excitations.

Two structural facts worth carrying around:

* **Dimension decides whether resonance is generic.** In one dimension three-wave resonance conditions
  are usually impossible except for special dispersion relations — hence the integrable, soliton-rich
  special cases. In two and three dimensions resonances are plentiful, so energy transfer is generic
  and turbulence (not solitons) is the default.
* **Nonlinearity can create the very conditions for its own breakdown.** Shocks form in finite time
  ($t_s=-1/\min_x\partial_x u$ for inviscid Burgers), which is why "the model stopped working" is often a
  *correct* prediction rather than a numerical failure.

## L4 · Computing with nonlinearity

* **Wider spectra.** Each nonlinear interaction doubles the bandwidth. A grid that resolves the initial
  condition may fail after a few eddy turn-over times; resolve the *developed* spectrum, not the input.
* **Aliasing.** Products of unresolved modes fold back as spurious large-scale structure. Use
  de-aliasing (the 2/3 rule for Fourier methods, or over-integration for collocation) unless you want
  numerical pseudo-phenomena.
* **Shocks need limiters or filters**, and the scheme must be *entropy stable*: honouring the discrete
  analogue of the entropy condition is a stronger requirement than linear stability
  ([limiters & WENO](../07-numerics/04-limiters-tvd-and-weno.md)).
* **Distinguish physical from numerical transfer.** Plot spectra, the spectral flux across scales, and
  the resolved-scale budget; refine and check that the *statistics* are grid-independent. Slope
  agreement between two grids with different resolution can both be wrong in the same way — use
  convergence, not resemblance.
* **Test on known nonlinear solutions.** Solitons, self-similar blast waves
  ([scaling](07-scaling-and-self-similarity.md)), and travelling fronts have exact or near-exact
  forms; they catch nonlinear bugs that linear test problems cannot.

\newpage

# Chaos and predictability


# Chaos and predictability

> **One-line picture.** Chaos is *deterministic stretching and folding*: no randomness in the law, but
> exponential amplification of ignorance in the state. It converts an arbitrarily small uncertainty into
> a large one within a predictable time — the **Lyapunov time** — and that time is a measurable property
> of the system, not a philosophical attitude.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Nonlinearity & mode coupling](04-nonlinearity-and-mode-coupling.md),
> [Random walks, Langevin, response](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Phase transitions & criticality](06-phase-transitions-and-criticality.md),
> [Inverse problems & data assimilation](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)

## L1 · The everyday version

Weather forecasts fail not because the equations are wrong but because the atmosphere *stretches*
differences between two nearly identical states until they become O(1). A butterfly's flap does not
cause the storm; here is the honest version: **two states that agree to within measurement precision
today will disagree completely in a finite, computable time** — about ten days for weather, about a
second for a cup of turbulence, about an hour for the rotation of an irregular moon.

Three things chaos is *not*:

* **not noise** — the rule has no random numbers; rerun the same simulation and you get the same answer;
* **not unpredictability of everything** — *statistics* (averages, distributions, spectra) often remain
  predictable long after individual trajectories are lost, which is why climate is predictable and
  weather is not;
* **not the same as turbulence** — chaos can be low-dimensional (three variables suffice), whereas
  [turbulence](../02-bulk/05-turbulence-and-eddy-diffusivity.md) is spatiotemporal chaos with an
  enormous number of active modes. The *mechanism* (stretching) is shared; the bookkeeping is not.

## L2 · Stretching, folding, and the Lyapunov exponent

Take two copies of a system with initial separation $\varepsilon$. Chaotic flow does two things:
it **stretches** (separations grow) and **folds** (they are bent back into the bounded region where the
motion lives). Stretching alone makes the separation grow as $\varepsilon e^{\lambda t}$, where
$\lambda$ is the **largest Lyapunov exponent**; folding is what keeps the motion bounded while making it
aperiodic and mixing.

* $\lambda\le0$: nearby states converge — predictable, periodic or steady;
* $\lambda>0$: nearby states diverge exponentially — the **predictability horizon** is
  $t_h\sim\frac{1}{\lambda}\ln(\Delta/\varepsilon)$;
* the **Lyapunov time** $t_\lambda=1/\lambda$ is the natural clock: after a few of them, nothing about
  the trajectory survives.

![**Figure R5.** Chaos in the Lorenz system. Left: two trajectories started $10^{-6}$ apart trace the
same butterfly but cease to be the same trajectory. Right: the separation grows exponentially (dashed
line: slope = the Lyapunov exponent $\lambda\approx0.91$) until it saturates at the size of the
attractor. Here the Lyapunov time is about 1.1 time units, so $10^{-6}$ becomes O(1) in about 14 of
them.](chaos-sensitive-dependence.pdf)

![**Animation A5.** Two trajectories started $10^{-6}$ apart, drawn as they evolve. The
separation grows exponentially until it saturates: after the Lyapunov time the two copies
agree about nothing except the statistics.](anim-lorenz-divergence.png)

Lyapunov times across the sciences, as commonly quoted (see the [bibliography](11-bibliography.md) for
provenance) — the spread of *fourteen orders of magnitude* is the point:

| System | Order of the Lyapunov time |
|---|---|
| Rotation of the moon Hyperion (tumbling) | about 36 days |
| Solar system (orbital chaos) | 5 million years (Pluto: 20 Myr) |
| Chemical reaction with oscillations | minutes (5.4 minutes quoted for a specific chaotic reaction) |
| Hydrodynamic ("a cup of water") | about 1–2 seconds |
| Molecular dynamics of argon | $\sim10^{-11}$ s (a few tens of picoseconds) |

The same mathematics appears as a growing, then saturating, separation curve; the practical question is
always *which* exponent and *at what amplitude the saturation sets in*, because that ratio is the
horizon.

![**Figure R6.** The standard road into chaos: the logistic map's period-doubling cascade (left), and the
Lyapunov exponent that *sees* it (right, red where positive). Note the windows inside the chaotic band
where $\lambda<0$ again: order is not abolished, it recurs at infinitely many parameter values. The same
cascade, with the same Feigenbaum constants, appears in a dripping tap, a driven pendulum and a
convection loop.](routes-to-chaos.pdf)

## L3 · The formal apparatus, and what it buys

* **Definitions.** For a flow on a compact invariant set, take the linearised flow along an orbit,
  reorthonormalise repeatedly, and the growth rates are the Lyapunov spectrum
  $\lambda_1\ge\lambda_2\ge\cdots$. Chaos means $\lambda_1>0$; the sum of the exponents is the mean
  divergence of the flow and, by **Pesin's identity**, the sum of the *positive* exponents equals the
  Kolmogorov–Sinai entropy — the rate at which the system creates information about its own initial
  condition.
* **Attractors can be fractal.** The Kaplan–Yorke formula turns the spectrum into an effective
  dimension, which is how a continuous system acquires a non-integer geometry — the strange attractor.
* **Shadowing.** A computed chaotic trajectory is *not* the true one, but the shadowing lemma (and its
  numerical versions) shows it usually follows a *true* trajectory from slightly different initial data.
  This is why chaotic simulations still produce correct *statistics* while saying nothing about the
  particular orbit — the single most useful idea here for anyone who runs a code.
* **Routes.** Period doubling (Feigenbaum universality), intermittency, and quasi-periodicity
  (Ruelle–Takens) are the generic roads; each has its own scaling signatures, all measurable.
* **Control and synchronisation.** A positive exponent is not a sentence: tiny, well-timed parametric
  kicks stabilise a chosen orbit (*chaos control*, OGY), and two chaotic systems can lock their states
  when coupled (*chaos synchronisation*) — the idea behind some secure-communication schemes.

## L4 · Using chaos honestly

1. **Estimate exponents with care.** Integrate the variational (or adjoint) equations and reorthonormalise
   (QR) each step; discard transients; check convergence in time and with initial condition. A single
   finite-time exponent is a number, not a verdict — its *fluctuations* are often as large as its mean.
2. **Never diagnose chaos from a wiggly signal.** Finite data from noisy, non-stationary or
   periodic-with-irregular-amplitude systems can masquerade as chaotic. Use surrogate-data tests, report
   the convergence of $\lambda$, and prefer a mechanism (stretching in a known model) to a visual
   impression.
3. **Separate the two horizons.** There is a *dynamical* horizon (Lyapunov time) and a *model-error*
   horizon (parameter and closure errors growing along the same stretched directions). In practice model
   error dominates: forecasting systems are limited by the second long before the first.
4. **Exploit ensembles.** Since individual trajectories are lost, the useful product is a distribution:
   ensembles quantify the spread, and data assimilation anchors it to observations
   ([inverse problems](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)).
5. **Treat the statistics as the deliverable.** Spectral slopes, transport coefficients and rare-event
   rates are a chaotic system's real output; the individual trajectory is an implementation detail.

\newpage

# Phase transitions and criticality


# Phase transitions and criticality

> **One-line picture.** Sometimes order appears *suddenly* and *collectively*: one number crosses a
> threshold and a whole population of degrees of freedom decides, at once, on a common state. At the
> critical point itself the system has no preferred scale — correlations stretch across the entire
> system, and everything you can measure behaves as a power of the distance from the threshold.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Linearisation & stability](02-linearization-and-stability.md),
> [Entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Scaling & self-similarity](07-scaling-and-self-similarity.md),
> [Collective behaviour & synchrony](../13-panta-rhei/03-collective-behaviour-and-synchrony.md)

## L1 · The everyday version

Heat ice and, at 0 °C, the *whole block* changes character — not gradually, but on one number. Cool a
magnet past its Curie point and suddenly it remembers a direction. Pack more and more cars on a road and
at some density a jam appears, not as a bigger jam but as a *new phase* of traffic
([networks, society & traffic](../08-bridges/09-networks-society-and-traffic.md)).

The common structure: there is a **control parameter** (temperature, density, coupling), an **order
parameter** that is zero in one phase and non-zero in the other, and a **transition** that can be sharp
(second order — continuous order parameter) or abrupt (first order — a jump, with latent heat or
hysteresis). And the most interesting place is not either phase but the **critical point** between
them, where the system looks the same at every magnification.

## L2 · The three signatures to look for

![**Figure R7.** The anatomy of a continuous transition. *Left*: the order parameter vanishes at $T_c$
with a power law, $m\sim(T_c-T)^\beta$. *Centre*: the susceptibility — the response to a small nudge —
diverges, so the system becomes infinitely soft exactly at the threshold. *Right*: below $T_c$ there are
two stable states, and sweeping a field up and down traces a hysteresis loop: the system's state is a
record of its history.](phase-transition-order-parameter.pdf)

1. **Diverging correlation length.** Fluctuations are correlated over a length $\xi$ that grows without
   bound as the control parameter approaches its critical value: $\xi\sim|T-T_c|^{-\nu}$. This single
   fact is why criticality matters in transport — near $T_c$ the system *cannot* be described by local
   laws, because every point is in communication with every other.
2. **Diverging susceptibility.** A small external field produces an enormous, often nonlinear response:
   $\chi\sim|T-T_c|^{-\gamma}$. Practically: the system is hypersensitive to perturbations whose
   wavelength matches the growing correlation dome.
3. **Hysteresis and multi-stability.** In a first-order transition (or a subcritical second-order one),
   two states coexist and the path you took determines the state you see. This is memory of a very
   specific kind — not storage, but branch selection.

Order parameters in transport-flavoured settings (the same mathematics, different nouns):

| System | Control parameter | Order parameter | Sharpness |
|---|---|---|---|
| Ferromagnet | temperature | magnetisation | 2nd order (mean field), fluctuation-changed |
| [Rayleigh–Bénard cell](../02-bulk/02-convection-and-buoyancy.md) | Rayleigh number $Ra$ | magnitude of the flow | 1st order if subcritical; else 2nd |
| Percolation in a porous medium | porosity / occupancy | connected-cluster fraction, conductivity | 2nd order |
| Traffic | density | fraction of cars that are jammed | jamming, with non-eq. hysteresis |
| [Coupled oscillators](../13-panta-rhei/03-collective-behaviour-and-synchrony.md) | coupling | synchrony order parameter | 2nd order at $K_c$ |
| Opinion consensus | imitation rate | consensus fraction | 1st order (with zealots, often abrupt) |

## L3 · Landau, fluctuations, and the renormalisation group

* **Landau theory** writes the free energy as a series in the order parameter,
  $F=\frac{a}{2}m^2+\frac{b}{4}m^4-hm$ with $a\propto(T-T_c)$. Minimising gives the mean-field
  exponents $\beta=\frac12$, $\gamma=1$, $\nu=\frac12$ and the classic square-root onset. It is the
  *first* answer, correct above the upper critical dimension and qualitatively right almost everywhere —
  which is why it is the most-used sentence in the subject.
* **Fluctuations break it.** Mean field ignores that the order parameter is not uniform. The Ginzburg
  criterion compares the fluctuation energy inside a correlation volume with the energy scale of the
  transition; when fluctuations win (low dimension, short-range interactions), the exponents change —
  Ising in 2-D is the canonical case, and the exponents become irrational, not the mean-field halves.
* **Scaling relations and universality.** Exponents are not independent: $\alpha+2\beta+\gamma=2$,
  $\nu d=2-\alpha$, and so on. Universality means that the exponents depend only on dimension, symmetry
  and interaction range — a *classification theorem for whole phenomena*, arrived at through the
  renormalisation group.
* **Finite-size scaling.** In a simulation there is no true divergence: $\xi$ is capped by the box $L$,
  so observables obey $O(L,T)=L^{\,\omega/\nu}f\big(L^{1/\nu}(T-T_c)\big)$ — a *data collapse* that lets
  you extract exponents from small systems ([scaling](07-scaling-and-self-similarity.md)).

## L4 · Criticality without self-deception

**Self-organised criticality (SOC).** Bak, Tang and Wiesenfeld's 1987 sandpile proposal claimed that a
system with slow driving and fast threshold relaxation drives *itself* to a critical state, so power-law
statistics appear with no tuning. It is one of the most seductive ideas in the field — and one of the
most over-claimed:

* **What is solid.** Dissipative threshold dynamics under slow drive really does fluctuate about a
  marginally stable state with scale-free avalanche statistics; the exponents are reproducible for that
  model, and the mechanism (separation of driving and relaxation timescales) is a *real* requirement, not
  an absence of one. The class of models has been applied with genuine success to earthquakes
  (Gutenberg–Richter), forest fires, solar flares, crackling magnets and neuronal avalanches.
* **What is not.** "Power law" does not imply criticality: myriad non-critical mechanisms produce
  power-law-looking histograms — log-normals, mixtures, superpositions of exponentials. "Self-organised"
  is not automatic: the timescale separation is a hidden tuning, and moderate departures from it give
  *different* exponents. And estimates are estimator-dependent: log-binning, finite-size cutoffs and
  subsampling (crucial for neuronal data) can shift exponents enough to change the conclusion. The
  literature's own critical literature — see the [bibliography](11-bibliography.md) — makes this point
  repeatedly and it has not been superseded.
* **The honest protocol.** (i) state the timescale separation you rely on; (ii) show the histogram across
  system sizes and fit with finite-size scaling, not a single log-log line; (iii) show the collapse under
  rescaling; (iv) test at least one non-critical alternative model that reproduces the same statistic;
  (v) report the exponent as a value with an interval and the method that produced it.

![**Figure R8.** SOC in a real simulation: avalanche sizes from a slowly driven 2-D sandpile (left, with
the fitted slope — the tail is cut off by the box size, not by any physical scale) and the pile itself
(right), hovering at the critical slope where one more grain may do nothing at all or rearrange the whole
pile. The finite-size *collapse* of such distributions is the real evidence for scaling, and it is shown
in the [scaling note](07-scaling-and-self-similarity.md).](soc-avalanche-statistics.pdf)

![**Animation A6.** A critical pile at work: grains drop, avalanches ripple out, and the
size distribution on the right assembles itself into a power law while you watch. Nothing is
tuned — the separation of driving and relaxation timescales does all of it.](anim-soc-avalanches.png)

\newpage

# Scaling and self-similarity


# Scaling and self-similarity

> **One-line picture.** A self-similar process looks the same at every magnification, so its *shape* is
> independent of its *size*. That single property is what makes a blast wave, a spreading drop, a
> coastline, an avalanche distribution and a turbulent cascade describable with **one exponent instead of
> a function** — and it is what makes dimensional analysis so unreasonably effective.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Dimensionless numbers](../01-foundations/05-dimensionless-numbers.md),
> [Phase transitions & criticality](06-phase-transitions-and-criticality.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Duality & transforms](08-duality-and-transforms.md),
> [the river as a model](../13-panta-rhei/06-the-river-as-a-model.md)

## L1 · The everyday version

Photograph a bomb blast at $1$ ms and at $10$ ms: apart from scale, the pictures are the same. Look at a
map of a coastline, then zoom in on a bay: same kind of raggedness. Shake a flour-and-water mixture and
the marbling repeats at every size you look. This "same picture, different ruler" property is
**self-similarity**, and it converts a hard function-of-many-variables problem into a one-exponent
problem.

Why it matters in transport: much of what we solve has *no characteristic scale* at some stage of its
life — the blast is past its initial radius but not yet reached its boundary; the avalanche is bigger
than a grain but smaller than the box; turbulence is between the outer and the viscous scales. In that
window, only ratios matter, and ratios of powers are powers.

## L2 · How to find the shape: $\pi$-groups, power laws, similarity

* **Buckingham $\pi$.** Reduce $n$ variables and $m$ dimensions to $n-m$ dimensionless groups. Those
  groups are the *only* arguments the answer can depend on, so the answer as a function of them is a
  theorem with unknown content. With fewer than three groups, you can often guess the function.
* **Scaling ansatz.** Assume the solution depends on $x$ and $t$ only through a combined variable
  $\xi=x/t^{\alpha}$. Substituting into the PDE turns it into an *ordinary* differential equation for
  the profile — the same trick that makes the diffusion kernel, the boundary-layer profile, the Blasius
  solution and the Taylor–Sedov blast all "one-line" results.
* **Power laws.** If a distribution has no scale, the only scale-free density is $P(s)\sim s^{-\tau}$
  (and then the moment exponents obey the scaling relations of note
  [6](06-phase-transitions-and-criticality.md)). The signature is a straight line on log–log axes, with
  a *cutoff* wherever the finite size of the system intervenes.
* **Fractals.** Self-similar sets have non-integer dimension, defined by how the number of boxes scales,
  $N(\epsilon)\sim\epsilon^{-d_f}$ — the geometric cousin of a scaling exponent.

## L3 · Intermediate asymptotics, and the collapse test

Barenblatt's framing is the cleanest one available: a self-similar solution is an **intermediate
asymptotic** — the state of a system after it has forgotten its initial conditions but before it has
felt its boundaries. Two kinds exist: *first kind*, where the exponents follow from dimensional analysis
alone (the diffusion kernel; Taylor–Sedov), and *second kind*, where the exponent is fixed by an
eigenvalue condition of the similarity ODE and cannot be obtained by $\pi$-groups (some fingering,
coalescence and wetting problems).

The operational test is the **data collapse**: suppose observables from systems of different size $L$
obey $P(s,L)=L^{-\theta}f\big(s/L^{D}\big)$. Plot $L^{\theta}P$ against $s/L^{D}$ for every $L$: if the
curves fall on one master curve, you have scaling with exponents $(D,\theta)$, valid across the whole
family — a far stronger claim than one fitted line.

![**Figure R9.** The collapse test. Left: distributions from four different system sizes, each with its own
cutoff. Right: the same data after dividing sizes by $L^{D}$ and rescaling the density — one curve. This is
what "universality" looks like as a figure, and it is the check to run before believing any power-law
exponent.](scaling-collapse.pdf)

![**Animation A7.** Coarsening, with the structure factor beside it: domains merge, and the
peak of $S(k)$ walks steadily to smaller $k$ while the curve keeps its shape — self-similarity
in time, which is the same collapse as in the figure above.](anim-coarsening.png)

## L4 · Doing scaling work that survives scrutiny

1. **Choose scales from physics, not from the data.** Fix $D$ and $\theta$ by an argument (or by theory),
   then collapse; fitting both expanders and exponents to a single dataset is circular.
2. **Report the residuals of the collapse**, not just the picture. A collapse that requires different
   scalings in different decades is not a collapse.
3. **Suspect the alternatives.** Log-normal, stretched-exponential and *mixtures* of simple distributions
   all look straight over two decades. Report at least one competing functional form and compare by
   likelihood or by the collapse quality — this is the standing criticism of the power-law literature.
4. **Watch the cutoff.** Physical cutoffs (box size, correlation length, measurement resolution) are
   often the most informative part of the data: their scaling with $L$ *is* the exponent $D$.
5. **Beware selection effects.** "Scaling" in city sizes, wealth, firm sizes and citation counts is often
   produced by the definition of the unit (how you count) rather than by the dynamics — a caveat that
   applies to transport-adjacent economics as much as to physics.

\newpage

# Duality and transforms


# Duality and transforms

> **One-line picture.** Duality is a *dictionary*: a change of variables, a transform, or an exact map
> between two theories, under which a hard problem becomes an easy one. When you meet a new equation, the
> first professional move is not to discretise it — it is to ask **which bookkeeping makes it simple**.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Spectral & Green-function view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md),
> [Scaling & self-similarity](07-scaling-and-self-similarity.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [the intersections map](10-intersections-map.md),
> [information, statistics & inference](../11-universality/07-information-statistics-and-inference.md)

## L1 · The everyday version

Two descriptions of one thing. Celsius or Fahrenheit: the physics is identical, the arithmetic is not.
A wobbly signal or its list of frequencies: which is "simpler" depends on the question. A queue of cars or
the density of cars: the same traffic, one easy to count, the other easy to differentiate.

Some dualities are mere conveniences. Others are *deep*: two theories that look completely different can
turn out to be the same theory, with strong coupling in one becoming weak coupling in the other — which is
how problems get solved that nobody could solve directly.

## L2 · The everyday duality: a change of variables

| Transform / substitution | Turns this into | Classic use |
|---|---|---|
| Fourier / Laplace | derivatives into multiplication; convolution into product | constant-coefficient linear PDEs, [spectral view](../01-foundations/06-spectral-and-green-function-view.md) |
| Similarity variable $\xi=x/t^{\alpha}$ | PDE into ODE | diffusion kernel, Blasius, Taylor–Sedov |
| Legendre | conjugate pairs; convex functions into dual variables | thermodynamics, [optimal transport](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md) |
| Cole–Hopf $u=-2\nu\,\partial_x\ln\phi$ | viscous Burgers into the heat equation | a genuinely nonlinear equation, linearised exactly |
| Kirchhoff / Madelung | nonlinear diffusion, or a wave, into a hydrodynamic form | porous-medium, quantum hydrodynamics |
| Change of measure (Girsanov, exponential tilting) | a drift into a weight | Feynman–Kac, rare events, [large deviations](../11-universality/07-information-statistics-and-inference.md) |

The pattern to notice: **nonlinearity is often an artefact of the variables.** Burgers' equation is
nonlinear — until you exponentiate. A path-dependent option price is complicated — until you change
probability measure. Choosing variables is a *theorem-producing* activity, not cosmetic tidying.

## L3 · Exact dualities between theories

* **Kramers–Wannier (1941).** The 2-D Ising model at low temperature maps onto *itself* at high
  temperature with $T\leftrightarrow T_c^2/T$: the coupling strength and its dual exchange roles. Because
  the model is self-dual at exactly $T_c$, this pins the critical temperature without solving anything —
  and the same duality later explained why the model can be solved by free fermions.
* **Waves and particles.** Wave–particle duality, the Fourier pairing of position and momentum, and the
  path integral: three costumes of one mathematical structure (a symplectic Fourier transform). Modern
  versions are the workhorses of quantum transport.
* **Holography (AdS/CFT).** A gravitational theory in the bulk is dual to a quantum field theory on its
  boundary: strong coupling on one side is weak and calculable on the other. It is the most ambitious
  duality currently in physics; it is also a genuine computational tool for strongly coupled transport
  (viscosity-to-entropy ratios, quark–gluon plasma).
* **Particle–vortex duality** in 2-D, and the dualities of statistical mechanics generally: an exact
  bijection between configurations, with the dual variables being topological defects (vortices, domain
  walls) of the original.
* **The general moral.** When a problem has a conserved quantity, a symmetry, or a graphical structure,
  there is usually a dual description in which the conserved thing becomes a defect, the symmetry becomes a
  constraint, and the difficulty moves somewhere you can handle.

## L4 · Using transforms without being used by them

* **Boundary conditions travel badly.** Fourier is natural for infinite domains and periodic boundaries;
  on a finite interval with mixed boundary conditions it produces series whose convergence *is* the
  problem. Verify that the transform respects the physics you imposed at the edges.
* **Transforms fail exactly where you need them most.** Cole–Hopf solves viscous Burgers for
  *special* initial data and cannot handle a general flux function; Fourier needs constant coefficients;
  the $J$-integral analogy only holds for a linear material. Know the hypothesis list by heart.
* **Numerically, the transform is a discretisation choice.** Spectral methods *are* the Fourier duality
  implemented on a grid; pseudospectral methods pay for their accuracy with aliasing (see
  [mode coupling](04-nonlinearity-and-mode-coupling.md)). Simulating in the dual variables can be the
  difference between spectral accuracy and no accuracy at all — or between a stable and an unstable scheme.
* **Dual descriptions must agree on observables.** If two descriptions are exact duals, they must predict
  the same measurable quantities. Use this as a test: transport coefficients, correlation functions and
  conservation laws computed in both worlds should match to numerical error. When they do not, you have
  found either a bug or a broken duality — and both are interesting.

\newpage

# Noise-induced phenomena


# Noise-induced phenomena

> **One-line picture.** Add randomness to a nonlinear system and you do not merely blur it: you *change*
> it. Noise can select which state survives, amplify a signal you could not otherwise hear, turn
> symmetric fluctuations into directed motion, and set the rate of events that would otherwise never
> happen. Noise is a mechanism with its own catalogue of effects.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Random walks, Langevin, response](../01-foundations/08-random-walks-langevin-response.md),
> [Fokker–Planck & Kramers](../05-kinetic/03-fokker-planck-and-kramers.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Stochastic PDEs & random media](../06-beyond/07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md),
> [the intersections map](10-intersections-map.md)

## L1 · Five things noise *does*

1. **It melts order.** A double-welled system with modest noise has two peaks in its stationary
   distribution; raise the noise and the peaks merge into one. The "state" was not destroyed by a change
   of parameters but by a change of *randomness*.
2. **It assists weak signals (stochastic resonance).** A signal too small to flip a bistable system does
   nothing when the noise is zero, drowns when the noise is huge — and, at an intermediate noise level,
   produces a clean periodic response. The system's signal-to-noise ratio is *maximal at nonzero noise*:
   less noise is not always better. Proposed for glacial cycles, observed in neurons, lasers, sensors and
   even human perception.
3. **It rectifies (ratchets).** With broken spatial symmetry and fluctuation that is out of equilibrium,
   random kicks produce systematic directed transport — the principle behind molecular motors and
   artificial Brownian ratchets, where the naive second-law objection is answered by the fact that the
   noise is not in equilibrium with the load.
4. **It selects and switches.** In multi-stable systems noise sets the *rate* of transitions between
   states (Kramers' law) and therefore the residence times: chemistry's reaction rates, nucleation of
   droplets, magnetic memory, and the spiking of neurons are all noise-driven escape problems.
5. **It makes bookkeeping exact.** For small systems, fluctuations are not a correction but the physics,
   and relations like Jarzynski's and Crooks' convert nonequilibrium work distributions into free-energy
   differences: thermodynamics becomes *measurable* in single-molecule experiments.

## L2 · The mechanisms in equations

| Effect | Mechanism | Where it shows up |
|---|---|---|
| Noise-induced transition | noise shifts the *maxima* of the stationary density $p\propto e^{-V/D}$ | [chemical bistability](../04-interaction/03-autocatalysis-and-oscillations.md), lasers, climate subsystems |
| Escape / reaction rate | Kramers: $\;k\sim e^{-\Delta V/D}$ (prefactor from curvature) | [nucleation](../04-interaction/09-phase-change-and-stefan.md), protein folding, switching |
| Stochastic resonance | periodic modulation + barrier crossing synchronised by noise | ice ages, sensory neurons, ring-laser gyroscopes |
| Ratchet drift | broken symmetry + nonequilibrium fluctuations | molecular motors, particle separation |
| Noise-induced pattern | multiplicative noise destabilises a uniform state | [stochastic PDEs](../06-beyond/07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md), ecology, vegetation stripes |
| Noise-driven cascade | amplification of rare excursions along unstable directions | [SOC avalanches](06-phase-transitions-and-criticality.md), power grids |

![**Figure R10.** Two faces of noise. Left: raising the noise intensity melts a two-peaked stationary
distribution into a single-peaked one — a *noise-induced transition*, not a parameter change. Right:
stochastic resonance — the signal-to-noise ratio at the signal frequency (computed here from a real
simulation of a periodically forced bistable system) peaks at an intermediate noise level, so an optimal
amount of noise makes a sub-threshold signal detectable.](noise-induced-transitions.pdf)

## L3 · The apparatus: Fokker–Planck, Euler–Maruyama, and large deviations

* **Two languages, one process.** The stochastic ODE (Langevin) and the equation for the probability
  density (Fokker–Planck) are the same statement; noise appearing *multiplied* by the state makes them
  subtly different statements, because the choice of stochastic calculus (Itô vs Stratonovich) is a
  modelling decision about how the noise is correlated in time. Pick by the physics — white-noise idealisation
  or finite correlation time — then be consistent, because the two conventions give different drift.
* **Stationary states are variational.** For additive noise, the stationary density is
  $p(x)\propto e^{-V(x)/D}$: the system occupies states according to an exponential of the landscape. This
  is why "noise melts order" is a *quantitative* statement — the peaks survive until $D$ compares with the
  barrier between them.
* **Rare events are exponential.** Kramers' theory gives the escape rate from a well as
  $k\simeq\frac{\omega_0\omega_b}{2\pi\gamma}e^{-\Delta V/D}$: an exponential in the barrier over the noise,
  with a prefactor from the curvatures. The **mean first-passage time** is the reciprocal of that rate, and
  it is the quantity that matters for reliability, ageing and memory lifetime.
* **Large deviation theory is the rigorous version.** Freidlin–Wentzell theory identifies, in the small-noise
  limit, the *most probable path* out of a basin (the instanton / minimum action path) and proves an
  exponential scaling of probabilities — the same machinery that produces the Arrhenius law as a theorem
  rather than a heuristic.
* **Noise can bifurcate.** The stationary density can change *shape* (peaks appearing or disappearing —
  a phenomenological bifurcation) at a noise value where the deterministic system changes nothing at all.
  It is essential to distinguish this from a dynamical bifurcation of the random attractor: they need not
  coincide, and conflating them produces nonsense.
* **Fluctuation theorems close the books.** Jarzynski's equality $\langle e^{-W/k_BT}\rangle=e^{-\Delta F/k_BT}$
  and the Crooks relation give exact results for driven, far-from-equilibrium systems: irreversibility
  becomes a measurable asymmetry in the statistics of work — with the [arrow of time](../13-panta-rhei/04-irreversibility-and-the-arrow-of-time.md)
  appearing as a ratio of forward and backward probabilities.

## L4 · Simulating noise without fooling yourself

1. **Choose the right integrator and order.** Euler–Maruyama is strong order $1/2$ and weak order 1;
   Milstein and higher-order schemes restore accuracy when the noise is multiplicative; additive-noise
   problems are usually handled by the exponential (exact) integrator for the linear part plus a stochastic
   convolution. Verify strong *and* weak convergence separately — they mean different things.
2. **Do not estimate tails from a histogram.** Rare events are estimated by their *rate*: fit
   $\ln p$ against $1/D$, or use importance sampling / splitting / instanton calculations. A histogram with
   three counts in the last bin is not evidence of anything.
3. **Beware the timestep as a physical parameter.** A too-large $\Delta t$ silently changes the effective
   noise amplitude and the drift (Itô correction), moving the stationary distribution. Refine $\Delta t$
   until the stationary statistics stop changing.
4. **Use common random numbers** for comparisons (same noise realisations across parameter values), and
   multilevel Monte Carlo for expectations of smooth functionals: it converts an $O(\epsilon^{-2})$ cost
   into nearly $O(\epsilon^{-1})$.
5. **Report the noise model, not just its amplitude.** White vs coloured, additive vs multiplicative,
   independent vs shared across components: each changes the answer qualitatively, and in spatially
   extended systems the *spatial* correlation of the noise decides whether patterns are nucleated or merely
   roughened ([stochastic PDEs](../06-beyond/07-stochastic-pdes-and-random-media.md)).

\newpage

# The intersections map


# The intersections map

> **One-line picture.** Every phenomenon in this atlas is (at least) two mechanisms sharing one equation.
> Once you can name the mechanisms separately, you can *build* the phenomenon deliberately: pick a base
> law, add one mechanism from column A and one from column B, check which dimensionless number decides
> the outcome, and the behaviour is usually recognisable before you compute anything.
>
> **Prerequisites.** Notes [1](01-equilibria-and-critical-points.md)–[9](09-noise-induced-phenomena.md)
> of this part — the map below is their intersection table ·
> **Leads to.** [Crossings & cross-fertilisation](../13-panta-rhei/05-crossings-and-cross-fertilisation.md),
> [the bibliography](11-bibliography.md)

## L1 · Why intersections are where the action is

Nothing interesting happens in a one-mechanism system: pure diffusion smooths, pure advection translates,
a pure oscillator oscillates forever. Each *single* mechanism is boringly predictable. Add a second and
the system acquires a *choice* — a threshold, a pattern, a wave, a crisis — and with the choice comes
everything worth studying.

This is not a metaphor. It is a fact about equations: a linear equation is a superposition of independent
modes (note [4](04-nonlinearity-and-mode-coupling.md)), and the moment two terms of different character
appear together their ratio becomes a dimensionless number that *classifies the behaviour*. That is what
all the cards in the [dimensionless-number appendix](../10-appendix/02-dimensionless-number-cards.md) are:
the names of the intersections.

## L2 · Reading the map

![**Figure R11.** The intersections map: rows and columns are the nine mechanisms of this part, the
cells are what appears when both are present in one model. Only the upper triangle is filled
(combinations are symmetric). Colour marks the *kind* of outcome, because that is usually the most
useful thing to know first: something structured appears, a new failure mode appears, memory enters, noise
or feedback becomes a tool, or an exact law constrains what is possible. It is a curated map, not a
measurement — but every cell on it has a literature.](intersections-matrix.pdf)

## L3 · Named combinations: the folk taxonomy

Almost every named phenomenon in transport-adjacent science is a two-mechanism sentence. Learning the
taxonomy is the cheapest form of expertise available.

| Combination | Named phenomenon | The equation that shows it |
|---|---|---|
| nonlinearity + dispersion | **solitons**, cnoidal waves | KdV, NLS ([integrable systems](../06-beyond/14-integrable-systems-and-solitons.md)) |
| nonlinearity + diffusion | **travelling fronts**, Fisher waves | $\partial_tu=u(1-u)+u_{xx}$ ([fronts](../04-interaction/05-fronts-and-travelling-waves.md)) |
| nonlinearity + diffusion + two species | **Turing patterns** | Gray–Scott, Schnakenberg ([reaction–diffusion](../04-interaction/04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md)) |
| nonlinearity + noise | **stochastic resonance**, noise-induced transitions | [note 9](09-noise-induced-phenomena.md) |
| nonlinearity + delay | **delay-induced chaos**, Mackey–Glass oscillations | [delay transport](../06-beyond/16-delay-and-retarded-transport.md) |
| nonlinearity + coupling | **synchronisation**, chimera states | [Kuramoto](../13-panta-rhei/03-collective-behaviour-and-synchrony.md) |
| nonlinearity + feedback | **limit cycles**, multistability, hysteresis | [variational inequalities](../06-beyond/12-variational-inequalities-and-hysteresis.md) |
| memory + diffusion | **anomalous (sub/super) diffusion** | fractional operators ([anomalous diffusion](../03-microscopic/08-anomalous-diffusion.md)) |
| memory + conservation | **non-Markov thermodynamics**, memory-dependent second law | [memory & viscoelasticity](../06-beyond/04-memory-viscoelastic-and-hereditary.md) |
| geometry + randomness | **anomalous transport in random media**, trapping | [homogenisation](../06-beyond/08-homogenization-and-upscaling.md) |
| geometry + coupling | **metastability, clustering on networks** | [networks & traffic](../08-bridges/09-networks-society-and-traffic.md) |
| scaling + noise | **extreme-value statistics, first-passage scaling** | [scaling note 7](07-scaling-and-self-similarity.md) |
| thresholds + slow drive | **self-organised criticality**, avalanches | [criticality note 6](06-phase-transitions-and-criticality.md) |

## L4 · A recipe for building a model on purpose

1. **Write the base balance law** — the one sentence everything else modifies
   ([balance laws](../01-foundations/01-balance-laws-and-flux.md)). Name the density and the flux.
2. **Add exactly one mechanism from the diagonal.** Now you have a working model with a known behaviour:
   diffusion smooths, advection translates, reaction saturates, coupling synchronises.
3. **Add the mechanism you actually care about**, and immediately form the dimensionless number that
   compares it with the first: this is the parameter whose value *classifies* the regime
   ([dimensionless cards](../10-appendix/02-dimensionless-number-cards.md)). If no such number exists,
   the two mechanisms are not in competition — check the units again.
4. **Ask the map what should appear.** Structure? Crisis? Memory? If the model does something the map does
   not predict, you have either made an error or found something — and the map tells you which literature
   to consult in either case.
5. **Verify the intersection experimentally.** Change the mechanism's strength by a factor of ten and
   confirm that the phenomenon follows the dimensionless number rather than the raw parameters. That
   single test distinguishes a real intersection from a coincidence of one parameter set.

Two warnings, both learned the hard way. First, **three-mechanism systems are where surprises live**: the
map's cells are pairwise, but reality is usually a triangle (nonlinearity + noise + delay; geometry +
randomness + coupling), and the triangle is genuinely harder. Second, **do not confuse a naming scheme
with an explanation**: the map tells you where to look, not what *causes* what. Causality still has to be
argued — by intervention, by scaling, or by an experiment.

Three habits make the map pay:

* **Read the cells as *sentences*.** "Nonlinearity × Dispersion = solitons" means: form an equation with
  a steepening term and a spreading term of comparable strength, and the hump becomes a particle. The
  *named phenomenon* is the sentence's subject; the mechanism pair is its grammar.
* **Colour first, details later.** If a problem shows a *new failure mode*, look for a crisis
  intersection (noise, delay, geometry, feedback); if it shows *structure*, look for a pattern
  intersection (nonlinearity, coupling, geometry); if it shows *memory*, look for delay or memory entering
  a transport law.
* **The diagonal is a trap.** "Nonlinearity × Nonlinearity" is not two mechanisms; it is one mechanism
  squared, and the phenomena on the diagonal (mode coupling, mixed noise, nested feedback) are the hardest
  to model because there is no separation of scales to exploit.

\newpage

# Bibliography


# Bibliography

> **How to read this list.** Entries are grouped by theme and marked **[P]** primary literature,
> **[R]** review or monograph, **[S]** sceptical/corrective, **[E]** encyclopaedic reference. Volume and
> page numbers are given where they are standard and stable, but **verify the DOI before citing anything
> in your own work**: this is a map of *where to look*, not a citation database. Where a claim in this
> part rests on a single number (Lyapunov times, exponents, dates), that number is marked "(as quoted)"
> and its source is given, so you can disagree with it deliberately rather than accidentally.

## The river and the idea of change

* **[P]** Heraclitus of Ephesus, fragments on the river (Diels–Kranz B12, B91) — "one cannot step twice
  into the same river". The fragments survive only as quotations by later authors, which is why
  interpretations differ so widely; see Plato, *Cratylus* 402a, and Aristotle's reports in the
  *Metaphysics*.
* **[R]** G. S. Kirk, J. E. Raven & M. Schofield, *The Presocratic Philosophers*, 2nd ed. (Cambridge,
  1983) — the scholarly treatment; use it to avoid the romanticised Heraclitus of popular writing.
* **[P]** Plutarch, *Life of Theseus* — the "Ship of Theseus" puzzle of identity through replacement:
  the philosophical twin of the steady-state river in
  [the river as a model](../13-panta-rhei/06-the-river-as-a-model.md).
* **[R]** I. Prigogine, *From Being to Becoming* (1980); I. Prigogine & I. Stengers, *Order out of Chaos*
  (1984) — dissipative structures for a broad audience; read the sweeping claims with the caveats in
  [irreversibility & the arrow of time](../13-panta-rhei/04-irreversibility-and-the-arrow-of-time.md).

## Equilibria, stability, bifurcation

* **[P]** A. M. Lyapunov (1892), *The General Problem of the Stability of Motion* — the second method,
  and the reason "Lyapunov function" is a verb in this literature.
* **[R]** S. H. Strogatz, *Nonlinear Dynamics and Chaos*, 2nd ed. (2015) — the standard accessible route
  to equilibria, Jacobians, the four portraits, bifurcations and the logistic map.
* **[R]** J. Guckenheimer & P. Holmes, *Nonlinear Oscillations, Dynamical Systems, and Bifurcations of
  Vector Fields* (Springer, 1983) — normal forms, centre manifolds, Hopf theory.
* **[P]** E. Hopf (1942), *Ber. Math.-Phys. Kl. Sächs. Akad. Wiss. Leipzig* 94, 3 — the original Hopf
  bifurcation, giving birth to a limit cycle from a fixed point.
* **[P]** A. M. Turing (1952), "The chemical basis of morphogenesis", *Phil. Trans. R. Soc. B* 237, 37 —
  diffusion-driven instability; the paper that made "which wavelength survives?" a physical question.
* **[P]** R. Thom (1972), *Stabilité structurelle et morphogenèse*; **[R]** E. C. Zeeman (1976),
  "Catastrophe theory", *Scientific American* 234, 65 — source and popularisation.
* **[S]** H. Zahler & H. J. Sussmann (1977), "Claims and accomplishments of applied catastrophe theory",
  *Nature* 269, 759 — the critique that ended the uncritical boom and that later applications still have
  to answer.
* **[R]** M. Scheffer et al. (2009), "Early-warning signals for critical transitions", *Nature* 461, 53 —
  critical slowing down as a measurable leading indicator.

## Chaos, predictability and computation

* **[P]** E. N. Lorenz (1963), "Deterministic nonperiodic flow", *J. Atmos. Sci.* 20, 130 — the butterfly,
  discovered in a model of convection.
* **[P]** M. J. Feigenbaum (1978, 1979), *J. Stat. Phys.* 19, 25 and 21, 669 — universality of the
  period-doubling cascade: $\delta$ and $\alpha$ as measured constants.
* **[R]** J.-P. Eckmann & D. Ruelle (1985), "Ergodic theory of chaos and strange attractors",

* **[P]** E. Ott, C. Grebogi & J. A. Yorke (1990), *Phys. Rev. Lett.* 64, 1196 — chaos control;
  **[P]** L. M. Pecora & T. L. Carroll (1990), *Phys. Rev. Lett.* 64, 821 — chaos synchronisation.
* **[P]** C. Grebogi, S. M. Hammel, J. A. Yorke & T. Sauer (1990), *Phys. Rev. Lett.* 65, 1527 —
  shadowing of numerical trajectories: why chaotic simulations still yield correct statistics.
* **[E]** Lyapunov times as quoted in note [5](05-chaos-and-predictability.md) (solar system
  $\sim10^7$ yr; Pluto $\sim2\times10^7$ yr; Hyperion's rotation $\sim36$ days; chemical chaos
  $\sim5$ min; hydrodynamic $\sim1$ s; liquid argon $\sim10^{-11}$ s). Underlying sources include
  Sussman & Wisdom (1988) on Hyperion's chaotic rotation; the values are safest read as *orders of
## Criticality, scaling and avalanches

* **[P]** P. Bak, C. Tang & K. Wiesenfeld (1987), "Self-organized criticality: an explanation of 1/f
  noise", *Phys. Rev. Lett.* 59, 381; and (1988) *Phys. Rev. A* 38, 364 — the sandpile papers.
* **[R]** G. Pruessner, *Self-Organised Criticality* (Cambridge, 2012) — the technical monograph,
  including where the mechanism really does need tuning.
* **[R]** M. A. Muñoz (2018), "Colloquium: Criticality and dynamical scaling in living systems",
  *Rev. Mod. Phys.* 90, 031001 — modern survey with the debates laid out honestly.
* **[S]** J. Touboul & A. Destexhe (2010), "Can power-law scaling and neuronal avalanches arise from
  non-equilibrium dynamics?", *PLoS ONE* 5, e8982 — a concrete alternative mechanism producing the same
  statistics, which is exactly the test SOC claims must pass.
* **[S]** M. P. H. Stumpf & M. A. Porter (2012), "Critical truths about power laws", *Science* 335, 665 —
  the standard warning against reading exponents off a straight line.
* **[P]** J. M. Beggs & D. Plenz (2003), "Neuronal avalanches in neocortical circuits",
  *J. Neurosci.* 23, 11167; **[R]** J. P. Sethna, K. A. Dahmen & C. R. Myers (2001), "Crackling noise",
  *Nature* 410, 242 — the review that gathered the phenomena under one mechanism.
* **[P]** B. D. Malamud, G. Morein & D. L. Turcotte (1998), *Science* 281, 1840 (forest fires);
  Lu & Hamilton (1991), *Astrophys. J.* 380, L89 (solar flares); the Gutenberg–Richter law for
  earthquake magnitudes (1944) is the historical prototype.
* **[P]** M. E. Fisher & M. N. Barber (1972), *Phys. Rev. Lett.* 28, 1516 — finite-size scaling: the basis
  of the collapse test in note [7](07-scaling-and-self-similarity.md).
* **[P]** L. Onsager (1944), *Phys. Rev.* 65, 117 — the exact two-dimensional Ising solution, the exception
  that proved universality matters.
* **[P]** L. D. Landau (1937), *Zh. Eksp. Teor. Fiz.* 7, 19 — the free-energy expansion in the order
  parameter; **[P]** K. G. Wilson (1971) and L. P. Kadanoff (1966) for the renormalisation-group and
  scaling pictures that corrected it.
* **[P]** G. I. Taylor (1950), "The formation of a blast wave by a very intense explosion",
  *Proc. R. Soc. A* 201, 159 — dimensional analysis, self-similarity, and (famously) an estimate of an
  explosive yield from photographs alone.
* **[R]** G. I. Barenblatt, *Scaling, Self-similarity, and Intermediate Asymptotics* (Cambridge, 1996) —
  the book that makes "self-similar = intermediate asymptotic" precise.

## Noise, and the thermodynamics of small systems

* **[P]** H. A. Kramers (1940), "Brownian motion in a field of force and the diffusion model of chemical
  reactions", *Physica* 7, 284 — the escape-rate formula that still governs nucleation and switching.
* **[R]** H. Risken, *The Fokker–Planck Equation* (Springer, 2nd ed. 1989) — the standard technical
  reference, including the Itô/Stratonovich question in detail.
* **[P]** R. Benzi, A. Sutera & A. Vulpiani (1981), *J. Phys. A* 14, L453 — stochastic resonance,

* **[R]** L. Gammaitoni, P. Hänggi, P. Jung & F. Marchesoni (1998), "Stochastic resonance",

* **[R]** W. Horsthemke & R. Lefever, *Noise-Induced Transitions* (Springer, 1984) — noise as a

* **[R]** M. I. Freidlin & A. D. Wentzell, *Random Perturbations of Dynamical Systems* (Springer, 1984) —

* **[P]** C. Jarzynski (1997), *Phys. Rev. Lett.* 78, 2690; **[P]** G. E. Crooks (1999),

* **[P]** R. Landauer (1961), *IBM J. Res. Dev.* 5, 183 — erasure costs $k_BT\ln2$; verified

* **[P]** J. D. Cole (1951), *Quart. Appl. Math.* 9, 225, and E. Hopf (1950), *Comm. Pure Appl. Math.*

  impassioned van Gogh paintings", *J. Math. Imaging Vis.* 30, 275 — the widely-discussed analysis of
  multiscale luminance statistics in late Van Gogh; read it as a quantitative observation of a perceptual
  effect, not as proof about how the paintings were made.
* **[P]** P. Gray & S. K. Scott (1983), *Chem. Eng. Sci.* 38, 29; **[P]** J. E. Pearson (1993),
  *Science* 261, 189 — the Gray–Scott system and the parameter map of its two-dimensional patterns, the

* **[P]** H. Aref (1984), "Stirring by chaotic advection", *J. Fluid Mech.* 143, 1 — the blinking-vortex
  prototype that produces marbling on purpose, and the basis of the flow artwork in

## How to check a claim in this part

A short protocol, offered because most disagreements in this literature are about method rather than
about physics:

1. **Find the definition.** "Stable", "critical", "chaotic" and "self-organised" all have precise
   meanings and loose uses. Ask which one the claim needs.
2. **Separate a mechanism from a statistic.** A power law, a fat tail or a $1/f$ spectrum is an
   *observation*; the mechanism must be argued separately, and any alternative mechanism that reproduces
   the statistic has to be ruled out.
3. **Look for the collapse.** One dataset with a fitted exponent is weak evidence; a family of datasets
   collapsing under rescaling is strong. Exponents should reproduce across sizes and methods.
4. **Check the separation of scales.** Delay, memory, criticality and homogenisation each require a
   timescale or lengthscale separation. If you cannot state the ratio and check it, the mechanism may
   simply not be operating.
5. **Check the estimator.** Log-binning, subsampling, finite windows and surrogate tests all shift
   reported exponents. Report the method with the number, or the number is not usable.
6. **Prefer an intervention.** The strongest evidence for a mechanism is that changing its strength
   changes the phenomenon as predicted — a knob, not a correlation.
