# Panta Rhei


# Panta Rhei

> **πάντα ῥεῖ** — *all things flow*. Attributed to Heraclitus of Ephesus (c. 535–475 BC).
>
> "No man ever steps in the same river twice, for it is not the same river and he is not the same man."
>
> The atlas takes this as a **working hypothesis, not a slogan**. The hypothesis has a provable core
> (a quantity can be counted and its changes accounted for by movement — the
> [balance law](../01-foundations/01-balance-laws-and-flux.md)), a set of mechanisms that ride on it
> ([part 12](../12-recurring-mechanisms/index.md)), and a boundary beyond which it stops being helpful
> ([the limits of the claim](../11-universality/09-limits-of-the-claim.md)). This part is where the
> hypothesis is *enjoyed* as well as tested.

## Why this name

Panta Rhei is what the atlas actually is. Not a catalogue of equations, but the observation that the
world is made of things *passing through*: water in a river, heat in a wall, cars on a road, opinions in a
population, probabilities in a mind, packets in a fibre. The Greek phrase has the right three-word brevity
for a title, and the right three-thousand-year provenance for a theme that keeps being rediscovered —
by fluid dynamicists, by criticality theorists, by network scientists, by anyone who has ever watched a
river and wondered what, exactly, is persisting.

## What is in this part

| # | Note | What it covers |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | [Heraclitus and the river](01-heraclitus-and-the-river.md) | the fragments, the metaphysics, and the honest modern translation of "all things flow" |
| 2 | [Beauty and the flow](02-beauty-and-the-flow.md) | why these patterns please the eye — marbling, Turing patterns, fractals, and the psychology and mathematics of aesthetic pleasure |
| 3 | [Collective behaviour and synchrony](03-collective-behaviour-and-synchrony.md) | fireflies, flocks, grids and brains: coupled oscillators as a transport problem for phase |
| 4 | [Irreversibility and the arrow of time](04-irreversibility-and-the-arrow-of-time.md) | why the flow has a direction, and why the equation has to be told which way |
| 5 | [Crossings and cross-fertilisation](05-crossings-and-cross-fertilisation.md) | the same mechanism solving problems in unrelated fields — with the case studies where it worked and the ones where it was oversold |
| 6 | [The river as a model](06-the-river-as-a-model.md) | a capstone: what "the same river" means mathematically, from steady states to Lagrangian chaos |

![**Figure P1.** Panta Rhei, computed. Two point vortices blinking in turn — an incompressible flow with a
perfectly simple rule — stir tracer into filaments that never repeat. Nothing material persists in this
picture: the dye is always different dye. What persists is the *flow*, and the pattern it keeps producing.
This is chaotic advection, and it is the honest picture behind the phrase "all things flow".](generative-flow-art.pdf)

## The epigraph of the site

> **All things flow — so the only honest way to describe anything is to say what moves, what carries it,
> and what is conserved while it does.**
>
> The Transport Atlas writes that sentence in fifteen hundred ways, and Panta Rhei is the shortest of
> them.

If you are here for the mathematics, start at [the atlas](../00-atlas/index.md). If you are here for the
theme, read the six notes above in order; the first three are about *what flows*, the last three about
*what that costs*: direction, memory, and the impossibility of cleanly separating one field from another.

> **On the moving pictures.** Some phenomena in this atlas *are* motion, and a still frame cannot show
> them, so seven figures are animated: they are numbered **A1–A7** and marked by a small badge in the
> caption. They play as animated WebP images (a GIF fallback of each lives in `figures/`), the print
> edition shows a four-panel filmstrip in their place, and the single-file edition
> (`dist/atlas-standalone.html`) carries them inside the file. Click any figure to see it enlarged;
> press `/` to search, `a` for the atlas, `g` for the connection graph.

# Heraclitus and the river


# Heraclitus and the river

> **One-line picture.** Heraclitus noticed something that mathematics later made exact: in a river the
> *material* is always new while the *pattern* persists. "All things flow" is therefore not a claim that
> nothing lasts — it is a claim about **what kind of thing lasts**, and that question can be answered.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Balance laws & flux](../01-foundations/01-balance-laws-and-flux.md),
> [Equilibria & critical points](../12-recurring-mechanisms/01-equilibria-and-critical-points.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [The river as a model](06-the-river-as-a-model.md),
> [Irreversibility & the arrow of time](04-irreversibility-and-the-arrow-of-time.md)

## L1 · The fragments, honestly

Almost everything "Heraclitus said" reaches us as a **quotation inside someone else's argument** — which
is why the popular version is so much tidier than the evidence.

* **The river.** The two fragments usually cited (Diels–Kranz B12 and B91) say that *upon those who step
  into the same rivers, different and again different waters flow*, and — in the other wording — that one
  cannot step twice into the same river. The first is a claim about the water; the second is a claim
  about *you*. Together they are a statement about identity under replacement.
* **The logos.** Heraclitus' own word for what holds the flow together: a common law or structure that
  makes a changing world intelligible rather than arbitrary. In modern language there is a *rule*
  generating the change, and understanding means grasping the rule, not the material.
* **Fire.** The cosmological picture — fire as the transforming element, change as *exchange at a rate*.
  Behind the poetry there is a quantitative instinct: transformation measured, which is exactly what a
  flux is.
* **The opposition.** Parmenides argued that change is illusion and being is one; Zeno produced his
  paradoxes to defend that; the atomists split the difference by inventing **persistent particles in a
  changing void** — matter that lasts, arrangements that do not. Four coexisting positions in one
  century, and modern physics still uses the atomist compromise as its default language.

## L2 · The transport translation

Philosophy gives the image; transport theory gives the theorem. "Nothing persists except the rule" has
several *distinct* precise versions, and it is worth knowing which one is being used:

| Heraclitean claim | The precise modern statement | Where it lives |
|---|---|---|
| "different and different waters flow" | material elements are not conserved as identities; the *density field* obeys a balance law | [balance laws](../01-foundations/01-balance-laws-and-flux.md), [material derivative](../01-foundations/02-material-derivative.md) |
| "the same river" | a **steady state**: the profile is time-independent although the flux is not | [equilibria](../12-recurring-mechanisms/01-equilibria-and-critical-points.md) |
| "the law that holds it together" | the governing equation, its conserved quantities and its symmetries | [variational principles & Noether](../06-beyond/15-variational-principles-and-noether.md) |
| "you are not the same" | your constituents are exchanged at a rate set by residence times; your persistence is *metabolic* | [biological transport](../08-bridges/03-biology-and-medicine.md) |
| "the river is one thing" | the *attractor* or invariant measure: the statistics that survive irrespective of initial details | [chaos](../12-recurring-mechanisms/05-chaos-and-predictability.md) |

The third row is where modern mathematics does something the poetry cannot: it *proves* the persistence.
Conservation laws follow from symmetries (Noether), steady profiles exist for large classes of equations,
and attractors are unique for wide families of flows. "All things flow" becomes a *list* of what does not.


## L3 · Identity through change, made testable

The Ship of Theseus — a ship whose planks are replaced one at a time — is usually presented as an
unresolvable puzzle. Physics and mathematics each take a side, and neither is mystical:

* **The continuous version.** Replace planks continuously and you get a river: the *object* is a flux
  field plus boundary conditions, and "the same ship" is the *pattern* those boundary conditions enforce.
  Change the pattern (widen the hull) and you have a different ship, even if no plank was swapped at once.
* **The discrete version.** Replace planks one at a time and you have a network whose *nodes* are
  replaced; identity is then carried by the *graph*, not the nodes. A graph can have zero flux and zero
  dynamics and still be the bearer of identity — a reminder that flow is not the only way to persist.
* **The statistical version.** An ergodic system has one long-run distribution: individual trajectories
  never repeat, but the ensemble does. This is the cleanest modern sense of "the same river".
* **The Lagrangian subtlety.** In a real river, parcels of water are stretched into filaments; in a
  chaotic flow, one filament can visit almost every region
  ([the flow artwork](02-beauty-and-the-flow.md)). So *Eulerian* persistence (the pattern) and
  *Lagrangian* persistence (a coherent structure) can come apart: the pattern stands still while every
  element is transported, stretched and mixed.

## L4 · Using the idea without abusing it

1. **Ask "what is invariant?" first.** Before building a model, name the quantities, symmetries and
   patterns that are supposed to persist. If nothing persists, you are not modelling — you are narrating.
2. **Distinguish the three kinds of persistence** — a conservation law, a steady state, an attractor —
   because each demands different mathematics and different evidence. Confusing them is the most common
   flaw in popular "flow" writing.
3. **Beware nominalism about objects.** "Everything is a process" is true at the level of thermodynamics
   and useless at the level of engineering: bridges, cells and contracts persist long enough to be
   *treated as* objects. The honest statement is that objects are *slow variables* of a faster flow.
4. **Respect the measurement problem.** Saying "the river is different every instant" is easy; measuring
   a river requires a **frame** that persists — a weir, a gauge, a coordinate system. Every observational
   claim smuggles in a reference structure that does not flow.
5. **Know the boundary.** Not everything is usefully modelled as a flow of a density: discrete identities
   with semantics, algorithms with branching, decisions with meaning. That boundary is the subject of
   [the limits of the claim](../11-universality/09-limits-of-the-claim.md), and crossing it silently is
   exactly the failure mode this atlas exists to avoid.

The third row is where modern mathematics does something the poetry cannot: it *proves* the persistence.
Conservation laws follow from symmetries (Noether), steady profiles exist for large classes of equations,
and attractors are unique for wide families of flows. "All things flow" becomes a *list* of what does not.

\newpage

# Beauty and the flow


# Beauty and the flow

> **One-line picture.** The pictures in this atlas are not decoration: they are the *only* way most of
> these mechanisms are ever recognised. Flow makes beauty with a small repertoire of tricks — folding,
> symmetry breaking, hierarchy, edge-of-chaos — and human eyes are tuned to read exactly those tricks.
> This note takes the aesthetics seriously, because design is how science is transmitted.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Mixing & chaotic advection](../02-bulk/07-mixing-and-chaotic-advection.md),
> [Reaction–diffusion & Turing](../04-interaction/04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [The river as a model](06-the-river-as-a-model.md),
> [scaling & self-similarity](../12-recurring-mechanisms/07-scaling-and-self-similarity.md)

## L1 · Four ways a flow makes something beautiful

1. **Folding and stretching (marbling).** Chocolate, Turkish paper, cream in coffee, Jupiter's clouds:
   all result from a flow that repeatedly stretches and folds a boundary. The result is an *infinite*
   layered structure with a finite number of material regions — beauty from pure kinematics, no forces
   required.
2. **Symmetry breaking (patterns).** A uniform mixture that *chooses* a wavelength produces spots,
   stripes, hexagons, spirals. The selection is not arbitrary: a growth-rate curve picks a band, and
   nonlinear competition picks the survivor
   ([Turing patterns](../04-interaction/04-reaction-diffusion-and-turing.md)).
3. **Self-similarity (fractals).** Coastlines, ferns, viscous fingering, turbulence, and the branching of
   rivers share the property that the *shape* survives a change of scale
   ([scaling](../12-recurring-mechanisms/07-scaling-and-self-similarity.md)). A fractal is an image with
   no natural zoom level, which is why it holds attention.
4. **Edge of chaos (critical avalanches).** Systems poised at a critical point produce event
   distributions with heavy tails: the eye is drawn to the occasional large event against a background of
   many small ones ([criticality](../12-recurring-mechanisms/06-phase-transitions-and-criticality.md)).

![**Figure P1.** Marbling, computed rather than photographed. Two point vortices blink on and off in turn
in an incompressible flow, and tracer particles — coloured by where they started — are stirred for a few
thousand timesteps. Nothing here is random: the filaments are the *deterministic* consequence of repeated
stretch-and-fold. This is Aref's blinking vortex, and it is the same mechanism that swirls cream into
coffee and then, given time, mixes it away.](generative-flow-art.pdf)

![**Animation A1.** Marbling, in motion: the same two blinking vortices as the figure
above, run for a few thousand timesteps. Watch the boundary being stretched into filaments —
this is how a stirred cup mixes *without* any turbulence at all.](anim-flow-marbling.png)

![**Figure P2.** The other kind of beauty: a Turing pattern grown from two chemicals with a simple
autocatalytic rule (left) and the reason it looks like this (right) — only a band of wavelengths can grow,
so the system selects a size. This is the standard Gray–Scott system, and it is the ancestor of half the
"natural" texture in computer graphics.](reaction-diffusion-art.pdf)

![**Animation A2.** The same pattern growing: seeds are placed, spread, collide and
settle. The wavelength is chosen by the dynamics, not by the seeds — and every spot stops
itself by eating the fuel around it.](anim-turing-growth.png)

## L2 · Why they please: the psychology, briefly and without hand-waving

The most useful account in experimental aesthetics is **processing fluency**: the ease with which a
stimulus is processed is itself *hedonically marked* — fluent processing feels good, and people read that
feeling as information about the object ("this is good, this is true"). Fluency is raised by *good form,
symmetry, figure–ground contrast and prototypicality*, and by the viewer's own experience (repeated
exposure, familiarity).

Two consequences matter here, because both are visible in the figures above:

* **The inverted U.** Complexity hurts twice: too complex and processing is disfluent (hard work, no
  pleasure); too simple and the ease is discounted as uninformative ("of course it's easy, it's plain").
  Beauty peaks between — precisely where a *pattern with structure* lives. Flow systems sit exactly
  there: a marbled image is locally intricate but globally organised, so it is fluent *and* surprising.
* **Fluency is about structure, not about symmetry alone.** A Turing pattern is not symmetric in the
  wallpaper sense; it is *comprehensible* — you can see the rule after a moment. That comprehension
  is the aesthetic pay-off, which is also why the *explanation* panels in this atlas are as attractive
  as the pretty ones.

Add two more ingredients that the fluency account does not fully capture, but that describe what is
happening: **scale-hierarchy** (detail at every zoom level, so exploration never ends — the fractal
appeal) and **motion/flow reading** (humans are extremely good at reading implied motion, which is why a
frozen filament pattern still looks like something pouring).

## L3 · Mathematics inside art, and art inside mathematics

* **Composition by geometry.** Linear perspective (Alberti, Piero della Francesca), proportion systems
  (Polykleitos' *Canon*, Pacioli's *De divina proportione* with Leonardo's illustrations), and the use of
  polyhedra and the camera obscura are mathematics *applied* to art.
* **Symmetry as ornament.** The classification of planar patterns (the 17 wallpaper groups) and of
  frieze patterns is, in practice, a *complete taxonomy of decorative design* — from Moroccan zellige to
  Alhambra tilings to Escher's tessellations. Group theory does not merely describe ornament; it exhausts
  it. Escher's later work goes further, into hyperbolic geometry, after his correspondence with Coxeter.
* **The golden ratio: handle with care.** The claim that ancient art and architecture were *designed*
  around $\varphi$ is repeated far more often than it is evidenced; the standard critiques (notably
  Markowsky) show that many measurements are selective, and that the ratio can be "found" in almost any
  rectangle with sufficient imagination. The defensible statement is that $\varphi$ appears in art that
  *deliberately* uses it (some Renaissance and modernist work), and in mathematics constantly.
* **Fractals and computer art.** Mandelbrot's geometry supplied both a scientific language for natural
  roughness and an aesthetic object (the Mandelbrot and Julia sets). The generative-art tradition —
  L-systems, cellular automata, reaction–diffusion textures, flow-field marbling, and now diffusion
  models — runs entirely on the observation that a simple rule plus iteration produces inexhaustible
  structure. Everything in [this atlas's figure set](reaction-diffusion-art.pdf) is a
  member of that tradition.
* **Mathematics *as* art.** Symmetry, minimality, unexpected necessity ("it *had* to be that"): the
  aesthetic of proof is real, and some of it is the same inverted-U fluency story with a longer training
  period.
* **Quantitative claims about famous paintings.** The best-known example is the analysis of *multiscale
  luminance statistics* in late Van Gogh — swirl patterns whose spatial statistics resemble turbulent
  flow. It is a genuinely interesting observation, and it is routinely over-stated. The honest reading:
  the paintings show *scale-free, directionally coherent structure* consistent with turbulent
  phenomenology; that does not establish that the painter was simulating Navier–Stokes, and it does not
  need to. See the [bibliography](../12-recurring-mechanisms/11-bibliography.md) for the reference and its
  caveats.

## L4 · Making a figure that carries an idea

Practical rules, learned from the figure factory behind this atlas (the scripts are in `scripts/figlib.py`
and `scripts/figpart*.py`, and they are worth reading as an argument about clarity):

1. **One idea per figure, stated in the title.** If the title needs a conjunction, split the figure.
2. **Colour as a *scale*, not as decoration.** Sequential palettes for magnitudes, diverging palettes for
   signed fields, hue only when the quantity is genuinely cyclic (phase, angle). Never encode two
   quantities with two unrelated colour channels unless you provide a legend that explains the mapping.
3. **Contrast beats resolution.** Vector output, thin grid lines, ink-dark axes, one bright accent for
   the quantity that matters. A figure with five bold colours carries nothing.
4. **Annotate the mechanism, not the data.** The arrows and the one-line caption are what convert a plot
   into an explanation; put them where the eye already is.
5. **Show the generative rule when you show a pattern.** The dispersion curve beside the Turing pattern,
   the phase portrait beside the time series: the second panel is the *reason*, and it is what someone
   remembers.
6. **Respect the medium.** SVG for the web, PDF for print, a raster fallback for viewers that cannot
   render either — and check the file sizes, because a beautiful figure that takes four seconds to load
   is a worse figure.

\newpage

# Collective behaviour and synchrony


# Collective behaviour and synchrony

> **One-line picture.** Thousands of independent units with their own rhythms can agree on a common
> rhythm — abruptly, at a critical coupling, and with the same mathematics whether the units are
> fireflies, neurons, generators on a grid, or people clapping. The trick is to stop tracking each unit
> and start tracking the **density of phases**, which is a transport problem.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Phase transitions & criticality](../12-recurring-mechanisms/06-phase-transitions-and-criticality.md),
> [Networks, society & traffic](../08-bridges/09-networks-society-and-traffic.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Crossings & cross-fertilisation](05-crossings-and-cross-fertilisation.md),
> [the river as a model](06-the-river-as-a-model.md)

## L1 · The phenomena

* **Fireflies** in a mangrove forest flash in loose unison, and then in tight unison, without a leader.
* **Pacemaker cells** in the heart fire together; if they fail to synchronise, the heart does not pump.
* **A power grid** holds a common frequency (50 or 60 Hz) across a continent; when synchronism is lost,
  the grid *is* the failure, and the 2003 and 2009 blackouts are studies in desynchronisation.
* **Flocks and schools** align their headings; **crowds** lock their footsteps (the Millennium Bridge
  wobble); **audiences** converge on synchronised applause and then abandon it.
* **Neurons** show oscillations that lock to stimuli and to each other, which is how the brain is
  believed to bind features across regions.

What unites them: many *self-sustained oscillators*, weak *coupling*, and (usually) *heterogeneity* —
no two fireflies or generators have exactly the same natural frequency. Synchrony is not the absence of
disagreement; it is a *collective decision* taken in the presence of it.

## L2 · The Kuramoto model: order from frequency disorder

Set aside the physics of each unit and describe only its **phase** $\theta_i$ (where it is in its cycle),
with a natural frequency $\omega_i$ drawn from a distribution $g(\omega)$, and a sinusoidal coupling to
the mean field:

$$\dot\theta_i=\omega_i+\frac{K}{N}\sum_j\sin(\theta_j-\theta_i).$$

Define the **order parameter** $r$ as the magnitude of the mean of $e^{i\theta}$: $r=0$ means the phases
are scattered, $r\approx1$ means they are locked. The exact result (for an infinite population) is a
sharp threshold:

$$K_c=\frac{2}{\pi\,g(0)},\qquad r\simeq\sqrt{1-K_c/K}\ \ \text{for }K>K_c,$$

so the transition is continuous with the *mean-field critical exponent* $1/2$ — the same square root as
in the [phase-transition note](../12-recurring-mechanisms/06-phase-transitions-and-criticality.md).
Note what $K_c$ depends on: not the mean frequency (a common rotation is unobservable in a phase
comparison) but the **density of oscillators at the mean**, $g(0)$. Rarer agreement at the centre means
harder lock-in.

![**Figure P3.** Kuramoto synchronisation from a real simulation ($N=400$, Lorentzian frequencies, threshold
$K_c=2\gamma$ here $=1$). Left: the order parameter rises from zero at the predicted threshold, following
the theoretical square-root law until finite-size fluctuations blur it. Right: phase snapshots below and
above threshold, coloured by natural frequency — below, a smeared circle; above, a locked cluster with a
tail of still-drifting outliers. That tail is *not* a defect: it is the mathematically necessary
consequence of having frequencies far from the mean.](kuramoto-synchronisation.pdf)

![**Animation A3.** Locking, in real time. Turn the coupling up and a scattered cloud of
phases collapses onto a locked cluster; the order parameter on the right traces the
$\sqrt{1-K_c/K}$ law as it happens, with the threshold exactly where the theory puts
it.](anim-kuramoto-locking.png)

Where the same structure appears (a partial list, because the model is deliberately indifferent to the
substrate):

| System | The oscillators | The coupling | What $r$ would mean |
|---|---|---|---|
| Fireflies | flashing cycles | visual reset by neighbours | fraction of the population in phase |
| Heart / circadian | pacemaker cells, SCN neurons | gap junctions, neurotransmitters | coherent tissue rhythm |
| Power grid | generators (rotor angles) | transmission lines (power flow) | continental frequency lock |
| Neurons | membrane potentials | synaptic currents | a travelling oscillation |
| Crowds | walking gaits | mechanical coupling through the bridge | synchronised footfalls |
| Lasers | cavity modes | light field | phase locking of a laser array |

## L3 · Why this belongs in a transport atlas

Because the thing that actually evolves is a *density*: the distribution of phases, $\rho(\theta,t)$,
obeys an exact **continuity equation** on the circle,

$$\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t}+\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\big[(\omega+K\,r\sin(\psi-\theta))\rho\big]=0,$$

where $(r,\psi)$ is the mean field computed from $\rho$ itself. Synchronisation is therefore a
*self-consistent transport problem*: the flux of phases depends on the distribution, which depends on the
flux. Three consequences follow directly from material already in this atlas:

* **It is a critical phenomenon.** At $K_c$ the phase distribution loses stability of the incoherent
  state in a way that is analysed exactly as in note
  [6 of part 12](../12-recurring-mechanisms/06-phase-transitions-and-criticality.md) — a linear stability
  problem around a uniform state, with $r$ as the order parameter.
* **It has a low-dimensional description.** For Lorentzian frequencies the infinite-$N$ dynamics reduces
  *exactly* to a two-dimensional equation for the mean field (the Ott–Antonsen reduction). This is a
  textbook example of the atlas's central claim: track the density, and a
  $10^{23}$-dimensional problem becomes two equations.
* **Geometry enters through the coupling operator.** Replace the all-to-all sum by a network or a
  nonlocal kernel and the threshold becomes a spectral property of that operator. Degree heterogeneity
  can *lower* the threshold sharply (and, in some degree-correlated networks, produce an explosive,
  first-order transition), while sparse expander-like graphs can synchronise with an asymptotically
  vanishing coupling. The structure of the coupling is therefore a first-class part of the physics, not a
  technical detail.

Two further collective phenomena worth knowing by name:

* **Chimera states.** With nonlocal coupling, one system can split into coexisting coherent and incoherent
  domains: half your fireflies blink together while the other half flash erratically. Discovered as a
  theoretical curiosity, now seen in mechanical, optical and electrochemical systems.
* **Metastability.** Partially locked clusters can form, dissolve and re-form on long timescales, so the
  order parameter fluctuates between several values rather than choosing one. In networks this is the
  bridge to memory and computation: metastable states are what a network can *hold*.

## L4 · Measuring and claiming synchrony honestly

1. **Separate three different things:** *co-occurrence* (events happen near each other), *correlation*
   (signals covary), and *phase locking* (the phase difference is bounded). Only the third is synchrony
   in the technical sense, and a common input — a shared stimulus, a shared driver, a shared noise — can
   produce the first two with no coupling at all.
2. **Estimate phases deliberately.** Hilbert transform, wavelet phase, or an event-based phase from the
   timing of spikes or flashes; each has artefacts for broadband signals. Report the method.
3. **Test significance against surrogates.** Phase-randomised surrogates destroy coupling while preserving
   spectra; if your synchrony index is not significantly above the surrogate band, you have measured
   your own window length. Below $K_c$ the order parameter *fluctuates* like $1/\sqrt{N}$ — finite-size
   noise looks a lot like weak synchrony.
4. **Distinguish a smooth transition from a sudden one.** Where the transition is first-order (explosive),
   early-warning logic from [bifurcation theory](../12-recurring-mechanisms/03-bifurcation-theory.md)
   applies to the *synchronised* branch, not the incoherent one, and hysteresis means the response
   depends on history.
5. **Be sceptical of famous anecdotes.** The synchrony literature contains celebrated effects that failed
   replication (human menstrual synchrony is the standard cautionary case), and effects that are real but
   explained by a shared external driver (the Millennium Bridge wobble is genuinely driven by footfall
   coupling, but the 50 Hz grid frequency is *imposed* by control loops as much as it emerges). Name the
   coupling mechanism or say plainly that you have not identified one.
6. **Remember the engineering reading.** In a power grid, synchrony is not a curiosity but a service:
   loss of phase locking *is* the blackout, and the useful question is how far from the threshold you are
   operating and how the threshold moves under load and network changes.

# Irreversibility and the arrow of time


# Irreversibility and the arrow of time

> **One-line picture.** Panta Rhei has a direction. The equations of transport are usually
> *time-reversible* — reverse the velocities and everything runs backwards — yet nothing in the world
> does, and diffusion even has a preferred direction by construction. The resolution is not a hidden
> force: it is a *boundary condition on the whole system, plus coarse-graining*, and both are physically
> inescapable.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Entropy & variational structure](../01-foundations/07-entropy-and-variational-structure.md),
> [Optimal transport & gradient flows](../06-beyond/10-optimal-transport-and-gradient-flows.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Noise-induced phenomena](../12-recurring-mechanisms/09-noise-induced-phenomena.md),
> [crossings & cross-fertilisation](05-crossings-and-cross-fertilisation.md)

## L1 · The everyday version

Film an egg breaking, then play it backwards: everyone spots the trick instantly. Why? Not because the
microscopic laws forbid reassembly — they do not — but because reassembly requires every atom to be
aimed back with the right momentum, and the *number of microscopic arrangements consistent with*
"a broken egg" is astronomically larger than the number consistent with "an intact egg". Mixing is easy;
un-mixing requires a conspiracy.

That is the whole content of the arrow, and it is worth stating in the transport language of this atlas:

* **Advection alone is reversible.** Turn the velocity field around and the dye returns (shear dispersion
  and reversible flows in microfluidics are real, and they are demonstrations of exactly this).
* **Diffusion is not.** Spreading a concentration gradient over a box and then "un-spreading" it requires
  knowing the fine details of the field to a precision that grows without bound: the reverse problem is
  *ill-posed*.
* **Coarse-graining is the step where the arrow enters.** Comparing a fine-grained description (which is
  reversible) with a coarse one (which is not) is a statement about *what information you have*, not
  about a force in nature.

## L2 · Three arrows, two famous objections

| Arrow | Statement | Character |
|---|---|---|
| Thermodynamic | entropy of an isolated system does not decrease | statistical, robust, exception-free in practice |
| Cosmological | the universe started in a low-entropy state and expands | a *boundary condition* on the whole system |
| Psychological / informational | we remember the past and act into the future; entropy estimates increase in the direction we remember | tied to the thermodynamics of memory (Landauer) |

The two classical objections to Boltzmann's derivation are worth stating because they are the reason the
modern statement is statistical rather than deductive:

* **Loschmidt's reversibility objection.** For every trajectory there is an equally valid time-reversed
  one, so any "entropy must increase" conclusion must be an artefact of the assumptions. Answer: it is —
  the assumption being the *initial* condition. Reversal symmetry is not violated; the unlikely branch
  exists and is simply never sampled because its measure is negligible.
* **Zermelo's recurrence objection.** Poincaré recurrence says a bounded, volume-preserving system returns
  arbitrarily close to its initial state — so why does entropy not eventually decrease? Answer: the
  recurrence time is astronomically longer than the age of the universe (and for dissipative systems,
  longer than the system itself survives). Both objections refine the statement without overturning it.

![**Figure P4.** Irreversibility as a transport property. Left: a rough field evolving under the heat
equation — structure dissolving, with the reverse reading never observed. Middle: the reason in the
spectrum, where the decay rate is exactly $Dk^2$, so fine scales die fastest and are hardest to recover.
Right: the coarse-grained entropy, which increases monotonically from the initial condition; the dashed
part is the extrapolation "before", which requires detail no observer has.](irreversibility-arrow-of-time.pdf)

## L3 · The statements a transport theorist can actually use

* **Monotone functionals.** Many transport equations are **gradient flows**: the heat equation, the
  Fokker–Planck equation for an equilibrium system, the Cahn–Hilliard equation, and the porous-medium
  equation are all steepest descent of a free-energy (or entropy) functional in a suitable metric. For
  those, irreversibility is a *theorem*: dissipation of the functional is monotone in time.
* **Reversible versus irreversible operators.** The wave equation, ideal fluids, and the Vlasov equation
  are time-reversal symmetric. Dissipative terms (viscosity, diffusion, collisions) break the symmetry,
  and the broken part is exactly the part that produces entropy. This is why "inviscid" is a modelling
  convenience, never a physical claim about the world.
* **Fluctuation theorems quantify the arrow.** Jarzynski's equality and the Crooks relation make
  irreversibility *measurable*: the asymmetry between the probability of a forward work value and its
  reverse is directly related to entropy production. Far from being a philosophical puzzle, the arrow of
  time in a small system is a number you can extract from experimental trajectories.
* **Coarse-graining creates the arrow.** Eliminate fast variables and you get the Mori–Zwanzig
  equation with a memory kernel and noise
  ([linear response & memory](../01-foundations/09-linear-response-and-memory.md)). The neglected
  information is exactly what makes the reduced description dissipative — memory and noise are the price
  of not tracking everything.
* **Landauer's principle closes the loop with information.** Erasing one bit costs at least $k_BT\ln2$
  of dissipated heat, so information processing is itself an irreversible transport process, verified
  experimentally. This is why "Maxwell's demon" cannot win: the demon's ledger-keeping is a physical
  process with an entropy cost.

## L4 · Working with (not against) the arrow

1. **Do not integrate backwards what physics runs forwards.** The backward heat equation is
   *ill-posed*: small errors in the final state grow exponentially as you go back because the operator
   $e^{+Dk^2t}$ amplifies exactly the fine scales that measurement cannot resolve. Retrodiction of a
   mixed field is always a *regularised inverse problem*
   ([inverse problems](../07-numerics/10-inverse-problems-and-data-assimilation.md)), never a simulation
   run in reverse.
2. **The numerical scheme has its own arrow.** Lax–Richtmyer theory picks the stable direction:
   marching towards a future state is stable for diffusion-like problems, while the same scheme run
   backwards is unstable. Numerical dissipation *adds* irreversibility — one practical reason your
   simulation's entropy budget will not match theory unless you account for the scheme.
3. **Test irreversibility in data properly.** The usable statistics are asymmetries: time-asymmetry of
   cross-correlations, entropy-production estimators, or the ratio of forward/backward path
   probabilities. All are biased by finite windows and non-stationarity, so pair them with surrogate
   tests and report the window lengths.
4. **Distinguish irreversibility from hysteresis.** Hysteresis (a system remembering how it was driven)
   is a *multi-stability* phenomenon and is perfectly compatible with time-reversal symmetry of the
   microscopic laws. A river rising and falling at different stages for the same discharge is hysteresis,
   not entropy production — but a flood wave that never returns is the arrow.
5. **Expect the boundary condition, not the law, to carry the arrow.** Every careful argument ends in the
   same place: the microscopic equations are reversible; what selects a direction is the *special initial
   condition* of the universe plus coarse-graining. Saying "entropy increases because the second law says
   so" is circular; saying "we live on the low-entropy branch and we coarse-grain" is a physical
   statement — and it is the one you should make.

\newpage

# Crossings and cross-fertilisation


# Crossings and cross-fertilisation

> **One-line picture.** The same equation in a different subject is not a coincidence — it is a *free
> research programme*. But the transfer only works when the objects being transported, the conserved
> quantities and the relevant dimensionless numbers actually correspond. This note is about doing it
> consciously: the successes, the conditions, and the failures that are now textbook.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [The intersections map](../12-recurring-mechanisms/10-intersections-map.md),
> [Why everything flows](../11-universality/01-why-everything-flows.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Limits of the claim](../11-universality/09-limits-of-the-claim.md),
> [the river as a model](06-the-river-as-a-model.md)

## L1 · Ideas migrate

The history of transport theory is largely a history of *imports*:

* **Fourier's heat equation** (1822) became diffusion in chemistry, Brownian motion in physics, thermal
  noise in electronics, and — via the Black–Scholes equation — option pricing in finance.
* **The Ising model** (1925, magnets) became opinion dynamics, segregation models, neural networks and
  the mathematics of phase transitions in general.
* **Sandpile criticality** (1987, toy physics) became earthquake statistics, forest-fire models, solar
  flares, neuronal avalanches and (much more speculatively) financial crashes.
* **The SIR epidemic model** (1927) became the mathematics of rumours, computer malware, innovation
  diffusion and social contagion.
* **The Fokker–Planck equation** became the theory of learning and sampling in machine learning, of
  molecular motors, and of population genetics.
* **Optimal transport** (1781, moving earth) became a theory of gradient flows, of image registration,
  and of generative models in machine learning.

Why it works: the objects of a transport model are *generic* — a conserved quantity, a flux, a
constitutive law, a boundary condition. If your new field has those four things, it inherits the whole
mathematical apparatus, including its theorems *and its known failure modes*.

## L2 · Three kinds of crossing (and their different warranties)

| Kind | What is transferred | Warranty | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Isomorphism** | the exact equations | full: the theorems transfer verbatim | heat ↔ diffusion ↔ Black–Scholes (for log-prices, without jumps) |
| **Analogy** | the structure, not the equations | partial: predictions must be re-derived | fluid turbulence ↔ financial volatility cascades |
| **Technique** | a method (asymptotics, scaling, a numerical scheme) | only as good as the mapping it is applied to | dimensional analysis imported from fluid mechanics into biology |

The most common error is mistaking an **analogy** for an **isomorphism**. An isomorphism lets you import
*results*; an analogy lets you import *questions*. Both are useful, but only one lets you skip the work.

## L3 · When the crossing holds, and when it famously does not

**The five conditions for a successful transfer.** Check them *before* writing the paper:

1. **A conserved (or at least balanced) quantity** exists in the target field. If you cannot name it, you
   do not have a transport model — you have a metaphor.
2. **The same symmetry or constraint** holds (a maximum principle, positivity, reciprocity, a divergence
   condition). Laws that depend on a symmetry are only as valid as that symmetry in the new context.
3. **The dimensionless number exists** and has the same meaning. If the Reynolds number of a market
   cannot be defined operationally, the imported phenomenology has no anchor.
4. **The variables are measurable** in the target field, at the relevant scale. Quantities that only exist
   in the model (a "market pressure potential") are where the trouble starts.
5. **The boundary conditions are legitimate.** Equations are imported easily; *boundary conditions* are
   where most transfers fail — an open market, a free surface, and a magnet's edge are not the same
   object, and the solution depends on which one you chose.

**Documented failures and disputes**, included because a part about crossings that only listed successes
would be advertising:

* **Economics and physics-envy.** Borrowing equilibrium thermodynamics or sandpile criticality for
  financial markets produced elegant models and famously weak predictions; the 2008 crisis is the
  standard example of the cost. The mathematics transferred; the *identification of the conserved
  quantity* did not.
* **Criticality claims in neuroscience and ecology.** Power-law-like statistics have been reported
  everywhere, and alternatives (non-critical mechanisms, subsampling artefacts, estimator bias) explain
  many of the same numbers. The dispute is not whether the mathematics is right but whether the mapping
  is.
* **"Quantum" social science and consciousness.** Importing the vocabulary of quantum mechanics into
  psychology and decision theory has produced mostly unfalsifiable models, and it is the classic
  example of transferring the *words* rather than the *structure*.
* **Scale-free networks as a universal explanation.** The discovery was real and important; the
  subsequent habit of finding power laws in every network (often from biased sampling) is the reason the
  statistical caveats of [note 7 of part 12](../12-recurring-mechanisms/07-scaling-and-self-similarity.md)
  exist.

## L4 · A worked mini-example, and a reporting checklist

**Does traffic jam like a fluid or like a sandpile?** Both crossings have been made, and comparing them is
instructive:

* *Fluid crossing.* Treat cars as a compressible continuum with density $\rho$ and flux $q(\rho)$; the
  balance law gives a kinematic-wave (Lighthill–Whitham–Richards) model. Successes: the *upstream
  propagation* of traffic jams at a predictable speed, and shock formation as the mechanism of a jam's
  leading edge — both follow from the [conservation-law theory](../06-beyond/01-conservation-laws-and-shocks.md)
  with no new physics. Failures: the model's velocity is a function of density alone, so it cannot
  produce *spontaneous* jams out of smooth flow, because the underlying instability is missing.
* *Sandpile crossing.* Treat stop-and-go traffic as threshold relaxation with slow drive — a
  [self-organised-criticality](../12-recurring-mechanisms/06-phase-transitions-and-criticality.md)
  structure. Successes: the *power-law-like* distributions of jam sizes and the existence of a critical
  density, observed experimentally in the "jam without a bottleneck" experiments. Failures: exponents are
  not universal, and the "self-organised" part is fragile — the timescale separation is imposed by
  drivers, not guaranteed by the dynamics.
* *The lesson.* Each crossing captured something the other missed, and the decisive test was an
  **intervention**: change the driver behaviour (the coupling) and see whether the predicted scaling
  survives. That is the protocol.

**Reporting checklist for any imported mechanism.** (i) State the mapping explicitly, quantity by
quantity. (ii) State which parts of the mapping are *assumed* rather than derived. (iii) Give the
dimensionless number and its measured range. (iv) Name a prediction that would fail if the crossing is
wrong, and test it out of sample. (v) Report one competing mechanism that explains your data. Do these
five things and cross-fertilisation stops being a gamble and becomes a method.

\newpage

# The river as a model


# The river as a model

> **One-line picture.** A river is the oldest transport model there is, and it contains, in one object,
> almost every phenomenon in this atlas: advection and diffusion, boundary layers at the bed, shear
> dispersion, moving boundaries (the bed moves), hysteresis (a flood rises and falls differently),
> Lagrangian chaos in the eddies, and a steady state that persists while nothing material persists in it.
>
> **Prerequisites.** [Advection](../02-bulk/01-advection.md),
> [Shear dispersion (Taylor–Aris)](../02-bulk/06-shear-dispersion-taylor-aris.md),
> [Mixing & chaotic advection](../02-bulk/07-mixing-and-chaotic-advection.md) ·
> **Leads to.** [Heraclitus and the river](01-heraclitus-and-the-river.md),
> [limits of the claim](../11-universality/09-limits-of-the-claim.md)

## L1 · What you are looking at

Stand on a bridge. The water is *not* the river in the Heraclitean sense, and yet the river is
recognisably there: the same meanders, the same riffles, the same profile of the surface. Four distinct
things are visible simultaneously, and they are the four levels of this atlas:

* **the surface pattern** — a snapshot of a *field* (Eulerian view), the level of the balance law;
* **the motion of a leaf** — a *trajectory*, which will be chaotic in the eddies and may be swept back and
  forth across the channel (Lagrangian view), the level of mixing;
* **the persistence of the shape** — a *steady state*, with flux running through it, the level of
  equilibria and stability; and
* **the statistics of the turbulence** — a *distribution*, stable while every realisation differs, the
  level of criticality and attractors.

The bridge is also doing useful work: it is a *persistent frame*. Without something that does not flow —
a weir, a gauge, a coordinate system — you cannot even state what changed.

## L2 · The river's own physics

A river is a shallow-water flow with a free surface, a deformable bed and a large Peclet number. Each of
the atlas's models corresponds to one aspect:

| Phenomenon | Model | The dimensionless number that decides |
|---|---|---|
| Depth-averaged flow, flood waves | shallow-water (Saint-Venant) equations | Froude number $Fr$ (sub/supercritical) |
| Vertical velocity profile | turbulent boundary layer, log-law near the bed | Reynolds number, roughness $k_s^+$ |
| Spreading of a pollutant | advection–diffusion | Péclet number $Pe=uL/D$ |
| Enhanced downstream spreading | Taylor–Aris shear dispersion | $D_{\rm eff}\sim u^2a^2/D$ (transverse mixing) |
| Separation behind an obstacle, eddies | vorticity dynamics, coherent structures | $Re$, Strouhal |
| Meandering and bank erosion | morphodynamics, moving boundary | sediment transport (Exner, Shields) |
| Residence times, flushing | transport with exchange in dead zones | Damköhler / exchange ratio |

Three of these deserve emphasis because they surprise people:

* **Shear dispersion is the reason rivers spread pollution faster than molecular diffusion by orders of
  magnitude** — the velocity shear in a channel converts transverse diffusion into enormous longitudinal
  dispersion. This is one of the great "emergent transport coefficients" of physics: the coefficient is
  not a material property but a *consequence of the flow geometry*.
* **The bed is a moving boundary.** Sediment transport means the domain changes; the mathematics is
  free-boundary or morphodynamic, and it introduces one of the longest memory timescales in the system
  (decades of bank migration).
* **The residence-time distribution is the river's real output.** What matters for water quality, for
  ecology, and for a tracer experiment is not the mean velocity but the *distribution* of times spent in
  the reach — including the long tail produced by dead zones and bed storage. This is a
  [population-balance](../01-foundations/01-balance-laws-and-flux.md) problem with an age structure, and
  the tail governs everything from eutrophication to microplastic retention.

## L3 · What, exactly, is "the same river"?

Four mathematically respectable answers, in increasing order of sophistication:

1. **The steady state.** Fix the channel geometry, the discharge and the boundary conditions; the profile
   $\mathrm{d}P/\mathrm{d}x=0$ is then a single, stable solution. The river is *that solution*. It is
   provable (existence, uniqueness and stability are theorems under mild conditions), and it is
   independent of which water molecules are present.
2. **The control volume and the boundary conditions.** The river is the pair (geometry + forcing). Change
   the discharge and you have a different river *state*; change the geometry and you have a different
   river. This is why "restoring" a river by re-meandering it is a legitimate physical claim, and dredging
   it is too — both alter the thing that defines identity.
3. **The Lagrangian coherent structure.** In a time-dependent flow, material lines and surfaces act as
   *organising barriers*: coherent structures that attract, repel and separate fluid, moving and
   deforming while remaining recognisable. Meander bends, oxbow formation, and the persistence of
   pollutant filaments are all Lagrangian statements about structures, not about the velocity field at a
   point.
4. **The invariant measure.** For the small-scale turbulence, no trajectory repeats; but the statistical
   distribution of, say, velocity increments is stationary and reproducible. If you want a
   one-sentence statement of what the river "is" that survives every possible flow realisation, this is
   it.

Put together, they give the honest modern reading of Heraclitus: **the river is a solution, a geometry,
a set of organising structures, and a measure — none of which is a thing you can point at, and all of
which are objective.** The water is a *medium*, not the subject.

![**Figure P1.** The same figure as in [beauty and the flow](02-beauty-and-the-flow.md), read differently
here: the filaments are Lagrangian structures. Every tracer particle has followed a different history; the
stripes are the visible consequence of coherent stretching and folding. Change the flow slightly and the
pattern changes, but the *statistics* of the striping are robust — the two statements
(the pattern is a structure; the statistics are an invariant) are simultaneously true, and that is the
whole content of "the same river".](generative-flow-art.pdf)

## L4 · Where rivers are hard, and what that teaches

Rivers are not a solved textbook case; they are the *test bench* of this atlas, and their difficulties are
instructive for everyone else:

1. **Moving boundaries and wetting/drying.** River models must handle a free surface and a bed that moves,
   appear and disappear on a mesh; this is why shallow-water solvers for rivers are so much harder than
   the equations suggest ([free boundaries](../06-beyond/09-free-boundaries-level-sets-and-phase-field.md)).
2. **Hysteresis in the hydrograph.** At the same discharge, a rising flood and a falling flood produce
   *different* water levels — the unsteady term is being measured. This is the cleanest everyday example
   of path-dependence in a transport system, and it is a reminder that "steady state" is a limit, not a
   description.
3. **Closure for roughness.** The bed friction is a *model*, not a law: Manning, Chézy and log-law
   parametrisations are closures whose parameters are calibrated per site and drift with vegetation,
   sediment and season. Predicting a flood is therefore an exercise in uncertainty quantification, not in
   solving an equation.
4. **The long tail of residence times.** Ecological and water-quality conclusions depend on the tails —
   the water that takes weeks, not hours, to leave. Tails are exactly what short measurement campaigns
   under-resolve, which is the same statistical trap as rare-event estimation in
   [noise-driven systems](../12-recurring-mechanisms/09-noise-induced-phenomena.md).
5. **Non-stationarity.** Change the climate and the boundary conditions change, so the historical record
   stops being a sample of the future distribution. This is the applied version of the difficulty
   discussed in [limits of the claim](../11-universality/09-limits-of-the-claim.md): the mathematics is
   stationary, the world is not, and calibrating on a non-stationary world is where models fail quietly.

**Closing thought: the atlas is a river too.** Every note here is a *stationary pattern*: the text stays
still while the reader's attention, the references, and the examples flow through it. That is exactly the
transport model of this note — a steady state with flux, whose identity is carried by its structure and
not its material. The pattern is not the point either: what persists is the *balance law*, and — to use
Heraclitus' word for it — the **logos** that keeps the flow intelligible. Everything else is the next drop
of water.
